### [1] [Bounded-Fidelity Sim-as-Demo-Stage: Mocap Handoff for Governance Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00008)
*Xue Qin,Simin Luan,Cong Yang,Zhijun Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: For LLM-driven robot governance benchmarking, the paper proposes "bounded-fidelity sim-as-demo-stage," which suppresses contact physics inside explicitly bracketed handoff envelopes to eliminate contact-force integration noise that causes audit-chain divergence unrelated to the governance property under test, achieving byte-identical audit chains across 1000 replays.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sim-to-real research prioritizes physics fidelity, judging simulators by how well they reproduce real-world contact dynamics. However, for governance benchmarking of LLM-driven robots—where the goal is to demonstrate that an admission/policy/contract/audit pipeline behaves correctly—contact fidelity at object handoffs (grasp, carry, place) becomes a liability: contact-force integration noise injects audit-chain divergence structurally unrelated to the governance property under test.

Method: The authors propose bounded-fidelity sim-as-demo-stage, a design pattern that suppresses contact physics within explicitly bracketed handoff envelopes while preserving full dynamics elsewhere. The construction uses MuJoCo's mocap-body primitive driven by a 220-line Python adapter invoked by the governance bridge via structured intents. Audit-chain stability is formalized as byte-equality of the hashed event log across replays, with two structural envelope properties identified that imply it.

Result: Across N=1000 replays per posture, the mocap variant produces one distinct audit-chain hash (1000/1000 byte-identical; Wilson 95% CI [0.997, 1.000]); the contact-force baseline produces 584 distinct hashes (993/1000 diverged; CI [0.987, 0.998]). A timestep sweep (1, 2, 5, 10 ms) shows divergence is structural, not a tuning artefact (stays at 0.985 at every timestep). Envelope-edge timing jitter (±10 simulation steps, 1,400 replays) produces 0 divergence, and audit chains remain byte-equal across K in {1,2,3} sequentially handed-off objects (1,500 replays) with sub-linear per-pick-and-place overhead.

Conclusion: The pattern gives benchmark designers audit-chain reproducibility at near-zero engineering cost. The authors also map where it is harmful (sim-to-real validation, policy training, contact-rich tasks) so it is not mis-deployed.

Abstract: Sim-to-real research pursues physics fidelity as a primary objective: simulators are judged by how closely they reproduce real-world contact dynamics. For governance benchmarking of LLM-driven robots, where the simulator demonstrates that an admission/policy/contract/audit pipeline behaves correctly, contact fidelity at object handoffs (grasp, carry, place) becomes a liability: contact-force integration noise injects audit-chain divergence that is structurally unrelated to the governance property under test. We propose bounded-fidelity sim-as-demo-stage, a design pattern that suppresses contact physics within explicitly bracketed handoff envelopes while preserving full dynamics elsewhere. The construction uses MuJoCo's mocap-body primitive driven by a 220-line Python adapter that the governance bridge invokes via structured intents. We formalise audit-chain stability as byte-equality of the hashed event log across replays and identify two structural envelope properties that imply it. Across N=1000 replays per posture, the mocap variant produces one distinct audit-chain hash (1000/1000 byte-identical; Wilson 95% CI [0.997, 1.000]); the contact-force baseline produces 584 distinct hashes (993/1000 diverged; CI [0.987, 0.998]). A timestep sweep (1, 2, 5, 10 ms) shows the divergence is structural, not a tuning artefact: it stays at 0.985 at every timestep. Envelope-edge timing jitter (+/-10 simulation steps, 1,400 replays) produces 0 divergence, and audit chains remain byte-equal across K in {1, 2, 3} sequentially handed-off objects (1,500 replays) with sub-linear per-pick-and-place overhead. The pattern gives benchmark designers audit-chain reproducibility at near-zero engineering cost; we also map where it is harmful (sim-to-real validation, policy training, contact-rich tasks) so it is not mis-deployed.

</details>


### [2] [Multi-Reference Path Tracking Control for an Agricultural Tractor with Nonlinear Model Predictive Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00057)
*Marcel Moll,Timo Oksanen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper presents an NMPC-based path tracking controller for agricultural tractors that embeds multiple segments of a piecewise-linear reference path directly in the objective function, validated in a field test with mean absolute cross-track error of 6.1 cm.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Guiding a tractor along a predefined reference path is a key component of precision agriculture, motivating a robust and accurate path tracking controller.

Method: A Nonlinear Model Predictive Control (NMPC) path tracking controller that incorporates multiple segments of a piecewise-linear reference path directly into the objective function, along with methods for selecting viable reference segments from the full path; the system is controlled via the Tractor Implement Management (TIM) steering interface.

Result: In a field test, the NMPC solver converged on average after 3.45 ms and tracked the curved reference path with a mean absolute cross-track error of 6.1 cm.

Conclusion: The proposed NMPC controller achieves fast convergence and centimeter-level tracking accuracy for tractor path following, demonstrating its suitability for precision agriculture applications.

Abstract: Guiding a tractor along a predefined reference path is a key component of precision agriculture. This study develops a path tracking controller based on Nonlinear Model Predictive Control, which incorporates multiple segments of a piecewise-linear reference path directly into the objective function. In addition, methods for selecting viable reference segments from the full path are presented. The control system is evaluated during a field test with a tractor controlled via the Tractor Implement Management steering interface. The NMPC solver converged on average after 3.45 ms and tracked the curved reference path with a mean absolute cross-track error of 6.1 cm.

</details>


### [3] [Probabilistic Plan Legibility with Off-the-shelf Planners](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00065)
*Michele Persiani,Thomas Hellström*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes a method for legible planning in arbitrary PDDL domains by extending legibility research to classical planning without constructing ad-hoc planners, estimating the observer's perspective via a second-order theory of mind linking planner and observer task spaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Legible planning aims to create plans that best disambiguate their goals from other candidates from an observer's perspective, enabling implicit communication of intent (e.g., in human-robot teaming). Prior legibility research needed extension to classical planning for arbitrary PDDL domains without requiring ad-hoc planners.

Method: The method extends previous legibility research to classical planning for arbitrary PDDL domains without building ad-hoc planners. The observer's perspective is estimated through a second-order theory of mind that connects the planner's and the observer's task spaces.

Result: Benchmark results on several PDDL planning domains generally show that plan legibility is a trade-off with plan efficiency. However, not all planning domains allow legibility to be increased in the same way, and a regularizing factor was proved necessary to balance legibility and efficiency.

Conclusion: Legible planning for arbitrary PDDL domains is achievable without ad-hoc planners, and a regularizing factor is necessary to balance legibility against efficiency, with the trade-off varying across planning domains.

Abstract: Legible planning is the creation of plans that best disambiguate their goals from a set of other candidates from an observer's perspective. In this paper we propose a method for legible planning for arbitrary PDDL domains, by extending previous research on legibility to classical planning without requiring to construct ad-hoc planners. We also discuss how the observer perspective may be estimated through a second order theory of mind that connects the planner's and the observer's task spaces. Our solution can for example be deployed in human-robot teaming scenarios, where an autonomous robot in a team can implicitly communicate its goal by producing legible plans. We present benchmark results on several PDDL planning domains. Our results generally show that plan legibility is a trade-off with plan efficiency, however, not all planning domains allows to increase legibility in the same way and a regularizing factor to balance legibility and efficiency was proved necessary.

</details>


### [4] [HumanoidTTT: Test-Time Capability Reuse for Efficient Humanoid Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00198)
*Jingtai Yang,Yining Wu,Yanjun Li,Zeyu Zhang,Hao Tang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: HumanoidTTT is a framework for test-time capability reuse in continual humanoid control, enabling safe replay of validated motions from certified entry states and adaptively consolidating which capabilities to retain under bounded storage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Humanoid robots increasingly execute diverse motions, but the same capabilities are requested repeatedly during continual deployment. Blind replay of previously successful motions is risky because intervening motions alter the robot's entry state, and bounded storage forces a decision on which validated capabilities are worth keeping.

Method: The framework introduces two components: (1) Selective Full-Motion Reuse, which permits direct reuse of validated complete motions only from certified applicable entry states, letting accepted reuse bypass fresh generation; and (2) Test-Time Capability Consolidation, which decides which qualified capabilities persist in a bounded Full-Motion Store, using subsequent deployment reuse as feedback.

Result: Experiments show zero unsafe accepts and a 16.4x end-to-end speedup over fresh generation. Online consolidation improves avoided generator calls by 13.2 per 200 requests compared with a frozen counterpart.

Conclusion: HumanoidTTT achieves reliable and efficient reuse of validated motion capabilities while adaptively retaining useful capabilities throughout continual humanoid deployment.

Abstract: Recent advances in motion generation and whole-body tracking have enabled humanoid robots to execute increasingly diverse motions, yet the same motion capabilities may be requested repeatedly during continual deployment. Reliable reuse is challenging because intervening motions can change the robot's entry state, making previously successful motions unsafe to replay blindly. Meanwhile, validated capabilities accumulate during deployment, while bounded storage requires deciding which ones are worth retaining. To address these challenges, we present HumanoidTTT, a framework for test-time capability reuse in continual humanoid control. Specifically, we introduce Selective Full-Motion Reuse, which authorizes direct reuse of validated complete motions only from certified applicable entry states, allowing accepted reuse to bypass fresh generation. We further introduce Test-Time Capability Consolidation, which adapts which qualified capabilities persist in a bounded Full-Motion Store using subsequent deployment reuse as feedback. Experiments demonstrate zero unsafe accepts and a 16.4$\times$ end-to-end speedup over fresh generation, while online consolidation improves avoided generator calls by 13.2 per 200 requests over its frozen counterpart. Overall, HumanoidTTT enables reliable and efficient reuse of validated motion capabilities while adaptively retaining useful capabilities throughout continual deployment. Code: https://github.com/AIGeeksGroup/HumanoidTTT. Website: https://aigeeksgroup.github.io/HumanoidTTT.

</details>


### [5] [Real-Time Whole-Body Safe Motion Generation for Multi-Segment Tendon-Driven Continuum Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00220)
*Fangju Yang,Siyi Ma,Tonghao Guan,Tingcong Liu,Hang Yang,Zhengqiang Zhang,Jian S. Dai,Ke Wu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper introduces a unified actuation-space framework for real-time, collision-free motion generation in planar multi-segment tendon-driven continuum robots, combining an energy-based variable-curvature model with a multipoint control barrier function QP.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-time motion generation for tendon-driven continuum robots is challenging because it must account for nonuniform bending (spatially varying tendon spacing and bending stiffness) as well as whole-body collision avoidance under obstacle motion and actuation-velocity limits.

Method: An energy-based variable-curvature model captures spatially varying tendon spacing and bending stiffness and offers analytical Jacobians used for differential inverse kinematics and safety monitoring. A multipoint CBF-QP enforces backbone clearance under obstacle motion and actuation-velocity bounds, with decision dimension scaling only with the number of independently actuated segments.

Result: The model matches GVS references with a maximum curvature error of 5.223 × 10⁻² m⁻¹. Across 100 MuJoCo trials, collision-free success rates reach 96% (static) and 100% (dynamic), versus ~60% and ~80% without CBF constraints. Hole-traversal tests confirm safe motion in constrained environments. With 600 backbone monitoring points, mean control-step time is 6.66 ms.

Conclusion: The proposed actuation-space framework enables accurate variable-curvature modeling and real-time, whole-body safe motion generation for tendon-driven continuum robots.

Abstract: Real-time motion generation for tendon-driven continuum robots requires accurate modeling of nonuniform bending and whole-body collision avoidance. This paper presents a unified actuation-space framework for planar multi-segment tendon-driven continuum robots. An energy-based variable-curvature model captures spatially varying tendon spacing and bending stiffness and provides analytical Jacobians for differential inverse kinematics and safety monitoring. A multipoint CBF-QP enforces backbone clearance under obstacle motion and actuation-velocity bounds, while its decision dimension depends only on the number of independently actuated segments. The model closely agrees with GVS references, with a maximum curvature error of $5.223 \times 10^{-2}\,\mathrm{m}^{-1}$. Over 100 MuJoCo trials, the proposed method achieves collision-free success rates of 96% and 100% in static and dynamic scenarios, respectively, compared with approximately 60% and 80% without CBF constraints. Hole-traversal tests further demonstrate safe motion in constrained environments. With 600 backbone monitoring points, the mean control-step time is 6.66 ms, demonstrating real-time whole-body safe motion generation.

</details>


### [6] [DriftOPD: Sequence-Level Reverse-KL Distillation for One-Step VLA Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00317)
*Youngjun Jun,Kyumin Choi,Youngmin Kim,Seonghyun Jin,Sunwoo Park,Jangho Park,Jong Chul Ye*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DriftOPD is a teacher-free, rollout-free framework that distills sequence-level, long-horizon behavior into one-step VLA action experts using only offline data, via a decomposition of sequence-level reverse KL into a chunk-level reverse-KL term and a future-potential term.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLA models rely on action experts trained at the chunk level under receding-horizon control, which optimizes local action likelihood but ignores long-horizon task success. Sequence-level RL could fix this, but requires costly policy rollouts and closed-loop interaction, impractical for real-robot manipulation.

Method: DriftOPD decomposes the sequence-level reverse KL divergence into (1) a chunk-level reverse-KL term and (2) a future-potential term capturing the long-horizon effect of the current action. The first is optimized with a one-step drifting objective; the second with a Q-function critic learned from offline demonstrations. This yields sequence-level optimization using only offline data and one-step action generation, with no teacher and no rollouts.

Result: Across multiple VLA architectures in simulation and real-world manipulation, DriftOPD generally outperforms existing one-step distillation baselines and achieves task success comparable to multi-step teacher policies.

Conclusion: Long-horizon behavior can be effectively distilled into one-step VLA action experts without online interaction or a separate teacher.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models increasingly rely on action experts that generate short action chunks under receding-horizon control. While chunk-level training is convenient across robot embodiments, it optimizes local action likelihood without explicitly accounting for long-horizon task success. Sequence-level reinforcement learning can address this limitation, but typically requires policy rollouts and closed-loop interaction, which are costly for real-robot manipulation. We introduce DriftOPD, a teacher-free, rollout-free framework for sequence-level on-policy distillation of continuous VLA action experts. We show that the sequence-level reverse Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence decomposes into a chunk-level reverse-KL term and a future-potential term that captures the long-horizon effect of the current action. DriftOPD optimizes these two terms using a one-step drifting objective and a Q-function critic learned from offline demonstrations, respectively, enabling sequence-level optimization with only offline data and one-step action generation. Across multiple VLA architectures in simulation and real-world manipulation, DriftOPD generally outperforms existing one-step distillation baselines while achieving task success performance comparable to multi-step teacher policies. These results demonstrate that long-horizon behavior can be effectively distilled into one-step VLA action experts without online interaction or a separate teacher.

</details>


### [7] [Retrospective Open-Vocabulary Memory for Long-Term Object Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00330)
*Jiaming Wang,Zhiwei Xue,Chen Jizhuo,Peng Shiqi,Harold Soh*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper studies long-term object search in changing environments by framing open-vocabulary memory as probabilistic inference from censored observations, introducing ECROM to estimate long-term concept prevalence and drive active search, plus a controlled HM3D benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robots observing an environment repeatedly but unevenly need to learn where objects usually appear, and standard memory approaches fail to account for the fact that non-detections are only informative in proportion to the robot's actual opportunity to observe a location.

Method: The authors formulate retrospective open-vocabulary memory as probabilistic inference from censored observations, reasoning with evidence per opportunity so detections and non-detections update belief according to observation opportunity. Their method, ECROM, estimates long-term prevalence for concepts specified only at query time and converts the resulting belief into an active-search prior.

Result: On a new controlled long-term benchmark in ten HM3D homes that independently varies object placement and observation opportunity across repeated traversals, ECROM improves support-level AP on held-out queries by 4.5 points and search SPL by 4.2 points over the strongest competing memory in each metric.

Conclusion: Modeling observation opportunity is key to long-term open-vocabulary memory for object search, and the benchmark, dataset, and code will be open-sourced.

Abstract: Long-term object search requires learning where objects usually appear from repeated but uneven observations of a changing environment. We formulate retrospective open-vocabulary memory as probabilistic inference from censored observations, where the key idea is to reason with evidence per opportunity: a detection or non-detection should influence belief only in proportion to the robot's opportunity to observe the corresponding location. We introduce ECROM, which uses this principle to estimate long-term prevalence for concepts specified only at query time and converts the resulting belief directly into an active-search prior. To evaluate this problem, we introduce a controlled long-term benchmark in ten HM3D homes that independently varies object placement and observation opportunity across repeated traversals. ECROM improves support-level AP on held-out queries by 4.5 points and search SPL by 4.2 points over the strongest competing memory in each metric. The benchmark, dataset, and code will be open-sourced.

</details>


### [8] [IndoorBEV: A Lightweight Real-Time LiDAR BEV Perception System for Indoor Mobile Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00355)
*Haichuan Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: IndoorBEV is a lightweight LiDAR perception framework that uses a height-aware BEV representation and geometry-conditioned feature fusion to balance accuracy, latency, and memory for indoor mobile robots.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Efficient indoor LiDAR perception is difficult: mobile robots must interpret cluttered 3D environments under strict latency and memory limits. Point-based and voxel-based methods are computationally heavy, while conventional BEV representations gain efficiency but discard vertical geometric information.

Method: IndoorBEV summarizes vertical point distribution per BEV cell via statistical height features and multi-frequency height encoding, enabling 3D cues to be processed by 2D convolutions. A lightweight encoder fuses geometric features with multi-scale local representations and compact global scene context. Decoupled dense prediction heads output semantic BEV maps and oriented object bounding boxes.

Result: The model has 0.6M parameters and 2.3 MB storage. On an NVIDIA AGX Orin, it uses 21.52 MB GPU memory per inference, achieves 169.6 ms mean latency under a 200 ms deadline, and a 1.8% deadline miss ratio. Evaluations on simulated scenes, real robot scans, and an open-source indoor point-cloud dataset show a favorable accuracy-latency-memory tradeoff.

Conclusion: Explicitly encoding vertical geometry within a compact BEV representation is an effective approach to resource-efficient indoor LiDAR perception.

Abstract: Efficient indoor LiDAR perception is challenging because mobile robots must understand cluttered three-dimensional environments under strict latency and memory constraints. Existing point-based and voxel-based methods often incur substantial computational overhead, whereas conventional bird's-eye-view (BEV) representations improve efficiency at the cost of discarding vertical geometric information. We present IndoorBEV, a lightweight LiDAR perception framework that mitigates this tradeoff through a height-aware BEV representation and geometry-conditioned feature fusion. IndoorBEV summarizes the vertical point distribution in each BEV cell using statistical height features and multi-frequency height encoding, allowing informative three-dimensional cues to be processed efficiently by two-dimensional convolutions. A lightweight encoder then integrates complementary geometric features with multi-scale local representations and compact global scene context. Decoupled dense prediction heads jointly produce semantic BEV maps and oriented object bounding boxes. IndoorBEV contains only 0.6M parameters and requires 2.3 MB of model storage. On an NVIDIA AGX Orin, it uses 21.52 MB of GPU memory per inference and achieves a mean latency of 169.6 ms under a 200 ms perception deadline, with a deadline miss ratio of 1.8\%. Evaluations on simulated scenes, real-world robot scans, and an open-source indoor point-cloud dataset demonstrate a favorable tradeoff among perception accuracy, latency, and memory consumption. These results indicate that explicitly encoding vertical geometry within a compact BEV representation provides an effective approach to resource-efficient indoor LiDAR perception.

</details>


### [9] [DexPolicy: Scheduled Exploration for Trajectory-Guided Dexterous Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00360)
*Haoyu Wang,Siyuan Qian,Yanjun Li,Zeyu Zhang,Yandong Guo,Boxin Shi,Hao Tang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DexPolicy anneals the RL exploration noise scale as an explicit function of training steps, improving deterministic Target success over PPO on five YCB objects and on a real robot arm across three policy-optimization settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dexterous manipulation RL must first discover finger-object contacts and then control the object precisely; the action noise that helps the first goal interferes with the second. In trajectory-guided settings like ViViDex, baseline PPO remains near its initial action noise after 5M steps, so exploration scale needs explicit control.

Method: DexPolicy makes the exploration scale an explicit function of training steps, annealing from broad to narrow while holding loss, architecture, reward, and optimizer fixed. Three policy-optimization settings are studied: PPO, critic-free GRPO continuation, and a flow-parameterized PPO variant (FPO).

Result: On five YCB objects and three seeds, mean deterministic Target success rises from 49.4% to 68.1% (FPO), 14.1% to 45.4% (GRPO), and 32.0% to 35.7% (PPO). On a RealMan RM75 arm with Inspire/RH56 hand, 360 trials over three objects raise mean Target success from 25.0% to 85.0% (FPO), 10.0% to 63.3% (GRPO), and 8.3% to 43.3% (PPO). PPO screening favors noise control over optimizer contraction; the selected schedule beats linear decay with identical endpoints on three objects.

Conclusion: Training return, deterministic Target success, and execution-noise tolerance dissociate, so schedules should be evaluated by terminal task success under the intended execution conditions, per task and policy-optimization setting.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) for dexterous manipulation must discover finger-object contacts and then control the object precisely; the action noise that serves the first goal can interfere with the second. In trajectory-guided settings such as ViViDex, where RL refine hand-object trajectories from human video, our baseline PPO runs end near their initial action noise after 5M steps, motivating explicit control of exploration scale. DexPolicy makes that scale an explicit function of training steps, annealing from broad to narrow exploration while holding loss, architecture, reward, and optimizer settings fixed. We study three policy-optimization settings: PPO, critic-free GRPO continuation, and a flow-parameterized PPO variant (FPO). Across five YCB objects and three training seeds, mean deterministic Target success rises from 49.4% to 68.1% (FPO), 14.1% to 45.4% (GRPO), and 32.0% to 35.7% (PPO). On a RealMan RM75 arm with an Inspire/RH56 hand, 360 trials over three objects raise mean Target success from 25.0% to 85.0% (FPO), 10.0% to 63.3% (GRPO), and 8.3% to 43.3% (PPO), with one trained model per object-method condition. PPO component screening favors noise control over the tested optimizer contraction; the selected PPO schedule yields higher mean Target success than linear decay with the same endpoints on three tested objects. Training return, deterministic Target success, and tolerance to execution noise dissociate; schedules should therefore be judged by terminal task success under the intended execution conditions, per task and policy-optimization setting. Code: https://github.com/AIGeeksGroup/DexPolicy. Website: https://aigeeksgroup.github.io/DexPolicy.

</details>


### [10] [DeepJEPA: Scaling World Models from Within](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00368)
*Zijian Jin,Yunbei Zhang,Yuanzhe Liu,Ming Liu,Baian Chen,Weirui Ye,Shilong Liu,Marco Pavone*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DeepJEPA is a weight-tied JEPA world model that scales transition depth adaptively at test time, spending extra recurrent updates only at decision-critical transitions, achieving strong planning with ~1 update per transition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard world-model planners scale outward (longer rollouts, more samples, longer optimization) while giving every imagined transition identical computation. This uniform depth wastes compute and can degrade planning, because useful refinement concentrates at a few decision-critical events.

Method: DeepJEPA treats transition depth as an inner test-time scaling axis within a weight-tied joint-embedding predictive (JEPA) world model. It learns when an additional recurrent update is worth computing for each candidate and rollout step, allocating more updates selectively.

Result: Across five visual-control settings, DeepJEPA improves or matches the strongest fixed-depth planner while averaging only 1.00-1.26 updates per transition. Extra computation concentrates at contact onset and sustained object interaction, where latent corrections alter elite-set membership and action selection. Representation probes show improved planning does not require uniformly better object-state decodability.

Conclusion: World-model scaling should be reframed as allocating internal computation where it can change the planner's decision: think deeper at decision-critical transitions rather than making every rollout uniformly deeper or longer.

Abstract: World-model planners typically scale outward by rolling farther, sampling more trajectories, or optimizing longer, while assigning the same computation to every imagined transition. We show that making every transition uniformly deeper wastes computation and can degrade planning because useful refinement is concentrated at a small set of decision-critical events. We introduce DeepJEPA, a weight-tied joint-embedding predictive world model that treats transition depth as an inner test-time scaling axis and learns when another recurrent update is worth computing for each candidate and rollout step. Across five visual-control settings, DeepJEPA improves or matches the strongest fixed-depth planner while averaging only 1.00-1.26 updates per transition. Its additional computation concentrates at contact onset and sustained object interaction, where latent corrections can change which candidates enter the planner's elite set and which action is selected. Representation probes further show that improved planning does not require uniformly better object-state decodability. DeepJEPA therefore reframes world-model scaling as a problem of allocating internal computation where it can change the planner's decision: think deeper at decision-critical transitions instead of making every rollout uniformly deeper or longer.

</details>


### [11] [Whole-Body Aerial Grasping and Lifting via Partial Visual Observations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00404)
*Jiaye Jin,Rui Jin,Xinhang Xu,Haotian Jin,Ruiyang Liu,Yi Wang,Jiayan Zhao,Kun Cao,Lihua Xie*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A recurrent teacher-student framework learns a single vision-based policy for aerial grasp-and-lift, achieving up to 99.97% full-task success in simulation via a critical-state curriculum and closure-timing supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Aerial grasp-and-lift requires whole-body coordination across approach, acquisition, and lifting under partial target observations. Early approach failures reduce exposure to later task stages during training, while changing visibility complicates alignment and closure timing during execution.

Method: A privileged teacher is trained with reinforcement learning under a critical-state curriculum that exposes acquisition and lifting states before connecting them to normal approach trajectories. The teacher is distilled into a recurrent visual student that replaces privileged target states with dual-view point clouds and proprioception, integrating observation history for closed-loop control. A dedicated closure objective supervises gripper closure timing from sustained model-defined readiness sequences. The policy jointly commands flight, arm motion, and gripper closure without an explicit task-phase input.

Result: Using a simulated acquisition-and-payload model with condition-triggered latching, virtual attachment, and wrench-based payload loading for short-distance lifting, the frozen student attains full-task success rates of 99.97% (nominal), 97.14% (physics/control-randomized), and 95.84% (additional camera-randomized) across 8,996 completed episodes. The nominal latch-count-weighted mean of per-seed 90th-percentile alignment error at acquisition is 8.12 mm.

Conclusion: The framework enables a single, phase-agnostic, vision-based policy to robustly coordinate approach, acquisition, and lifting for aerial grasping, with high success under nominal and randomized conditions and millimeter-level alignment accuracy.

Abstract: Aerial grasp-and-lift tasks require whole-body coordination across approach, acquisition, and lifting under partial target observations. Early approach failures can limit exposure to later task stages during training, while changing visibility complicates alignment and closure timing during execution. We present a recurrent teacher-student framework that learns a single policy in simulation to jointly command flight, arm motion, and gripper closure without an explicit task-phase input. A privileged teacher learns through reinforcement learning with a critical-state curriculum that exposes acquisition and lifting states before connecting them to normal approach trajectories. Its behavior is distilled into a recurrent visual student that replaces privileged target states with dual-view point clouds and proprioception, integrating observation history for closed-loop control. A dedicated closure objective supervises closure timing from sustained model-defined readiness sequences. Training and primary evaluation use a simulated acquisition-and-payload model with condition-triggered latching, virtual attachment, and wrench-based payload loading for short-distance lifting. Across 8,996 completed simulation episodes under this model, the frozen student achieves full-task success rates of 99.97%, 97.14%, and 95.84% under nominal, physics/control-randomized, and additional camera-randomized conditions, respectively. The nominal latch-count-weighted mean of per-seed 90th-percentile alignment errors at acquisition is 8.12 mm.

</details>


### [12] [Towards a General Humanoid Loco-Manipulation Model via Egocentric Whole-Body Human Data Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00438)
*Chongyang Xu,Zhao Wu,Jin Chen,Yiming Jiang,Jinhui Ye,Yuming Jiang,Shifeng Zhang,Ziliang Feng,Mu Xu,Yilun Chen,Li Lu,Steven C. H. Hoi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper introduces HumanVerse-500, a 500-hour dataset of human loco-manipulation behaviors with synchronized egocentric video and body/hand motion, and λ0, a whole-body humanoid vision-language-action policy trained in three stages to transfer human experience to robot control, achieving state-of-the-art results on SIMPLE and 4 real-world tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Humanoid whole-body manipulation has advanced, and egocentric human video offers scalable supervision without robot operation. However, existing video supervision poorly covers whole-body movement coordinated with hand-object interaction, and humanoid teleoperation data is costly and hard to scale. The paper thus explores whether human experience can support scalable learning of humanoid loco-manipulation.

Method: The authors build HumanVerse-500, a 500-hour dataset of open-world human loco-manipulation collected via a lightweight wearable system that synchronizes egocentric video with body and hand motion. Based on it, they develop λ0, a whole-body humanoid vision-language-action policy trained in three stages: (1) learning interaction from diverse egocentric datasets, (2) coordinating body and hand motion using HumanVerse-500, and (3) adapting to downstream tasks and robot embodiments. λ0 learns a shared representation space for human experience transfer, while domain-specific interfaces handle human-robot state and action differences.

Result: λ0 achieves state-of-the-art performance on SIMPLE and 4 real-world loco-manipulation tasks. The authors further analyze scaling behavior, generalization, and the contributions of each training stage.

Conclusion: Human data can effectively support downstream whole-body humanoid control. Code, models, and data will be released to support further research.

Abstract: Humanoid whole-body manipulation has advanced rapidly, enabling policies to coordinate locomotion, posture, bimanual interaction, and dexterous hand movements. Meanwhile, egocentric human videos provide diverse examples of everyday interactions across objects and scenes, offering scalable supervision without robot operation. However, existing supervision from these videos provides limited coverage of whole-body movement and coordination with hand-object interaction, while obtaining such supervision through humanoid teleoperation is also costly and difficult to scale. We therefore explore how human experience can support scalable learning of humanoid loco-manipulation. To support this study, we introduce HumanVerse-500, a 500-hour dataset of diverse human loco-manipulation behaviors in open-world environments, collected with a lightweight wearable system that synchronizes egocentric video with body and hand motion. Building on this dataset, we develop $λ_0$, a whole-body humanoid vision-language-action policy, through three-stage training that first learns interaction from diverse egocentric datasets, then coordinates body and hand motion using HumanVerse-500, and finally adapts the policy to downstream tasks and robot embodiments. Across these stages, $λ_0$ learns a shared representation space for human experience transfer, while domain-specific interfaces handle differences between human and robot states and actions. We evaluate $λ_0$ on SIMPLE and 4 real-world loco-manipulation tasks, achieving state-of-the-art performance, and further analyze its scaling behavior, generalization, and training-stage contributions to understand how human data support downstream whole-body humanoid control. We will release our code, models, and data to support further research.

</details>


### [13] [ScaffoldM3C: A Multimodal Sequential Monte Carlo Framework for Generative Stable Construction Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00487)
*Gadiel Sznaier Camps,Chengyang He,Guillaume Sartoretti,Eduardo Montijano,Mac Schwager*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ScaffoldM3C is a lightweight multimodal auto-regressive model that formulates block-based construction as probabilistic next-block generation with an auxiliary scaffold token, using Sequential Monte Carlo to track multiple assembly sequences, achieving comparable quality to SOTA at 4x smaller size and 5x-20x faster inference with higher stability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous 3D structure construction is hard due to combinatorial action spaces, interchangeable components, equifinal assembly sequences, and strict stability requirements. Existing SOTA methods fine-tune LLMs for text-based generative construction but lack multimodal (text, image, sketch) conditioning, ignore scaffolding's practical role in stabilizing intermediate structures, and are slow at inference.

Method: The authors formulate construction as probabilistic next-block generation with multiple potential assembly actions and conditioning modalities, introducing an auxiliary scaffold block token to explicitly model scaffolding utility. They present ScaffoldM3C, a multimodal, lightweight, auto-regressive model proposing next-step candidate blocks, and leverage Sequential Monte Carlo (SMC) to maintain a population of possible assembly sequences for exploring multiple assembly directions. Training extends the StableText2Brick dataset with image conditioning prompts and scaffold-stabilized build sequences.

Result: ScaffoldM3C is 4x smaller than competing baselines, delivers 5x-20x inference speedup, and achieves comparable construction quality to SOTA methods with higher overall stability. Effectiveness is demonstrated via simulations and real-world robot assembly demonstrations.

Conclusion: ScaffoldM3C offers an efficient, multimodal, scaffold-aware approach for stable block-based construction, matching SOTA quality while being substantially smaller and faster, and is validated in both simulation and physical robot assembly.

Abstract: Autonomously constructing physically realizable 3D structures remains a significant challenge due to combinatorial action spaces, interchangeable components, equifinal assembly sequences, and strict stability requirements during construction. State-of-the-art methods fine-tune large language models for text-based generative construction. However, these approaches do not allow for Multimodal (text, image, sketch) conditioning, overlook the practical role of scaffolding for stabilizing intermediate structures, and suffer from slow inference speeds. Therefore, we formulate construction as a probabilistic next-block generation task with multiple potential assembly actions and multiple potential task conditioning modalities. Concurrently, we explicitly consider the utility of scaffolding by introducing an auxiliary scaffold block token. We present Scaffold Multimodal Monte Carlo (ScaffoldM3C), a multimodal, lightweight, auto-regressive model for stable block-based construction, that proposes a set of next-step candidate blocks. Leveraging these candidates, we utilize Sequential Monte Carlo (SMC) to maintain a population of possible assembly sequences, allowing us to consider multiple, potentially different, assembly directions simultaneously. We train our multimodal architecture by extending the StableText2Brick dataset to contain image conditioning prompts and scaffold-stabilized build sequences. ScaffoldM3C is 4x smaller than competing baselines, yielding a 5x to 20x speedup during inference, while achieving comparable construction quality to state-of-the-art methods and higher overall stability. We demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach through simulations and real-world robot assembly demonstrations.

</details>


### [14] [Same Scene, Different Task: Skill Alignment for Compositional Generalization in VLAs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00524)
*Taegeun Yang,Youngju Na,Yoonki Cho,Sung-Eui Yoon*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: CRAFT improves compositional generalization of VLA models by training on counterfactual instruction pairs and transferring supervision from demonstrated executions of the required skill via reusable skill representations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLA models fail to generalize to skill combinations absent from fine-tuning demonstrations, even when each constituent skill was demonstrated. A key failure mode is the vision shortcut: visual observations act as a proxy for the instruction, so the policy executes a demonstrated combination matching similar observations rather than the instructed combination.

Method: Train with counterfactual pairs formed by holding a demonstration observation fixed while changing the instruction to specify an undemonstrated combination. Since these pairs have no demonstrated action targets, CRAFT transfers supervision from demonstrated executions of the required skill using skill representations that can be reused across executions of the same skill, addressing that the same skill may require different actions across observations.

Result: Across three VLA models and two simulation benchmarks, CRAFT improves success on undemonstrated combinations while maintaining high success on demonstrated ones. It also improves compositional generalization on a real robot.

Conclusion: Counterfactual instruction pairing combined with skill-representation-based supervision transfer is an effective approach to mitigate the vision shortcut and enhance compositional generalization in VLA models, validated in both simulation and real-robot settings.

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models often struggle to generalize to skill combinations absent from their fine-tuning demonstrations, even when every constituent skill has been demonstrated. We focus on a vision shortcut as one failure mode: during fine-tuning, visual observations can serve as a proxy for the instruction, so a policy may execute a demonstrated combination associated with similar observations rather than the instructed combination. This motivates training with counterfactual pairs formed by holding a demonstration observation fixed while changing the instruction to specify an undemonstrated combination. These pairs, however, lack corresponding demonstrated action targets. Crucially, the currently required skill has already been demonstrated, but actions from those executions cannot serve as direct targets because the same skill can require different actions across observations. We propose CRAFT, which transfers supervision from demonstrated executions of the required skill to counterfactual pairs using skill representations that can be reused across executions of the same skill. Across three VLA models and two simulation benchmarks, CRAFT improves success on undemonstrated combinations while maintaining high success on demonstrated ones; it also improves compositional generalization on a real robot. Project website: https://taegeunyang.github.io/craft/

</details>


### [15] [Does Continual Imitation Learning Remain Grounded? A Language-Perturbed Benchmark for Robotic Task Retention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00542)
*Siddeshwar Raghavan,Ziqin Yuan,Fengqing Zhu,Byung-Cheol Min*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A benchmark protocol that measures whether language-guided robot policies remain grounded in instructions across continual learning stages, using meaning-preserving and meaning-changing instruction variants on LIBERO.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Continual imitation learning typically measures whether a robot retains old skills, but retained task performance does not guarantee that behavior is still driven by language. Policies may instead rely on scene cues, object associations, or memorized task structure, so it is unclear whether instructions remain the true driver of behavior as tasks accumulate.

Method: Constructs meaning-preserving and meaning-changing instruction variants for the Goal, Spatial, Object, and Long suites of LIBERO. Policy experiments focus on LIBERO-Goal, testing Original and Paraphrase instructions after each continual-learning stage. Representative continual imitation learning methods are compared under their original assumptions, with task competence separated from language sensitivity. Diagnostics measure semantic robustness, goal adaptation, and language sensitivity as complements to standard learning and forgetting metrics.

Result: Strong continual-learning performance does not always translate into reliable language grounding. The proposed diagnostics reveal cases where retained skills are not correctly guided by their instructions.

Conclusion: Standard learning and forgetting metrics are insufficient for verifying language grounding in continual imitation learning. The proposed diagnostics complement them and clarify whether retained skills remain correctly guided by instructions.

Abstract: Continual imitation learning evaluates whether a robot can learn new knowledge without forgetting previously learned skills. However, retaining task performance does not ensure the behavior remains grounded in language because policies may rely on scene cues, object associations, or memorized task structure. We introduce a benchmark protocol to study how language-guided behavior changes as robotic policies learn successive tasks. We construct meaning-preserving and meaning-changing instruction variants for the Goal, Spatial, Object, and Long suites of LIBERO. Policy experiments focus on LIBERO-Goal, evaluating Original and Paraphrase instructions after each continual-learning stage. We compare representative continual imitation learning methods under their original assumptions while separating task competence from language sensitivity. The proposed diagnostics complement standard learning and forgetting metrics by measuring semantic robustness, goal adaptation, and language sensitivity. Results show that strong continual-learning performance does not always translate to reliable language grounding, and our diagnostics help determine whether retained skills remain correctly guided by their instructions. Additional materials are available at https://sites.google.com/view/stillgrounded

</details>


### [16] [Token-World: World Modeling in Vision-Language Model Token Space for Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00575)
*Chuyao Fu,Xiaowei Chi,Yuhan Rui,Yu-kai Wang,Zezhong Qian,Xiaojie Zhang,Yunfan Lou,Kevin Zhang,Kuangzhi Ge,Chak Wing Mak,Zhiyang Chen,Athena Zhuoming Zhong,Hongyang Chen,Haoran Li,Yike Guo,Sirui Han,Shanghang Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Token-World is an action-conditioned world model that compresses VLM visual tokens into a compact state, learns future dynamics there, and maps predictions back for policy use, improving fidelity, rollout stability, and closed-loop policy correlation over prior simulators.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing world-model simulation for vision-language-action systems predicts future RGB observations and re-encodes them into policy inputs, creating an indirect interface between simulation and downstream policy execution. The authors investigate whether world dynamics can instead be modeled directly in a compact, policy-oriented state derived from VLM visual tokens, addressing the challenge that raw VLM tokens are high-dimensional and hard to model autoregressively with efficiency and accuracy.

Method: Token-World is an action-conditioned world model that compresses VLM features into a compact token state, learns future dynamics in this reduced space, and maps predicted states back to the original policy-facing representation for downstream use.

Result: On manipulation benchmarks, Token-World improves open-loop feature fidelity and policy-action consistency over recent world-model simulators, with slower degradation over long rollout horizons. In closed-loop evaluation, simulated policy performance correlates more strongly with reference policy performance than Ctrl-World (r=0.794 vs. 0.583) at lower simulation latency. Ablations show compact-representation design and dimensionality substantially affect future-state prediction.

Conclusion: Modeling world dynamics in a compact, policy-oriented token state derived from VLM visual tokens yields more faithful and stable simulation with better closed-loop policy correlation and lower latency than RGB-based world-model simulators. Code will be available at https://chuyaofu.github.io/Token-World/.

Abstract: A common approach to world-model simulation for vision-language-action (VLA) systems is to predict future RGB observations and then re-encode them into policy inputs, introducing an indirect interface between simulation and downstream policy execution. We instead investigate whether world dynamics can be modeled in a compact, policy-oriented state derived from VLM visual tokens. A key challenge is that raw VLM visual tokens are high-dimensional, making efficient and accurate autoregressive dynamics modeling challenging. To address this, we introduce Token-World, an action-conditioned world model that compresses VLM features into a compact token state, learns future dynamics in this reduced space, and maps predicted states back to the original policy-facing representation for downstream use. Across manipulation benchmarks, Token-World improves open-loop feature fidelity and policy-action consistency over recent world-model simulators, with slower degradation over long rollout horizons. In closed-loop evaluation, its simulated policy performance correlates more strongly with reference policy performance than Ctrl-World ($r=0.794$ vs.\ $0.583$), while requiring lower simulation latency. Ablations further show that compact-representation design and dimensionality substantially affect future-state prediction. Code will be available at https://chuyaofu.github.io/Token-World/.

</details>


### [17] [When Reasoning Helps Action: Monitoring and Steering Chain-of-Thought in Vision-Language-Action Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00601)
*Sathwik Karnik,Joseph JR. Lee,Aryaman Gupta,Somil Bansal*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper defines two evaluation axes—correctability and actionability—for using chain-of-thought as a runtime safety interface in VLA policies, and introduces TRUST, an offline-trained token-level value model that monitors and steers reasoning in frozen VLA policies. TRUST improves reasoning correctness and, in driving, reduces collisions, but reasoning gains do not automatically translate to embodied performance gains (manipulation), showing correctability and actionability must be evaluated separately.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reasoning-enabled VLA policies produce CoT traces that appear to explain and guide actions, suggesting a runtime safety interface via reasoning monitoring and correction. However, it is unclear when such an interface can actually improve embodied behavior, so the paper argues for explicitly defining and operationalizing criteria to assess this.

Method: The authors define two evaluation axes: correctability (whether unreliable reasoning can be detected and improved during generation) and actionability (whether reasoning corrections yield behaviorally meaningful changes in the intended direction). To enable correctability, they introduce TRUST (Token-level Reward for Utility-Steered Chain-of-Thought), an offline-trained value model that predicts eventual reasoning correctness from partial prefixes and uses these estimates to monitor and selectively steer generation in frozen VLA policies.

Result: On Alpamayo 1.5 driving VLA, TRUST monitors correctness with 88.9% accuracy and raises reasoning correctness from 75.9% to 90.0%; on a challenging AlpaSim subset it cuts collision rate by 30.4% and maximum trajectory error by 11.5%, beating a compute-matched Best-of-4 baseline. On DeepThinkVLA manipulation, TRUST improves grasp-state claim correctness from 69.3% to 90.2% and action-choice claims from 68.8% to 85.9%, yet closed-loop LIBERO-Plus performance is largely unchanged. Analysis shows intent-consistent behavioral effects in Alpamayo 1.5 but limited effects in DeepThinkVLA.

Conclusion: Gains in reasoning correctness do not automatically imply gains in embodied performance. Using CoT as a runtime safety interface therefore requires evaluating both correctability and actionability, since improved reasoning may translate into behavior in some policies (driving) but not others (manipulation).

Abstract: Reasoning-enabled VLA policies expose chain-of-thought (CoT) traces that appear to explain and guide their actions, creating a potential interface for runtime safety through reasoning monitoring and correction. In this work, we define and operationalize two evaluation axes for assessing when this interface can improve embodied behavior: correctability, which measures whether unreliable reasoning can be detected and improved during generation, and actionability, which measures whether reasoning corrections produce behaviorally meaningful changes in the intended direction. To enable correctability, we introduce Token-level Reward for Utility-Steered Chain-of-Thought (TRUST), an offline-trained value model that predicts eventual reasoning correctness from partial prefixes and uses these estimates to monitor and selectively steer reasoning generation in frozen VLA policies. On the Alpamayo 1.5 driving VLA, TRUST monitors correctness with 88.9% accuracy and improves reasoning correctness from 75.9% to 90.0%. On a baseline-defined challenging subset in AlpaSim, TRUST reduces collision rate by 30.4% and maximum trajectory error by 11.5% relative to the unsteered policy, outperforming a compute-matched Best-of-4 baseline. On the DeepThinkVLA manipulation VLA, TRUST improves the correctness of grasp-state claims from 69.3% to 90.2% and action-choice claims from 68.8% to 85.9%, yet closed-loop task performance on LIBERO-Plus remains largely unchanged. Empirical analysis reveals intent-consistent behavioral effects in Alpamayo 1.5 but limited effects in DeepThinkVLA, helping interpret these different task-level outcomes. Together, our results show that gains in reasoning correctness do not automatically imply gains in embodied performance, motivating evaluation of correctability and actionability when using CoT as a runtime safety interface.

</details>


### [18] [TacDyn-WAM: Learning Implicit Tactile Dynamics in a Heterogeneous Visuo-Tactile World Action Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00638)
*Enyi Wang,Mingxin Wang,Quan Shi,Hetian Guo,Hongyu Wang,Xi Wang,Bin Qian,Yupeng Zheng,Wenxuan Song,Houde Liu,Yong Xu,Cheng Chi,Wenchao Ding,Yilun Chen,Yan Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: TacDyn-WAM is a heterogeneous visuo-tactile world action model that predicts implicit tactile dynamics instead of reconstructing future tactile pixels, achieving SOTA-level robotic manipulation performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing tactile world action models inherit video-generation pipelines that reconstruct future tactile observations via iterative denoising. This is unreliable under deployment drift, because small changes in contact position or force can substantially alter tactile pixels even when the underlying contact evolution remains predictable.

Method: The model predicts implicit tactile dynamics rather than future tactile observations. It learns TacRep, a dynamics-aware tactile target space trained through masked spatio-temporal prediction on tactile clips and regularized by relational structure distillation. A visual expert and an Implicit Tactile Dynamics Expert predict future visual and tactile representations in separate target spaces, interacting via joint attention. The tactile expert predicts future representations and their changes at multiple horizons in a single forward pass, and a read-only tactile memory supplies the current tactile state.

Result: On UniVTAC, TacDyn-WAM achieves 81.5% average success using only provided demonstrations, reaching state-of-the-art-level performance and remaining competitive with models pretrained on large-scale visuo-tactile trajectories. Ablations confirm the benefits of both tactile pathways and TacRep over pixel-reconstruction and static alternatives. On five real-robot tasks, it reaches 71.0% average success, rising to 85.0% with modest-scale pretraining.

Conclusion: Predicting implicit tactile dynamics, combined with a dynamics-aware representation space and heterogeneous expert interaction, offers a robust and data-efficient alternative to pixel-reconstruction world action models for visuo-tactile robotic manipulation.

Abstract: World action models improve robotic manipulation by conditioning actions on predicted futures, yet existing tactile variants largely inherit video-generation pipelines that reconstruct future tactile observations through iterative denoising. Such prediction can become unreliable under deployment drift: small changes in contact position or force may substantially alter tactile pixels even when the underlying contact evolution remains predictable. We introduce TacDyn-WAM, a heterogeneous visuo-tactile world action model that predicts implicit tactile dynamics rather than reconstructing future tactile observations. It learns TacRep, a dynamics-aware tactile target space trained through masked spatio-temporal prediction on tactile clips and regularized by relational structure distillation. A visual expert and an Implicit Tactile Dynamics Expert predict future visual and tactile representations in separate target spaces while interacting through joint attention; the tactile expert predicts future representations and their changes at multiple horizons in a single forward pass, and a read-only tactile memory supplies the current tactile state. On UniVTAC, TacDyn-WAM achieves an average success rate of 81.5% using only the provided demonstrations, reaching state-of-the-art-level performance and remaining competitive with models pretrained on large-scale visuo-tactile trajectories. Ablations confirm the benefits of both tactile pathways and TacRep over pixel-reconstruction and static alternatives. On five real-robot tasks, TacDyn-WAM reaches 71.0% average success, and modest-scale pretraining raises it to 85.0%, further validating our method.

</details>


### [19] [Toward Humanoid Robots in Construction: A Teleoperation Feasibility Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00718)
*Parastoo Ali Pour,David R. Martin,Chang Min Hur,Bo Zhang,Tommy Zhou,Brandon Thomas Lichter,Shane Stanfield,Pramod Khargonekar,Mohammad Abdullah Al Faruque*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A teleoperation system lets a single operator control a Unitree G1 humanoid for construction tasks by combining XR-based upper-body control with pedal-based locomotion, achieving 100% success on tool transport and 80% on surface painting, though slower than manual execution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Persistent labor shortages, hazardous working conditions, and limitations in humanoid autonomy motivate teleoperation as a practical near-term approach to reduce physical strain on workers and to generate high-quality demonstration data.

Method: The system integrates extended reality (XR)-based upper-body control with pedal-based locomotion, enabling a single operator to perform simultaneous manipulation and locomotion on a Unitree G1 humanoid. It was evaluated on two representative construction tasks drawn from the O*NET occupational database, reporting task success and completion time against a manual baseline.

Result: The system achieved 100% success on tool transport and 80% success on surface painting. Teleoperation required substantially more time than manual execution.

Conclusion: Teleoperation of a humanoid for construction tasks is feasible with high success rates, but remains slower than manual work; it offers a near-term means to reduce worker strain and collect demonstration data.

Abstract: We present a teleoperation system that enables a single operator to perform construction tasks on a Unitree G1 humanoid, combining extended reality (XR) based upper body control with pedal-based locomotion to enable simultaneous manipulation and locomotion. Motivated by persistent labor shortages, hazardous working conditions, and challenges in humanoid autonomy, we investigate teleoperation as a practical near-term approach for reducing physical strain on workers while generating high quality demonstration data. We evaluate the system on two representative construction tasks drawn from O*NET occupational database, and report task success and completion time relative to a manual baseline. The system achieved 100% success on tool transport and 80% success on surface painting, with teleoperation requiring substantially more time compared to manual execution.

</details>


### [20] [CF-JEPA: Improving Robustness of JEPA World Models via Controllability Factorization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00727)
*Morgan Byrd,Robert Wright,Sehoon Ha*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: CF-JEPA is a JEPA-style latent world model that factorizes its latent space into controllable and uncontrollable subspaces to isolate distractor information, preventing latent collapse and improving control under distraction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-based agents must distinguish task-relevant information from irrelevant background distractors. JEPA-style latent world models avoid pixel-level reconstruction, making them appealing for this purpose, yet they remain sensitive to distractor signals and suffer from latent collapse.

Method: The authors propose Controllability Factorized JEPA (CF-JEPA), which splits the latent space into controllable and uncontrollable subspaces. Distractor information is captured in the uncontrollable region, while control-relevant latent information is used for the task.

Result: CF-JEPA achieves comparable performance on 2D and 3D control tasks under nominal conditions and improved performance under distracted conditions, being the only model that avoids latent collapse. It is further validated on a simulated robot task under distracted conditions.

Conclusion: Factorizing the latent space by controllability provides an effective scheme for handling distractors in JEPA-style world models, with practical applicability demonstrated in simulated robotic control.

Abstract: Controlling an agent with vision requires being able to separate useful information from irrelevant background information. JEPA-style latent world models seem like a natural approach for this, as they do not perform pixel-level reconstruction; however, they are still sensitive to these distractor signals and experience latent collapse. In this work, we introduce Controllability Factorized JEPA (CF-JEPA), a JEPA-style world model which splits the latent space into controllable and uncontrollable subspaces. This factorization allows us to capture all the distractor information into the uncontrollable region, while we use the control-relevant latent information for our task. With this, we show comparable performance across 2D and 3D control tasks under nominal conditions and improved performance under distracted conditions, where CF-JEPA is the only model that does not experience latent collapse. We also validate our model under distracted conditions for a simulated robot task, highlighting the practical application of such a scheme.

</details>


### [21] [Measuring Asset and Scene Reconstruction Effects in Real-to-Sim Robot Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00731)
*Sanya Verma,Luca Cilio,Velissarios Christodoulou*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A metrically scaled authored reconstruction (geometry, textures, physics, custom splat) yields substantially higher sim-to-real agreement than a default open-source reconstruction: r = 0.90 vs. 0.51, with mean score error reduced by 10.56 percentage points.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Simulated evaluation is attractive as a cheaper, more repeatable alternative to real-world robot policy evaluation, but its usefulness hinges on how well simulated scores track real-robot outcomes. The paper asks whether a better-constructed simulation environment can narrow this sim-to-real gap.

Method: Two simulated versions of one bimanual robot cell were built from the same object photographs and scene video: an "authored reconstruction" (metrically scaled object geometry, projected textures, authored physics, custom scene splat) and a "default reconstruction" (open-source recipe: generative single-image mesh, engine-default physics, Gaussian-splat scene). Two policies ran five tasks each (10 task-policy "cells"), with 20 runs per cell per reconstruction, all other settings fixed. Both were scored against identical real trials graded by a third-party evaluator.

Result: Pearson correlation between the ten simulated and real cell means was r = 0.90 for the authored reconstruction versus 0.51 for the default. Mean score error was 6.97 percentage points (authored) versus 17.54 (default), a reduction of 10.56 percentage points.

Conclusion: Higher visual fidelity, authored physical parameters, and metric scale make simulation more faithful to reality and narrow the sim-to-real gap. The harness, per-trial scores, run configurations, and assets/scenes of both reconstructions are released.

Abstract: Simulated evaluation is increasingly used alongside real-world evaluation of robot policies because it is cheaper and easier to repeat; however, its value depends on how closely its outcomes track the real robot's. We test whether our reconstruction pipeline, combining metrically scaled object geometry, authored physical parameters and scene reconstruction, reduces disagreement between simulated and real robot scores relative to a default open-source recipe. We constructed two simulated versions of one bimanual robot cell: an authored reconstruction, using object geometry at estimated metric scale, projected textures, authored physics and our own scene splat; and a baseline, referred to as the default reconstruction, using the open-source recipe of a generative single-image mesh, engine-default physics and a Gaussian-splat scene. Both reconstructions use the same object photographs and scene video. Two policies ran five tasks each, giving ten task-policy pairs, which we call cells; each cell was run twenty times in each reconstruction with all other settings held fixed. Both reconstructions were scored against the same real trials, graded by a third-party evaluator. Pearson correlation between the ten simulated and real cell means is r = 0.90 for the authored reconstruction and 0.51 for the default. Mean score error is 6.97 percentage points for the authored reconstruction and 17.54 for the default, a reduction of 10.56 percentage points. These results show that improving the quality of the environment reconstruction through higher visual fidelity, authored physics and metric scale makes the simulation more faithful to the real world and narrows the sim-to-real gap. We release the harness, the per-trial scores, every reported run's configuration, and the assets and scenes of both reconstructions.

</details>


### [22] [DITTO-X: Forward and Reverse Teleoperation for Dexterous Manipulation and Human Intervention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00781)
*Zhanpeng He,Joaquin Palacios,Zhangyu Wang,Chenhao Li,Katelyn Lee,Matei Ciocarlie,C. Karen Liu,Jiajun Wu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DITTO-X is a hand-agnostic dexterous teleoperation interface that renders joint-level force and fingertip contact feedback and drives three commercial dexterous hands without per-hand redesign; an actuated exoskeleton enables reverse teleoperation to match the operator's fingers to the robot's configuration before takeover. It improves demonstration quality and throughput over a commercial hand-tracking glove in data collection and human-in-the-loop intervention.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most teleoperation systems close the loop through vision alone and are built for parallel-jaw grippers, limiting both robot capability and operator expressiveness. This is especially problematic in shared autonomy, where operators see the scene only through occluded cameras and must take over a dexterous hand mid-task, often with an object already grasped.

Method: DITTO-X is a hand-agnostic dexterous teleoperation interface that renders joint-level force and fingertip contact events using sensing already present on the robot hand, and drives three commercial dexterous hands (Sharpa, Wuji, Inspire) without per-hand redesign. Because the exoskeleton is actuated, it additionally supports reverse teleoperation: the robot back-drives the operator's fingers into its own configuration before control is transferred, so the human enters the loop already matched to the inherited state.

Result: DITTO-X improves demonstration quality and throughput over a commercial hand-tracking glove, both in regular data collection and in human intervention during policy deployment for contact-rich manipulation tasks.

Conclusion: A hand-agnostic, force- and contact-aware, actuated exoskeleton interface enables higher-quality dexterous teleoperation and more effective human-in-the-loop correction in shared autonomy, with reverse teleoperation aligning the operator to the robot's state at takeover. Further information: https://tml.stanford.edu/ditto-x/.

Abstract: Teleoperated demonstrations are a primary source of data for robot manipulation, and teleoperated interventions are a primary mechanism for correcting policies at deployment. Yet most teleoperation systems close the loop through vision alone and are built around parallel-jaw grippers, limiting both what the robot can execute and what the operator can express through it. This is most damaging in shared autonomy, where the operator sees the scene only through occluded cameras and must take over a dexterous hand mid-task, often with an object already grasped. We present DITTO-X, a hand-agnostic dexterous teleoperation interface that renders joint-level force and fingertip contact events from sensing already on the robot hand, and drives three commercial dexterous hands (Sharpa, Wuji, and Inspire) without per-hand redesign. Because the exoskeleton is actuated, DITTO-X also supports reverse teleoperation, in which the robot back-drives the operator's fingers into its own configuration before control is transferred, so the human enters the loop already matched to the state they inherit. Our results show that DITTO-X improves demonstration quality and throughput over a commercial hand-tracking glove, both in regular data collection and in human intervention during policy deployment for contact-rich manipulation tasks. More information can be found from our website: https://tml.stanford.edu/ditto-x/.

</details>


### [23] [ECoMEM: Explicit Concept Memory for Memory-Dependent Robot Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00801)
*Yize Liu,Ke Wang,Mac Schwager,Yiqing Xu,Jiajun Wu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ECoMEM introduces an Explicit Concept Memory for VLA policies, separating memory maintenance (grounded concept library + evidence-based Writer) from action learning (learned Reader producing memory tokens), outperforming baselines on 15/16 RoboMME tasks and achieving 86.1% vs 8.6% success on real-robot tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon robot tasks require recalling objects, demonstrations, or progress after they leave the current observation, yet VLA policies fail once task-relevant information disappears from view. Existing remedies (longer histories or implicit memory learned from observation-action trajectories) are inadequate because action supervision indicates how to act, not what to remember—it does not specify which past facts should persist or how they should be updated with new evidence.

Method: ECoMEM separates maintaining an evidence-grounded account of the past from learning to act on it. It represents task-relevant history via a reusable library of grounded concepts; an evidence-based Writer selects and updates these records, and a learned Reader converts them into memory tokens that directly condition the VLA policy.

Result: ECoMEM leads evaluated robot policies on 15 of 16 RoboMME tasks. On two new real-robot tasks, the same memory library transfers directly or needs only one new concept, reaching 86.1% success versus 8.6% for a no-memory VLA.

Conclusion: Explicit, grounded concepts provide a reusable and extensible memory interface for robot control, enabling effective long-horizon behavior and efficient transfer across tasks.

Abstract: A robot may lose sight of an object it must later retrieve, need to recall what a person demonstrated earlier, or track which steps of a task it has already completed. Current vision-language-action (VLA) policies often fail once the information needed for action disappears from the current observation, making memory critical for long-horizon robot behavior. Existing approaches typically provide longer histories or learn implicit memory from observation-action trajectories. But action supervision tells a policy how to act, not what to remember: it does not specify which past facts should persist or how they should change as new evidence arrives. We therefore separate maintaining an evidence-grounded account of the past from learning how to act on it. This insight motivates Explicit Concept Memory (ECoMEM), which represents task-relevant history with a reusable library of grounded concepts. An evidence-based Writer selects and updates these records, while a learned Reader turns them into memory tokens that directly condition the VLA. Across 16 RoboMME tasks, ECoMEM leads the evaluated robot policies on 15 tasks. On two new real-robot tasks, the same memory library either transfers directly or requires only one new concept, achieving 86.1% success versus 8.6% for a no-memory VLA. These results show that explicit concepts provide a reusable and extensible memory interface for robot control. Project website: https://ecomem.github.io/

</details>


### [24] [Getting Out and Getting Back: World and Behavior Grounding in Real2Sim2Real Co-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00821)
*Samuel Liu,Youngsun Kim,Martin Matak,Gilwoo Lee*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper separates simulation fidelity into "world grounding" (alignment with the real system) and "behavior grounding" (alignment with human motion), and shows via a real2sim2real co-training pipeline that both independently improve policy success, with world grounding the larger contributor.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real demonstrations are scarce, and simulation can supplement them for co-training, but it is unclear how different notions of simulation fidelity—matching the physical world versus matching human behavior—affect learned policy performance.

Method: The authors introduce a real2sim2real pipeline that varies world grounding and behavior grounding independently to generate co-training data. They evaluate on a dynamic dexterous pick-and-sort task and analyze learned policies through latent-space analysis to understand how real and simulated data are combined.

Result: Fully grounded co-training raises success from 52% to 86%. Averaged across configurations, world grounding improves success by 18 percentage points and behavior grounding by 10. Deployed policies behave like a mixture of real-derived and simulation-derived policies, imitating real demonstrations in covered states and relying on simulated behavior elsewhere.

Conclusion: World grounding and behavior grounding play complementary roles: world grounding lets policies exploit simulated experience beyond real-data coverage, while behavior grounding matters mainly when world grounding is imperfect. Grounded simulation remains beneficial even when co-training foundation models.

Abstract: Simulation can expand scarce real demonstrations for co-training, yet how world fidelity and similarity to human behavior affect policy performance remains unclear. We distinguish world grounding, which aligns simulation with the real system, and behavior grounding, which aligns simulated trajectories with human motion. We build a real2sim2real pipeline that varies these axes independently to generate data for co-training.
