On a dynamic dexterous pick-and-sort task, fully grounded co-training raises success from 52% to 86%; averaged across configurations, world grounding improves success by 18 percentage points and behavior grounding by 10. Deployed policies behave like a mixture of real-derived and simulation-derived policies, imitating real demonstrations in covered states and relying on simulated behavior elsewhere, which we examine through latent-space analysis. Together, these results suggest complementary roles: world grounding lets policies use simulated experience beyond real-data coverage, while behavior grounding matters mainly when world grounding is imperfect. Grounded simulation remains beneficial when co-training foundation models.

</details>


### [25] [TRACE: Privacy-Preserving Next-Best-View Selection over Distributed 3D Gaussian-Splat Maps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00822)
*Amirhossein Mollaei Khass,Athanasios Cosse,Qiyu Sun,Nader Motee*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: TRACE enables privacy-preserving, multi-robot next-best-view selection for 3D Gaussian Splatting maps by decomposing each robot's expected information gain into two communicable ray quantities: transmittance in front of and radiance behind a splat. Robots exchange only aggregated depth-bin sums (with pose derivatives) instead of raw map data, keeping message size constant. The reconstruction is exact except when a depth bin mixes hits from two robots, with a bounded error otherwise. In Habitat-Sim experiments, TRACE achieves headings within 15° of the centralized optimum in 83.3% of cases and reaches 97.9% of the centralized EIG.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Next-best-view (NBV) selection is essential for efficient active 3D reconstruction, but in multi-robot settings each robot typically needs the global map to evaluate the expected information gain (EIG) of a candidate view—because other robots' splats both occlude and are occluded by its own. Requiring robots to share full maps is communication-heavy, scales poorly with map size, and violates privacy. The paper seeks a decentralized NBV protocol that yields centralized-equivalent decisions without any robot exposing its map.

Method: The authors derive that the EIG coupling across robots passes through only two ray quantities: transmittance in front of a splat and radiance behind it. Both are sums over ray hits, so they decompose additively across robots and depth bins. Each robot, along the rays of a candidate view, computes these quantities as sums over depth bins in its own 3DGS map, along with their pose derivatives, and transmits these aggregates. The viewing robot combines the received aggregates into its EIG and its gradient on SO(3). The protocol is named TRACE (Transmittance and Radiance Aggregates Communicated for the EIG). No robot shares its splats, and the message size is independent of map size. The paper proves the reconstruction is exact unless a depth bin behind a splat contains hits from two different robots, and derives an error bound for that case.

Result: Across over 100 next-best-view decisions in Habitat-Sim, TRACE selects a heading within 15° of the centralized (full-map) decision in 83.3% of cases, and the selected views attain 97.9% of the centralized EIG. Communication cost is constant per message, independent of map size.

Conclusion: TRACE shows that multi-robot NBV selection for private 3D Gaussian Splatting maps can be performed in a decentralized manner with near-centralized performance, without sharing raw splat data and with message size that does not grow with map size. The exactness guarantee (with a bounded error in the mixed-hit case) provides theoretical grounding for the decomposition, and empirical results in Habitat-Sim validate its practical effectiveness.

Abstract: Share the light, not the map. We study next-best-view selection for a team of robots, each of which builds its own 3D Gaussian Splatting map and keeps it private. A robot picks the view with the largest expected information gain (EIG) about the splats along its own path. This gain depends on the other maps. Their splats occlude its own and shine behind them, so the gain has to be evaluated against the pooled map. No robot has this map. We show that the coupling passes through only two ray quantities, the transmittance in front of a splat and the radiance behind it, and that both are sums over the hits of the ray. Hence, they decompose across the robots, and each robot sums them over depth bins in its own map, along the rays of a candidate view, and sends the sums with their pose derivatives. The robot planning the view turns them into its EIG and gradient on SO(3). Transmittance and Radiance Aggregates, communicated for the EIG, give the protocol its name: TRACE. No robot shares its splats, and the message size does not grow with a map. We prove that the reconstruction is exact unless a depth bin behind a splat mixes hits of two robots, and we bound the error otherwise. Over 100 next-best-view decisions in Habitat-Sim, TRACE picks a heading within 15 degrees of the centralized one in 83.3% of the cases, and its views reach 97.9% of the centralized EIG.

</details>


### [26] [Reactive Humanoid Multi-Contact Using Learned Stability Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00823)
*Stephen McCrory,Beomyeong Park,Nicholas Kitchel,Nehar Poddar,Robert Griffin*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper presents a planning and control approach that reactively uses hand contacts to stabilize a humanoid robot in low-stability scenarios where feet-only recovery may fail.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Humanoid robots relying solely on foot contacts can fall in low-stability situations. Hand contacts offer an additional stabilization modality, but evaluating candidate contact points via traditional optimization is too slow for reactive use.

Method: Candidate contacts are sampled in the reachable workspace. A preview is rolled out through centroidal dynamics across pre-impact, impact, and post-impact phases, and points are scored by Center of Pressure (CoP) control authority at post-impact. A learned model of the robot's post-impact CoP region enables rapid contact evaluation versus optimization. The planner has two stages: selecting an optimal bracing region, then computing an optimal bracing point within it.

Result: Simulations show an average 89% increase in impulse resilience over recovery without hand contacts and 17% over a naive planning strategy (closest reachable region). Hardware validation via push tests showed standing trials with 43% average reduction in stabilization time versus naive hand placement, and walking trials with 18% reduction versus baseline recovery without hand contacts.

Conclusion: A learned CoP-region model enables fast, reactive hand-contact bracing planning that outperforms unaided recovery and naive hand placement in both simulation and hardware.

Abstract: We present a planning and control approach to reactively use hand contacts to stabilize a humanoid in low stability scenarios, where only using feet contacts may result in a fall. Candidate contacts are sampled within the robot's reachable workspace, and a preview is computed by rolling out the centroidal dynamics through pre-impact, impact and post-impact phases. Sampled points are scored based on the Center of Pressure (CoP) control authority at the post-impact phase. Central to our approach is a learned model of the robot's CoP region during post-impact, which enables rapid evaluation of candidate contact points compared to traditional optimization-based methods. The presented planner has two stages: the first selects an optimal bracing region and the second computes an optimal bracing point within the region. Our simulation results demonstrate an average increase in impulse resilience of 89% over recovery without hand contacts and 17% over a naive planning strategy (closest reachable region). We validate our framework on hardware, performing push tests while standing and walking. The standing trials show an average 43% reduction in stabilization time compared to naive hand placement and the walking trials demonstrate a 18% reduction compared to baseline recovery (without hand contacts).

</details>


### [27] [Are Frontier VLM Agents Ready to Be Robot Generalists? An Empirical Study with the Embodied Agent Arena](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00854)
*Haojian Huang,Pukun Zhao,Zexi Li,Yehang Zhang,Yangkai Wei,Wenqian Li,Han Yang,Kaiwen Zhou,Ying-Cong Chen,Yinchuan Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Embodied Agent Arena benchmarks seven VLMs across five embodied competencies (1,000 cases from 32 sources plus the new GeoProbe benchmark), finding that strong local estimation and contact localization do not translate into complete, goal-directed robotic task success.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To assess whether frontier VLMs are ready to serve as robot generalists by determining how their component abilities (scene estimation, interaction grounding, executable actions) combine, and whether local competence is sufficient for complete task success.

Method: The authors build Embodied Agent Arena spanning Geometry, Spatial Reasoning, Affordance, Task Planning, and Manipulation, with 1,000 cases from 32 established sources plus GeoProbe (a new geometric-estimation benchmark on Blender renders and real-scene images). A minimal harness preserves source observations and operations while disentangling metric precision, functional grounding, and native goal completion. Seven VLMs are evaluated, including analysis of Astra's task-specific advantages and comparisons of richer-observation execution protocols and multi-round review.

Result: Across the arena, Astra shows its clearest advantages in precise estimation and usable-contact localization, while completing coordinated, goal-directed actions remains the main shortfall.

Conclusion: Local perceptual and grounding competence does not suffice for robot generalism; bridging the gap to complete, coordinated goal-directed action execution is the central challenge for frontier VLMs.

Abstract: Frontier vision-language models (VLMs) combine scene estimation, interaction grounding, and executable actions. Understanding how these abilities support complete robotic tasks is central to evaluating their readiness as robot generalists. We introduce Embodied Agent Arena to examine where local competence supports, or falls short of, complete task success across Geometry, Spatial Reasoning, Affordance, Task Planning, and Manipulation. The arena contains 1,000 cases drawn from 32 established sources and GeoProbe, our new benchmark for geometric estimation on Blender renders and real-scene images. A minimal harness preserves source observations and operations while separating metric precision, functional grounding, and native goal completion. We evaluate seven VLMs, analyze Astra's task-specific advantages, and compare richer-observation execution protocols and multi-round review. Across the arena, Astra's advantage is strongest in precise estimation and usable-contact localization; completing coordinated, goal-directed actions remains the key gap to robot generalism.

</details>


### [28] [Kinematic MeanFlow: One-Step Action Generation Policy for Robotic Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00864)
*Jiawei Fan,Sifeng Wang,Yuqing Hou,Anbang Yao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: K-MF is a one-step action policy for Robotic Foundation Models that overcomes MeanFlow's performance collapse by decoupling the time-derivative term into two sub-intervals, enabling one-step generation that beats multi-step flow matching with large latency savings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: One-step action generation in Robotic Foundation Models (RFMs) is desired to overcome the high inference latency of multi-step flow matching. MeanFlow is a promising framework for this, but its direct application causes performance collapse. This collapse is traced to two distinctive dynamics in the RFM velocity field: (1) local acceleration is stable early but surges sharply near the end of denoising, and (2) the spread of its magnitudes across samples widens as denoising progresses.

Method: Kinematic MeanFlow (K-MF), a one-step action policy tailored for RFMs. Grounded in a kinematic identity, K-MF decouples the time-derivative term in the MeanFlow formulation into two sub-interval terms separated by an intermediate point. This decoupled formulation lets the two terms capture early-stage and late-stage denoising dynamics respectively, while mitigating error amplification across the process.

Result: K-MF enables RFMs to achieve one-step action generation in both training-from-scratch and fine-tuning paradigms across diverse tasks, outperforming multi-step flow matching in most settings. In inference efficiency, it reduces action-head latency of GR00T-N1.6 by 67.5%-74.4% on L40 and Jetson Orin in eager and compiled modes, yielding end-to-end latency reductions of 30.3%-54.9%.

Conclusion: K-MF effectively addresses the performance collapse of directly applying MeanFlow to RFMs by handling the two distinctive velocity-field dynamics, achieving efficient and competitive one-step action generation in Robotic Foundation Models.

Abstract: In this paper, we study how to achieve one-step action generation in Robotic Foundation Models (RFMs), aiming to overcome the high inference latency of multi-step flow matching. MeanFlow provides a promising framework for this goal, yet its direct application leads to performance collapse. We discover that this stems from two distinctive dynamics exhibited in the RFM velocity field: (1) the ``local acceleration" exhibits stability early on, but surges sharply towards the end of the denoising process, and (2) the spread of its magnitudes across samples widens as denoising progresses. To address these issues, we introduce Kinematic MeanFlow (K-MF), a novel one-step action policy tailored for RFMs. Specifically, grounded in a kinematic identity, K-MF decouples the time derivative term in the MeanFlow formulation into two sub-interval terms separated by an intermediate point. This decoupled formulation enables the two terms to capture early-stage and late-stage denoising dynamics, respectively, while mitigating the error amplification across the process. As a result, our K-MF empowers RFMs to achieve one-step action generation in both training from scratch and fine-tuning paradigms across diverse tasks, while outperforming multi-step flow matching in most settings. In terms of inference efficiency, K-MF reduces action-head latency of GR00T-N1.6 by 67.5%~74.4% across L40 and Jetson Orin in eager and compiled modes, yielding end-to-end latency reductions of 30.3%~54.9%. Code will be available at https://github.com/IntelChina-AI/K-MF.

</details>


### [29] [UniTrackPLA: Unified Panorama-Language-Action Model for Instruction-Guided Navigation and Dynamic Person Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00878)
*Pengfei Qi,Haoran Lin,Sizhuang Chen,Kai Luo,Sirui Zhang,Xinqi Liu,Fei Cheng,Wenrui Chen,Liming Yin,Kailun Yang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: UniTrackPLA is a unified panorama-language-action model that jointly handles instruction-guided navigation and dynamic person tracking using omnidirectional panoramic observations, achieving notable gains in tracking and VLN success rates plus strong real-world performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing embodied robot methods typically rely on forward-facing observations and treat navigation and person tracking as separate policies, which limits omnidirectional perception and prevents unified closed-loop control. General-purpose robots need to support both language-specified destination navigation and dynamic person tracking from arbitrary initial target azimuths.

Method: The approach uses Panoramic-Aware Encoding (PAE) to preserve temporal and azimuthal structure of perspective views projected from panoramas, letting perspective-pretrained visual encoders process omnidirectional observations. A shared vision-language backbone grounds instructions in panoramic context and predicts continuous robot-centric waypoint chunks for both tasks. World-Action Consistency (WAC) predicts action-conditioned future visual states and verifies waypoint prefixes online, reusing reliable actions and triggering replanning on inconsistency.

Result: UniTrackPLA raises overall tracking SR from 23.50% to 35.00% and Omni-VLN SR/SPL from 13.00%/12.77% to 19.75%/19.29%. Adding 76 real-world routes improves held-out EP@0.2m from 42.92% to 92.08%. Closed-loop experiments on a Go2-W robot demonstrate unified panoramic tracking and navigation indoors and outdoors.

Conclusion: A single unified panorama-language-action model can successfully unify instruction-guided navigation and dynamic person tracking with omnidirectional perception and closed-loop control, validated both in the introduced OmniTrackNav-Bench benchmark and on real-world robot deployment.

Abstract: General-purpose embodied robots should support both navigation toward language-specified destinations and dynamic person tracking under arbitrary initial target azimuths. However, existing methods typically rely on forward-facing observations and address these tasks with separate policies, limiting omnidirectional perception and unified closed-loop control. We present UniTrackPLA, a unified panorama-language-action model for instruction-guided navigation and dynamic person tracking. Its Panoramic-Aware Encoding (PAE) preserves the temporal and azimuthal structure of perspective views projected from each panorama, enabling perspective-pretrained visual encoders to process omnidirectional observations. A shared vision-language backbone grounds instructions in the panoramic context and predicts continuous robot-centric waypoint chunks for both tasks. World-Action Consistency (WAC) further predicts action-conditioned future visual states and verifies waypoint prefixes online, allowing reliable actions to be reused while triggering replanning upon inconsistency. We also introduce OmniTrackNav-Bench, comprising 5,000 simulated tracking trajectories, 10,000 simulated VLN routes, and 96 verified real-world routes, providing 919,978 waypoint-supervision instances. UniTrackPLA improves overall tracking SR from 23.50% to 35.00% and Omni-VLN SR/SPL from 13.00%/12.77% to 19.75%/19.29%. Incorporating 76 real-world routes further improves held-out EP@0.2m from 42.92% to 92.08%. Closed-loop experiments on a Go2-W robot demonstrate unified panoramic tracking and navigation across indoor and outdoor environments. The project page is at https://tw5775.github.io/UniTrackPLA.

</details>


### [30] [Real-Time Human-Adaptive Task Allocation for Multi-Human Multi-Robot Supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00897)
*Seabin Lee,Sujeong Park,Nayoung Kim,Sungjin Park,Haechan Jung,Changjoo Nam*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes a human-factor-aware method that dynamically regulates each operator's supervisory capacity and greedily allocates robot supervision tasks to maintain balanced workload and reduce fatigue.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most existing supervision-task allocation methods assume a fixed supervisory capacity per operator and ignore fluctuations in human factors such as workload and fatigue. This leads to unbalanced workload distribution, with some operators overloaded while others are underutilized.

Method: The method dynamically regulates supervisory capacity and allocates tasks using a greedy strategy that minimizes estimated operator workloads with task prioritization. Robots are assigned to operators reflecting their current supervisory capacity, where the required effort depends on the task type.

Result: In a user study, analysis across predefined time intervals showed that the proposed method consistently achieved higher performance and lower behavioral signs of fatigue compared to a baseline that does not consider human factors.

Conclusion: Adaptive capacity adjustment is an effective preventive mechanism for sustaining operator performance in long-duration, high-demand settings.

Abstract: We propose a human-factor-aware method of allocating robot supervision tasks to multiple human operators. In scenarios where multiple operators occasionally teleoperate multiple robots to help the robots overcome difficulties, the allocation of the supervisory control tasks to humans needs to consider the real-time cognitive states of individual operators. However, most existing methods assume fixed supervisory capacity per operator and overlook fluctuations in the human factors such as workload and fatigue. As a result, workload distribution can be unbalanced where some operators become overloaded while the others remain underused. Our method dynamically regulates supervisory capacity and allocates tasks in a way that maintains balanced mental workload, prevents overload, and improves overall team performance. The allocation method uses a greedy strategy that minimizes estimated operator workloads with task prioritization. Robots are assigned to operators by reflecting their current supervisory capacity where the required effort depends on the types of tasks. In the user study, the analysis across predefined time intervals shows that the proposed method consistently achieves higher performance and lower behavioral signs of fatigue compared to a baseline method that does not consider human factors. These results highlight adaptive capacity adjustment as an effective preventive mechanism for sustaining operator performance in long-duration, high-demand settings.

</details>


### [31] [TOAST: Stochastic Robot Action Tokenization for Autoregressive Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00899)
*Keisuke Shirai,Tomohiro Motoda,Hanbit Oh,Ryoichi Nakajo,Roman Mykhailyshyn,Ryo Hanai,Shotaro Miwa,Yukiyasu Domae*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: TOAST is a stochastic action tokenization method for autoregressive Vision-Language-Action models that samples alternative tokenizations of the same quantized action sequence during training, improving policy learning—especially under limited data—without extra demonstrations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autoregressive VLA models often represent continuous robot actions as discrete token sequences, and FAST compresses diverse temporal frequencies into few tokens. However, FAST's compression does not necessarily improve learning efficiency from limited demonstrations, and it assigns a single deterministic tokenization even though multiple token sequences can decode to the same robot motion. The paper investigates whether exploiting this representational redundancy can improve policy learning.

Method: The authors propose TOAST (TOkenization of Action sequences with STochastic sampling), which during policy training samples alternative tokenizations of the same quantized action sequence. This diversifies the discrete supervision while preserving the underlying robot action and requires no additional demonstrations.

Result: On LIBERO, TOAST consistently outperforms its deterministic counterpart, with gains increasing as training data decreases and a 6.8-point success-rate improvement when only 1/16 of training data is available. Across four real-robot manipulation tasks, TOAST improves mean success rate by 15.8 points over the deterministic counterpart.

Conclusion: Stochastic action tokenization is effective for autoregressive robot policy learning, particularly when training data are limited.

Abstract: Autoregressive Vision-Language-Action models often represent continuous robot actions as discrete token sequences, enabling action prediction with standard next-token objectives. FAST has substantially improved this representation by compactly encoding action containing diverse temporal frequencies into relatively few tokens. However, while such compression reduces the number of action tokens required for autoregressive prediction, it does not necessarily improve the efficiency of policy learning from limited demonstrations. In particular, FAST typically assigns a single deterministic tokenization to each quantized action sequence, although multiple token sequences can represent and decode to the same robot motion. We investigate whether exploiting this representational redundancy can improve policy learning. In this paper, we propose TOkenization of Action sequences with STochastic sampling (TOAST), a stochastic action tokenization method that samples alternative tokenizations of the same quantized action sequence during policy training. This diversifies the discrete supervision while preserving the underlying robot action and requires no additional demonstrations. Experiments on LIBERO show that TOAST consistently improves over its deterministic counterpart, with the improvement increasing as training data decreases, achieving a 6.8 point gain in success rate when only 1/16 of training data is available. Across four real-robot manipulation tasks, TOAST further improves mean success rate by 15.8 points over the deterministic counterpart. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of stochastic action tokenization for autoregressive robot policy learning, particularly when training data are limited.

</details>


### [32] [Screw Attention: Rigid-Body Algebra Inside a Transformer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00904)
*Aly Magassouba*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Screw Attention is a transformer layer where each token is a rigid body with a pose; pairwise relations are spatial transforms (relative pose and joint screw) rather than graph edges, yielding frame-equivariant messages and a single layer that can express rigid-body velocity recursion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learned manipulation policies must rediscover from data the spatial relations that rigid-body mechanics provides in closed form. This is data-inefficient and makes policies fragile to geometric changes such as altered frame conventions or pose perturbations.

Method: Each token represents a body carrying a pose. Every token pair carries the relative pose, and robot joints additionally carry the joint screw. Messages are transported along this relation into the receiver's frame, while attention scores are computed only from frame-invariant quantities. By construction, messages are equivariant to an independent change of frame at every token, and a single layer can express the velocity recursion of rigid-body mechanics.

Result: On simulated manipulation, Screw Attention matches or outperforms same-size graph, transformer, and flat networks on LIBERO-Spatial. With 16,162 parameters it reaches 97.3% success from object poses alone (no images or language), surpassing a flat network with 27x more parameters. Under a change of per-link frame convention its success is unchanged, while all other learned networks drop below 3%. Used as a gated residual on an analytic controller it improves insertion success by 17.3 points. It is robust to pose noise up to 10 mm and to joint offsets within a Franka arm's factory calibration.

Conclusion: The results suggest a criterion: geometry is decisive when the task requires relations between frames that no other part of the system supplies. Code and trained policies will be released.

Abstract: Learned manipulation policies rediscover from data the spatial relations that rigid-body mechanics supplies in closed form. This costs data, and it leaves the policies fragile to geometric changes in the scene. We present Screw Attention, a transformer layer in which the relation between two bodies is a spatial transform rather than a graph edge. Every token is a body with a pose. Each pair of tokens carries the relative pose and, for robot joints, the joint screw. Messages are transported along this relation into the receiver's frame, while the attention scores see only frame-invariant quantities. By construction, the messages are equivariant to an independent change of frame at every token, and a single layer can express the velocity recursion of rigid-body mechanics. On simulated manipulation tasks, Screw Attention matches or outperforms controls of the same size, including graph, transformer and flat networks on LIBERO-Spatial. With 16,162 parameters it reaches 97.3% on LIBERO-Spatial from object poses (without images or language), above a flat network with 27x more parameters. Under a change of per-link frame convention its success is unchanged, while every other learned network falls below 3%. Placed on an analytic controller as a gated residual, it raises insertion success by 17.3 points. It is unaffected by pose noise up to 10,mm and by joint offsets within the factory calibration of a Franka arm. These results suggest a criterion: geometry is decisive when the task requires relations between frames that no other part of the system supplies. Code and trained policies will be released.

</details>


### [33] [eRLT: Efficient VLA Reinforcement Learning via Action-Relevant Token Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00913)
*Dehao Huang,Jianbang Liu,Jianpan Gao,Chao Tang,Zilang Cen,Zedong Dan,Jiaheng Wang,Tingguang Li,Yue Wang,Hong Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: eRLT constructs task-specific action-relevant state representations by routing information across tokens and layers of a frozen VLA, improving sample efficiency of online RL for robotic manipulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Adapting frozen Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models to downstream tasks via online RL is challenging due to limited sample efficiency, which depends on the state representation quality used by the actor and critic. Existing approaches either use VLA-independent visual encoders or fixed compression of internal VLA representations, neither explicitly extracting task-specific action-relevant VLA features needed for action refinement and action-value estimation.

Method: eRLT builds an effective state representation by routing task-specific action-relevant information across both tokens and layers of the frozen VLA. Learned routing tokens dynamically aggregate visual-language features at multiple depths, and a lightweight layer router combines these summaries into a fixed-dimensional RL token. The routing module is initialized using expert demonstrations to capture features predictive of expert actions, then refined using critic feedback from online interactions for action-value estimation.

Result: Across seven LIBERO and RoboTwin tasks, eRLT improves mean normalized learning-curve AUC by up to 23.7% over representative baselines. Real-robot experiments on USB connector insertion and motherboard ribbon-cable insertion show AUC improvements of 108.9% and 46.7% respectively over the strongest baseline.

Conclusion: eRLT demonstrates that explicitly extracting task-specific action-relevant features from a frozen VLA via cross-token and cross-layer routing yields substantial gains in sample efficiency for online RL, validated in both simulated benchmarks and real-robot manipulation tasks.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models provide strong behavioral priors for robotic manipulation, yet efficiently adapting them to downstream tasks remains challenging. Recent work addresses this challenge by adapting frozen VLAs through online reinforcement learning (RL), whose sample efficiency depends on the quality of the state representation used by the actor and critic. Existing methods construct such representations either with VLA-independent visual encoders or through fixed compression of internal VLA representations. Neither design explicitly extracts the task-specific action-relevant VLA features most useful for downstream action refinement and action-value estimation, therefore limiting sample efficiency. To address this limitation, we introduce eRLT, which constructs an effective state representation by routing task-specific action-relevant information across both tokens and layers of the frozen VLA. Specifically, learned routing tokens dynamically aggregate visual-language features at multiple depths, while a lightweight layer router combines these summaries into a fixed-dimensional RL token. The routing module is initialized using expert demonstrations to capture features predictive of expert actions and then refined using critic feedback from online interactions for action-value estimation. Across seven LIBERO and RoboTwin tasks, eRLT improves mean normalized learning-curve AUC by up to 23.7% over representative baselines. Real-robot experiments on USB connector insertion and motherboard ribbon-cable insertion further show AUC improvements of 108.9% and 46.7%, respectively, over the strongest baseline.

</details>


### [34] [A Survey on End-to-End Autonomous Driving Training from the Perspectives of Data, Strategy, and Platform](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00926)
*Chengkai Xu,Yiming Cui,Jiaqi Liu,Yicheng Guo,Cheng Qin,Geyuan Zhang,Xinwei Dong,Shiyu Fang,Peng Hang,Jian Sun*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This review surveys training methods and ecosystems for end-to-end autonomous driving (E2E-AD), proposing a Data-Strategy-Platform taxonomy that frames training as an interdependent system shaping model performance, robustness, and deployability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: End-to-end learning has become a transformative paradigm for autonomous driving by mapping multimodal sensory inputs directly to driving actions via unified differentiable models. However, the effectiveness of E2E-AD is ultimately bounded by the quality of its training ecosystem, which has not been systematically reviewed.

Method: The authors conduct a comprehensive literature review and introduce a Data-Strategy-Platform taxonomy, conceptualizing training as an interdependent system: the data layer defines what can be learned, the strategy layer governs how learning aligns with driving objectives, and the platform layer supports scalability and continuous evolution. Within this framework, they survey advances in data-centric pipelines, learning paradigms, and training infrastructures, and analyze their interplay.

Result: The review organizes recent advances across the three layers and analyzes how their interplay shapes model performance, robustness, and deployability, while identifying current limitations of E2E-AD training.

Conclusion: The paper articulates a forward-looking vision emphasizing a shift from data quantity to data value, from isolated optimization to foundation-driven generalization, and from static training to integrated training-testing loops, toward robust, scalable, and trustworthy autonomous driving systems. A continuously updated repository tracks cutting-edge literature.

Abstract: Autonomous driving is a cornerstone technology for the future of intelligent transportation, where end-to-end learning has emerged as a transformative paradigm that directly maps multimodal sensory inputs to driving actions through unified differentiable models. While offering advantages, the effectiveness of end-to-end autonomous driving (E2E-AD) is ultimately determined by the quality of its training ecosystem. This paper provides a comprehensive review of training methods and ecosystem for E2E-AD. We introduce a Data-Strategy-Platform taxonomy that conceptualizes training as an interdependent system. The data layer defines what can be learned, the strategy layer governs how learning aligns with driving objectives, and the platform layer supports scalability and continuous evolution. Within this framework, we survey recent advances across data-centric pipelines, learning paradigms, and training infrastructures, and analyze their interplay in shaping model performance, robustness, and deployability. Finally, we reflect on current limitations and articulate a forward-looking vision that emphasizes a shift from data quantity to data value, from isolated optimization to foundation-driven generalization, and from static training to integrated training-testing loops, aiming toward robust, scalable, and trustworthy autonomous driving systems. We maintain a continuously updated repository tracking cutting-edge literature and works at \href{https://github.com/Jiaaqiliu/Awesome-Training-Ecosystem-for-E2E-AD}{Our Project Page}.

</details>


### [35] [ShowerFlex: Achieving Pseudo-Static Balancing in a Continuum Shower Hose](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00936)
*Zhiyu Ren,Girish Krishnan*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper presents a low-cost, passively balanced continuum mechanism (friction-locked ball-and-socket joints plus a spring-loaded retractable reel) for a showerhead that enables an intuitive "Push-and-Stay" interaction, restoring independent bathing for older adults.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The global aging population makes independence in ADLs, especially bathing/showering, critical. Existing options for seated showering—rigid overhead showerheads or hand-held hoses—require continuous gripping and precarious maneuvers, raising fall risk. There is a need for an accessible, ergonomic, low-cost bathing aid.

Method: The authors designed a modular, highly articulated (51-DOF) pseudo-static balanced continuum mechanism using friction-locked ball-and-socket joints (loc-line) integrated with a retractable spring-loaded reel for gravity compensation. They developed a recursive forward kinematics framework and a static equilibrium analysis to assess holding capacity, and used Monte Carlo numerical simulations plus physical prototype experiments to validate morphological adaptability, stability, and actuation force.

Result: Monte Carlo simulations confirmed the system's morphological adaptability and stability across extreme positions within a standard bath space. Physical experiments validated intrinsic static stability against gravity and showed that actuation force stays within older adults' ergonomic capabilities, with low repositioning effort and no external electronic power.

Conclusion: The proposed showerhead design is low-cost and intrinsically safe, reducing physical strain and restoring dignity and autonomy in personal hygiene for older adults.

Abstract: With the global population rapidly aging, maintaining independence in Activities of Daily Living (ADLs), particularly bathing or showering, has become a critical challenge. Older adults who sit while showering currently have limited options, often relying on rigid overhead showerheads or flexible hand-held hoses that require continuous gripping and precarious user maneuvers, increasing the risk of falls. To address this need, this paper introduces a highly articulated, pseudo-static balanced continuum mechanism designed specifically for accessible bathing assistance. The proposed mechanism features a modular continuum architecture composed of friction-locked ball-and-socket joints (loc-line), seamlessly integrated with a retractable spring-loaded reel for effective gravity compensation. This hybrid design achieves an intuitive "Push-and-Stay" interaction logic, maintaining an approximate static equilibrium with ergonomically low actuation force, without any external electronic power, while significantly minimizing the force needed for repositioning. Theoretically, we established a recursive forward kinematics framework to construct the geometric model of the structure, coupled with a comprehensive static equilibrium analysis to evaluate the system's holding capacity across its 51-DOF structure. Numerical simulations utilizing Monte Carlo methods validate the system's morphological adaptability and stability at various extreme positions within a standard bath space. Physical experiments further validate the prototype's real-world performance, confirming its intrinsic static stability against gravity and ensuring that the actuation force remains well within the ergonomic capabilities of older adults. Ultimately, this research provides a low-cost, intrinsically safe showerhead design that reduces physical strain, restoring dignity and autonomy in personal hygiene.

</details>


### [36] [NarrativeFlow: Flow-Based Vision-Language-Action Model Using Robot Velocity Fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00981)
*Shota Kobayashi,Koki Seno,Daichi Yashima,Komei Sugiura*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: NarrativeFlow models language-conditioned robot manipulation as continuous velocity fields via a flow-matching formulation, achieving gains over baselines in both simulated and real-world settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language-conditioned manipulation is essential for practical robotic systems, but scaling robot foundation models is hindered by the labor-intensive collection of embodiment-specific data. Robot flows (robot velocity fields) are attractive as embodiment-agnostic, motion-centric representations for exploiting data from multiple robot platforms, yet existing methods either coarsely approximate flows with sparse keypoint displacements or fail to handle language conditioning.

Method: NarrativeFlow represents robot flows as continuous velocity fields, learned through a flow-matching formulation conditioned on language. This generative modeling produces robot flows that are physically consistent with real-world manipulation, rather than relying on sparse keypoint displacement approximations.

Result: On standard datasets for language-conditioned manipulation, NarrativeFlow outperforms representative baseline methods on standard evaluation metrics. In real-world experiments, it also achieves higher success rates than baselines across multiple manipulation tasks.

Conclusion: Modeling language-conditioned robot flows as continuous velocity fields via flow matching yields a more effective, embodiment-agnostic representation for manipulation, validated by both benchmark metrics and real-robot success rates.

Abstract: We focus on language-conditioned flow-based manipulation, where robot flows (robot velocity fields) serve as embodiment-agnostic, motion-centric representations for leveraging data collected from multiple robot platforms. This task is crucial because language-conditioned manipulation is essential for practical robotic systems, yet scaling robot foundation models remains limited by the labor-intensive collection of embodiment-specific data. Existing methods either coarsely approximate robot flows with sparse keypoint displacements, or cannot handle language-conditioned manipulation. To address this limitation, we propose NarrativeFlow, which models robot flows as continuous velocity fields using a flow-matching formulation conditioned on language. Accordingly, NarrativeFlow generates robot flows that are physically consistent with real-world manipulation. To validate NarrativeFlow, we have conducted experiments on standard datasets for language-conditioned manipulation. The experimental results show that NarrativeFlow outperforms representative baseline methods on standard evaluation metrics. Furthermore, through real-world experiments, we show that NarrativeFlow achieves higher success rates than baseline methods across multiple manipulation tasks. The project page is available at https://shota0520.github.io/NarrativeFlow-project-page/

</details>


### [37] [Divide-and-Remember: Recursive Action-Relevant Memory for Long-Horizon VLA Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00982)
*Xuehui Yu,Eason Yu,Meiyi Wang,Haozhe Du,Stefano V. Albrecht,Harold Soh*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: D&R formulates memory selection for VLA policies as an optimization problem: the optimal memory maximizes conditional mutual information I(a_t; m_t | o_t). It learns a recursive, shared-selector memory function that supports unbounded history at low compute, achieving state-of-the-art results on RoboMME with only a 64-token budget.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-language-action models fail on history-dependent manipulation tasks where the current observation alone is insufficient to determine the action. Existing memory methods use hand-designed heuristics (e.g., keeping frames with large pixel changes) and show inconsistent gains across tasks, so a principled criterion for what to remember is needed.

Method: Starting from the POMDP formulation of imitation learning, the authors derive that optimal memory maximizes the conditional mutual information I(a_t; m_t | o_t), meaning it preserves history information relevant to the action that is not already in the current observation. They propose Divide-and-Remember (D&R), a recursive memory method that learns m_t = M(h_t). It uses two strategies: (1) recursive division of full-history selection into top-K selection subproblems over 2K tokens, enabling fixed-size lightweight end-to-end learnable selectors to cover unbounded history; (2) a single shared selector across all recursion blocks to capture the common selection rule and maintain efficiency.

Result: On RoboMME, a benchmark of 16 long-horizon manipulation tasks requiring memory of when, where, what, and how to act, D&R attains state-of-the-art average success rate with consistent gains across all four suites under a 64-token budget. Real-robot experiments show the same improvement.

Conclusion: Treating memory selection as an optimization problem grounded in conditional mutual information yields a principled, compute-light, and scalable memory mechanism for VLA policies. D&R delivers consistent, state-of-the-art gains in both simulated and real-robot settings, with code and checkpoints released.

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models struggle on history-dependent manipulation tasks, where the current observation alone does not determine the action, and the policy needs a memory of the history. Existing memory methods decide what to remember by design, for example, keeping frames with large pixel changes, and show inconsistent gains across tasks. We view what to remember as an optimisation problem. From the POMDP formulation of imitation learning, we show that the optimal memory maximises the conditional mutual information $I(a_t; m_t \mid o_t)$ between the action and the memory given the current observation. Intuitively, this means preserving the action-relevant information in the history that is not already contained in the current observation. Based on our analysis, we propose Divide-and-Remember (D&R), a recursive memory method that learns a memory function $m_t = M(h_t)$ and scales to long contexts while staying compute-light. It involves two strategies: (1) the selection over the full history is divided recursively into subproblems of top-$K$ selection over $2K$ tokens, so that fixed-size, lightweight selectors learned end-to-end support an unbounded history; (2) all recursion blocks share one selector, which captures the selection rule common to every block and keeps the method efficient. On RoboMME, a benchmark of 16 long-horizon manipulation tasks that require remembering when, where, what, and how to act, D&R achieves a state-of-the-art average success rate with consistent gains across all four suites under a budget of only 64 tokens; real-robot experiments show the same gain. Code, checkpoints and more results are at https://dnr-memory.github.io/

</details>


### [38] [Online Planning for Sparse Ground Target Search from a High-Altitude UAV under Partial Observability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01067)
*Ashik E Rasul,Hyung-Jin Yoon*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper formulates UAV ground-target search using a pan-tilt-zoom (PTZ) camera as a POMDP and solves it with POMCP, using scale-conditioned sensing reliability and selective ensemble detection to find small-scale targets in far fewer steps and with minimal dependence on sensor resolution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: When UAVs search for ground targets from high altitude, targets may lie within the field of view yet remain effectively unobservable due to their small apparent scale. Standard object detectors degrade under resolution downscaling and limited context. While active object search can direct the UAV to better poses, urban airspace flight regulations often restrict such physical movement, motivating an alternative active-sensing approach that avoids repositioning the vehicle.

Method: The authors exploit the onboard camera's PTZ mechanism to dynamically adjust the field of view and sequentially gather enhanced visual information from regions of interest. The sequential exploration with PTZ operation is formulated as a POMDP in which the agent maintains a belief state over the target's true location. It is solved using partially observable Monte Carlo planning (POMCP), with sensing reliability conditioned on target object scale and a selective ensemble detection step applied as additional reasoning once candidate locations are identified.

Result: Experiments in a photorealistic simulator, spanning different environmental conditions and vehicle states, show detection of ground targets at variable scales with significantly fewer steps and minimal dependence on sensor resolution compared to baseline methods.

Conclusion: PTZ-based active sensing with POMDP planning offers an effective, regulation-compliant alternative to physical UAV repositioning for small-scale ground-target search, achieving efficient, resolution-robust detection.

Abstract: Unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs) searching for ground targets from a high altitude face a unique challenge, particularly when the target is already within the field of view but effectively unobservable because of its small apparent scale. Standard object detectors often underperform in such scenarios because of resolution downscaling and limited context. In contrast, active object search frameworks address this challenge by directing the agent to a suitable pose to gather richer visual information. However, flight regulations in urban airspace often restrict such physical movements for UAVs. As an alternative active sensing approach, the UAV can leverage the pan-tilt-zoom (PTZ) mechanism of the onboard camera to dynamically adjust its field of view and sequentially gather enhanced visual information from specific regions of interest. Once the candidate locations of the target are identified, it can deploy more expensive object detection schemes, such as an ensemble of multiple models, to get better reasoning at a fixed scale. In this work, we formulate the sequential exploration with PTZ operation as a partially observable Markov decision process (POMDP), in which the agent maintains a belief state over the target's true location. To solve the POMDP, we deploy partially observable Monte Carlo planning (POMCP), where we condition the sensing reliability on target object scale and deploy selective ensemble detection as an additional reasoning step.
  We validate our methodology with experiments in a photorealistic simulator under different environmental conditions and vehicle states, showing detection of ground targets at variable scales with significantly fewer steps and minimal dependence on sensor resolution compared to baseline methods.

</details>


### [39] [quARtet Marker: A 3D-Printable Multi-Tag Fiducial for Robust Near-Frontal Pose Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01072)
*Araki Wakiuchi,Hikaru Sasaki,Takamitsu Matsubara*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper introduces quARtet, a 3D-printable fiducial made of four tilted AprilTags within one compact footprint, addressing the poor reliability of single planar tags in near-frontal views while preserving graspability. Evaluated in fixed-camera and robot-mounted-camera experiments, the three layouts reduce frontal orientation error from 2.18° to 0.24–0.47° and RMS position error from 1.50 mm to 0.17–0.20 mm; layouts with flat contact strips retain objects with ~2 mm in-grasp slip during swing-down trials versus ~100 mm for the layout without flat strips.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robotic manipulation of transparent or reflective labware is hard because such objects are difficult to identify and localize. Coded planar fiducials serve as a practical, printable retrofit that keeps the marked face flat and graspable, but a single planar tag is least reliable in near-frontal views where perspective cues fade. Non-planar geometries restore those cues, yet they intrude on the flat face that a parallel-jaw gripper must contact, creating a tension between pose-estimation accuracy and graspability.

Method: The idea is to tilt multiple tags within one compact footprint so each tag is seen at a non-frontal angle even when the marker faces the camera. The proposed quARtet marker is a 3D-printable fiducial embodying this: all detected corners of its four tilted AprilTags enter a single Perspective-n-Point solve, and a shared configuration defines both the fabricated geometry and the detector model. Three layouts are designed that trade pose-estimation consistency against graspability, since tilting consumes flat area. Validation uses robot-referenced, same-setup fixed-camera experiments and a robot-mounted-camera pose-hold test under closed-loop visual feedback, plus swing-down trials under identical conditions.

Result: In fixed-camera experiments, all three layouts reduced the mean frontal orientation error from 2.18° for a single planar tag to 0.24–0.47°, and the root-mean-square position error from 1.50 mm to 0.17–0.20 mm. A robot-mounted-camera pose-hold test confirmed this separation under closed-loop visual feedback. In swing-down trials under identical conditions, the two layouts with flat contact strips retained the object with about 2 mm of in-grasp slip, whereas the layout without flat strips slipped by roughly 100 mm.

Conclusion: For the tested conditions, the results support a selection rule: choose the layout without flat strips when pose-estimation consistency dominates, and a layout with flat strips when the marked face must remain graspable.

Abstract: Robotic manipulation of labware is difficult when transparent or reflective objects must be identified and localized. Coded planar fiducials are a practical retrofit: easy to print, they leave the marked face flat and graspable. Yet a single planar tag is least reliable in near-frontal views, where perspective cues fade. Non-planar geometries restore those cues but intrude on the flat face that a parallel-jaw gripper must contact. Our idea is to tilt multiple tags within one compact footprint, so that each tag is seen at a non-frontal angle even when the marker faces the camera. We propose the quARtet marker, a 3D-printable fiducial embodying this idea: all detected corners of its four tilted AprilTags enter one Perspective-n-Point solve, and a shared configuration defines the fabricated geometry and the detector model. Because tilting consumes flat area, its three layouts trade pose-estimation consistency against graspability. In robot-referenced, same-setup fixed-camera experiments, all three layouts reduced the mean frontal orientation error from 2.18 degree for a single planar tag to 0.24-0.47 degree and the root-mean-square position error from 1.50 to 0.17-0.20 mm. A robot-mounted-camera pose-hold test confirmed this separation under closed-loop visual feedback. In swing-down trials under identical conditions, the two layouts with flat contact strips retained the object with about 2 mm of in-grasp slip, whereas the layout without flat strips slipped by roughly 100 mm. For the tested conditions, the results support a rule: the layout without flat strips when pose-estimation consistency dominates, a layout with flat strips when the marked face must remain graspable.

</details>


### [40] [WBAG: A Whole-Body and Attached-Geometry Safety Framework for Vision-Language-Action Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01083)
*Samuel Zhen,Siwon Jo,Yanze Zhang,Wenhao Luo*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: WBAG is an inference-time safety framework for VLA manipulation policies that models the robot's whole-body and grasp-dependent attached geometry, converting a grasp-conditioned safe set into differentiable CBF constraints to minimally adjust the action for collision avoidance; it achieves 97.38% Scene Safety and 59.38% Safe Success on SafeLIBERO.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deploying VLA policies in the real world is limited by potential collisions involving the robot, manipulated objects, and the environment. Existing inference-time safety frameworks rely on simplified end-effector-centered representations that fail to explicitly model the full articulated robot and attached-object geometry, leaving whole-body and grasped-object collisions insufficiently handled.

Method: WBAG models the robot's whole-body and grasp-dependent attached geometry. It constructs a grasp-conditioned safe set that adapts the protected geometry as objects are grasped, then converts this evolving geometry into differentiable Control Barrier Function (CBF) constraints. These constraints minimally modify the VLA's native six-dimensional operational-space action to avoid collisions across the robot, scene, and attached geometry.

Result: On SafeLIBERO (a LIBERO variant augmented with obstacles for safety evaluation), WBAG achieves the best overall safety and safe task success among evaluated methods under a scene-level safety evaluator monitoring all eligible non-task objects, reaching 97.38% aggregate Scene Safety and 59.38% Safe Success.

Conclusion: Explicitly modeling whole-body and grasp-conditioned attached geometry via differentiable CBF constraints provides an effective inference-time safety mechanism for VLA manipulation, improving both collision avoidance and safe task success.

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) policies have demonstrated impressive capabilities in generalizable robotic manipulation, but their deployment in the real world remains challenging due to potential collisions involving different parts of the robot, manipulated objects, and the surrounding environment. Existing inference-time VLA safety frameworks typically rely on simplified end-effector-centered representations that do not explicitly model the full articulated robot and attached-object geometry. In this paper, we present WBAG, a safety framework that models the robot's whole-body and grasp-dependent attached geometry. WBAG constructs a grasp-conditioned safe set that adapts the protected geometry as objects are grasped, then converts this evolving geometry into differentiable CBF constraints that minimally modify the VLA's native six-dimensional operational-space action for collision avoidance across robot, scene, and attached geometry. On the SafeLIBERO benchmark, a variant of LIBERO augmented with obstacles for safety evaluation, WBAG achieves the best overall safety and safe task success among the evaluated methods under a scene-level safety evaluator that monitors all eligible non-task objects, reaching 97.38\% aggregate Scene Safety and 59.38\% Safe Success.

</details>


### [41] [OrbitTAMP: Grounding Language Models for Task and Motion Planning in Spacecraft Rendezvous](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01093)
*Yuji Takubo,Daniele Gammelli,Marco Pavone,Simone D'Amico*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper introduces a hierarchical framework for spacecraft task-and-motion planning (TAMP) that grounds LLM reasoning in a graph of reusable behaviors and domain-specific planners to enable natural-language-driven rendezvous and proximity operations planning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spacecraft rendezvous and proximity operations (RPO) currently rely on an expertise-intensive process where engineers manually translate high-level operational intent into safe, dynamically feasible trajectories. This creates a bottleneck to scalable operations. While LLM-based agents could provide an intuitive interface, their outputs are not inherently grounded in orbital dynamics, operational constraints, or admissible spacecraft maneuver structures, risking physically invalid plans.

Method: A hierarchical framework for spacecraft TAMP that grounds LLM reasoning in a graph of reusable behaviors and domain-specific planning modules. A pretrained LLM maps a natural-language command to a partial mission specification; associated planners resolve unspecified decisions within the admissible operational space; and trajectory optimization converts the completed mission specification into a dynamically feasible trajectory. Test-time-compute experiments include verifier-guided revision and broader behavior-plan search.

Result: The architecture substantially improves intent recovery over direct LLM generation, achieving 98% exact recovery of partial mission specifications across all evaluated splits when backed by frontier LLMs. For a compact 9B model, verifier-guided revision increases exact recovery from 75% to 88%, while broader behavior-plan search independently improves selection among admissible trajectory realizations.

Conclusion: These results establish a scalable and auditable foundation for language-driven agentic planning of spacecraft RPO.

Abstract: Spacecraft rendezvous and proximity operations (RPO) are currently planned through an expertise-intensive process in which engineers translate high-level operational intent into safe, dynamically feasible trajectories, creating a bottleneck to scalable operations. Large language model (LLM)-based agents could offer an intuitive interface for this process, although their outputs are not inherently grounded in orbital dynamics, operational constraints, or the structure of admissible spacecraft maneuvers. To exploit their semantic reasoning while ensuring the generated plan's physical validity, this paper presents a hierarchical framework for spacecraft task-and-motion planning (TAMP) that grounds LLM reasoning in a graph of reusable behaviors and domain-specific planning modules. Within this framework, a pretrained LLM maps a natural-language command to a partial mission specification. The associated planners then resolve unspecified decisions within the admissible operational space. Finally, trajectory optimization converts the completed mission specification into a dynamically feasible trajectory. Numerical experiments demonstrate that this architecture substantially improves intent recovery over direct LLM generation, achieving 98% exact recovery of partial mission specifications across all evaluated splits when backed by frontier LLMs. Additional test-time-compute experiments show that, for a compact 9B model, verifier-guided revision increases exact recovery from 75% to 88%, while broader behavior-plan search independently improves selection among admissible trajectory realizations. Overall, these results establish a scalable and auditable foundation for language-driven agentic planning of spacecraft RPO.

</details>


### [42] [MASkillBlender: Decentralized Whole-Body Coordination for Multi-Humanoid Loco-Manipulation via Skill Blending](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01102)
*Yifan Hu,Luhang Hong,Mingkang Long,Danning Wang,Chengfeng Jia,Rong Su,Junjie Fu,Guanghui Wen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: MASkillBlender is a general decentralized multi-agent RL framework for multi-humanoid loco-manipulation that learns a shared high-level policy over pre-trained single-humanoid skills, requiring only task-level rewards. It adds permutation-based data augmentation for homogeneous systems with theoretical policy-gradient preservation, achieving strong coordination across tasks and embodiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Coordinated multi-humanoid loco-manipulation is challenging due to high-dimensional whole-body control, decentralized decision making, and scalability. Existing RL methods work for single-humanoid whole-body control but extending them to multi-humanoid settings is nontrivial, often demanding substantial reward engineering or task-specific design.

Method: The authors propose MASkillBlender, a general multi-agent RL framework for decentralized multi-humanoid whole-body coordination. It learns a shared decentralized high-level policy over reusable pre-trained single-humanoid skills, enabling coordination with only task-level rewards and no task-specific motion references. They also introduce a permutation-based data augmentation strategy for homogeneous multi-humanoid systems, with theoretical proof that permuted samples preserve the policy-gradient direction of original samples under the homogeneous Markov game formulation.

Result: Evaluated on multiple multi-humanoid coordination tasks across two humanoid embodiments, the framework consistently achieves strong task performance and enables coordinated behaviors across different tasks and humanoid embodiments.

Conclusion: MASkillBlender provides a general and effective solution for decentralized multi-humanoid whole-body coordination, leveraging reusable single-humanoid skills and permutation-based augmentation to improve learning efficiency and demonstrate cross-task, cross-embodiment generalization.

Abstract: Coordinated multi-humanoid loco-manipulation is promising yet challenging due to high-dimensional whole-body control, decentralized decision making, and scalability. While recent reinforcement learning methods have improved single-humanoid whole-body control, extending them to the multi-humanoid setting remains nontrivial and often requires substantial reward engineering or task-specific design. We propose MASkillBlender, a general multi-agent reinforcement learning framework to achieve decentralized multi-humanoid whole-body coordination. By learning a shared decentralized high-level policy over reusable pre-trained single-humanoid skills, MASkillBlender enables coordinated behaviors using only task-level rewards, without requiring task-specific motion references. To improve learning efficiency, we further introduce a permutation-based data augmentation strategy for homogeneous multi-humanoid systems, and theoretically show that the permuted samples preserve the policy-gradient direction of the original samples under the homogeneous Markov game formulation. We evaluate MASkillBlender on multiple multi-humanoid coordination tasks across two humanoid embodiments. Simulation results demonstrate that the proposed framework consistently achieves strong task performance and enables coordinated behaviors across different tasks and humanoid embodiments.

</details>


### [43] [Extreme Length Generalization in a Compact Recurrent Architecture for One-Shot Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01105)
*Izen Thornton,Aaron Shey,William Su*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: FRANK, a 507K-parameter recurrent architecture combining tau-gated recurrence, content-addressable memory, and a feedforward reflex pathway, retains perfect accuracy far beyond its training horizon where recurrent, state-space, and modular baselines fail, and transfers to a physical ground vehicle.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous robots on one-shot missions must operate over horizons vastly longer than training trajectories, under a fixed onboard compute budget. Existing sequence models degrade over such extended horizons, motivating an architecture that sustains long-horizon reliability.

Method: FRANK is a 507K-parameter recurrent architecture with three components: tau-gated recurrent modules, content-addressable memory, and a feedforward reflex pathway. It was benchmarked against recurrent, state-space, and reduced modular baselines at matched parameter count on four algorithmic sequence tasks, trained at length 5-20 and evaluated to two million tokens. Component-reliance was probed via targeted lesions, and a simulation-trained policy was deployed on a physical ground vehicle.

Result: At 100,000x the maximum training length, 6 of 10 FRANK seeds retain exactly 100.0% accuracy, whereas none of 50 baseline configurations (five architectures, ten seeds each, none incomplete) does (Fisher exact, two-sided p = 4.2E-6). Targeted lesions yield four distinct component-reliance profiles across tasks. The simulation-trained FRANK policy drives a physical ground vehicle to commanded waypoints through obstacles without teleoperation.

Conclusion: The three-pathway recurrent design sustains long-horizon sequence performance beyond all baselines, with task-dependent allocation across recurrent, memory, and reflex pathways. FRANK also supports zero-teleoperation sim-to-real waypoint navigation, indicating practical viability for long-horizon autonomous missions.

Abstract: Autonomous robots on one-shot missions run over horizons far longer than the trajectories seen during training, under a fixed onboard compute budget. We present FRANK, a 507K-parameter recurrent architecture that combines tau-gated recurrent modules, content-addressable memory, and a feedforward reflex pathway. We evaluate it against recurrent, state-space, and reduced modular baselines at matched parameter count on four algorithmic sequence tasks, trained at length 5-20 and evaluated out to two million tokens. At 100,000x the maximum training length, 6 of 10 FRANK seeds retain exactly 100.0% accuracy, while none of the 50 baseline configurations does, five architectures at ten seeds each with none left incomplete (Fisher exact, two-sided p = 4.2E-6. Targeted lesions across the four tasks yield four distinct component-reliance profiles, consistent with task-dependent allocation across the recurrent, memory, and reflex pathways. Separately, a FRANK policy trained in simulation drives a physical ground vehicle to commanded waypoints through obstacles without teleoperation.

</details>


### [44] [Experience-Based Feasibility-Aware Generative Adversarial Imitation from Observation under Embodiment Mismatch](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01171)
*Yoshiki Takebayashi,Giovanni Perantoni,Hikaru Sasaki,Matteo Saveriano,Takamitsu Matsubara*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: EF-GAIfO performs generative adversarial imitation from observation by estimating demonstration feasibility from the robot's own experience and progressively expanding the feasible region as policy learning advances.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robot-free demonstration interfaces provide only state trajectories without action labels, making imitation from observation attractive, but human-robot differences in embodiment and dynamics can render demonstrated motions infeasible and degrade policy performance. Existing feasibility solutions require explicit dynamics models or large prior exploration datasets, which are costly and may not adapt during learning.

Method: EF-GAIfO estimates the feasibility of state-only demonstrations using the robot's own collected experience, avoiding explicit dynamics models or large prior exploration datasets. The feasibility notion evolves with policy learning: as the policy improves and experiences broader state transitions, the feasible region expands so more demonstrations can be incorporated, yielding feasibility-aware imitation adapted to the current learning stage.

Result: Validated on a simulated locomotion task and on a real quadruped robot performing an object-reaching-and-grasping task, demonstrating effectiveness.

Conclusion: Feasibility-aware imitation from observation can be achieved online from the robot's own experience, with a self-expanding feasible region that improves over training, effectively handling infeasible human demonstrations without requiring explicit dynamics models or large pre-collected datasets.

Abstract: With the increasing use of robot-free demonstration interfaces that provide state trajectories without action labels, imitation from observation has become a promising approach for learning robot behaviors from human demonstrations. However, due to differences in embodiment and dynamics between humans and robots, demonstrated human motions may not be feasible for the robot, potentially degrading policy performance. In this study, we propose Experience-Based Feasibility-Aware Generative Adversarial Imitation from Observation (EF-GAIfO), which estimates the feasibility of state-only demonstrations from the robot's own experience rather than relying on explicit dynamics models or large prior exploration datasets. A key feature of EF-GAIfO is that the notion of feasibility evolves with policy learning: as the policy improves and the robot experiences a broader range of state transitions, the feasible region is progressively expanded, allowing additional demonstrations to be incorporated into learning. This enables feasibility-aware imitation that adapts to the current stage of policy learning, rather than relying on a pre-designed feasibility criterion. We validate the effectiveness of EF-GAIfO on a locomotion task in simulation and on a real quadruped robot performing a object-reaching-and-grasping task.

</details>


### [45] [Recova: Agent-Guided Failure Recovery for Autonomous Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01178)
*Isabella Liu,An-Chieh Cheng,Johan Bjorck,Zhiding Yu,Hongxu Yin,Jan Kautz,Linxi Fan,Yuke Zhu,Sifei Liu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Recova is an agent-guided framework that jointly learns task execution and failure recovery in a reconstructed digital twin and refines both with real-world rollouts and human demonstrations via DAgger, achieving large success-rate gains on LIBERO-Pro and MolmoSpaces and reducing human intervention to zero over rounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manipulation failures can leave scenes unrecoverable for a task policy, and learning corrective behaviors requires scalable failure exploration plus physical grounding, which existing approaches lack.

Method: Agent-guided framework developing task execution and recovery in a reconstructed digital twin, then verifying and refining them in the real world. In the twin, an agent diagnoses failures, tests corrective programs, and collects rollouts for separate task and recovery policies. At deployment it monitors progress, invokes learned or programmatic recovery, verifies scene restoration, and resumes execution; unresolvable failures are addressed by human demonstrations that re-enter the learning loop. Physical rollouts and demonstrations are routed to the corresponding policy for DAgger training.

Result: On six LIBERO-Pro settings and four MolmoSpaces categories, Recova achieves 78.8% and 64.9% mean success versus 71.7% and 38.0% for the strongest baselines. Parallel collection across four real-robot workstations raises success from 23.8% to 77.5% with DAgger fine-tuning, and recovery skills further raise it to 87.5%. Human intervention drops from 87.5% to 0% over four collection rounds on one task.

Conclusion: Agent-guided recovery converts failures into reusable capabilities, improving robustness while progressively reducing human intervention.

Abstract: Manipulation failures can leave scenes in states from which a task policy cannot recover. Learning corrective behaviors requires scalable failure exploration and physical grounding. We present Recova, an agent-guided framework that jointly develops task execution and recovery in a reconstructed digital twin, then verifies and refines both through real-world experience. In the twin, the agent diagnoses failures, tests corrective programs, and collects successful task and recovery rollouts for separate policies. During deployment, it monitors progress, invokes a learned or programmatic recovery, verifies scene restoration, and resumes execution. When no suitable recovery is available, a human demonstration resolves the failure and enters the learning loop, allowing the system to expand its recovery capabilities. Physical rollouts and human demonstrations are routed to the corresponding policy for DAgger training. Across six LIBERO-Pro settings and four MolmoSpaces categories, Recova achieves 78.8% and 64.9% mean success, compared with 71.7% and 38.0% for the strongest baselines. With parallel collection across four real-robot workstations, DAgger fine-tuning raises mean success from 23.8% to 77.5%, and recovery skills further raise it to 87.5%. Over four collection rounds on one task, observed human intervention falls from 87.5% to 0%. Together, these results show how agent-guided recovery turns failures into reusable capabilities, improving robustness while progressively reducing human intervention. Project page: https://www.liuisabella.com/Recova

</details>


### [46] [AFD-CAMLs: Agile Force-Distribution-Aware Planning and Control for Cable-Suspended Aerial Multi-Lifting Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01185)
*Antreas Kourris,Sihao Sun*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A hybrid planning-and-control framework enables multiple UAVs to cooperatively transport heavy payloads with balanced cable-force distributions while preserving agility and tracking performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cooperative multi-UAV payload transport benefits from trajectory-based methods that ensure agility and satisfy system constraints, but these methods can produce uneven force distributions when the tension-to-wrench allocation is redundant or ill-conditioned, especially under geometric mismatch and low-level tracking errors.

Method: The proposed framework combines: (1) a global planner that generates payload trajectories and cable-force references by exploring the allocation null space under a prescribed internal-force setting; (2) a centralized local planner whose cost is augmented by these references to promote feasible force distributions while generating trajectories for all UAVs; and (3) an admittance filter that compares planned forces with onboard cable-tension estimates and adjusts kinematic references to improve force tracking in degenerate or near-degenerate configurations.

Result: Simulations and experiments with four to ten UAVs showed more balanced tension distributions during hovering and demanding agile maneuvers, without compromising agility or payload-tracking performance.

Conclusion: The hybrid planning-and-control framework improves force distribution balance in cooperative multi-UAV payload transport while maintaining agile maneuverability and accurate payload tracking.

Abstract: Multiple UAVs can cooperatively transport heavy payloads while controlling their position and orientation. Trajectory-based methods offer high agility while satisfying system constraints, but can produce uneven force distributions when the tension-to-wrench allocation is redundant or ill-conditioned, particularly under geometric mismatch and low-level tracking errors. We propose a hybrid planning-and-control framework to address this problem. A global planner generates payload trajectories and cable-force references by exploring the allocation null space under a prescribed internal-force setting. These references augment the cost of a centralized local planner, promoting feasible force distributions while generating trajectories for all UAVs. An admittance filter then compares the planned forces with onboard cable-tension estimates and adjusts the kinematic references to improve force tracking in degenerate or near-degenerate configurations. Simulations and experiments involving four to ten UAVs demonstrate more balanced tension distributions during both hovering and demanding agile maneuvers, without compromising agility or payload-tracking performance.

</details>
