### [47] [EIDA: Execution-Interface Dynamics Adaptation for Real-to-Sim-to-Real Robot Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01219)
*Yiwei Qian,Shanze Wang,Qingyuan Hu,Xinming Zhang,Wei Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper introduces EIDA, a method that adapts the execution interface (motion and velocity feedback) from target-platform data, without modeling actuator dynamics, to improve sim-to-robot navigation transfer. EIDA outperforms baselines in simulation benchmarks and achieves 20/20 collision-free Go2 trials versus 4/20 for the baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Simulation-to-robot transfer often fails because velocity commands produce motion and feedback on the real platform that differ from what was modeled during policy training. Reconstructing detailed actuator dynamics is difficult, so an alternative is needed that adapts the execution interface directly from target-platform data.

Method: EIDA fits execution-interface responses from target-platform execution data rather than reconstructing actuator dynamics. It uses a model of body-frame pose increments to update simulator geometry, and a separate model to predict the velocity feedback observed by the policy. A short history of velocity feedback is added to the policy input. The fitted models are deployed within a lightweight GPU-parallel simulator.

Result: On the full Jackal and Go2 validation sets, the fitted models reduced position and yaw prediction errors relative to the simulator's predefined motion model. Across 100 benchmark navigation environments in a separate physics-based simulator, EIDA achieved the highest success rate and navigation score among compared learned policies, both with and without global guidance. Feedback ablations confirmed the need to match policy-facing velocity estimates. On a physical Unitree Go2, EIDA reached the goal without collision in all 20 static-scene trials, versus 4 of 20 for the baseline.

Conclusion: Execution-interface adaptation can improve navigation transfer without requiring detailed actuator simulation, as demonstrated by reduced prediction errors, superior benchmark performance, and substantially higher real-robot success.

Abstract: Simulation-to-robot transfer can fail when velocity commands produce motion and feedback that differ from those modeled during policy training. We present execution-interface dynamics adaptation (EIDA), which fits these responses from target-platform execution data without reconstructing actuator dynamics. A model of body-frame pose increments updates simulator geometry, while a separate model predicts the velocity feedback observed by the policy; a short history of velocity feedback is included in the policy input. The fitted models are used within a lightweight GPU-parallel simulator. On the full Jackal and Go2 validation sets, the fitted models reduced position and yaw prediction errors relative to the simulator's predefined motion model. Across 100 benchmark navigation environments evaluated in a separate physics-based simulator, EIDA achieved the highest success rate and navigation score among the compared learned policies, both with and without global guidance. Feedback ablations further supported the need to match policy-facing velocity estimates. On a physical Unitree Go2, EIDA reached the goal without collision in all 20 static-scene trials, compared with 4 of 20 for the baseline. These results show that execution-interface adaptation can improve navigation transfer without detailed actuator simulation.

</details>


### [48] [ColoACT: Multi-Cue Action Chunking for Smooth Autonomous Colon Navigation on a Self-Propelled Endoscopic Robot](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01258)
*Jian Hu,Shujing He,Leixin Chang,Zongze Li,Ding Huang,Chaoyang Shi,Chengzhi Hu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ColoACT is an autonomous colonoscopy navigation system combining an RGB-D-E Action Chunking Transformer policy with a self-propelled Bevel-Gear-Based Endoscopic Robot (BGER), achieving high success rates in ex-vivo porcine colons including curved segments and turns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous colonoscopic navigation can reduce operator burden and the risk of loop formation or tissue trauma, but it remains challenging due to deformable anatomy, weak-texture and specular endoscopic visuals, and contact-rich viscoelastic interactions. Existing geometry-driven pipelines are efficient and interpretable yet brittle due to manually engineered features and switching logic, while learning-based policies produce temporally inconsistent or overly smooth depth/geometry under weak texture and specular highlights, and simulation-trained variants (e.g., deep reinforcement learning) may suffer from a sim-to-real gap.

Method: The authors propose ColoACT, an autonomous navigation system integrating an RGB-D-E based Action Chunking Transformer policy (ColoACT policy) with a compact self-propelled Bevel-Gear-Based Endoscopic Robot (BGER). The policy augments RGB with estimated relative depth and a gradient-based pseudo-elevation map to enhance fold-ridge saliency and other high-frequency geometric cues, and enables smooth continuous control of the BGER by predicting overlapping action chunks and fusing them via temporal ensembling.

Result: In different ex-vivo porcine colons (approximately 60 cm), the system achieves success rates of 85.4% and 72.5% in straight and curved segments, respectively, 70% success in 90-degree turns and 60% in double-bend sequences, with feasibility further demonstrated in challenging triple-bend segments.

Conclusion: ColoACT demonstrates feasible autonomous colonoscopic navigation on a self-propelled endoscopic robot, with strong performance across straight, curved, single-turn, and multi-bend scenarios in ex-vivo porcine colons. The project page is available at: https://Adamhu1.github.io/ColoACT/.

Abstract: Autonomous colonoscopic navigation can reduce operator burden and the risk of loop formation or tissue trauma, but remains challenging due to deformable anatomy, weak-texture and specular endoscopic visuals, and contact-rich viscoelastic interactions. Existing methods either rely on geometry-driven pipelines, which are efficient and interpretable yet brittle due to manually engineered features and switching logic, or adopt learning-based policies, whose inferred depth/geometry can become temporally inconsistent or overly smooth under weak texture and specular highlights while simulation-trained variants (e.g., deep reinforcement learning) may further suffer from a sim-to-real gap. We propose ColoACT, an autonomous navigation system that integrates an RGB-D-E based Action Chunking Transformer policy (ColoACT policy) for a compact self-propelled Bevel-Gear-Based Endoscopic Robot (BGER). The ColoACT policy augments RGB with estimated relative depth and a gradient-based pseudo-elevation map to enhance fold-ridge saliency and other high-frequency geometric cues, and enables smooth continuous control of the BGER by predicting overlapping action chunks and fusing them via temporal ensembling. In different \textit{ex-vivo} porcine colons (approximately 60 cm), our system achieves success rates of 85.4\% and 72.5\% in straight and curved segments, respectively, and achieves 70\% success in 90-degree turns and 60\% in double-bend sequences, with feasibility further demonstrated in challenging triple-bend segments. The project page is available at: https://Adamhu1.github.io/ColoACT/.

</details>


### [49] [PROMO: Preference-conditioned Multi-Objective Reinforcement Learning for Quadrupedal Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01260)
*Amr Mousa,Rifny Rachman,Neil Karavis,Michele Caprio,Richard Allmendinger*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: PROMO introduces preference-conditioned multi-objective RL for quadruped locomotion, allowing runtime trade-offs among command tracking, stability, and energy efficiency within a single deployable policy, achieving broad Pareto coverage and zero-shot transfer to a Unitree Go2.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional RL for quadrupedal locomotion hardcodes conflicting objectives (command tracking, stability, energy efficiency) into a fixed scalar reward at training time, preventing runtime adjustment of priorities and requiring separate specialists for different trade-offs.

Method: PROMO is a semantic multi-objective approach that conditions a single locomotion policy on deployment-facing preferences while keeping embodiment-specific locomotion priors fixed. It separates operator intent from reward shaping terms needed for viable gait generation, and is compared against fixed-objective controllers, multi-objective baselines, and independently trained specialists.

Result: Across 100 sampled preferences in simulation, 67 behaviors are non-dominated under exact Pareto dominance, with a mean preference-objective correlation of 0.843. The same policy transfers zero-shot to a Unitree Go2, where preference changes alone reduce specific energy by up to 30.4%, position error by 38.7%, and peak body-attitude deviation by 59.0% relative to the balanced preference.

Conclusion: Preference-conditioned multi-objective RL serves as a practical runtime interface for adaptive legged locomotion, extending its role beyond offline Pareto-set construction and enabling objective specialization and robustness from a single deployable policy.

Abstract: Quadrupedal locomotion requires balancing conflicting objectives such as command tracking, stability, and energy efficiency, yet conventional reinforcement learning (RL) hardcodes these priorities into a fixed scalar reward at training time. We present PROMO (Preference-Conditioned Multi-Objective Reinforcement Learning), a semantic multi-objective approach that makes this trade-off an explicit runtime input to a single locomotion policy. PROMO conditions the policy on deployment facing preferences while keeping embodiment-specific locomotion priors fixed, thereby separating operator intent from reward shaping terms required for viable gait generation. Compared with fixed-objective controllers, multi-objective baselines, and independently trained specialists, PROMO achieves objective specialization and robustness from a single deployable policy. Across 100 sampled preferences in simulation, 67 behaviors are non-dominated under exact Pareto dominance, with a mean preference-objective correlation of 0.843, demonstrating broad Pareto coverage and predictable preference response. The same policy transfers zero-shot to a Unitree Go2, where preference changes alone reduce specific energy by up to 30.4%, position error by 38.7%, and peak body-attitude deviation by 59.0% relative to the balanced preference. These results establish preference-conditioned multi-objective RL as a practical runtime interface for adaptive legged locomotion, extending its role beyond offline Pareto-set construction. Open-source code and videos are available at https://amrmousa.com/promo/.

</details>


### [50] [Interaction-Stiffness-Guided Basis Allocation in Dynamic Movement Primitives for Efficient Skill Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01288)
*Chan Xu,Silu Chen,Dehao Wang,Xiyu Chen,Dexin Jiang,Chi Zhang,Guilin Yang,Chenguang Yang,Zaojun Fang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes Stage-Criticality-Guided Dynamic Movement Primitives (SC-DMPs), which adaptively redistribute basis centers and refine bandwidths according to a stage-criticality index, enabling precision-focused trajectory representation while preserving the compact, stable structure of classical DMPs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical DMPs use a predefined basis layout, which limits how approximation capacity can be allocated according to stage-dependent precision requirements in robot skill learning.

Method: A stage-criticality index is constructed from operator-robot interaction stiffness and a trajectory-consistency cue derived from cross-demonstration task-space variability. Guided by this index, basis centers are redistributed in normalized time via inverse cumulative criticality and mapped to the canonical phase domain, while bandwidths are refined to adjust local approximation support.

Result: Experiments on handwriting trajectories and three real-robot tasks show that inferred criticality concentrates in geometrically demanding and task-constrained regions. Comparisons with DMPs, ProMPs, ProDMP, GP-MP, and KMP demonstrate improved trajectory reproduction, endpoint generalization, and task-critical accuracy.

Conclusion: SC-DMPs achieve denser, more flexible representation at high-criticality stages and sparser allocation elsewhere, improving precision-critical skill learning while retaining a compact model and the stable structure of classical DMPs.

Abstract: Dynamic Movement Primitives (DMPs) provide a compact and stable formulation for trajectory representation and generalization in robot skill learning. However, their predefined basis layout limits the allocation of approximation capacity according to stage-dependent precision requirements. To address this issue, this article proposes Stage-Criticality-Guided Dynamic Movement Primitives (SC-DMPs) with adaptive basis allocation for precision-critical skill learning. Operator-robot interaction stiffness and a trajectory-consistency cue derived from cross-demonstration task-space variability are integrated to construct a stage-criticality index. Guided by this index, basis centers are redistributed in normalized time through inverse cumulative criticality and mapped to the canonical phase domain, while their bandwidths are refined to adjust local approximation support. This enables denser and more flexible representation at high-criticality stages while retaining sparser allocation elsewhere. Experiments on handwriting trajectories and three real-robot tasks show that the inferred criticality is concentrated in geometrically demanding and task-constrained regions. Comparisons with DMPs, ProMPs, ProDMP, GP-MP, and KMP demonstrate improved trajectory reproduction, endpoint generalization, and task-critical accuracy while retaining a compact model and the stable structure of classical DMPs.

</details>


### [51] [Continual Learning for 6-DoF Grasp Synthesis via Experience and Demonstrations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01301)
*Giulio Schiavi,Andrei Cramariuc,Michael Pantic,Roland Siegwart*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A continual-learning framework for single-view 6-DoF grasp synthesis in clutter that adapts online via memory in a learned embedding space, achieving >90% real-world success on hard categories after ~50 trials.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most grasp synthesis systems are trained offline and fixed at deployment, so performance degrades under unseen conditions such as unfamiliar objects. The paper targets online adaptation for single-view 6-DoF grasping of a parallel-jaw gripper in cluttered scenes.

Method: Instead of finetuning a large parametric model, adaptation occurs through memory in a learned embedding space: grasp outcomes update future grasp scores, and optional user demonstrations are recalled and transferred to new scenes as additional candidate grasps.

Result: Evaluated in simulation and real-world experiments with over 1500 grasp trials: the method matches existing 6-DoF grasping baselines before adaptation, improves online on unseen objects from categories absent or underrepresented in training, supports long-horizon continual learning with limited forgetting, and exceeds 90% success on several challenging categories after only 50 online grasp attempts.

Conclusion: Memory-based adaptation in an embedding space enables effective, low-forgetting continual learning for 6-DoF grasp synthesis, providing strong online gains on unseen objects without sacrificing baseline performance.

Abstract: Most current grasp synthesis systems are trained offline and remain fixed during deployment. While this works well when deployment conditions resemble the training data, performance can degrade when robots encounter conditions they have not seen before, such as unfamiliar objects. In this work, we present a continual-learning framework for single-view 6-DoF grasp synthesis for a parallel-jaw gripper in cluttered scenes. Rather than finetuning a large parametric model, our method adapts through memory in a learned embedding space: grasp outcomes update future grasp scores, while optional user demonstrations are recalled and transferred to new scenes as additional candidate grasps. We evaluate our method in simulation and in extensive real-world experiments comprising over 1500 grasp trials. We show that our method matches the performance of existing 6-DoF grasping baselines even before adaptation, improves online on unseen objects from categories absent or underrepresented during training, and supports long-horizon continual learning with limited forgetting. In real-world experiments, our method reaches over 90\% success rates on several challenging object categories after only 50 online grasp attempts. Videos and code at https://giuschio.github.io/cl_grasping/.

</details>


### [52] [Ex vivo breach detection using electrical conductivity during robotic pedicle drilling in the spine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01310)
*Jorge Andrés Pérez Velásquez,François Teyssere,Thibault Chandanson,Quentin Grimal,Brahim Tamadazte*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper presents robotic pedicle drilling with a proprioceptive electrical bioimpedance sensor for real-time breach prevention during scoliosis surgery.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pedicle screw placement in scoliosis treatment is technically demanding, requiring high precision due to limited visibility, anatomical variability, and complication risks. Existing robotic systems rely on ionizing intraoperative imaging and complex registration.

Method: A robotic drilling approach was combined with a pedicle-drilling tool equipped with a proprioceptive electrical bioimpedance sensor developed by SpineGuard. A real-time detection algorithm analyzed the bioimpedance signal during drilling to identify abrupt conductivity changes associated with potential breaches toward the spinal canal, without external devices or sensors.

Result: Ex vivo experiments demonstrated that the proposed method prevented breaches in all 51 drilling cases, showing the system's ability to detect hazardous events during drilling and stop the procedure beforehand.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates the feasibility of robotic pedicle drilling with electrical bioimpedance sensing for real-time breach prevention, eliminating the need for external sensing systems and supporting safer pedicle screw placement.

Abstract: Purpose: Pedicle screw placement is technically demanding in scoliosis treatment. High precision is required due to limited visibility, anatomical variability, and the risk of complications. Although robotic systems assist CT-based planning and execution, they still rely on ionizing intraoperative imaging and complex registration. This study proposes robotic pedicle drilling with real-time preventive breach detection using electrical bioimpedance sensing.
  Methods: We developed a robotic approach combined with a pedicle-drilling tool equipped with a proprioceptive electrical bioimpedance sensor developed by SpineGuard. A real-time detection algorithm was designed to analyze the electrical bioimpedance signal during drilling and identify abrupt changes in conductivity associated with potential breaches towards the spinal canal. The method operates without external devices or sensors.
  Results: The ex vivo experiments showed that the proposed method prevented breaches in 100 of the 51 drilling cases. These findings demonstrate the system's ability to detect potentially hazardous events during drilling and to stop the procedure before. The ex vivo experiments demonstrated that the proposed method prevented breaches in all 51 drilling cases.
  Conclusions: This work demonstrates the feasibility of robotic pedicle drilling with electrical bioimpedance sensing for real-time breach prevention. Using only the tool signal, the method eliminates the need for external sensing systems and supports safer pedicle screw placement.

</details>


### [53] [Cross-entropy optimization with prioritized constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01319)
*Francisco Roldan Sanchez,Pau de las Heras Molins,David Fridovich-Keil,Georgios Bakirtzis*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: TierCEM is a variant of the cross-entropy method that embeds strict lexicographic constraint priorities into elite selection, avoiding numerical importance weights, and is validated on navigation and contact-rich pushing tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: When constraints conflict, optimizers must decide which requirements to keep and which to relax. Priority orderings define precedence, while penalty-based formulations use numerical weights; depending on these weights, solutions can obtain better weighted scores while still violating intended priorities. This motivates a method that respects strict priorities without per-constraint weights.

Method: TierCEM sequentially filters sampled candidates from the highest- to the lowest-priority constraint. If a constraint eliminates all remaining candidates, it reverts to the last nonempty candidate set and selects elites with minimal violation of that blocking constraint, recursively maintaining satisfaction of all higher-priority constraints.

Result: Evaluated on 2D navigation and contact-rich pushing tasks in proprioceptive and learned world-model settings, TierCEM shows that reversing the constraint ordering changes which constraints are violated under conflict. Prioritizing progress toward the task objective also lets TierCEM relax lower-priority constraints when they would otherwise block further progress.

Conclusion: TierCEM integrates strict constraint priorities directly into cross-entropy elite selection without per-constraint importance weights, providing a principled way to resolve conflicting constraints and demonstrating that priority ordering materially affects conflict resolution behavior in the evaluated tasks.

Abstract: When constraints conflict, an optimizer must determine which requirements to preserve and which to relax. On the one hand, a priority ordering specifies which requirements take precedence. On the other hand, penalty-based formulations encode their relative importance through numerical weights. Depending on these weights, a solution can improve its weighted score while violating intended priorities. We introduce TierCEM, a variant of the cross-entropy method that incorporates strict constraint priorities directly into elite selection without requiring per-constraint importance weights. TierCEM works by sequentially filtering sampled candidates, from highest- to lowest-priority constraint. If and when a constraint eliminates all remaining candidates, TierCEM returns to the last nonempty set and selects elites with the smallest violations of that blocking constraint, recursively preserving satisfaction of all higher-priority constraints. We evaluate TierCEM on 2D navigation and contact-rich pushing tasks in proprioceptive and learned world-model settings. Experiments show that reversing the constraint ordering changes which constraints are violated under conflict. Prioritizing progress toward the task objective also enables TierCEM to relax lower-priority constraints when they would otherwise prevent further progress.

</details>


### [54] [Is Success All You Need? Investigating the Impact of Input Perturbations on VLA Behaviour in Tabletop Manipulation Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01351)
*Sophie Higham,Riccardo Andrea Izzo,Matteo Matteucci,Alessandro Suglia*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes a benchmark-agnostic evaluation framework that measures VLA behavioural robustness by characterizing how successful trajectories are executed under perturbation, rather than relying solely on Task Success Rate (TSR).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLA models achieve high TSR on manipulation benchmarks, and robustness to perturbations is increasingly evaluated, yet robustness is still predominantly measured only through TSR. This is insufficient because TSR indicates whether a task is completed, not how it is completed.

Method: Extends LIBERO and LIBERO-Plus benchmarks with a benchmark-agnostic framework assessing successful-trajectory execution. Evaluates three state-of-the-art VLA models across four LIBERO task suites and seven perturbation conditions, tracking changes in typical successful behaviour and its variability via motion smoothness, efficiency, and gripper behaviour metrics.

Result: Perturbations alter the behaviour of successful trajectories in ways not inferable from TSR alone. In several LIBERO suites, models achieve comparable TSR under the same perturbation yet diverge substantially in behaviour on successful trajectories.

Conclusion: Robustness assessment should capture not only whether a task is completed but also how the robot behaves while completing it. TSR should be complemented by behavioural evaluation metrics characterizing the nature and variability of successful task execution.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have achieved high task success rates on robot manipulation task benchmarks. More recently, there has been an emphasis on evaluating the robustness of VLA models to perturbations. However, this robustness is still predominantly measured through Task Success Rate (TSR). In this work, we propose a benchmark-agnostic evaluation framework to measure the behavioural robustness of models by characterising how successful trajectories are executed under perturbation. We implement this methodology by extending the widely-used LIBERO and LIBERO-Plus benchmarks. Across three state-of-the-art VLA models, four LIBERO task suites and seven perturbation conditions, we evaluate changes in both typical successful behaviour and its variability, including metrics of motion smoothness, efficiency and gripper behaviour. We find that perturbations can alter the behaviour of successful trajectories, a phenomenon which cannot necessarily be inferred from TSR alone. Across LIBERO suites, we identify cases where state-of-the-art VLA models achieve comparable TSR under the same perturbation condition, yet behaviour on successful trajectories diverges substantially. Therefore, to have a more robust assessment of task performance, we argue that suitable measures of robustness should capture not only whether a task is completed, but also how the robot behaves while completing it. When evaluating the robustness of VLA models, TSR may be complemented by behavioural evaluation metrics that characterise the nature and variability of successful task execution by robots.

</details>


### [55] [Continue, Abort, or Fall: Viability-Aware Policy Selection (VAPS) for Safe Humanoid Acrobatics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01397)
*Siwei Ju,Lu Liu,Jan Peters,Oleg Arenz*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: VAPS treats safety during dynamic humanoid maneuvers as a policy-conditioned, receding-horizon decision: at each control step, learned predictors assess whether the nominal tracking policy and an abort policy remain viable over a short horizon, and a least-sacrificial hierarchy activates the most task-ambitious viable behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dynamic humanoid maneuvers like flips risk hardware damage when policies are suboptimal or when disturbances and sim-to-real gaps push the robot off its reference. A motion tracking policy has no recovery mechanism once the maneuver deviates, and a backup policy must take over to enable a minimum-damage landing. Choosing which backup to use is as important as deciding when to switch.

Method: VAPS combines a protective fall policy with an abort policy that can terminate the motion at any time and land on its feet. Learned viability predictors estimate, at every control step, whether the nominal tracking policy and the abort policy remain viable over a short receding horizon. A least-sacrificial hierarchy then executes the most task-ambitious behavior that is still predicted viable.

Result: In simulation with randomized disturbances, VAPS sharply reduces head and hand contact, the dominant sources of hardware damage, on both a Unitree G1 and a LimX Oli; the viability predictors and full controller are validated on LimX Oli for side-flip motions. VAPS Pareto-dominates the strongest single-network alternatives, including an end-to-end safe-tracking policy and students distilled from VAPS's oracle-routed decisions, in both task success and head impact.

Conclusion: VAPS provides a policy-conditioned, receding-horizon safety framework that protects humanoid hardware during dynamic maneuvers by switching among viable behaviors. It also serves as a powerful supervision mechanism for undertrained policies.

Abstract: Dynamic humanoid motions such as flips risk hardware damage due to suboptimal policies, disturbances or sim-to-real gaps. A motion tracking policy offers no way out once the maneuver leaves its reference, and a backup policy needs to take over to protect the hardware for a minimum-damage landing. Which backup to use matters as much as when to switch. We present Viability-Aware Policy Selection (VAPS), which treats safety as a policy-conditioned, receding-horizon decision. Besides a protective fall policy, we also train an abort policy which can abort the motion at any time, landing on its feet. At every control step, learned predictors estimate whether the nominal tracking policy and the abort policy remain viable over a short horizon, and a least-sacrificial hierarchy keeps the most task-ambitious behavior that remains viable. In simulation with randomized disturbances, VAPS sharply reduces head contact and hand contact, which are the dominant sources of hardware damage, with both a Unitree G1 and a LimX Oli; on the LimX Oli, we validate the viability predictors and the full VAPS controller for side-flip motions. VAPS Pareto-dominates the strongest single-network alternatives we could train, including an end-to-end safe-tracking policy and students distilled from VAPS's own oracle-routed decisions, in both task success and head impact. We also show that VAPS is a powerful framework to supervise undertrained policies and protect the hardware.

</details>


### [56] [ALFRED: Requirement-driven development of an open-source mobile manipulator for long-term plant monitoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01477)
*Ciarán Miceal Johnson,Christopher Quail,Garry Ellard,Alistair McConnell,Steve Tonneau,Fernando Auat Cheein*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ALFRED is an open-source, commercially sourced mobile manipulator (6-DoF arm, LiDAR, RGB-D, RTK GNSS, IMU on an Ackermann base) developed over four builds; its final build raised usable arm-reach poses from 34.0% to 66.1% and kept the LiDAR's horizontal view clear, completing a year of monthly forest surveys (528 traversals) without a missed collection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tracking seasonal change in crops and forests requires repeated observation of the same plants. Ground robots with manipulators offer close-range sensing and extra viewpoints, but long-term field datasets are rarely released with design files, and the influence of a robot's own structure on arm reach and sensor occlusion is seldom compared across builds.

Method: The authors built ALFRED from commercially available components: a six-degree-of-freedom arm, LiDAR, RGB-D cameras, RTK GNSS and an IMU on an Ackermann-steered base, mounted on a reconfigurable aluminium strut frame and running containerised ROS software. It was developed across four builds against six requirements for repeated outdoor deployment (durability, modularity, repairability, sensing reach, endurance, reproducibility). Model-based analysis of the last three builds quantified usable arm-reachable poses, and ray casting assessed frame-mounted LiDAR view clearance; the system was then deployed in monthly forest surveys.

Result: Across the last three builds, the usable share of arm-reachable poses rose from 34.0% to 60.0% and then 66.1%, and ray casting showed only the final build keeps the frame-mounted LiDAR's horizontal view clear both forwards and backwards. ALFRED completed a year of monthly forest surveys (528 traversals) without missing a scheduled collection, despite battery degradation, reconfiguration for another researcher's study, and parallel development of ALFRED 2.0 for autonomous crop-row operation, with each build switch taking about six hours.

Conclusion: ALFRED demonstrates that an open-source, commercially sourced mobile manipulator can sustain long-term outdoor field campaigns, and that model-based evaluation of arm reach and sensor occlusion across builds yields measurable design gains. The deployment also showed that mechanical modularity is only as dependable as the robot description that tracks it.

Abstract: Tracking seasonal change in crops and forests requires observing the same plants repeatedly. Ground robots can do this at close range, and a manipulator gives their sensors more viewpoints. Yet the robots behind long-term field datasets are rarely released with their design files, and how a robot's own structure limits arm reach and occludes its sensors is seldom compared between builds. We present ALFRED, an open-source mobile manipulator built from commercially available components. It carries a six-degree-of-freedom arm, LiDAR, RGB-D cameras, RTK GNSS and an IMU on an Ackermann-steered base, all mounted on a reconfigurable aluminium strut frame, and runs containerised ROS software. It was developed through four builds against six requirements for repeated outdoor deployment: durability, modularity, repairability, sensing reach, endurance and reproducibility. Model-based analysis of the last three builds shows the usable share of the arm's reachable poses rising from 34.0% to 60.0% and then 66.1%, and ray casting shows that only the final build keeps the frame-mounted LiDAR's horizontal view clear both forwards and backwards. ALFRED completed a year of monthly forest surveys (528 traversals) without missing a scheduled collection. This was despite battery degradation, reconfiguration for another researcher's study, and the parallel development of ALFRED 2.0 for autonomous crop-row operation, with each switch between builds taking about six hours. The deployment also showed that mechanical modularity is only as dependable as the robot description that tracks it.

</details>


### [57] [OpenSpace Lab Solution to the IROS 2026 Indoor Exploration Competition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01505)
*Yuxuan Zhang,Dong Li,Zezhou Sun,Yuxuan Xu,Siyu Teng,Yuchen Li,Jianjian Yang,Long Chen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: OpenSpace Lab's competition solution for IROS 2026 Intelligent Information Gathering: a pre-trained map-completion-guided global planner with remaining-time-based exploration for single-robot systems, and a utility-driven target selection strategy for multi-robot coordination, achieving 1st place in the Single-Robot Public Track and 3rd in both Private Tracks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous exploration under limited operation time requires reconciling map coverage with homing constraints in single-robot settings, while multi-robot exploration must eliminate redundant search and maximize coordination efficiency through shared information.

Method: Single-robot framework uses pre-trained map completion predictions for global planning to prioritize unexplored areas, with a remaining-time-based exploration strategy integrating homing constraints. Multi-robot exploration employs utility-driven target selection balancing observation gains, movement costs, and budget constraints, leveraging shared map and intent data.

Result: Achieved 61.04% coverage in the Single-Robot Public Track, and 39.53% and 39.91% coverage in the Single- and Multi-Robot Private Tracks, respectively. Placed 1st in the Single-Robot Public Track and 3rd in both Private Tracks.

Conclusion: The proposed exploration strategies demonstrate competitive performance in the IROS 2026 competition. An extended full-length paper is in preparation, with source code to be released upon acceptance at the provided repository.

Abstract: This report presents the \textbf{OpenSpace Lab}'s solution to the Competition on Intelligent Information Gathering for Single and Multi-Robot Systems Workshops, organized as part of the IEEE/RSJ International Conference on Intelligent Robots and Systems (IROS) 2026. Our team reached 1st place in the Single-Robot Public Track and 3rd place in both the Single- and Multi-Robot Private Tracks. The single-robot framework utilizes pre-trained map completion predictions for global planning to prioritize unexplored areas. To reconcile map coverage with limited operation time, we introduce a remaining-time-based exploration strategy that integrates homing constraints into the decision-making process. For multi-robot exploration, we utilize a utility-driven target selection strategy that balances observation gains, movement costs, and budget constraints, leveraging shared map and intent data to eliminate redundant search and maximize coordination efficiency. Our solution reached a 61.04\% coverage rate in the Single-Robot Public Track, while reaching 39.53\% and 39.91\% coverage in the Single- and Multi-Robot Private Tracks, respectively. An extended full-length paper based on this report is currently being prepared for submission, and the source code will be released upon acceptance of the full manuscript at https://github.com/OpenSpace-Lab/Indoor-Exploration-IROS2026.

</details>


### [58] [Completion Aware Guidance for World Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01559)
*Seungyeon Kim,Junhoo Lee,Baekseung Kim,Minkyu Kim,Nojun Kwak*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: WAMs fail at task-incomplete imagination due to short-chunk control bias; the training-free Completion Aware Guidance (CAG) sampling method mitigates this, boosting success rates and halving incomplete imaginations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: World Action Models (WAMs) predict visual futures and robot actions but are susceptible to task-incomplete imagination: they produce plausible, action-consistent predictions that omit the transition required for task completion. The paper aims to show this failure is not intrinsic to the world model backbone but arises when adapted for short-chunk control, which repeatedly favors plausible local continuations over task-completing transitions.

Method: The authors propose Completion Aware Guidance (CAG), a training-free sampling method that guides generation toward task completion, requiring no additional training of the underlying WAMs.

Result: Across representative WAMs, CAG improves success from 64% to 70% on a RoboTwin 2.0 subset and from 69% to 75% in zero-shot simulation, while reducing task-incomplete imagination from 79% to 40%.

Conclusion: Task-incomplete imagination stems from short-chunk control adaptation rather than the world model backbone itself, and can be effectively addressed at sampling time via training-free guidance (CAG), yielding consistent gains in task success and substantial reductions in incomplete imagination.

Abstract: World Action Models (WAMs) predict visual futures and robot actions, yet they remain susceptible to task-incomplete imagination, where plausible, action-consistent predictions omit the transition needed for task completion. In this paper, we show that this failure is not inherent to the world model backbone, but emerges when adapted for short-chunk control, which can repeatedly favor plausible local continuations over task-completing transitions. To address this, we introduce Completion Aware Guidance (CAG), a training-free sampling method that guides generation toward task completion. Across representative WAMs, CAG improves success from 64% to 70% on a RoboTwin 2.0 subset and from 69% to 75% in zero-shot simulation, while reducing task-incomplete imagination from 79% to 40%.

</details>


### [59] [LiDARFlow: Real-Time Panel-Based MAV Guidance in Unknown Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01573)
*João Machado,Zeynep Bilgin,Matthieu Verdoucq,Murat Bronz*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A lightweight, onboard-sensing guidance algorithm lets micro aerial vehicles avoid obstacles in unknown, cluttered indoor environments by combining an aerodynamic potential-flow panel method with a nominal guiding vector field.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Micro aerial vehicles need to navigate cluttered, unknown environments using only onboard sensing, which demands computationally lightweight obstacle avoidance suitable for onboard implementation.

Method: The approach adapts a panel formulation derived from aerodynamic potential-flow theory to generate smooth, collision-free guidance vectors from locally perceived obstacles. The obstacle representation is built and updated online from onboard LiDAR measurements, and the resulting obstacle-avoidance field is fused with a nominal guiding vector field to produce the final control input.

Result: Indoor flight tests in waypoint navigation and directional guidance scenarios showed the vehicle completing both tasks while avoiding all obstacles in real time using only onboard perception. The method proved computationally lightweight and onboard-suitable, with pointcloud processing identified as the main practical limitation.

Conclusion: The results support the feasibility of lightweight onboard guidance in unknown environments.

Abstract: This paper presents a guidance algorithm for micro aerial vehicles operating in unknown, cluttered environments using only onboard sensing. The method is based on a panel formulation originally derived from aerodynamic potential-flow theory and generates smooth, collision-free guidance vectors from locally perceived obstacles. The approach is extended to unknown environments by constructing and updating the obstacle representation online from onboard LiDAR measurements. The resulting obstacle-avoidance field is integrated with a nominal guiding vector field to produce the final control input. The system is experimentally validated in indoor flight tests under two scenarios: waypoint navigation and directional guidance. In both cases, the vehicle successfully completes its task while avoiding all obstacles in real time using only onboard perception. The results demonstrate that the method is computationally lightweight and suitable for onboard implementation, with pointcloud processing identified as the main practical limitation. These results support the feasibility of lightweight onboard guidance in unknown environments.

</details>


### [60] [ReCo: Response-Consistent Locomotion with Policy-Aware MPC for Legged Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01612)
*Kuankuan Sima,Yichao Gao,Chenxi Gu,Kefan Zhao,Lin Zhao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ReCo couples response-consistent locomotion with policy-aware MPC: response shaping trains a locomotion policy for repeatable command responses, an identified closed-loop model lets MPC jointly plan locomotion commands and arm motion, improving tracking on a legged manipulation benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Continuous legged manipulation requires accurate end-effector tracking while the base walks. RL+MPC is promising, but MPC can only compensate for base motion it can predict, and a learned policy's command response varies with gait phase, contact, and payload, limiting compensation.

Method: ReCo: (1) response shaping trains the locomotion policy to respond to commands consistently and repeatably across randomized dynamics; (2) an identified closed-loop response model enables MPC to jointly plan locomotion commands and arm motion.

Result: On the simulation benchmark, ReCo reduces position and orientation RMSE by 28.7% and 27.4% relative to the best baseline for each metric. Real-world experiments demonstrate onboard continuous legged manipulation with coordinated base and arm motion.

Conclusion: Response-consistent locomotion combined with policy-aware MPC improves end-effector tracking during continuous legged manipulation and transfers to real-world onboard experiments.

Abstract: Continuous legged manipulation requires accurate end-effector tracking while the base keeps walking. Combining reinforcement learning (RL) with model predictive control (MPC) suits this task: the learned policy provides robust locomotion, while MPC coordinates the base and arm to compensate for tracking errors. However, MPC can compensate only for base motion that it can predict, and a learned policy's command response varies with gait phase, contact, and payload. We present ReCo, a framework that couples response-consistent locomotion with policy-aware MPC for legged manipulation. Response shaping trains the policy to respond to commands consistently and repeatably across randomized dynamics. An identified closed-loop response model then lets MPC jointly plan locomotion commands and arm motion. On the simulation benchmark, ReCo reduces position and orientation root-mean-square error (RMSE) by 28.7% and 27.4% relative to the best baseline for each metric. Real-world experiments demonstrate onboard continuous legged manipulation with coordinated base and arm motion.

</details>


### [61] [SonarVoxNet: Diver Detection in 3D Bounding Box using 3D Sonar](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01644)
*Eugene Park,Jiwon Lee,Seyoung Kan,Trung Dong,Xiaomin Lin,Jane Shin*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper introduces SonarVoxNet, the first 3D sonar detector for full 9-DoF oriented bounding box estimation of divers, and Diver3D, the first public 3D sonar dataset with full 3D orientation labels for divers in non-upright poses. The key finding is that switching from yaw-only to full-SO(3) rotation representation is the dominant factor enabling accurate detection and orientation estimation from 3D sonar.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AUVs assisting human divers need continuous tracking of the diver's 3D position and full-body orientation. However, underwater vision is unreliable, and conventional forward-looking sonar discards elevation information required for orientation estimation. Although newly commercialized 3D sonar preserves elevation, its returns are sparse and noisy, and existing detectors are designed for dense LiDAR data and targets that stay upright and rotate only about the yaw axis (e.g., vehicles, pedestrians). They therefore cannot represent a diver who freely pitches and rolls. There is a gap: no method or dataset adequately supports full-body 3D orientation estimation of divers using 3D sonar.

Method: The authors present SonarVoxNet, which adapts a voxel-based encoder and an anchor-free center-based detection head to 3D sonar data, replacing the conventional yaw-only rotation representation with a continuous 6D rotation parameterization to predict full 9-DoF oriented bounding boxes. They also introduce Diver3D, the first public 3D sonar dataset with full 3D orientation labels for divers in diverse, non-upright poses, collected at a natural cave-diving site. They conduct controlled ablations over the backbone and detection head.

Result: Controlled ablations show that the dominant factor behind accurate 3D sonar-based diver detection is the transition from yaw-only rotation to full-SO(3) rotation. This transition substantially improves detection accuracy and reduces orientation error.

Conclusion: Full-body diver orientation is recoverable from 3D sonar alone. SonarVoxNet is, to the authors' knowledge, the first 3D sonar diver detector to predict full 9-DoF oriented bounding boxes, and Diver3D is the first public 3D sonar dataset with full 3D orientation labels for non-upright divers. These results lay the groundwork for future work on diver pose estimation and diver-robot interaction.

Abstract: Autonomous underwater vehicles (AUVs) assisting human divers must continuously track not only the diver's 3D position but also their full-body orientation. However, vision-based perception is unreliable underwater, and forward-looking sonar -- despite being widely used -- discards the elevation information needed for orientation estimation, posing a fundamental limitation. Recently commercialized 3D sonar preserves elevation but produces sparse, noisy returns, and existing detectors are built for dense LiDAR data and for targets that remain upright and rotate only about the yaw axis (e.g., vehicles, pedestrians), making them unable to represent a freely pitching and rolling diver. To address this gap, we present two contributions. First, SonarVoxNet adapts a voxel-based encoder and an anchor-free center-based detection head to 3D sonar data, replacing the conventional yaw-only rotation representation with a continuous 6D rotation parameterization to predict full 9-DoF oriented bounding boxes -- to our knowledge, the first 3D sonar diver detector to do so. Second, Diver3D is the first public 3D sonar dataset with full 3D orientation labels for divers in diverse, non-upright poses, collected at a natural cave-diving site. Through controlled ablations over the backbone and detection head, we show that the dominant factor behind accurate 3D sonar-based diver detection is the transition from yaw-only rotation to full-SO(3) rotation. This transition substantially improves detection accuracy and reduces orientation error. These results demonstrate that full-body diver orientation is recoverable from 3D sonar alone, laying the groundwork for future work on diver pose estimation and diver-robot interaction.

</details>


### [62] [Learning a Resolution-Consistent Jacobian Field for Bio-Inspired Rigid-Soft Finger](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01668)
*Tianyou Liang,Haisen Zeng,Shanjun Chen,YiMing Zhu,Zhongyue Lu,Zirong Luo*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes Jacobian Flow Matching (JFM), a Conditional Flow Matching-based framework that learns a resolution-consistent Jacobian field for tendon-driven rigid-soft coupled dexterous fingers, improving single-step and long-horizon prediction accuracy over discrete Jacobian learning baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bio-inspired tendon-driven rigid-soft coupled dexterous fingers exhibit strong nonlinearity and configuration-dependent sensitivity, making accurate modeling difficult. Jacobian-based kinematic algorithms in discrete-time control rely on point-wise local linear approximations, making performance sensitive to sensor sampling frequency and controller update frequency.

Method: JFM is a structured learning framework based on Conditional Flow Matching (CFM) that learns a resolution-consistent Jacobian field, modeling actuation-to-motion transitions as a dynamical flow. It supports both single-step prediction and continuous rollout via ODE integration, enabling consistent inference across temporal resolutions.

Result: On a tendon-driven rigid-soft finger, JFM suppresses outlier errors and improves single-step prediction accuracy, reducing global average RMSE by over 53% versus a baseline discrete Jacobian learning approach. For long-horizon prediction under sparse sampling (Stride = 8), ODE-integrated trajectories reduce RMSE median by 14.43% and error variance by 24.87%.

Conclusion: The learned flow-based Jacobian field provides an effective local model for offline multi-step trajectory optimization in rigid-soft coupled nonlinear systems.

Abstract: Bio-inspired tendon-driven rigid-soft coupled dexterous fingers exhibit strong nonlinearity and configuration-dependent sensitivity, making accurate modeling challenging. In discrete-time control, Jacobian-based kinematic algorithms typically rely on point-wise local linear approximations, which makes their performance sensitive to sensor sampling frequency and controller update frequency. To address this issue, we propose Jacobian Flow Matching (JFM), a structured learning framework based on Conditional Flow Matching (CFM), to learn a resolution-consistent Jacobian field that models actuation-to-motion transitions as a dynamical flow. The proposed framework supports both single-step prediction and continuous rollout via ODE integration, enabling consistent inference across temporal resolutions. Experiments on a tendon-driven rigid-soft finger show that the proposed method suppresses outlier errors and improves single-step prediction accuracy, reducing the global average RMSE by over 53% compared with a baseline discrete Jacobian learning approach. For long-horizon prediction, trajectories recovered via ODE integration achieve higher fidelity under sparse sampling (Stride = 8), reducing the RMSE median by 14.43% and the error variance by 24.87%. These results demonstrate that the learned flow-based Jacobian field provides an effective local model for offline multi-step trajectory optimization in rigid-soft coupled nonlinear systems.

</details>


### [63] [Beyond Leaderboard Scores: A Deployment-Focused Protocol for Interpretable Tracking Evaluation in Pedestrian-Centric Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01682)
*Dominik Wojcikiewicz,Diego Paez-Granados*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes a deployment-focused, tracker-only evaluation protocol using shared detections to isolate tracker behavior in pedestrian-centric mobile robot settings, and applies it to six open-source trackers plus PedRefTrack on JRDB.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Aggregate tracking scores provide limited insight into when and how trajectories fail, and varying detector inputs can confound tracker and detector quality, so a protocol is needed that isolates tracker behavior and reveals deployment-relevant capability profiles.

Method: A tracker-only evaluation protocol using shared detections that directly evaluates initialization, detector-gap continuation, identity recovery, close-neighbor association, and load-dependent tracker-step runtime, with HOTA retained as a complementary aggregate measure. Applied to JRDB with six open-source trackers, PedRefTrack, and a GT-assisted variant estimating the remaining tracker-side gap under idealized association and motion.

Result: Under fixed detections, non-GT trackers span only 24.26%-29.67% HOTA yet show markedly different capability profiles. After 1.0 s without detector support, no tracker without GT assistance maintains spatially correct, same-identity output in more than half of eligible cases. Close-neighbor failures are smaller and increase mainly at the shortest separations. Tracker-step runtime on an NVIDIA Jetson Orin is heavy-tailed and load-sensitive, causing several trackers to fall below the 10 Hz real-time target in crowded frames.

Conclusion: Missing-observation continuation is the dominant limitation among the tested properties. The protocol provides a reproducible way to characterize tracker behavior and deployment suitability in pedestrian-centric environments, with code and evaluation scripts released.

Abstract: Mobile robots operating among pedestrians need trajectories that become available quickly, remain spatially credible through missed observations, preserve identity, and fit within an embedded computing budget. Aggregate tracking scores provide limited insight into when and how trajectories fail, while varying detector inputs can confound tracker and detector quality. We present a deployment-focused, tracker-only evaluation protocol that uses shared detections to isolate tracker behavior and directly evaluates initialization, detector-gap continuation, identity recovery, close-neighbor association, and load-dependent tracker-step runtime, while Higher Order Tracking Accuracy (HOTA) is retained as a complementary aggregate measure. We apply the protocol to the JackRabbot Dataset and Benchmark (JRDB) using six open-source trackers and our lightweight Pedestrian Reference Tracker (PedRefTrack), together with a GT-assisted variant that estimates the remaining tracker-side gap under idealized association and motion. Under fixed detections, the non-GT trackers span only 24.26%-29.67% HOTA yet exhibit markedly different capability profiles. After 1.0 s without detector support, no tracker without GT assistance maintains spatially correct, same-identity output in more than half of eligible cases, making missing-observation continuation the dominant limitation among the tested properties. Close-neighbor failures are smaller and increase mainly at the shortest separations. Tracker-step runtime on an NVIDIA Jetson Orin is heavy-tailed and load-sensitive, causing several trackers to fall below the 10 Hz real-time target in crowded frames. The protocol provides a reproducible way to characterize tracker behavior and deployment suitability in pedestrian-centric environments. Code and evaluation scripts are released at https://github.com/SCAI-Lab/tracker_eval.

</details>


### [64] [ActiveWAM: Evidence-Aware Active Vision for World-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01698)
*Renjun Wu,Luzhou Ge,Xuesong Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper introduces ActiveWAM, a unified world-action model for active vision manipulation that jointly learns observation and control, formulated as an evidence-aware retain-acquire problem, and demonstrates substantial gains on a new benchmark and real-world tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Active vision manipulation requires a policy to control both its camera and its end-effectors. However, camera motion determines which evidence remains visible within finite observation windows: acquiring a new view can displace task-critical cues, while retaining a view forgoes potentially useful observations. This tension motivates an evidence-aware formulation of when to retain versus reacquire views.

Method: The authors formulate the setting as an evidence-aware retain-acquire problem and propose ActiveWAM, a unified world-action model that learns observation and manipulation jointly. They introduce training-time inversion, which constrains a frozen video prior by task-bearing source evidence and visible temporal changes, removing the need for test-time inversion or candidate ranking. At deployment, the policy generates bimanual and pan/tilt actions, including stay and reacquisition behaviors, from view-aware history, and updates context from newly measured RGB observations. Future-video prediction is used as a co-training signal, while action generation requires neither future-video decoding nor optimal viewpoint annotations. They also introduce RoboTwin-AV, a 50-task benchmark with executable pan/tilt control and automatically generated demonstrations.

Result: ActiveWAM improves TAVIS out-of-distribution success by up to 17.0 percentage points over the strongest baselines, achieves 20.0 additional points over Fast-WAM on RoboTwin-AV, and outperforms Fast-WAM by 26.7 points on real-world physical kitchen tasks.

Conclusion: ActiveWAM effectively unifies observation and manipulation under an evidence-aware retain-acquire formulation, with training-time inversion enabling efficient deployment without test-time inversion or viewpoint annotations. Its consistent gains across a new 50-task benchmark and real-world kitchen tasks indicate the value of jointly modeling camera and end-effector control for active vision manipulation.

Abstract: Active vision manipulation requires a policy to control both its camera and its end-effectors, yet camera motion determines which evidence remains visible within finite observation windows. Acquiring a new view can displace task-critical cues, while retaining a view forgoes potentially useful observations. We formulate this as an evidence-aware retain--acquire problem and present ActiveWAM, a unified world--action model that learns observation and manipulation jointly. To this end, we propose training-time inversion which constrains a frozen video prior by task-bearing source evidence and visible temporal changes, eliminating the need for test-time inversion or candidate ranking. At deployment, the policy generates bimanual and pan/tilt actions-including stay and reacquisition behaviors-from view-aware history, and updates context from newly measured RGB observations. Future-video prediction serves as a co-training signal, while action generation requires neither future-video decoding nor optimal viewpoint annotations. We introduce RoboTwin-AV, a 50-task benchmark with executable pan/tilt control and automatically generated demonstrations. ActiveWAM improves TAVIS out-of-distribution success by up to 17.0 percentage points over the strongest baselines, achieves 20.0 additional points over Fast-WAM on RoboTwin-AV, and outperforms it by 26.7 points on real-world physical kitchen tasks.

</details>


### [65] [Query-Conditioned Articulation Estimation from a Single Image](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01726)
*Abdelrhman Werby,Fabio Scaparro,Kai O. Arra*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: QueryArt estimates 3D articulation parameters of unseen articulated objects from a single RGB image, a 2D query point, and camera intrinsics, predicting geometry relative to the query point in depth units; a single depth reading then recovers metric scale.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robots need kinematic parameters of articulated objects for interaction and manipulation, but must infer them from what they currently observe—often a single RGB image of a novel object. Existing single-image methods entangle part segmentation with articulation estimation, making predictions fragile to missed detections and incorrect part associations, and they regress metric 3D geometry that a single view only determines up to scale.

Method: QueryArt takes a single RGB image, a 2D query point, and camera intrinsics. Instead of coupling segmentation with estimation, it predicts 3D articulation geometry relative to the queried point and expressed in units of that point's depth, keeping the target identifiable from the image alone. A single depth measurement at the query point supplies the scale to recover metric parameters. The model is trained on a curated mixture of synthetic and real-world articulation datasets.

Result: QueryArt outperforms recent baselines on most articulation metrics across several benchmarks, including on out-of-distribution data. On a mobile manipulator, it achieved a 70.2% success rate across 57 manipulation trials covering 16 object parts and five viewpoint classes.

Conclusion: Decoupling articulation estimation from part segmentation and predicting scale-relative geometry with a query point yields a robust, metric-capable, single-image articulation estimator that works on unseen objects and transfers to real-world mobile manipulation.

Abstract: Enabling robots to estimate the kinematic parameters of articulated objects unlocks a wide range of capabilities for interaction and manipulation. The estimation has to happen from the information the robot currently observes, often just a single RGB image of an object it has never seen before. Current single-image approaches couple articulation part segmentation with articulation estimation, making their predictions vulnerable to missed detections and incorrect part associations, and they regress metric 3D geometry that a single view fixes only up to scale. We present QueryArt, a model that estimates articulation parameters from a single RGB image, a 2D query point, and camera intrinsics. QueryArt is trained to estimate the 3D articulation geometry relative to the queried point and in units of its depth, which keeps its target identifiable from the image alone. A single depth measurement at the query point then supplies the scale and recovers the metric parameters. We train QueryArt on a curated mixture of synthetic and real-world articulation datasets. We evaluate QueryArt on several benchmarks and compare it against recent baselines. QueryArt outperforms recent baselines on most articulation metrics, including on out-of-distribution data. To demonstrate the model's capabilities in real-world settings, we evaluate QueryArt on a mobile manipulator across 57 manipulation trials spanning 16 object parts and five viewpoint classes, achieving a 70.2% success rate. We provide code and videos at: https://abwerby.github.io/queryart/

</details>


### [66] [World Motion Models: Flexible Sequence Modeling of SE(3) Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01742)
*Jiahui Lei,Qianqian Wang,Trevor Darrell,Angjoo Kanazawa*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes World Motion Models (WMMs), a generative framework that represents dynamic 3D scenes as sparse SE(3) pose trajectories and casts their joint distribution as a sequence modeling problem using flow-matching with per-token noise levels, unifying many vision and robotics tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spatial intelligence in artificial agents demands a comprehensive generative prior over the dynamic 3D world. Existing approaches lack a unified representation that captures the "what was, is, and will be where across time" across heterogeneous dynamic entities.

Method: WMMs approximate dynamic scene elements as a set of rigid SE(3) trajectories, a minimal yet expressive 4D primitive. This representation unifies articulated objects, human bodies, hand-object interactions, piecewise-rigid scene dynamics, camera motion, and robot states/actions into a shared space. The joint distribution of these entities is modeled via flow-matching with per-token noise levels, combined with a context token mechanism for non-sequential conditioning, enabling any-to-any marginal conditioning over arbitrary numbers of entities and time steps.

Result: Tasks including future prediction, motion infilling, model-predictive control, inverse kinematics, cross-embodiment retargeting, and policy learning all reduce to applying different masks over the same network. Experiments across 6 diverse applications in 3D vision and robotics demonstrate strong performance and versatility.

Conclusion: WMMs offer a flexible and versatile generative prior over the dynamic 3D world, unifying diverse 3D vision and robotics tasks within a single representation and network through mask-based conditioning.

Abstract: Equipping artificial agents with spatial intelligence requires a comprehensive generative prior over the dynamic 3D world. We propose World Motion Models (WMMs) that capture "what was, is, and will be where across time" via sparse SE(3) pose trajectories. WMMs are built on the observation that elements of dynamic scenes can be well approximated by a set of rigid SE(3) trajectories, a minimal yet expressive primitive for 4D modeling. This representation unifies articulated objects, human bodies, hand-object interactions, piecewise-rigid scene dynamics, camera motion, and even robot states and actions into a single shared space. Given this representation, we cast the joint distribution of these entities as a flexible sequence modeling problem, utilizing flow-matching with per-token noise levels. Coupled with a context token mechanism for non-sequential conditioning, this formulation supports any-to-any marginal conditioning across an arbitrary number of entities and time steps. Tasks such as future prediction, motion infilling, model-predictive control, inverse kinematics, cross-embodiment retargeting, and policy learning all reduce to the application of different masks over the same network. Experiments on 6 diverse applications of 3D vision and robotics demonstrate the versatility and flexibility of WMMs with strong performance.

</details>


### [67] [End-to-End Learning vs. Modular Architectures: Comparative Insights into Autonomous Driving Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01746)
*Kartik B. Kapse*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper comparatively analyzes End-to-End Learning versus Modular Architectures for autonomous driving, covering their strengths, limitations, and trade-offs, and proposes a Four-Dimensional Architecture Selection Framework (twelve binary criteria across safety, operating environment, data/computational resources, and deployment context) validated on ten published systems with 7/10 correct recommendations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous driving systems rely on two dominant design paradigms—End-to-End Learning and Modular Architectures—but practitioners lack a structured basis for choosing between them. The paper aims to clarify their relative strengths, limitations, and trade-offs across factors such as interpretability, scalability, robustness, and real-world applicability, and to provide guidance for matching paradigms to specific applications.

Method: A comprehensive comparative literature synthesis is conducted, contrasting E2E Learning's direct mapping of raw sensory inputs to actuators against Modular Architectures' pipeline of perception, cognition, planning, and control subsystems. Building on this analysis, a Four-Dimensional Architecture Selection Framework is proposed, comprising twelve binary criteria spanning safety, operating environment, data/computational resources, and deployment context, and is validated against ten published autonomous driving systems.

Result: E2E Learning offers simplicity and adaptability in dynamic environments but lacks transparency and depends heavily on large datasets. Modular Architectures provide superior interpretability and task-specific optimization but face integration complexity and scalability challenges. Hybrid approaches that combine both paradigms emerge as a promising direction. The proposed framework correctly recommends 7 out of 10 deployed architectures in validation.

Conclusion: The paper synthesizes existing literature to highlight key trade-offs between E2E Learning and Modular Architectures and identifies hybrid architectures as a promising direction for future research, while offering a practically validated framework to guide architecture selection for autonomous driving applications.

Abstract: Autonomous driving systems have become a central focus of intelligent transportation research, with End-to-End Learning and Modular Architectures offering two prominent design paradigms for their implementation. E2E Learning uses deep learning algorithms to map raw sensory inputs directly to driving actuators, providing a streamlined and adaptable solution. while Modular Architectures employ a pipeline-based approach, dividing the system into distinct subsystems for perception, cognition, planning, and control. This paper presents a comprehensive comparative analysis of these paradigms, focusing on their strengths, limitations, and trade-offs to provide insights into their suitability for various autonomous driving applications. The study evaluates key factors such as interpretability, scalability, robustness, and real-world applicability. While End-to-End Learning emphasizes simplicity and adaptability in dynamic environments, it lacks transparency and is highly dependent on large datasets. Conversely, Modular Architectures offer superior interpretability and task-specific optimization, but face challenges related to integration complexity and scalability. To address these limitations, hybrid approaches that combine the strengths of both paradigms have emerged, offering a promising direction for overcoming these challenges. Beyond this comparative synthesis, following work proposes a Four-Dimensional Architecture Selection Framework, comprising twelve binary criteria across safety, operating environment, data/computational resources, and deployment context, and validate it against ten published autonomous driving systems, correctly recommending 7/10 deployed architectures. This work synthesizes existing literature to highlight key trade-offs between the paradigms and identifies hybrid architectures as a promising direction for future research.

</details>


### [68] [FlashDexRetarget: Accelerating Dexterous Manipulation Data Generation through Multi-Motion Retargeting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01849)
*Kyungmin Lee,Sibeen Kim,Dongyoon Hwang,Yoonsang Oh,Donghu Kim,Youngdo Lee,I Made Aswin Nahrendra,Jaegul Choo,Hojoon Lee*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: FlashDexRetarget is an RL-based framework that retargets human hand-object demonstrations to dexterous robot hands with high success and training efficiency, achieving 90% success on a 50-motion benchmark (~2.5x sampling-based baselines) while using up to 100x less compute than RL baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Human hand-object demonstrations are a reusable data source for dexterous robot manipulation, but cross-embodiment transfer requires physically feasible retargeting. Existing physics-based retargeting methods are limited either in success rate, training efficiency, or both, motivating a method that achieves both high success and low compute cost.

Method: The framework combines object point-cloud observations, hand-object distance features, and future trajectory encodings with complementary rewards supervising object motion and reference hand-object relationships to ease learning. It uses separate left- and right-hand actor-critic networks and adapts the off-policy algorithm FlashSAC to dexterous motion tracking to accelerate learning.

Result: On a benchmark of 50 motions covering single- and two-object interactions, FlashDexRetarget reaches 90% success, ~2.5x the sampling-based baselines, with up to 100x less training compute than RL baselines. Consistent gains are shown on XHand and Sharpa Wave Hand. Scaling experiments with 200, 500, and 1,000 motions show stable performance and greater efficiency as the training set grows. Qualitative replay using real-world captured demonstrations illustrates applicability to recorded human manipulation.

Conclusion: FlashDexRetarget provides a high-success, compute-efficient RL solution for dexterous motion retargeting that scales to large motion sets and generalizes across robot hands, with ablations validating its design choices and real-world replays demonstrating practical applicability.

Abstract: Human hand-object demonstrations offer a reusable source of dexterous robot manipulation data, but transferring them across embodiments requires physically feasible retargeting. Existing physics-based approaches face limitations in retargeting success, motion-specific training efficiency, or both. To address these limitations, we introduce FlashDexRetarget, an RL-based framework for high-success, efficient dexterous motion retargeting. To make the demonstrated interaction easier to learn, we combine object point-cloud observations, hand-object distance features, and future trajectory encodings with complementary rewards that supervise object motion and reference hand-object relationships. To further accelerate learning, we employ separate left- and right-hand actor critic networks and adapt the off-policy algorithm, FlashSAC to dexterous motion tracking. On a benchmark of 50 motions spanning single-object and two-object interactions, FlashDexRetarget achieves a 90% success rate, approximately 2.5x that of the evaluated sampling-based baselines, while requiring up to 100x less training compute than the evaluated RL-based baselines. Evaluations on both XHand and Sharpa Wave Hand show consistent gains, and component-wise ablations examine the contributions of our design choices. Beyond the 50-motion benchmark, experiments with 200, 500, and 1,000 motions demonstrate that our method remains stable at larger scales and produces successful retargeted motions more efficiently as the training set grows. Qualitative replay results using real-world-captured demonstrations further illustrate the applicability of our framework to recorded human manipulation. Videos and code are available at https://davian-robotics.github.io/FlashDexRetarget/

</details>


### [69] [ChunkVLA-AM: Parallel Action Chunking for Vision-Language-Action Robot Control in Additive Manufacturing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01856)
*Zhugang Liu,Kaichuang Zhang,Jinman Zhang,Pu Sun,Martha Asare,Jose Hernandez,Maxim Ermolinsky,Efren Saenz,Qi Lu,Jinghao Yang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper deploys OpenVLA-OFT on a FAIRINO FR3 robot in an additive manufacturing workcell using a cloud-edge architecture and a TFDS/RLDS conversion pipeline, achieving 92.9% success (39/42) on object-transfer trials, with all failures tied to release-height control and a preferred luminance range of 85-125.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-language-action (VLA) models integrate perception, language, and action, but deploying them in additive manufacturing is hindered by costly adaptation to unseen robot embodiments and performance degradation under environmental changes. This work addresses these deployment barriers for a specific AM workcell.

Method: A framework deploys OpenVLA-OFT on a FAIRINO FR3 in a fixed AM workcell. A data pipeline converts monocular real-world demonstrations into OpenVLA-compatible TFDS/RLDS datasets for FR3 adaptation. At runtime, each inference predicts an eight-step chunk of 7-D actions; the FR3 executes each chunk open loop and then captures a new observation, yielding inter-chunk closed-loop feedback. A cloud-edge architecture streams observations from the FR3 client to a remote inference server over a FastAPI interface.

Result: In 42 physical A-to-B object-transfer trials evenly split between red and blue targets, 39 succeeded (92.9%). All three failures occurred during final placement due to insufficient release-height control, causing the object to topple. An illumination sweep found a low-error luminance range of 85-125 (0-255 scale), with the lowest mean spatial error at 95.

Conclusion: OpenVLA-OFT can be practically deployed on an FR3 AM workcell with high task success using chunked action execution and cloud-edge inference; robustness is limited mainly by release-height control and lighting conditions, indicating targeted improvements for placement and illumination.

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models unify visual perception, language understanding, and action generation, offering new opportunities for automation in additive manufacturing (AM). However, deployment in AM remains challenging because adapting these models to unseen robot embodiments is costly, and performance can degrade under environment changes. In this work, we present a framework for deploying OpenVLA-OFT on a FAIRINO FR3 robot in a fixed AM workcell. A data pipeline converts monocular real-world demonstrations into OpenVLA-compatible TFDS/RLDS datasets to support adaptation to the FR3 embodiment. At runtime, each inference request predicts an eight-step chunk of 7-D actions. The FR3 executes each chunk open loop before capturing a new observation, providing closed-loop feedback between chunks. The system uses a cloud-edge architecture in which the FR3 client streams observations to a remote inference server through a FastAPI interface. In 42 physical A-to-B object-transfer trials, evenly split between red and blue targets, the system succeeded in 39 (92.9%).
