All three failures occurred during final placement, when insufficient release-height control caused the object to topple. An illumination sweep identified a low-error luminance range of 85-125 on a 0-255 scale, with the lowest mean spatial error at 95.

</details>


### [70] [Towards Physical Underwater Robotic Assistance for Scuba Diver Movement in Confined Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01906)
*Demetrious T. Kutzke,Junaed Sattar*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper presents RADMCS, a wearable robotic system that uses monocular depth estimation and thruster-generated haptic force feedback to help scuba divers maintain a fixed distance from subsea structures, validated with eight IRB-approved human diver studies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scuba divers are trained to control depth to avoid rapid ascents/descents that can cause gas embolisms and barotrauma, but many underwater tasks require lateral control—maintaining distance from subsea structures such as coral reefs, submerged drilling instrumentation, or unexploded ordnance. Existing wearable robotics for divers focus on propulsive assistance rather than directional guidance, leaving a gap for a device that supports distance-keeping in confined underwater spaces.

Method: The authors introduce RADMCS (Robotic Assisted Diver Movement in Confined Spaces), a first-of-its-kind wearable robot providing thruster-actuated directional guidance. It combines monocular depth estimation for perception with submersible thrusters that deliver force-feedback haptic cues to the diver. The compact form factor serves as a foundational platform extensible to more sophisticated control and navigation behaviors. Evaluation involved IRB-approved in-water studies with eight human scuba divers: threshold sensitivity tests in both a closed-water swimming facility and ocean environments, distance-maintaining experiments in a closed-water facility, and form/fit/function testing in the ocean.

Result: Threshold sensitivity and distance-maintaining experiments with eight human scuba divers showed that relatively low thrust values (10 percent of maximum) are sufficient to robotically direct a human's movement through the physical sensation of the robot's guidance, both in closed-water and ocean environments.

Conclusion: A small, compact wearable robotic platform can haptically guide diver movement using low-magnitude thruster force feedback, establishing a foundational basis for future expansion toward more sophisticated underwater control and navigation capabilities.

Abstract: Scuba divers are taught to control their depth to avoid rapid ascents and descents, which could result in serious injuries such as gas embolisms and barotrauma. However, many underwater tasks necessitate lateral control, maintaining distance between subsea structures such as coral reefs, submerged drilling instrumentation, or unexploded ordnance. In this work, we discuss a first-of-its-kind wearable robotic solution providing thruster-actuated directional guidance to a diver, as distinct from prior propulsive-assistance exoskeletons. We introduce ``Robotic Assisted Diver Movement in Confined Spaces'' (RADMCS), a wearable robot that assists divers in maintaining a fixed distance from subsea structures by leveraging perception techniques in monocular depth estimation and force-feedback from submersible thrusters to provide haptic feedback. Its small and compact form factor creates a foundational platform that could be expanded to include more sophisticated control and navigation behaviors. We present results from Institutional Review Board (IRB) in-water studies with eight human scuba diver participants on threshold sensitivity tests in both a closed-water swimming facility and ocean environments; distance-maintaining experiments in a closed-water facility; and form, fit, and function testing in the ocean. We demonstrate that relatively low thrust values (10 percent of maximum) allow robotic direction of a human's movement using the physical sensation of the robot's guidance.

</details>


### [71] [Robot Learning on Discrete Surfaces: Theory and Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01910)
*Matteo Dalle Vedove,Fares J. Abu-Dakka,Luigi Palopoli,Daniele Fontanelli,Matteo Saveriano*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes a unified discrete Riemannian framework for robot learning directly on polyhedral surface meshes, defining discrete logarithmic/exponential maps, parallel transport, and projections across faces, edges, and vertices, and instantiates it in DMPs, GP regression, and Riemannian Flow Matching with simulation and real-robot validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most robot learning and motion generation frameworks treat surfaces merely as constraints, ignoring their intrinsic geometry, and the discrete geometric structure of polyhedral meshes (standard CAD/3D reconstruction output) remains unexploited.

Method: A unified discrete Riemannian framework built on discrete differential geometry, providing well-defined log/exp maps, parallel transport, and ambient-space projections across faces, edges, and vertices. It is instantiated in three paradigms: (i) DMPs with improved exponential-map computation and fixed-tangent-cone forcing-term encoding plus parallel transport; (ii) GP with a geodesic-based kernel and practical admissibility control; (iii) Riemannian Flow Matching using mesh-native operators.

Result: DMPs yield better cross-surface generalisation and stability than prior mesh-based approaches; the geodesic-based GP kernel enables regression at arbitrary mesh locations without smoothness assumptions; mesh-native RFM improves generative quality over spectral baselines while reducing training time. Validated in simulation against state-of-the-art methods and on two real-robot scenarios: generalising user-drawn trajectories across surfaces and planning polishing motions on RGB-D-reconstructed surfaces.

Conclusion: A unified discrete Riemannian framework enables effective robot learning directly on polyhedral meshes, supporting motion generation, regression, and generative modeling with demonstrated simulation and real-robot performance.

Abstract: All the objects composing our world are enclosed within surfaces. Yet, most robot learning and motion generation frameworks treat surfaces as constraints ignoring their intrinsic geometry. This gap is acute for polyhedral meshes--the standard output of CAD and 3D reconstruction--whose discrete geometric structure remains unexploited. In this paper, we propose a unified discrete Riemannian framework that enables robot learning directly on polyhedral surface meshes. Using discrete differential geometry, we define logarithmic and exponential maps, parallel transport, and ambient-space projections that remain well-defined across faces, edges, and vertices. We instantiate the framework in three learning paradigms: (i) Dynamic Movement Primitives (DMPs), an improved exponential-map computation and a fixed-tangent-cone forcing-term encoding with parallel transport yield better cross-surface generalisation and stability over prior mesh-based approaches. (ii) Gaussian Process (GP), a geodesic-based kernel with practical admissibility control, enables regression at arbitrary mesh locations without smoothness assumptions. (iii) Riemannian Flow Matching (RFM), mesh-native operators improve generative quality over spectral baselines while reducing training time. The framework is validated in simulation against state-of-the-art methods and demonstrated on two real-robot scenarios: generalising user-drawn trajectories across different surfaces and planning polishing motions on RGB-D-reconstructed surfaces.

</details>


### [72] [TouchTherm: Building Multimodal Digital Twins of Objects for Tactile and Thermal Rendering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01943)
*Yitao Zhang,Hong Ying,Haoran Guo,Xiaoying Zhou,Guanyu Chen,Chenxi Xiao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: TouchTherm constructs simulation-ready visuo-tactile-thermal object assets from real objects, combining structured-light scanning with photometric-stereo normal maps for micro-height tactile fields and physics-regularized multiview IR thermal reconstruction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robotic simulation and VR need object assets capturing not only visual geometry but also physical cues for tactile and thermal interaction. Existing 3D datasets and reconstruction methods mainly represent object-scale geometry and appearance, overlooking microscale surface structure for haptic rendering and transient temperature dynamics for temperature-aware interaction.

Method: For visual and tactile reconstruction, structured-light scanning is combined with multiview normal maps from photometric stereo; the normal maps are registered to scanned geometry and transformed into tangent space to recover local micro-height fields for optical tactile rendering, while the coarse mesh handles collision detection. For thermal reconstruction, synchronized multiview infrared videos of natural cooling after controlled heating are captured to reconstruct a physics-regularized dynamic thermal field.

Result: Experiments on 20 objects show the reconstructed micro-height fields preserve dominant surface structures and recover higher-frequency details beyond the coarse geometry, while thermal fields achieve held-out surface-temperature MAEs of 0.465 degrees C and 0.592 degrees C at 30 s and 45 s. Tactile assets support synthetic-to-real tactile object recognition, and a glove-based VR system demonstrates spatially and temporally varying thermal feedback.

Conclusion: TouchTherm shows potential for multimodal sensory simulation and temperature-aware virtual interaction.

Abstract: Robotic simulation and virtual reality increasingly require object assets that capture not only visual geometry but also the physical cues underlying tactile and thermal interaction. Existing 3D datasets and reconstruction methods primarily represent object-scale geometry and visual appearance, overlooking microscale surface structure for high-fidelity haptic rendering and transient temperature dynamics for temperature-aware interaction. We present TouchTherm, a framework for constructing simulation-ready visuo-tactile-thermal object assets from real-world objects. For visual and tactile reconstruction, we combine structured-light scanning with multiview normal maps obtained from photometric stereo. The normal maps are registered to the scanned geometry and transformed into tangent space to recover local micro-height fields for optical tactile rendering, while the coarse mesh handles collision detection. For thermal reconstruction, we capture synchronized multiview infrared videos of natural cooling following controlled heating and reconstruct a physics-regularized dynamic thermal field. Experiments on 20 objects show that the reconstructed micro-height fields preserve dominant surface structures and recover higher-frequency details beyond the coarse geometry, while the thermal fields achieve held-out surface-temperature MAEs of 0.465 degrees C and 0.592 degrees C at 30 s and 45 s, respectively. The resulting tactile assets support synthetic-to-real object recognition from tactile observations, while a glove-based VR system demonstrates spatially and temporally varying thermal feedback. These results highlight the potential of TouchTherm for multimodal sensory simulation and temperature-aware virtual interaction.

</details>


### [73] [Training-Free Diffusion Planning with Analytical Local Scores](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01959)
*Michael Y. Fatemi,Jinhao Liang,Ferdinando Fioretto*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes a training-free diffusion-based motion planner that replaces learned global trajectory scores with analytical local scores built from obstacle, smoothness, velocity, and inter-agent feasibility terms, enabling data-free, efficient, and scalable trajectory generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion-based planners can model multi-modal trajectory distributions and refine entire trajectories, but they require training on large collections of feasible trajectories, making them map-specific and hard to deploy when high-quality demonstrations are unavailable.

Method: The method leverages the observation that a trajectory's score can be reconstructed from local interactions between neighboring waypoints and nearby constraints. It replaces learned global trajectory scores with analytical local scores derived from obstacle, smoothness, velocity, and inter-agent feasibility terms, yielding a decomposed denoising procedure that retains classical trajectory optimization structure while inheriting the iterative refinement behavior of diffusion models.

Result: Experiments across many complex environments and large multi-agent planning tasks show the analytical score produces smooth, feasible trajectories at limited computational cost, e.g., feasible paths for 300+ agents in environments with 100+ obstacles in under 6 seconds on a GPU, outperforming strong learning-based and optimization baselines while avoiding the data requirements of learned diffusion planners.

Conclusion: A training-free diffusion-based planner using analytical local scores can generate smooth, collision-free, goal-directed trajectories for path finding and multi-robot motion planning, matching or surpassing learned and optimization baselines without requiring demonstration data.

Abstract: Path finding and multi-robot motion planning require trajectories that are smooth, goal-directed, and collision-free in environments with complex geometric constraints. Recent diffusion-based planners have shown that trajectory generation can be cast as iterative denoising which has opened the doors to learning-based approaches that can handle multi-modal trajectory distributions and refine entire trajectories. However, a key limitation is that diffusion planners require training on large collections of feasible trajectories, rendering them map-specific, and difficult to deploy when high-quality demonstrations are unavailable. This paper introduces a training-free diffusion-based motion planner that replaces learned global trajectory scores with analytical local scores derived from obstacle, smoothness, velocity, and inter-agent feasibility terms. The proposed idea relies on a key observation: the score of a trajectory can be reconstructed by considering only local interactions between neighboring waypoints and nearby constraints. This structure exploitation yields a decomposed denoising procedure that retains the optimization structure of classical trajectory methods while inheriting the iterative refinement behavior of diffusion models. Experiments on a large collection of complex environments and large multi-agent planning tasks show that the proposed analytical score produces smooth and feasible trajectories within limited computational costs, for example in generating feasible paths for 300+ agents in environments containing 100+ obstacles in under 6 seconds on a GPU, outperforming strong learning-based and optimization baselines, while avoiding the data requirements of learned diffusion planners.

</details>


### [74] [BLT*: Informed Belief Localization Trees for Uncertainty-Aware Planning on Digital Twins](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01972)
*Elliot Preston-Krebs,Abhishek Goudar,Timothy D. Barfoot*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Informed BLT* adapts RRT* and Informed RRT* to sampling-based belief space planning under the 2-Wasserstein metric, assuming isotropic Gaussian beliefs, to scale point-cloud-based planning to large outdoor digital twins.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sampling-based belief space planning must scale to large outdoor digital twins with point-cloud observations, where repeated observation propagation and probabilistic collision checking are computationally prohibitive.

Method: The authors adapt RRT* and Informed RRT* to belief space using the 2-Wasserstein (W2) metric. Assuming isotropic Gaussian beliefs, sampled belief states are connected efficiently while accounting for available information and probabilistic collision constraints. This enables steering and rewiring without repeatedly propagating observations and allows reuse of previously computed measurement information. A framework generates semantically labelled digital twins for planning in real-world environments with point-cloud-based localization.

Result: Experiments in simulated environments and digital twins show faster initial solution discovery in most maps with competitive cost convergence.

Conclusion: Informed BLT* provides a scalable sampling-based BSP approach for large outdoor digital twins with point-cloud observations, improving initial solution discovery while maintaining competitive asymptotic cost performance.

Abstract: We present Informed Belief Localization Trees* (Informed BLT*), a sampling-based belief space planning (BSP) algorithm that scales to large outdoor digital twins with point-cloud observations. We adapt RRT* and Informed RRT* to belief space using the $2$-Wasserstein ($W_2$) metric. Assuming isotropic Gaussian beliefs, sampled belief states can be connected efficiently while accounting for available information and probabilistic collision constraints. This enables steering and rewiring without repeatedly propagating observations, and allows previously computed measurement information to be reused. We present a framework to generate semantically labelled digital twins for planning in real-world environments with point-cloud-based localization. Experiments in simulated environments and digital twins show faster initial solution discovery in most maps with competitive cost convergence.

</details>


### [75] [H-SPAR: Hydrodynamic-aware Simulation for Particle Transport and Autonomous Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01985)
*Navid Zarrabi,Nariman Yousefi,Sajad Saeedi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: H-SPAR is a marine robotics simulator that couples shared, precomputed flow fields to both Lagrangian particle transport and current-induced USV forces within ROS 2/Gazebo, enabling joint evaluation of mission cost and sampling performance. Experiments show that SVF-RRT*'s 69.4% planning-level upstream cost advantage over RRT* shrinks to 41.7% under execution with time-varying currents, and that sweep orientation alters particle-sampling rates by up to 22.2%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Environmental robotic sampling is shaped by water currents affecting both vehicle motion and particle transport, yet existing marine robotics simulators typically model flow, autonomy, and sampling targets in isolation. This separation prevents joint assessment of mission cost alongside sampling performance under consistent hydrodynamics.

Method: H-SPAR integrates spatially and temporally varying velocity fields, Lagrangian particle transport, probabilistic sampling, and ROS 2/Gazebo-based USV autonomy. Shared precomputed flow fields drive both particle advection and current-induced forces during closed-loop vehicle execution. The framework is evaluated through path-planning experiments comparing the current-aware planner SVF-RRT* against conventional RRT*, and through coverage experiments varying sweep orientation.

Result: SVF-RRT* achieves 69.4% lower upstream cost than conventional RRT* at the planning level, but this advantage declines to 41.7% during execution under time-varying currents, attributable to temporal flow variation, vehicle motion constraints, and path deviation omitted during planning. In coverage experiments, sweep orientation changes the particle-sampling rate by up to 22.2% under the full H-SPAR configuration.

Conclusion: The gap between planning-level and execution-level performance, together with the sensitivity of sampling rates to sweep orientation, underscores the necessity of evaluating planning, vehicle execution, particle transport, and sampling jointly under consistent hydrodynamic conditions. H-SPAR provides an open-source platform supporting such integrated evaluation.

Abstract: Environmental robotic sampling requires considering the dual influence of water currents on robotic motion and particle transport. Existing marine robotics simulators generally model flow, autonomy, and sampling targets separately, limiting joint evaluation of mission cost and sampling performance. H-SPAR integrates spatially and temporally varying velocity fields, Lagrangian particle transport, probabilistic sampling, and ROS 2/Gazebo-based uncrewed surface vehicle (USV) autonomy. In this work, shared precomputed flow fields drive particle advection and current-induced forces during closed-loop vehicle execution. Path-planning experiments show that the existing current-aware planner SVF-RRT* achieves 69.4% lower upstream cost than conventional RRT* at the planning level, but this reduction falls to 41.7% during execution under time-varying currents, reflecting temporal flow variation, vehicle motion constraints, and path deviation omitted during planning. Coverage experiments show that sweep orientation changes the particle-sampling rate by up to 22.2% under the complete H-SPAR configuration. These findings highlight the importance of evaluating planning, vehicle execution, particle transport, and sampling together under consistent hydrodynamic conditions. The project webpage is available at https://sites.google.com/view/h-spar, and the open-source code is available on GitHub at https://github.com/naviiidz/h-spar-sim.

</details>


### [76] [UniWAM: Unified World-Action Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02054)
*Jiayi Chen,Wenxuan Song,Jingbo Wang,Shuai Zhou,Xicheng Gong,Zehua Fan,Ziyang Zhou,Junwu E,Haodong Yan,Fuhao Li,Qize Yu,Xu Huang,Pengwei Wang,Wen Chen,Shunbo Zhou,Haoang Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: UniWAM is a unified model combining a physical reasoner, world generator, and action predictor, trained on cleaned human-egocentric and robot data to achieve SOTA embodied performance and reveal a log-linear human-robot co-training scaling law.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-language-action models gain semantic reasoning from pretrained VLMs but suffer from action-only supervision with weak grounding in world dynamics; world-action models inherit spatiotemporal priors from video generation but lack semantic understanding and reasoning under distribution shifts. The paper aims to unify both strengths.

Method: A unified architecture with a physical reasoner, a world generator, and an action predictor. Includes a rigorous data cleaning/annotation pipeline for human egocentric and robot data; low-level actions represented in natural language with a pre-training recipe assigning complementary supervision from VQA, human egocentric data, and robot demos. Post-training uses future visual noise augmentation and history-conditioned flow matching that initializes action generation from encoded action history, reducing denoising steps.

Result: SOTA performance across multiple evaluations: in-distribution performance, robustness, generalization, instruction following, and long-horizon task execution. Reduced denoising steps while maintaining performance. A log-linear scaling law for unified human-robot co-training was identified.

Conclusion: Unifying semantic understanding, visual generation, and action prediction in one architecture is effective, and large-scale pre-training on mixed human and robot data yields demonstrated scaling benefits.

Abstract: Vision-language-action models benefit from the understanding and reasoning capabilities of pretrained vision-language models, but action-only supervision provides limited grounding in world dynamics. Conversely, world-action models inherit spatiotemporal priors from video generation models, yet remain limited in semantic understanding and reasoning under distribution shifts. We introduce UniWAM, a unified architecture that integrates a physical reasoner, a world generator, and an action predictor to jointly learn semantic understanding of the physical world, visual generation, and action prediction. To ensure the quality of the training data, we developed a rigorous data cleaning and annotation pipeline for both human egocentric data and robot data. To adapt the vision-language component to embodied tasks while preserving its inherited language capabilities, we represent low-level actions in natural language and introduce a pre-training recipe that assigns complementary supervision from visual question answering (VQA) data, human egocentric data, and robot demonstrations to the appropriate model components. During post-training, future visual noise augmentation reduces reliance on precise future predictions, while history-conditioned flow matching uses encoded action history to initialize action generation. Together, these designs significantly reduce denoising steps while maintaining performance. UniWAM achieves state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance across multiple evaluations, including in-distribution performance, robustness, generalization, instruction following, and long-horizon task execution. Furthermore, we uncover a log-linear scaling law of unified human-robot co-training, demonstrating the effectiveness of large-scale pre-training on a mixture of human and robot data.

</details>


### [77] [HumanoidToolBench: Benchmarking Humanoid Tool Use from Selection to Mobile Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02089)
*Kyochul Jang,Seohyeon Park,Ohchul Kwon,Sangjun Park,Junhyeok Choi,Seungyeop Yi,Chaeyun Kim,Sangkyu Lee,Idan Szpektor,Avi Caciularu,Jongmin Park,Youngjae Yu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: HumanoidToolBench is an 18-task benchmark with ToolBook (3.1k demonstrations) for evaluating humanoid tool-use, jointly testing tool selection, manipulation, and locomotion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Humanoids need tools to exceed their inherent physical limits, requiring both suitable tool selection and coordination of manipulation with locomotion. Existing benchmarks do not jointly evaluate these capabilities on a humanoid.

Method: The authors introduce HumanoidToolBench, an 18-task benchmark spanning three scenarios, three execution levels, and two tool-set modes, alongside ToolBook, a dataset of 3.1k demonstrations collected in simulation and on a real Unitree G1. They evaluate seven policies in simulation and three on the real robot, including focused GR00T N1.7 probes.

Result: Evaluation reveals substantial gaps between selecting a suitable tool and completing the task. GR00T N1.7 probes show reduced selection accuracy on unseen tools and continued task execution under unrelated instructions.

Conclusion: HumanoidToolBench and ToolBook expose key weaknesses in current humanoid tool-use policies, highlighting tool selection and instruction robustness as open challenges. Code and data are publicly available.

Abstract: As robotic hardware and learning methods advance, humanoids need tools to perform tasks beyond their inherent physical limits. Successful tool use requires selecting a suitable tool and coordinating manipulation and, when needed, locomotion to complete the task. Existing benchmarks do not jointly evaluate these capabilities on a humanoid. We introduce HumanoidToolBench, an 18-task benchmark spanning three scenarios, three execution levels, and two tool-set modes, together with ToolBook, a dataset of 3.1k demonstrations collected in simulation and on a real Unitree G1. Evaluation of seven policies in simulation and three on the real robot reveals substantial gaps between selecting a suitable tool and completing the task. Focused GR00T N1.7 probes show reduced selection accuracy on unseen tools and continued task execution under unrelated instructions. Code and data are available at https://snu-pi.github.io/HumanoidToolBench/.

</details>


### [78] [GlassGuard: Verified Glass Plane Mapping for Robot Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02110)
*Hanwen Guo,Zhengzhi Lin,Yusen Xie,Ji Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: GlassGuard reconstructs planar architectural glass from visual and LiDAR evidence for navigation, jointly optimizing glass coverage and free-space contamination, achieving 85% coverage (panoramic) / 82% (pinhole) with 5-17x fewer false voxels than baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transparent and specular surfaces challenge LiDAR-based SLAM and navigation since laser returns can pass through glass, leaving collision boundaries absent from the map. Prior reconstruction attempts may place obstacles inaccurately, causing the opposite failure: contamination of traversable free space. This dual requirement—preserving coverage while avoiding free-space contamination—motivates a navigation-oriented reconstruction approach.

Method: GlassGuard reconstructs planar architectural glass from complementary visual and LiDAR evidence, formulating success via both glass coverage and free-space contamination, applied across proposal verification and global map construction. A foundation vision model supplies glass-instance masks, structural 3D cues generate metric plane hypotheses, and depth-free 2D projective geometry checks their orientations before consolidation into a global map.

Result: Evaluated in nine building-scale scenes with diverse glass structures, spatial scales, and lighting, over one hour and 2.1 km of real-world robot traversal. GlassGuard reaches 85% total glass coverage (panoramic). Under identical pinhole inputs, it achieves 82% coverage versus at most 61% for baselines, with 5-17x fewer false voxels per frame. Qualitative examples with a navigation planner show reconstructed planes blocking paths through glass while leaving traversable routes open.

Conclusion: GlassGuard effectively reconstructs glass surfaces for navigation by balancing coverage and free-space contamination, outperforming baselines in coverage and false-voxel rate, and enabling navigation planners to block glass crossings while preserving traversable routes.

Abstract: Transparent and specular surfaces pose a serious challenge to LiDAR-based SLAM and navigation because laser returns may pass through glass, leaving collision boundaries absent from the map. Prior work attempts to reconstruct the missing surfaces, but inaccurate obstacle placement can create the opposite failure: contamination of traversable free space. Recognizing this dual requirement, we present GlassGuard, a navigation-oriented framework for reconstructing planar architectural glass from complementary visual and LiDAR evidence. We formulate success in terms of both glass coverage and free-space contamination and apply this principle throughout proposal verification and global map construction. A foundation vision model provides glass-instance masks, structural 3D cues generate metric plane hypotheses, and depth-free 2D projective geometry checks their orientations before they enter a consolidated global map. We evaluate GlassGuard in nine building-scale scenes spanning diverse glass structures, spatial scales, and lighting conditions, with more than one hour and 2.1 km of real-world robot traversal. GlassGuard achieves 85% of total glass coverage for its panoramic version. Under identical pinhole inputs, GlassGuard achieves 82% total coverage, compared with at most 61% for the evaluated baselines, while producing 5-17x fewer false voxels per frame. Qualitative examples with a navigation planner illustrate the reconstructed planes blocking paths through glass while leaving traversable routes open. The project page is available at https://glassguardproject.github.io/.

</details>


### [79] [SkeleWAM: Skeleton World-Action Modeling for Efficient Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02120)
*Juyi Sheng,Hua Wang,Mengyuan Liu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SkeleWAM is a compact world action model that represents manipulation scenes as sparse 3D skeletons (robot joints, object centers, interaction points), using future skeleton prediction as geometric supervision, achieving 85.9% success on LIBERO-Plus with 57.1M parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing world action models predict videos or learned visual latents, which encode interaction geometry only implicitly and retain appearance information irrelevant to control, limiting robustness and parameter efficiency.

Method: SkeleWAM represents a manipulation scene as a sparse 3D skeleton built online from RGB-D observations and robot proprioception. This skeleton serves as a unified geometric state for action generation and future skeleton prediction. Future skeleton prediction provides geometric supervision without visual reconstruction. At inference, actions are generated directly from the current skeleton and a language instruction, with Medoid Action Consensus (MAC) as an auxiliary consensus strategy for stochastic action samples.

Result: On LIBERO-Plus, SkeleWAM achieves 85.9% overall success rate with 57.1M parameters, surpassing Cosmos-Policy by 3.7 percentage points.

Conclusion: Sparse 3D robot-object structure provides an effective state space for robust and parameter-efficient world action learning.

Abstract: World action models (WAMs) combine robot action generation with future state prediction. Existing WAMs typically predict videos or learned visual latents, which represent interaction geometry only implicitly and may retain appearance information unrelated to control. We introduce SkeleWAM, a compact WAM that represents a manipulation scene as a sparse 3D skeleton composed of robot joints, object centers, and interaction points. Constructed online from current RGB-D observations and robot proprioception, the skeleton provides a unified geometric state for action generation and future skeleton prediction. Future skeleton prediction provides additional geometric supervision for action learning without requiring visual reconstruction. At inference, SkeleWAM generates actions directly from the current skeleton and language instruction, while Medoid Action Consensus (MAC) serves as an auxiliary consensus strategy for stochastic action samples. On LIBERO-Plus, SkeleWAM achieves an overall success rate of 85.9% with 57.1M parameters, outperforming Cosmos-Policy by 3.7 percentage points. These results demonstrate that sparse 3D robot--object structure provides an effective state space for robust and parameter-efficient world action learning. The project is available at https://skelewam-project.github.io/.

</details>


### [80] [DuoMind: Enabling Distributed Multi-Robot Coordination with Semantic Communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02161)
*Hanchu Zhou,Dechen Gao,Hang Wang,Brendan Lynch,Boqi Zhao,Qiyao Ma,Raman Goyal,Junshan Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DuoMind is a distributed hierarchical framework enabling multi-robot coordination via semantic communication, pairing VLM-based orchestrators with VLA-based action models, and RoboPoly is introduced as a benchmark for long-horizon coordinated manipulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLMs and VLAs have advanced general-purpose robots mainly in single-robot settings; extending them to multi-robot systems is difficult because robots must coordinate long-horizon behaviors while preserving reliable, fine-grained execution. Benchmark scarcity for multi-robot coordination further hinders progress.

Method: A distributed hierarchical framework. Each robot runs a VLA-based action model for low-level execution and a VLM-based orchestrator for high-level reasoning and inter-agent coordination. At each planning step, the orchestrator reasons over the task instruction, local observations, and received messages, then emits low-level instructions for its action model plus semantic messages for peers. This combines VLM semantic reasoning with VLA precise action generation. RoboPoly, a benchmark of long-horizon manipulation tasks requiring coordinated closed-loop execution under distributed control, is also developed.

Result: Experiments on RoboPoly and RoboTwin show DuoMind improves multi-robot task performance; ablations confirm contributions of hierarchical orchestration and semantic communication.

Conclusion: DuoMind effectively enables multi-robot coordination by exploiting complementary VLM/VLA strengths, and RoboPoly provides a needed testbed; hierarchical orchestration and semantic communication are validated as key design components.

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) and vision-language-action models (VLAs) have recently driven rapid progress in general-purpose robots, yet most progress has focused on single-robot settings. Extending these capabilities to multi-robot systems remains challenging because robots must coordinate long-horizon behaviors while maintaining reliable, fine-grained execution. We introduce DuoMind, a distributed hierarchical framework for multi-robot coordination through semantic communication. Each robot uses a VLA-based action model for low-level execution and a VLM-based orchestrator for high-level reasoning and inter-agent coordination. At each planning step, the orchestrator at each robot reasons over the task instruction, local observations, and messages received from other robots. It then generates low-level instructions for the action model and semantic messages for peer robots. This architecture exploits the complementary strengths of pretrained models by combining the semantic reasoning capabilities of VLMs with the precise action-generation capabilities of VLAs. To address the scarcity of benchmarks for multi-robot coordination, we further develop RoboPoly, a benchmark comprising long-horizon manipulation tasks that require coordinated, closed-loop execution under distributed control. Experiments on RoboPoly and RoboTwin demonstrate that DuoMind improves multi-robot task performance, while ablation studies confirm the contributions of hierarchical orchestration and semantic communication. More details are available on our project page.

</details>


### [81] [Watch, Infer, Coordinate: Inferring Robot Partner Constraints for Zero-Shot Coordination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02170)
*Suyu Ye,Zheyuan Zhang,Vaishnav Tadiparthi,Hossein Nourkhiz Mahjoub,Ehsan Moradi Pari,Tianmin Shu,Homanga Bharadhwaj,Nakul Agarwal*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper introduces WIC (Watch, Infer, Coordinate), a benchmark and inference method enabling a helper robot to infer a partner's hidden physical constraints from observing joint behavior, then use these inferences for zero-shot coordination on new physically coupled manipulation tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robots increasingly coordinate on manipulation tasks requiring collaboration, but hardware degradation or actuator faults impose physical constraints a robot may not know about its partner. A helper needs to infer these hidden limits to coordinate effectively on new tasks, which is hard because demonstrations only show what the constrained robot did—not what it could have done—and the partner may compensate for limitations.

Method: The key insight is that constraints shape the joint behavior of both robots, so both robots' actions are informative about the constrained partner's capability. The authors propose WIC, a benchmark spanning three physically coupled manipulation settings, plus an inference approach that scores candidate constraints using observed joint behavior.

Result: Across all three settings, the method substantially improves constraint inference and zero-shot coordination, approaching the performance of an oracle with access to the true constraints.

Conclusion: Observing joint team behavior allows effective inference of a partner's hidden physical constraints, enabling zero-shot coordination on novel tasks and approaching oracle-level performance.

Abstract: Robots operating in the physical world will increasingly need to coordinate with other robots, particularly in manipulation tasks where an object may be too large or heavy for a single robot to carry alone. Physical limitations caused by hardware degradation or actuator faults can restrict the actions a robot can reliably execute, yet these limitations may be unknown to its partner. We study whether a helper can infer a robot partner's physical constraints from observing it coordinate with another robot, then use the inferred capability to coordinate with the same partner on a new task. This is difficult because a demonstration shows what the constrained robot did, but not what it could have done. In physically coupled tasks, the other robot may also compensate for its limitations, making those limitations difficult to identify from the constrained robot's behavior alone. Our key insight is that these constraints shape the joint behavior of the team, making the actions of both robots informative about the constrained partner's capability. We introduce Watch, Infer, Coordinate, a benchmark spanning three physically coupled manipulation settings, together with an inference approach that scores candidate constraints using observed joint behavior. Across all three settings, our method substantially improves constraint inference and zero-shot coordination, approaching an oracle with access to the true constraints.

</details>


### [82] [InterEvolve: Test-Time Evolution of Reward Programs for Humanoid Loco-Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02196)
*Zhuo Lin,Sirui Xu,Liuyu Bian,Yu-Xiong Wang,Liang-Yan Gui*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: InterEvolve enables humanoid loco-manipulation at test time by repurposing a frozen controller's existing skills via an LLM-driven reward-program evolution loop grounded in an object-aware forward-backward behavioral foundation model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Controllers cannot be retrained for every new task, yet a broad pre-trained controller already holds much of the competence a novel task requires. The challenge is exposing this latent competence: an interface between planning and control must be expressive enough for contact-rich, multi-stage interactions while remaining executable and measurable so execution feedback can refine planning. The paper targets solving untrained tasks by repurposing existing skills, self-improvement from attempts, and retention of what is learned—all without retraining.

Method: InterEvolve builds the planning-control interface from two parts. (1) An object-aware forward-backward (FB) behavioral foundation model: object residuals layered on a frozen body prior convert a newly specified reward—about the body or objects—into loco-manipulation behavior at test time. (2) Task specification as reward programs: staged rewards with completion conditions and tunable constants. An LLM agent revises the program structure in context using execution feedback and a library of verified skill programs, while a numerical optimizer tunes the constants. Each candidate program is verified across parallel simulation scenarios, so the loop iteratively induces, repurposes, and composes the controller's motor competence. Learned skills are retained in the library for reuse.

Result: Human-designed rewards leave much of the FB model's loco-manipulation competence untapped, while InterEvolve's evolved programs release it—sometimes via novel strategies. The system produces behaviors for diverse tasks, complex scenes, and long-horizon compositions in simulation. Evolved skills run autonomously on a physical Unitree G1 using egocentric onboard perception.

Conclusion: A broad frozen controller plus an expressive, measurable reward-program interface—evolved by an LLM and a numerical optimizer with simulation-verified feedback—can unlock substantial untrained loco-manipulation capability at test time, transferring from simulation to real hardware without retraining.

Abstract: We study test-time evolution for humanoid loco-manipulation: solving tasks that a controller was never trained for by repurposing its existing skills, improving from its own attempts, and retaining what it learns, without retraining. Our key insight is that a broad controller already holds much of the competence a new task needs, and that this competence becomes accessible through an interface between planning and control that is expressive enough to specify contact-rich, multi-stage interactions, yet executable and measurable enough that execution feedback can guide planning from experience. InterEvolve realizes this interface with two components. First, we develop an object-aware forward-backward (FB) behavioral foundation model, whose object residuals on a frozen body prior turn a new reward about the body or objects into loco-manipulation behavior at test time. Second, we specify tasks as reward programs: staged rewards with completion conditions and tunable constants. A large language model (LLM) agent revises the program structure in context, drawing on execution feedback and a skill library of verified programs, while a numerical optimizer tunes its constants. With every candidate verified across parallel simulation scenarios, the program explores new ways to induce, repurpose, and compose the controller's existing motor competence for the task at hand, and thus improves over iterations. Experiments show that human-designed rewards leave much of the FB model's loco-manipulation competence untapped, whereas the programs InterEvolve evolves release it, sometimes through novel strategies. It further produces behaviors for diverse tasks, complex scenes, and long-horizon compositions in simulation, and evolved skills run autonomously on a physical Unitree G1 from egocentric onboard perception.

</details>


### [83] [Reconstruct, Practice, Go Real: Guided Self-Improvement for Embodied Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02204)
*Yen-Jen Wang,Haozhe Jiang,Shuying Deng,Haoru Xue,Weirui Ye,Rocky Duan,Nika Haghtalab,S. Shankar Sastry,Pieter Abbeel,Haozhi Qi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: RPG is a framework that lets robot execution systems self-improve without updating model weights: it mines manipulation skills from an offline dataset, builds practice tasks in simulation, diagnoses failures via execution feedback and privileged simulator state, then iteratively refines symbolic skills and the system prompt through cross-task evaluation before deploying a frozen multimodal-LLM system to real hardware.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Building reliable robot capabilities across diverse tasks demands substantial human effort for skill development, reward design, and perception-control integration. The paper aims to achieve autonomous improvement of robot execution systems while avoiding the instability and cost of continual weight updates.

Method: RPG (Reconstruct, Practice, Go Real) identifies manipulation capabilities from an offline dataset and constructs related practice tasks in simulation. During practice it uses execution feedback, privileged simulator state, and available dataset videos to diagnose failures, then develops new reusable symbolic skills, refines existing skills, and revises the system prompt. Cross-task evaluation tests individual candidate changes and merged revisions before retention. At test time a multimodal LLM uses the resulting system prompt and skill library to coordinate perception and control.

Result: On held-out initializations of 22 manipulation tasks, RPG raises success from 28.6% after the first practice round to 95.0% after 15 rounds, surpassing ASPIRE (75.5%) and CaP-Agent0 powered by GPT-6 Astra Pro (60.0%). After a common calibration and hardware-adaptation procedure, the frozen system succeeds in all 30 physical trials, ten on each of three tasks.

Conclusion: Without updating model weights, RPG autonomously builds and refines a symbolic skill library and system prompt through simulation practice, yielding large in-simulation gains over baselines and robust zero-shot transfer to real robot hardware.

Abstract: Building reliable robot capabilities across diverse tasks requires substantial human effort to develop and maintain skills, design rewards, and integrate perception with control. We present Reconstruct, Practice, Go Real (RPG), a framework for autonomous improvement of robot execution systems without updating model weights. RPG identifies manipulation capabilities in an offline dataset and constructs related practice tasks in simulation. During practice, RPG uses execution feedback, privileged simulator state, and available dataset videos to diagnose failures. It develops new reusable symbolic skills, refines existing skills, and revises the system prompt based on these diagnoses. Cross-task evaluation tests individual candidate changes and merged revisions before they are retained for reuse. At test time, a multimodal LLM uses the resulting system prompt and skill library to coordinate perception and robot control. On held-out initializations of 22 manipulation tasks, RPG improves task success from 28.6% after the first practice round to 95.0% after 15 rounds, outperforming all evaluated baselines, including ASPIRE (75.5%) and CaP-Agent0 powered by GPT-6 Astra Pro (60.0%). After a common calibration and hardware-adaptation procedure, the frozen system succeeds in all 30 physical trials, with ten trials on each of three tasks. Project Website: https://rpg-robot.github.io/

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [84] [Heavy-Tailed Memory Traces in Long-Horizon Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00010)
*Xinyuan Song,Zekun Cai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a "tail audit" of agent memory, showing retrieval traces are heavy-tailed and policy-dependent, and proposes CTWM, a rank-based controller with one exponent τ that keeps a summarized tail. CTWM cuts prompt tokens (5.9% on Synthetic Graph World, 24.48% on LongMemEval) and lowers bottom-half tail prediction error by 13.6% vs. a graph-memory baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Memory systems for long-horizon language agents are typically evaluated only by task success or token cost, overlooking the shape of memory use. Under finite context and repeated retrieval, memory can concentrate on a small core while rare states fall into a long tail where prediction errors accumulate, a phenomenon that is unmeasured and unaddressed by existing controllers.

Method: A conservative tail audit characterizes retrieval artifact distributions, finding concentration is reproducible but policy-dependent: random-walk agents yield log-normal-compatible artifacts, while semantic LLM policies yield the strongest truncated-power-law-compatible core–tail traces. Motivated by this, CTWM is a rank-based memory controller that allocates prompt budget via a single exponent τ while retaining a summarized tail. It is evaluated on Synthetic Graph World, ALFWorld, and LongMemEval against a graph-memory baseline in paired comparisons.

Result: On Synthetic Graph World, CTWM preserves full state and transition coverage, reduces prompt tokens by 5.9%, and lowers bottom-half tail prediction error by 13.6% relative to the graph-memory baseline. The same paired comparison shows consistent token savings on ALFWorld and a 24.48% token reduction on LongMemEval with aggregate accuracy parity.

Conclusion: Heavy-tailed memory traces are not merely a diagnostic of finite retrieval but also a practical control signal for token-efficient agent world models. Rank-based budget allocation with a summarized tail offers a principled way to control memory shape under finite context.

Abstract: Long-horizon language agents increasingly rely on external memory as a frozen world model, yet current memory systems are usually judged only by task success or token cost. We argue that the missing object is the shape of memory use: under finite context and repeated retrieval, agent memory can concentrate on a small core while leaving rare states in a long tail where prediction errors accumulate. We study this effect through a conservative tail audit and find that concentration is reproducible but policy-dependent. Random-walk agents produce log-normal-compatible retrieval artifacts, whereas semantic LLM policies yield the strongest truncated-power-law-compatible core--tail traces. Motivated by this audit, we propose Core--Tail World Model (CTWM), a rank-based memory controller that allocates prompt budget with a single exponent $τ$ while retaining a summarized tail. On Synthetic Graph World, CTWM preserves full state and transition coverage, reduces prompt tokens by 5.9%, and lowers bottom-half tail prediction error by 13.6% relative to a graph-memory baseline. The same paired comparison gives consistent token savings on ALFWorld and a 24.48% token reduction on LongMemEval with aggregate accuracy parity. These results suggest that heavy-tailed memory traces are not only a diagnostic of finite retrieval, but also a practical control signal for token-efficient agent world models.

</details>


### [85] [When Do Causal World Models Help Modular LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00012)
*Xinyuan Song,Zekun Cai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces FedCausalCompose, a causal world-model framework for modular LLM agents that learns cross-module interfaces from intervention-response evidence, showing causal structure beats observational models only when interfaces are statistically identifiable and decision-relevant.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents operate through modular services where actions in one module alter valid transitions in another. Standard world models fit observational traces, but this is insufficient for intervention-time planning: a trace showing payment before shipment cannot distinguish whether payment authorizes shipment, inventory mediates the effect, or a hidden trigger causes both. The paper targets this gap between observational fitting and the interventional quantity needed for planning.

Method: The authors propose FedCausalCompose, a causal world-model framework in which local actions supply intervention-response evidence for cross-module interfaces. They first establish theoretical results-analytically characterizing irreducible interventional error under unblocked back-door paths, the improvement of interface recovery with intervention-response coverage, and the condition under which oracle causal composition beats the non-causal lower bound. They then test the resulting prediction in diagnostic agent settings.

Result: Observational world models incur an irreducible interventional error under unblocked back-door paths. Interface recovery improves with intervention-response coverage, and oracle causal composition can outperform the non-causal lower bound when coverage and local mechanism errors are controlled. Empirically, causal interfaces help most in structured tool environments where API signatures expose preconditions and downstream effects, whereas dialogue and narrative environments often ignore raw edge lists unless a short attention anchor renders the causal information decision-relevant.

Conclusion: Causal world models benefit LLM agents under a concrete condition: causal structure helps when cross-module interfaces are both statistically identifiable and presented in a form the agent can use at action time. The framework identifies this joint requirement as the key determinant of when causal composition pays off.

Abstract: LLM agents increasingly act through modular systems, such as order, payment, inventory, and shipment services, where actions in one module change which transitions are valid in another. Standard world models usually fit observational traces, but this is not the quantity needed for intervention-time planning: a trace may show that payment precedes shipment without identifying whether payment authorizes shipment, inventory mediates the effect, or a hidden trigger explains both. We study this gap through FedCausalCompose, a causal world-model framework for modular LLM agents in which local actions provide intervention-response evidence for cross-module interfaces. We first show that observational world models incur an irreducible interventional error under unblocked back-door paths, that interface recovery improves with intervention-response coverage, and that an oracle causal composition can beat the non-causal lower bound when coverage and local mechanism errors are controlled. We then test the resulting prediction in diagnostic agent settings. Causal interfaces help most in structured tool environments, where API signatures expose preconditions and downstream effects. In contrast, dialogue and narrative environments often ignore raw edge lists unless a short attention anchor makes the causal information decision-relevant. These results identify a concrete condition for causal world models in LLM agents: causal structure helps when cross-module interfaces are both statistically identifiable and presented in a form the agent can use at action time.

</details>


### [86] [From Proposal to Verified Effect: Praxa, an Evidence-Bound Harness for Governed AI Agent Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00015)
*Stefan G. Creadore*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Praxa is an agent harness that makes the distinct claims of proposal, authority, dispatch, verified external effect, and serving promotion explicit and testable via deterministic admission, brokered execution, external read-back, reconciliation, and reviewed promotion. Four evidence lanes are reported; none establishes superiority, production safety, or user benefit—only an evidence-bound architecture is supported.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents can propose and execute actions, but proposal, authority, dispatch, verified external effect, and serving promotion are conflated as if they were a single claim. The paper aims to separate these states explicitly so that authority-to-effect transitions become deterministic, auditable, and testable rather than implicitly assumed.

Method: Praxa represents agent states through five mechanisms: deterministic admission, brokered execution, external read-back, reconciliation, and reviewed promotion. Evidence is gathered across four lanes: (1) an author-run repository-local audit at a pinned revision; (2) a provider-backed Terminal-Bench Core 0.1.1 pilot over 12 curated tasks comparing baseline and reliability-layer arms; (3) a post-debug two-order coordination-proxy development comparison of baseline vs. a source-authored candidate; and (4) deployed source/configuration inspection of bounded reflection, recall accounting, memory compilation, and tool-health paths.

Result: Lane 1: 1,027/1,027 unit tests and 89/89 Workerd tests passed, all 363 expected source files instrumented, four coverage floors met—but raw per-test transcripts and independent reproduction are unavailable. Lane 2: baseline and reliability-layer arms each passed 17/36 strict trials; the reliability layer consumed 37.49% more input and 50.73% more output tokens, so superiority is not supported. Lane 3: baseline and candidate each completed 180/180 trials with equal accuracy, full hermetic crash recovery, and zero protected violations; the candidate used 37.11% fewer tokens, 33.84% lower estimated endpoint cost, and 11.63% fewer steps—quality, latency, and production behavior improvements remain unestablished. Lane 4: bounded reflection, recall accounting, memory compilation, and tool-health paths exist, but no production outcome lift was observed.

Conclusion: Praxa's supported contribution is an evidence-bound architecture that makes authority-to-effect transitions explicit and testable. The current evidence does not establish adversarial security, production safety, general specialist superiority, autonomous recursive optimization, or user benefit.

Abstract: Large-language-model agents can propose and execute actions, but proposal, authority, dispatch, verified external effect, and serving promotion are different claims. We present Praxa, an agent harness that represents these states explicitly through deterministic admission, brokered execution, external read-back, reconciliation, and reviewed promotion. We report four evidence lanes. First, an author-run repository-local audit at a pinned revision passed 1,027/1,027 unit tests and 89/89 Workerd tests, instrumented all 363 expected source files, and met four coverage floors; raw per-test transcripts and independent reproduction are unavailable. Second, in a provider-backed Terminal-Bench Core 0.1.1 pilot across 12 curated tasks, baseline and reliability-layer arms each passed 17/36 strict trials. The reliability layer used 37.49% more input and 50.73% more output tokens, so the pilot does not support superiority. Third, in a post-debug, two-order coordination-proxy development comparison, baseline and a source-authored candidate each completed 180/180 trials with equal measured accuracy, full hermetic crash recovery, and zero protected violations. The candidate used 37.11% fewer tokens, 33.84% lower estimated endpoint cost, and 11.63% fewer steps; this does not establish improved quality, latency, or production behavior. Fourth, deployed source/configuration evidence shows bounded reflection, recall accounting, memory compilation, and tool-health paths, but no production outcome lift. Praxa's supported contribution is an evidence-bound architecture that makes authority-to-effect transitions explicit and testable. Current evidence does not establish adversarial security, production safety, general specialist superiority, autonomous recursive optimization, or user benefit.

</details>


### [87] [What Do Rationales Communicate? A Message-Intervention Study in Role-Specialized QA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00018)
*Jiameng Zhang,Hongqiu Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Rationales passed from a reasoner to a verifier mainly function as a verification-message channel rather than a source of answer accuracy: faithful rationales barely improve accuracy, while corrupted rationales strongly and often silently shift support judgments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Role-specialized QA pipelines increasingly pass rationales across a reasoner-to-verifier boundary, yet it is unclear whether this message improves answers, strengthens support assessment, or creates a new failure surface. The paper argues the rationale channel should be evaluated on its own terms.

Method: A message-intervention diagnostic that fixes the evidence and candidate answer while varying only the rationale crossing the reasoner-to-verifier boundary. Evaluated on 400 examples from MuSiQue, HotpotQA, and 2WikiMultiHopQA using DeepSeek as both generator and verifier, with blind and explicit rationale-checking verifier prompts plus human audits and cross-model/task-boundary checks.

Result: Faithful rationales add almost no accuracy over providing no rationale, but corrupted rationales sharply alter support judgments. Under a blind verifier prompt, harmless paraphrases shift support by only 0–2.5%, whereas corrupted rationales shift support by 10–22%; an explicit rationale-checking prompt amplifies this to 34–55%. Final answers move less (2–30%), and only 2.9–35.3% of corrupted support flips coincide with answer changes. Human audits: 16/42 valid corruptions are corruption-overtrust cases, and blind humans reject or flag as unclear 9/10 corrupted rationales that the model accepts.

Conclusion: Rationale sharing should be evaluated as a verification-message mechanism rather than merely as a route to higher answer accuracy, since it primarily reshapes support judgment and introduces an overtrust failure surface.

Abstract: Role-specialized QA pipelines increasingly pass rationales from a reasoner to a verifier, but it is unclear what this message actually buys: better answers, stronger support assessment, or a new failure surface. We introduce a message-intervention diagnostic that fixes the evidence and candidate answer while varying only the rationale passed across the reasoner-to-verifier boundary. On 400 MuSiQue, HotpotQA, and 2WikiMultiHopQA examples with DeepSeek as generator and verifier, faithful rationales add almost no answer accuracy over no rationale, while corrupted rationales strongly alter support judgments. Under a blind verifier prompt, harmless paraphrases shift support by only 0--2.5%, whereas corrupted rationales shift support by 10--22%; an explicit rationale-checking prompt amplifies the same pattern to 34--55%. Final answers move less (2--30%), and only 2.9--35.3% of corrupted support flips co-occur with answer changes. Human audits show why this matters: 16/42 valid corruptions are corruption-overtrust cases, and blind humans reject or mark unclear 9/10 audited corrupted rationales that the model accepts. Cross-model and task-boundary checks show when the channel is active, amplified, inert, or folded into the task label. Rationale sharing should be evaluated as a verification-message mechanism, not merely as a route to higher answer accuracy.

</details>


### [88] [Measuring the Microtask Eligibility Gap: When Is an Off-the-Shelf SLM Enough for an Agent Harness?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00025)
*Jundong Hu,Shekar Ramachandran*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Off-the-shelf small language models fail to meet practitioner-defined eligibility thresholds on all 16 microtask configurations (and 12/12 for Llama-3.x), mainly tracking model size rather than quantization precision; the authors recommend deploying SLMs only where a certified non-LLM baseline fails.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agent harnesses increasingly delegate microtasks around a frontier LLM planner (auto-approving shell commands, writing memory, selecting tools, ranking past turns) to small language models, but it is unclear whether off-the-shelf SLMs actually meet practitioner-defined thresholds, and why they fail.

Method: A benchmark of 4 microtasks with fixed prompts and automatic metrics, each with a pre-specified threshold τ anchored to a cheap non-LLM baseline and a CI-aware eligibility rule. Qwen3 0.6/1.7/4/8B evaluated in FP16 greedy with one frozen prompt (no tuning); a logprob decision-threshold diagnostic (with a context-length/cascade probe for T2) classifies failures into capability deficits vs. decoding-threshold-addressable failures across 4 regimes; 4-bit quantization (RTN/GPTQ/AWQ) tested; robustness checks via a τ-sweep, neutral prompt paraphrases, and Llama-3.x replication.

Result: 0 of 16 Qwen3 configurations pass eligibility (verified via raw outputs and parser behavior); 12/12 Llama-3.x configurations ineligible; 0/112 eligible across original plus 3 neutral paraphrases per cell. Quantization damage depends on model size and moves no configuration into eligibility (certified on reconstructable hard-label tasks T1/T3; diagnostic/windowed robustness on T2/T4), and the gap is robust to anchor choice (τ-sweep). A 4B re-ranker over a BM25 shortlist beats BM25 (+0.047 [0.020, 0.073]) without itself certifying eligibility.

Conclusion: The eligibility gap tracks model size more than precision. Practically, SLMs should be placed behind a baseline that meets the CI-backed threshold, and used only where the baseline fails to meet it.

Abstract: Agent harnesses increasingly want to run small language models (SLMs) on the microtasks around a frontier large language model (LLM) planner: auto-approving shell commands, writing memory, selecting tools, ranking past turns. We ask whether off-the-shelf SLMs meet practitioner-defined thresholds and, when they fail, why, and whether quantization changes the answer. We build a benchmark of 4 such microtasks with fixed prompts and automatic metrics, each with a pre-specified threshold $τ$ anchored to a cheap non-LLM baseline and a CI-aware eligibility rule (a configuration passes only if its confidence bound clears $τ$). Sweeping Qwen3 0.6/1.7/4/8B at their best (FP16, greedy, one frozen prompt, no tuning), we find an eligibility gap: 0 of 16 (4 tasks $\times$ 4 models) configurations pass (verified by checking the raw outputs and parser behavior). A logprob decision-threshold diagnostic (T1/T3/T4; T2 via a context-length/cascade probe) separates the failures into capability deficits and failures that can be addressed by changing the decoding threshold (4 regimes). Quantization to 4-bit (RTN/GPTQ/AWQ) does damage that depends on model size and moves no configuration into eligibility (certified on the reconstructable hard-label tasks T1/T3, diagnostic/windowed robustness on T2/T4), so the gap tracks model size more than precision; it replicates on Llama-3.x (12/12 ineligible) and is robust to the anchor choice (a $τ$-sweep) and to prompt wording (0/112 eligible across the original plus 3 neutral paraphrases per cell). The practical implication: place SLMs behind a baseline that meets the CI-backed threshold, and use the SLM only where the baseline fails to meet the threshold; e.g. a 4B re-ranker over a BM25 shortlist beats BM25 ($+0.047$ [0.020, 0.073], without itself certifying eligibility).

</details>


### [89] [Characterizing a Configuration Where Inference-Time PRM-Pruned Fragment Grafting Is Inert: Evidence from Three Reasoning LMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00047)
*Khawaja Murad ul Hassan,Mehran Ebrahimi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PRM-pruned fragment grafting (PPFG), an inference-time cross-trajectory transfer mechanism, is statistically equivalent to an independent parallel-CoT baseline across twelve Qwen/LLaMA cells, and the authors diagnose why it fails and offer an equivalence-testing template for mechanism nulls.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diversity collapse in parallel chain-of-thought motivates inference-time interventions. A natural, cost-minimal design is to extract the high-PRM prefix of a pruned chain and graft it verbatim as an in-context demonstration into a still-decoding sibling. Prior fragment-grafting work reports gains only with additional compensating ingredients, so the pure mechanism (PPFG) remains untested at the operating point where it operates alone.

Method: Isolate PPFG as the most cost-minimal operationalization of cross-trajectory step-level transfer. Evaluate Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct with Math-Shepherd on full MATH500 (n=500, three seeds) in stagnation- and random-targeting variants. Diagnose via a four-bucket classification of 322 stagnation-rule injection events, compound-gate refinement attempts, and a random control. Replicate across three base LMs, six benchmarks, a second PRM, and a compatibility-gate sweep. Apply two-one-sided-tests (TOST) equivalence analysis, a per-event spot-check comparing injected-chain versus matched-step pruning rates, a surviving-sibling counterfactual, and a hindsight oracle bound.

Result: PPFG is statistically indistinguishable from an independent parallel-CoT baseline on every measured axis, matching the random control's parity at 2.4x the firing rate. Only 14% of injection events targeted a genuinely struggling chain; the rest hit already-succeeded, near-complete, or flat-PRM-plateau chains that a rescue graft cannot change. No compound-gate refinement jointly achieves well-targeted firing and adequate density. TOST promotes parity to positive equivalence on all twelve Qwen/LLaMA cells. Injected chains prune at 2.75x the matched-step rate, but the surviving-sibling counterfactual shows no population-level compensation. The hindsight oracle bounds any per-problem gain from PPFG over independent at +0.13 pp.

Conclusion: PPFG provides no reliable benefit at its tested operating point; its inertness is structural rather than heuristic-specific, since most injection events land on states a rescue graft cannot alter. The authors contribute an equivalence-testing template for establishing inference-time mechanism nulls, with every claim scoped to its tested operating point.

Abstract: Diversity collapse in parallel chain-of-thought has motivated inference-time interventions built on a natural design: when a process reward model (PRM) prunes a chain, its high-PRM prefix is extracted and grafted verbatim as an in-context demonstration into a still-decoding sibling. We isolate this mechanism, PRM-Pruned Fragment Grafting (PPFG), as the most cost-minimal operationalization of cross-trajectory step-level transfer, and test it at the operating point where prior fragment-grafting work reports gains only under additional compensating ingredients. On Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct with Math-Shepherd on full MATH500 (n=500, three seeds), PPFG in both stagnation- and random-targeting variants is statistically indistinguishable from an independent parallel-CoT baseline on every measured axis. We characterize why: a four-bucket classification of 322 stagnation-rule injection events shows only 14% targeted a genuinely struggling chain; the rest landed on chains that had already succeeded, were near completion, or sat on a flat PRM plateau, states a rescue graft cannot change. No compound-gate refinement jointly achieves well-targeted firing and adequate density, and a random control matches the same parity at 2.4x the firing rate, so the inertness is not heuristic-specific. The finding replicates across three base LMs, six benchmarks, a second PRM, and a compatibility-gate sweep; two-one-sided-tests analysis promotes the parity to positive equivalence on all twelve Qwen/LLaMA cells. A per-event spot-check finds injected chains prune at 2.75x the matched-step rate, but a surviving-sibling counterfactual finds no population-level compensation. A hindsight oracle bounds any per-problem gain from choosing PPFG over independent at +0.13 pp. We contribute an equivalence-testing template for establishing inference-time mechanism nulls, with every claim scoped to its tested operating point.

</details>


### [90] [Gradient-Aligned Pair Selection for Personalized Preference Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00061)
*Ruoming Jin,Xinyu Li,Hao Zhou,Jianfeng Zhu,Ruixin Guo,Feodor Dragan,Lei Xu,Haixun Wang,Yang Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper formalizes personalized preference learning as a geometry-aligned optimization problem, showing that DPO's effectiveness depends on the directional alignment between preference margins and utility gradients, and proposes GAP-DPO for utility-aware, geometry-aligned pair selection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Personalizing LLMs requires aligning generation with user-specific preferences rather than aggregate quality. While DPO offers a stable preference-learning framework, its effectiveness in personalized settings depends critically on how preference pairs are selected. Existing approaches rely on heuristic criteria (e.g., likelihood-based extremes), which decouple optimization from explicit user utility and can degrade personalization.

Method: The authors analyze the first-order interaction between gradients of expected user utility and DPO update directions, formalizing personalized preference learning as a geometry-aligned optimization problem. They show that under off-policy sampling, the DPO update shifts from a purely error-corrective signal to a reinforcement-like update when preference margins are directionally aligned with utility gradients. Based on this, they propose GAP-DPO (Geometry-Aligned Preference DPO), an iterative algorithm performing utility-aware, geometry-aligned pair selection while controlling distribution shift via epoch-wise regeneration.

Result: Experiments on personalized text generation benchmarks show GAP-DPO consistently improves stylistic fidelity, preference alignment, and generation quality compared to standard DPO variants.

Conclusion: The results establish gradient alignment as a unifying principle for personalized preference optimization and demonstrate that pair selection is an intrinsic component of the optimization geometry rather than a heuristic preprocessing step.

Abstract: Personalizing large language models (LLMs) requires aligning generation behavior with user-specific preferences rather than aggregate quality. While Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) provides a stable framework for preference learning, its effectiveness in personalized settings critically depends on how preference pairs are selected. Existing approaches typically rely on heuristic criteria, such as likelihood-based extremes, which decouple optimization from explicit user utility and can lead to degraded personalization. We formalize personalized preference learning as a geometry-aligned optimization problem by analyzing the first-order interaction between gradients of expected user utility and DPO update directions. Our analysis reveals that, under off-policy sampling, the DPO update transitions from a purely error-corrective signal to a reinforcement-like update when preference margins are directionally aligned with utility gradients. This perspective exposes pair selection as a geometric decision that governs whether preference optimization advances or hinders personalization. Motivated by this insight, we propose GAP-DPO (Geometry-Aligned Preference DPO), an iterative algorithm that performs utility-aware, geometry-aligned pair selection while controlling distribution shift via epoch-wise regeneration. Experiments on personalized text generation benchmarks show that GAP-DPO consistently improves stylistic fidelity, preference alignment, and generation quality compared to standard DPO variants. Together, our results establish gradient alignment as a unifying principle for personalized preference optimization and demonstrate that pair selection is an intrinsic component of the optimization geometry rather than a heuristic preprocessing step.

</details>


### [91] [K-Dense BYOK: An Open-Source AI Research Assistant That Runs Locally and Keeps a Hash-Chained Lab Notebook](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00074)
*Aubrey M. Brueckner,Darshil Patel,Yuhuan He,Timothy Kassis*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: K-Dense BYOK is a free, open-source, locally-run AI research assistant for scientists, distinguished by built-in scientific scaffolding, an append-only Living Lab Notebook, and an observed (agent-inaccessible) activity log; it outperformed two managed platforms on interdisciplinary research prompts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: General-purpose chat assistants and coding agents are unsuitable for rigorous scientific work. The paper targets the most common failure mode—model overclaiming, identified in the authors' earlier benchmark of nine frontier models—and aims to make claims checkable rather than preventing them, while preserving researcher control, data locality, and long-term readability without dependence on the application.

Method: K-Dense BYOK runs on the researcher's own computer with user-supplied model access (hosted or local). Each project is an ordinary folder holding data, code, results, and the record. It provides three differentiators: (1) a library of written scientific procedures, guided workflow templates, research-data catalogs, and reviewer/writer agent roles; (2) an append-only Living Lab Notebook whose entries link into an argument; and (3) a tamper-resistant log that records agent behavior by observation (the agent has no tool to write to it). Evaluation used twenty interdisciplinary research prompts scored under a pre-registered rubric against two managed platforms.
