Result: K-Dense BYOK led both managed platforms on scientific quality and research execution. Its deliverables were the only ones that recorded the software they ran, and the only ones usually accompanied by a command to regenerate results. One managed platform used the same frontier model yet supplied neither. Caveat: environment records were files written by the agent, not part of the observed log, which does not yet capture the software environment itself.

Conclusion: A local, open-source (MIT-licensed) assistant with scientific scaffolding, an append-only notebook, and observation-based logging yields higher scientific quality and execution than managed platforms, even when the underlying model is identical. Future work includes extending the observed log to capture the software environment directly.

Abstract: K-Dense BYOK (bring your own keys) is a free, open-source AI research assistant for scientists in any field that runs on the researcher's own computer. The researcher supplies access to a model of their choice, hosted or running locally, and the application supplies everything else: a place for the work to run, a layer of scientific scaffolding, and a complete record. Each project is an ordinary folder, so the data, the code, the results, and the record stay on a machine the researcher administers and can be read years later without the application. Three things separate it from a chat assistant or a general-purpose coding agent. It ships a library of written scientific procedures, guided workflow templates, catalogs of where research data can be found, and reviewer and writer roles the agent can hand work to. It keeps a Living Lab Notebook whose entries link into an argument and are added to but never erased. And it records what happened by watching what the agent does rather than by taking the agent's word for it, in a log the agent has no tool that can write to. That choice targets the most common failure, model overclaiming, in our earlier benchmark of nine frontier models, by making claims checkable rather than preventing them. On twenty interdisciplinary research prompts, scored under a rubric fixed in advance, K-Dense BYOK led two managed platforms on both scientific quality and research execution. Its deliverables were the only ones that recorded the software they ran in, and the only ones that usually arrived with a command that regenerates the results. One of the managed platforms ran the same frontier model and supplied neither. Those environment records were files the agent wrote, not part of the observed log, which does not yet capture the software environment itself. The code is available under the MIT license at https://github.com/K-Dense-AI/k-dense-byok.

</details>


### [92] [Scientific Agents: Evaluating Profession-Specific System Prompts on Scientific Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00084)
*Timothy Kassis*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Evaluating 503 profession-specific AGENTS.md profiles across nine science benchmarks and a bioinformatics tool-use benchmark, full profession profiles yielded no consistent accuracy gain (-0.6 pp on average; -10.0 pp on tool-use tasks) while consuming 1.5-2.3x more output tokens and 2.2-4.5x more cost per call.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Detailed profession-specific system prompts are widely assumed to improve agent performance, yet they increase token usage and cost per response. This work tests whether that added cost buys any consistent accuracy benefit, and whether any observed gains stem from domain expertise or merely from prompt length/formatting.

Method: The authors release Scientific Agents, an open-source corpus of 503 profession-specific AGENTS.md profiles, and evaluate them with Gemini 3.8 Flash via OpenRouter in the Pi agent harness. They compare matched profiles against four controls: a minimal baseline ("You are a helpful assistant"), the profile's opening role sentence, a generic scientific rigor guide, and a profile from an unrelated domain. Evaluation covers nine text-based science benchmarks (4,531 sampled questions, 100 matched profiles; 4,488 items completing all five conditions after API-error retries) with automated rule-based grading, plus 60 tool-using BioMysteryBench bioinformatics problems run three times each for baseline and profile. Statistics use paired differences with 95% bootstrap intervals.

Result: The average profile-minus-baseline accuracy difference is -0.6 percentage points (95% bootstrap interval [-1.5, +0.2] across fixed tasks), with no benchmark showing a statistically clear improvement. Matched profiles produced 1.5-2.3x as many output tokens and cost 2.2-4.5x more per successful call. On BioMysteryBench, mean solve rate was 46.7% with the profile versus 56.7% at baseline (-10.0 pp, 95% interval [-16.7, -3.3]), driven by more frequent token- and time-limit stops under the profile. One operational advantage emerged: on SuperGPQA, frequent provider API drops left the short baseline with a correct first-pass answer on only 54.0% of items versus 71.6% with the profile. Generic and mismatched prompts were about as reliable, indicating this gain derives from prompt length or formatting rather than domain expertise.

Conclusion: For the tested model and tasks, loading full profession profiles by default does not improve accuracy and costs considerably more. The authors suggest that selective retrieval of profile sections or open-ended scientific tasks might change this outcome, but both remain untested.

Abstract: Detailed profession-specific system prompts raise token use and estimated cost per response without a consistent accuracy gain. We evaluate Scientific Agents, an open-source corpus of 503 profession-specific AGENTS.md profiles, with Gemini 3.8 Flash via OpenRouter in the Pi agent harness. We compare matched profiles with four controls: a minimal baseline ("You are a helpful assistant"), the profile's opening role sentence, a generic scientific rigor guide, and a profile from an unrelated domain. Across nine text-based science benchmarks (4,531 sampled questions, 100 matched profiles), 4,488 items completed all five conditions after API-error retries, scored with automated, rule-based grading. The average profile-baseline accuracy difference is -0.6 percentage points (95% bootstrap interval [-1.5, +0.2] across fixed tasks), and no benchmark shows a statistically clear improvement. Matched profiles produced 1.5-2.3 times as many output tokens and cost 2.2-4.5 times more per successful call. On 60 tool-using BioMysteryBench bioinformatics problems (three runs each for baseline and profile), mean solve rates were 46.7% with the profile and 56.7% at baseline, a difference of -10.0 percentage points (95% interval [-16.7, -3.3]) driven by more frequent token- and time-limit stops under the profile. Longer prompts had one unexpected operational advantage: on SuperGPQA, frequent provider API drops left the short baseline with a correct first-pass answer on only 54.0% of items, against 71.6% with the profile. Generic and mismatched prompts were about as reliable, so this gain comes from prompt length or formatting rather than domain expertise. For the tested model and tasks, loading full profession profiles by default does not improve accuracy and costs considerably more; whether selective retrieval of profile sections or open-ended scientific tasks would change this remains to be tested.

</details>


### [93] [Comedic Fool's Gold: Reward Exploits and Countermeasures in Conversational Humor](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00197)
*Sam Larson*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper studies reward exploits when training language models for conversational humor, showing that surprise- and audience-based rewards are hackable, that countermeasures can over-constrain behavior, and that iterative reward revision yields only partial improvement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automated rewards are attractive for training conversational humor, but designing them is difficult because rewards may be satisfied through exploitable shortcuts rather than genuine humor. The authors aim to characterize these exploits and test countermeasures.

Method: They examine two reward approaches: one targeting understandable surprise and one predicting audience amusement. Controlled tests probe vulnerabilities (word-shuffled replies for an embedding-based surprise reward; laughter cues for an audience model). They then apply countermeasures—a fluency filter and cross-speaker normalization of laughter cues—and run three reinforcement-learning training runs with successive reward revisions.

Result: The embedding-based surprise reward accepts word-shuffled replies as readily as witty ones; a fluency filter detects shuffles but also rejects some witty replies and fails further validation. The audience model is vulnerable to laughter cues from either speaker; normalizing cues across speakers blocks covered attacks, though unmatched expressions remain exploitable. The final RL run improves the combined evaluation score by 0.0903 and reduces zero-score sessions by 40%, yet humor-specific improvement stays below the preregistered target.

Conclusion: Countermeasures against reward exploits must block shortcuts without discouraging the intended humorous behavior. This tension remains unresolved, highlighting a broader challenge for automated reward design in humor training.

Abstract: We investigate automated rewards for training language models in conversational humor, focusing on reward exploits and countermeasures. Two approaches aim to capture understandable surprise and predicted audience amusement. Controlled tests show that an embedding-based surprise reward accepts word-shuffled replies as readily as witty ones. A fluency filter detects the shuffles, but the combined reward also rejects some witty replies and fails further validation. An audience model's predicted laughter is instead vulnerable to laughter cues in either speaker's messages. Normalizing these cues across speakers blocks the covered attacks, although unmatched expressions remain exploitable. Three reinforcement-learning runs evaluate training with successive reward revisions. The final run improves the combined evaluation score by 0.0903 and reduces zero-score sessions by 40%, but its humor-specific improvement remains below our preregistered target. These findings illustrate a broader challenge for automated reward design: countermeasures must block exploitable shortcuts while preserving the behavior the reward was intended to encourage.

</details>


### [94] [EviGraph: Proof-Carrying Selective Recommendation over Temporal Public-Service Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00212)
*Yixi Zhou,Sikun Wang,Lei Fan,Fan Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EviGraph separates critical decision requirements from non-decisive missing information, using a language agent plus a temporal knowledge graph and a deterministic checker to reduce unnecessary abstention in public-service recommendations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Public-service recommendations need evidence matching the requested service, scope, and date, but treating every missing detail as decisive causes useful recommendations to be withheld unnecessarily. The goal is to avoid needless abstention while keeping recommendations evidence-supported.

Method: The authors introduce EviGraph, which distinguishes critical decision requirements from information that can remain unresolved. A language agent links these requirements to evidence in a temporal knowledge graph, and a deterministic checker determines whether a recommendation is supported.

Result: On a bilingual Hong Kong public-service benchmark with executable policy references, this distinction reduces unnecessary abstention. Additional verification, however, can withdraw supported recommendations without improving decision quality.

Conclusion: Reliable evidence-based navigation depends on specifying what must be established for a decision, rather than simply adding more verification.

Abstract: Public-service recommendations require evidence that matches the requested service, scope, and date. Yet treating every missing detail as decisive can withhold useful recommendations. We introduce EviGraph, which distinguishes critical decision requirements from information that can remain unresolved. A language agent links these requirements to evidence in a temporal knowledge graph, while a deterministic checker establishes whether a recommendation is supported. Evaluation on a bilingual Hong Kong public-service benchmark with executable policy references shows that this distinction reduces unnecessary abstention. Additional verification, however, can withdraw supported recommendations without improving decision quality. These findings suggest that reliable evidence-based navigation depends on specifying what must be established for a decision, rather than simply adding more verification.

</details>


### [95] [Build2SPARQL: A Large-Scale Text-to-SPARQL Benchmark Dataset for Building Knowledge Graph Querying](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00224)
*Wooyoung Jung*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Build2SPARQL is a large-scale benchmark of 6,136 executable SPARQL queries and 30,680 natural-language questions over 201 building knowledge graphs, generated by a KG-grounded pipeline where graph-traversal code produces and validates queries and LLMs only phrase questions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Text-to-SPARQL would enable building operators to query semantic building KGs (Brick, ASHRAE 223P) via language agents, but progress is constrained by the scarcity of large natural-language/SPARQL benchmarks.

Method: A KG-grounded pipeline generates and validates SPARQL queries entirely through graph-traversal code, with LLMs used solely for natural-language question generation, decoupling query correctness from model behavior. It mines six query-pattern families (linear chains, branching, UNION, aggregation, OPTIONAL, attribute-filtered) and phrases each query across five vocabulary registers, applied to 201 building KGs (180 Brick, 21 ASHRAE 223P).

Result: The benchmark comprises 6,136 executable SPARQL queries and 30,680 questions. Two-rater human validation of 300 questions yielded 98.8% semantic fidelity, 98.8% naturalness, and 84.0% operational plausibility. Retrieval-augmented evaluation across three open-weight LLMs improved exact-match accuracy from 0.2-20% zero-shot to 56-65% three-shot retrieved.

Conclusion: Build2SPARQL provides a scalable, correctness-guaranteed benchmark for text-to-SPARQL over building KGs, and retrieval augmentation substantially boosts LLM exact-match performance, offering a foundation for language-driven building analytics.

Abstract: Building automation systems are increasingly represented as semantic knowledge graphs (KGs) using ontologies such as Brick and ASHRAE 223P, creating a machine-readable substrate for artificial-intelligence applications. One promising application is translating natural-language questions into SPARQL (text-to-SPARQL), which would let building operators query these graphs through language agents, but progress is limited by the scarcity of large natural-language/SPARQL benchmarks. This paper presents Build2SPARQL, a large-scale benchmark for building KGs generated by a KG-grounded pipeline: SPARQL queries are produced and validated entirely by graph-traversal code, while large language models generate only the natural-language questions, keeping query correctness independent of model behavior. The pipeline mines six query-pattern families -- linear chains, branching, UNION, aggregation, OPTIONAL, and attribute-filtered -- and phrases each query across five vocabulary registers. Applied to 201 building KGs (180 Brick, 21 ASHRAE 223P), it yields 6,136 executable SPARQL queries and 30,680 questions. A two-rater human validation of 300 questions found 98.8% semantic fidelity, 98.8% naturalness, and 84.0% operational plausibility. A retrieval-augmented evaluation across three open-weight language models raised exact-match accuracy from 0.2-20% (zero-shot) to 56-65% (three-shot retrieved).

</details>


### [96] [Robust Is Salient: An Informed Adversary Moves the Optimal Signal onto the Salience Pole](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00233)
*Cris Huynh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: In a constrained signalling channel with an informed adversary, the adversary-robust optimal signal coincides exactly with the salience pole from prior work on 108 confirmatory items, and differs on only 2,748 of 200,000 pool items (precisely where the salience-to-Bayes coordinate is undefined), creating a structural barrier to empirically evaluating adversary-awareness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Adversary-aware signalling is theoretically distinct from heuristic salience-based signalling, so one would expect to be able to empirically distinguish them. The authors investigate whether this distinction is actually measurable, motivated by the concern that if the robust optimum and a simple heuristic target coincide on evaluation items, then any observed behavioral movement cannot be attributed to adversary-awareness.

Method: The authors introduce an informed adversary to a forced-choice task abstracted from Deception: Murder in Hong Kong. The adversary knows the target, observes the signal, and argues for the strongest wrong answer under a persuasion budget β. The optimal signal is derived as β grows, with β=0 reproducing the original oracle model at listener temperature τ=1, and compared against the salience pole from prior work across a 200,000-item pool and 108 confirmatory items. Two adversary framings are then tested on seven language models (30–77 of 108 items affected).

Result: On the 108 confirmatory items, the adversary-robust optimum aligns exactly with the salience pole. Across the 200,000-item pool, the two differ on only 2,748 items, exactly where the salience-to-Bayes coordinate is undefined. As β grows, the optimal signal shifts from posterior-maximizing to margin-maximizing; 18.2% of the pool has an optimum that shifts under a finite budget, with each item's critical budget being exact. Two adversary framings change the chosen option of seven language models on 30 to 77 of 108 items against an exact no-effect rate.

Conclusion: Where the robust target is defined, robustness is achieved by moving entirely from Bayesian discrimination to salience. Because the adversary-aware optimum and the salience target are identical on the evaluated items, no measurement can determine whether observed model movement reflects adversary-awareness or salience — a structural limit, not a null result. The diagnostic check is cheap: before evaluating adversary-awareness, verify whether the robust target coincides with a heuristic target on the evaluation items.

Abstract: When an informed adversary shares the audience of a constrained signalling channel, the signal that best protects the truth is the signal that best describes it. On 108 confirmatory items, the adversary-robust optimum aligns exactly with the salience pole from prior work. Across a 200,000-item pool, the two differ on only 2,748 items --- lying exactly where the prior salience-to-Bayes coordinate is undefined. Where defined, robustness is achieved by moving from Bayesian discrimination entirely to salience. We show this by introducing an adversary to a forced-choice task (abstracted from Deception: Murder in Hong Kong). The adversary knows the target, observes the signal, and argues for the strongest wrong answer using a persuasion budget, $β$. As $β$ grows, the optimal signal shifts from the posterior-maximizing option to the margin-maximizing one; at $β= 0$, the game reproduces the original oracle model with a listener temperature of $τ= 1$. This effect is real: 18.2 percent of the pool has an optimum that shifts under a finite budget, and each item's critical budget is exact. This coincidence structurally limits empirical evaluation. Two adversary framings change the chosen option of seven language models on 30 to 77 of 108 items against an exact no-effect rate. Yet, no measurement can determine whether this movement is toward the adversary-aware optimum or toward salience, because the two options are identical. This is a structural limit, not a null result. The diagnostic check is cheap: before evaluating adversary-awareness, verify whether the robust target coincides with a heuristic target on the evaluation items.

</details>


### [97] [Conflicting Supervision Moves Commitment, Not Capability: A 12.29σ arrangement effect that is exactly zero under a convention-agnostic score](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00234)
*Wenhui Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper shows that learning-rate schedule acts as the averaging operator determining how data ordering is encoded into model parameters, proves a bound separating arrangement and schedule as multiplied factors, and measures ordering effects empirically across ten orderings and twelve arms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Train a model on identical problems written under two incompatible but both-correct conventions, and ask what ordering of that data writes into parameters. The learning-rate schedule is treated not as a background condition but as the averaging operator that decides the answer. The authors aim to establish the mechanism, the separation of arrangement and schedule effects, and a controlled measurement of both halves — since prior reports that decay moderates ordering effects in pretraining lacked this mechanistic account.

Method: A theoretical bound is derived in which arrangement and schedule enter the ordering effect as separate multiplied factors: arrangement only as a block period, schedule only as how much weight the endpoint can place on any one moment of the run. Empirically, ten orderings of one corpus under one budget, with everything but the path held fixed, are run twice under lr_scheduler_type families differing in nothing else. Contrast floors, allocation, and sigma statistics are measured; the conservation of acc_A+acc_B is read from the decayed family.

Result: At a constant rate the interior spans 0.2221 in allocation and 11.63 contrast floors, monotone in how blocked the arrangement is. Under the single cosine every published arm uses, the same ten arms occupy two distinguishable states where their own resolution would allow about ten. Across twelve arms acc_A+acc_B is constant to within 9.7% while allocation share runs 0.04 to 0.87, making the 12.29-sigma arrangement switch exactly zero under a convention-agnostic metric. Marking the convention in the prompt collapses the switch and reaches 87.5% of the union ceiling.

Conclusion: "Order matters" and "order does not matter" are the two ends of one knob, governed by the learning-rate schedule. The path writes which convention the model commits to, and no exact-match benchmark can see it. A decaying schedule cannot place a large step size and an uncontracted remainder at the same moment, whereas a constant one does exactly that at the last step — so decay moderates ordering effects, but arrangement and schedule remain separable multiplied factors.

Abstract: "Train a model on the same problems written under two incompatible conventions, both correct, and ask what the ordering of that data writes into the parameters. The learning-rate schedule is not a background condition for that question. It is the averaging operator, and it decides the answer. We prove a bound in which the arrangement and the schedule enter the ordering effect as separate multiplied factors: the arrangement only as a block period, the schedule only as how much weight the endpoint can place on any one moment of the run. A decaying schedule cannot put a large step size and an uncontracted remainder at the same moment; a constant one does exactly that at the last step. That decay moderates ordering effects has been reported in pretraining; the mechanism, the separation, and a controlled measurement of both halves are ours. Ten orderings of one corpus, one budget, everything but the path held fixed, run twice under families differing in lr_scheduler_type and nothing else: at a constant rate the interior spans 0.2221 in allocation, 11.63 contrast floors, monotone in how blocked the arrangement is. Under the single cosine every published arm uses, the same ten arms occupy two distinguishable states where their own resolution would allow about ten. "Order matters" and "order does not matter" are the two ends of one knob. What the path writes is which convention the model commits to, and no exact-match benchmark can see it. Across twelve arms acc_A+acc_B is constant to within 9.7% while the allocation share runs 0.04 to 0.87, so the 12.29-sigma arrangement switch this paper measures is exactly zero under a convention-agnostic metric. That conservation is quoted from the decayed family throughout, the constant-rate one being a noisier place to read it. Marking the convention in the prompt collapses the switch and reaches 87.5% of the union ceiling."

</details>


### [98] [Knowing When to Yield: Grounded Arbitration of User Corrections in Text-Based Embodied Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00282)
*Yezhou Cheng,Runjia Du,Zeming Liu,Hang Lyu,Zehua Yang,Bojun Lin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GAVA arbitrates grounded correction by choosing among accept, reject, inspect, or ask, using observation-bounded evidence and a one-step expected-loss rule; it achieves high accuracy and, with a semantic location prior, lowers interaction and joint cost in text-only ALFWorld.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: An embodied agent receiving a person's correction faces the problem that the correction may be wrong. The paper motivates a principled arbitration mechanism that decides whether to accept, reject, inspect the world, or ask the speaker, rather than assuming corrections are always reliable or always verifying them.

Method: GAVA is formulated as grounded correction arbitration over four actions: accept, reject, inspect, and ask. It uses observation-bounded evidence, legal probes, and a one-step expected-loss rule. In text-only ALFWorld, 162 checkpoints yield 972 paired true and false interventions; evaluation includes complete local inspections, same-episode execution, an exploratory training-only object-location prior, and replication on 77 non-overlapping seen checkpoints (308 scenarios) after freezing policy, costs, baselines, and multiplicity plan. Conditions use normalized claims, complete symbolic observations, and controlled speakers.

Result: Complete local inspections give GAVA and always-verify 100 percent correction accuracy, establishing the evidence contract rather than a comparative advantage. GAVA reduces interaction cost against always-verify but ties a cost threshold under a perfect speaker. The training-only object-location prior lowers interaction and declared joint cost on 340 unseen scenarios by 0.490 and 0.420 relative to uniform GAVA; replication yields 0.595 and 0.517, with both 95 percent checkpoint-bootstrap confidence intervals excluding zero. Joint cost also improves over an identical-prior fixed policy, while the matched calibrated no-VOI comparison remains inconclusive. Semantic GAVA makes four factual errors per cohort (98.8 and 98.7 percent accuracy), and all methods complete every task.

Conclusion: Results support selective information gathering with semantic priors under declared costs, but do not establish a general advantage of environmental value of information over clarification. The study is limited to normalized claims, complete symbolic observations, and controlled speakers; it evaluates neither human participants, visual input, nor physical robots.

Abstract: How should an embodied agent respond when a person's correction may be wrong? We formulate grounded correction arbitration as a choice among accepting, rejecting, inspecting the world, and asking the speaker. GAVA implements this interface with observation-bounded evidence, legal probes, and a one-step expected-loss rule. In text-only ALFWorld, 162 checkpoints produce 972 paired true and false interventions. Complete local inspections give GAVA and always verify 100 percent correction accuracy, establishing the evidence contract rather than a comparative advantage. In same-episode execution, GAVA reduces interaction cost against always verify but ties a cost threshold under a perfect speaker. An exploratory training-only object-location prior lowers interaction and declared joint cost on 340 unseen scenarios by 0.490 and 0.420 relative to uniform GAVA. After freezing the policy, costs, baselines, and multiplicity plan, the gains replicate on 77 non-overlapping seen checkpoints, covering 308 scenarios: 0.595 and 0.517, with both 95 percent checkpoint-bootstrap confidence intervals excluding zero. Joint cost also improves over an identical-prior fixed policy, while the matched calibrated no-VOI comparison remains inconclusive. Semantic GAVA makes four factual errors in each cohort, corresponding to 98.8 percent and 98.7 percent accuracy, and all methods complete every task. Results support selective information gathering with semantic priors under declared costs, but do not establish a general advantage of environmental value of information over clarification. The study uses normalized claims, complete symbolic observations, and controlled speakers; it evaluates neither human participants, visual input, nor physical robots.

</details>


### [99] [Rules to Tools: Executable Checks for LLM Agents in Scientific Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00313)
*Jingjie Ning,Guojiang Zhao,Chen Xu,Shanshan Zhong,Xiaochuan Li,Ji Zeng,Guolin Ke*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Prepared executable checks (Rules to Tools) from public scientific requirements improve coding-agent repair of scientific programs relative to written text, but the benefit is task-dependent and raises public CPU cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scientific coding agents must assess and revise programs given equations, boundary conditions, and output requirements in writing; the paper tests whether supplying prepared executable checks of public scientific requirements improves repair outcomes compared with text-only descriptions.

Method: Matched SciCode repair groups share written checks, starting programs, model, and budgets; the tool group instead receives a callable implementation. Comparisons are run across task-ID cohorts, a shared-definition SciCode cohort, development-exposed tasks with alternate starting programs, a fresh source-through-Python arm, and a matched PDE comparison. A task-cluster bootstrap estimates uncertainty.

Result: Complete repair is 26/30 with text versus 29/30 with prepared checks (three task IDs favor tools, one favors text, eleven tie). The eight-ID cohort scores 13/16 versus 15/16, with a 95% task-cluster bootstrap interval of [-12.5, 43.75] percentage points. The larger shared-definition cohort ties at 13/24 per group. Development-exposed tasks score 3/10 versus 7/10. Tools favor tasks 17, 77, and 11; initial checks flag 17 and report no violation for 77 and 11, while task 37 favors text and shows no initial reported violation. The fresh source-through-Python arm reaches 15/16, matching the dedicated command. In the PDE comparison, text scores 23/24 and checks 24/24, with 31.2% lower reported model output for checks. Agent-side output savings vary by cohort, while public CPU use rises in both task-ID cohorts.

Conclusion: Prepared executable checks yield task-dependent repair gains and reduce reported model output and agent-side costs, but increase public CPU use; overall effects are modest and cohort-dependent rather than uniformly superior to text.

Abstract: Scientific coding agents receive equations, boundary conditions, and output requirements in writing, then must assess the programs they revise. Rules to Tools (R2T) supplies prepared executable checks of public scientific requirements. Matched SciCode repair groups share written checks, starting programs, model, and budgets; the tool group receives a callable implementation. Across two task-ID cohorts, complete repair is 26/30 with text and 29/30 with the prepared checks. Three task IDs favor tools, one favors text, and eleven tie. The eight-ID cohort scores 13/16 versus 15/16, with a task-cluster bootstrap 95% interval of [-12.5, 43.75] percentage points for the difference. The larger shared-definition SciCode cohort ties at 13/24 per group. Five development-exposed tasks with alternate starting programs score 3/10 versus 7/10. The tool group favors tasks 17, 77, and 11; initial checks flag task 17 and report no violation for tasks 77 and 11. Task 37 favors text and has no initial reported violation. A fresh source-through-Python arm also reaches 15/16, matching the dedicated command's aggregate. In a matched PDE comparison, detailed text scores 23/24 and checks score 24/24, with 31.2% lower reported model output for checks. Agent-side output savings vary by cohort, while public CPU use rises in both task-ID cohorts. These results measure task-dependent repair outcomes and agent-side costs with prepared checks.

</details>


### [100] [Predictive Credit: Measuring What Scientific Explanations Add to Experimental Forecasts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00314)
*Jingjie Ning,Xueqi Li,Yibo Kong,Dongting Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A preregistered paired-forecast protocol measured whether research agents' explanations earn predictive credit; frozen credit decisions were inconclusive across 336 prospective states, 12 Tox21 endpoints, and 24 OpenML tasks, leaving natural-explanation credit unconfirmed at tested donor resolutions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Research agents produce explanations of planned experiments, yet it is unclear whether these explanations carry genuine predictive credit (information value for forecasting outcomes) or merely accompany the forecaster. The paper seeks a rigorous, controlled way to attribute credit to an explanation versus a description, and to test whether matched explanations confer predictive benefit across controlled learning, Tox21, and OpenML settings.

Method: Predictive credit is measured with paired forecasts that share an intervention, forecaster, and outcome while varying description, matched explanation, and donor context. Five checks are tracked: commitment, delivery, predictive gain, alignment, and known-signal uptake. Evaluation spans 336 prospective states in controlled learning, 12 Tox21 endpoints, and 24 OpenML tasks. Statistical tests include a preregistered ROC AUC interval-score harm test (Tox21), a joint formation/point-equivalence/repeatability rule (OpenML), seed-donor intervals, interval-score equivalence, and a researcher-authored mechanism positive control.

Result: The v5 frozen credit decision was inconclusive. Tox21's preregistered ROC AUC interval-score harm test was unmet (D-M = -.0026, 95% interval [-.0174, .0104]) and OpenML's joint rule was unmet. Matched point-accuracy gains over description remained unconfirmed; Tox21/OpenML seed-donor intervals spanned zero. Under DeepSeek V4 Pro, matched and donor cards reduced secondary Tox21 drift by 64.5% and 59.1%. A DeepSeek V4 Flash replay raised matched point MAE from .01823 to .02020 and missed matched-donor interval-score equivalence. OpenML full-card assignment widened nominal 80% intervals by 21%, with 49.3% coverage versus 51.4% for description/content in 66/144 cards. Direct-text Flash delivered all 144 notes with no detectable matched point-accuracy gain. The mechanism positive control lowered point MAE by 2.60 percentage points versus description.

Conclusion: The protocol operationalizes measurement of predictive credit for research-agent benchmarks and scientific forecasting. Credit for natural explanations remained unconfirmed at the tested donor resolutions, even though the positive control demonstrates the protocol can detect a genuine mechanism effect; the authors therefore treat explanation-based credit as unresolved under their preregistered criteria.

Abstract: Research agents explain planned experiments. We measure predictive credit with paired forecasts sharing an intervention, forecaster, and outcome while varying description, matched explanation, and donor context. Five checks track commitment, delivery, predictive gain, alignment, and known-signal uptake. Across 336 prospective states in controlled learning, 12 Tox21 endpoints, and 24 OpenML tasks, v5's frozen credit decision was inconclusive. Tox21's preregistered ROC AUC interval-score harm test was unmet ($D-M=-.0026$, 95 percent interval [$-.0174$, .0104]); OpenML's joint formation, point-equivalence, and repeatability rule was unmet. Matched point-accuracy gains over description remained unconfirmed, and Tox21/OpenML seed-donor intervals spanned zero. Under requested DeepSeek V4 Pro, matched and donor cards reduced secondary Tox21 drift by 64.5 and 59.1 percent. A DeepSeek V4 Flash replay raised matched point MAE from .01823 to .02020 and missed matched-donor interval-score equivalence. OpenML full-card assignment widened nominal 80 percent intervals by 21 percent, with 49.3 percent coverage versus 51.4 percent for description and content in 66/144 cards. Direct-text Flash delivered all 144 notes without detectable matched point-accuracy gain. A researcher-authored mechanism positive control lowered point MAE by 2.60 percentage points versus description. The protocol measures predictive credit for research-agent benchmarks and scientific forecasting; natural-explanation credit remained unconfirmed at the tested donor resolutions.

</details>


### [101] [ContractRL: Shielded Group-Relative Policy Optimization for Auditable Tool-Call Repair](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00328)
*Miaobo Hu,Shuhao Hu,Xiaobo Guo,Xin Wang,Bokun Wang,Yina Sa,Daren Zha,Jun Xiao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ContractRL treats verifier-guided JSON repair as a bounded decision process, using contract-derived action masks and a group-relative objective to achieve higher semantic success with fewer generated tokens than patch SFT or full regeneration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Structured tool calls frequently fail when a few fields violate a schema or execution contract; regenerating the whole object expands the action space and makes iterative repair hard to audit.

Method: Contract-constrained sequential repair protocol: at each step the policy observes candidate, typed verifier feedback, JSON Pointer, immutable repair history, and remaining budget; a contract-derived action mask filters malformed/prohibited RFC-6902 operations; a deterministic validator executes transitions. A contract-constrained group-relative objective governs patch, retry, and abstention decisions, with targets and labels kept outside the online state until trace freeze.

Result: Under identical verifier information: 0.9362 semantic success with 34.4 generated tokens vs 0.9076/44.9 for Patch-SFT and 0.9148/137.2 for full regeneration (192 cases/seed, 5 seeds). Policy optimization raises success from 0.9186 (supervised) to 0.9375. Three-seed paired evaluation vs Patch-SFT: +0.0396 semantic difference (95% CI [+0.0137, +0.0662], p=0.0039).

Conclusion: Feedback, action-mask, budget, and schema-shift analyses link the gains to localized correction; adversarial and multi-turn evaluations characterize remaining failure modes.

Abstract: Structured tool calls often fail after only a small number of fields violate a schema or an execution contract. Regenerating the complete object enlarges the action surface and makes repeated repair difficult to audit. We introduce ContractRL, a contract-constrained sequential repair protocol that models verifier-guided JSON repair as a bounded decision process. At each step the policy observes the candidate, typed verifier feedback, JSON Pointer, immutable repair history, and remaining budget; a contract-derived action mask filters malformed or prohibited RFC-6902 operations before a deterministic validator performs the transition. We specify a contract-constrained group-relative objective for patch, retry, and abstention decisions while keeping canonical targets and semantic labels outside the online state until trace freeze. Under identical verifier information, ContractRL attains 0.9362 semantic success with 34.4 generated tokens, compared with 0.9076 and 44.9 tokens for Patch-SFT and 0.9148 and 137.2 tokens for full regeneration over 192 cases per seed and five seeds. Policy optimization improves semantic success from 0.9186 for supervised ContractRL to 0.9375. A separate three-seed paired evaluation against Patch-SFT yields a semantic difference of +0.0396 (95% CI $[+0.0137,+0.0662], p=0.0039$). Feedback, action-mask, budget, and schema-shift analyses connect these gains to localized correction, while adversarial and multi-turn evaluations characterize the remaining failure modes.

</details>


### [102] [Mathematical Transfer in LLMs Follows Reasoning Approach More Than Topic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00331)
*Sajad Goudarzi,Samaneh Zamanifard,Seyed Amin Seyed Haeri,Moloud Nasiri,Hamed Rahimian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Fine-tuning LLMs on math data matched by reasoning approach (same solution method, different topic) transfers better than matching by topic (same domain, different method), across all 40 model-target comparisons tested.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: When curating mathematical training data for LLMs, the default organizing principle is topical: probability examples for probability targets. The paper questions whether this is optimal, hypothesizing that sharing the reasoning approach (solution method) with the target may yield greater transfer even when the mathematical domain differs.

Method: Two counterbalanced 2x2 designs: (1) probability vs. combinatorics crossed with invariant reasoning vs. double counting (2,000 problems); (2) number theory vs. geometry crossed with complement vs. pigeonhole reasoning (800 problems). Each cell is held out as the target in turn; same-approach (SA) sources share the target's method but differ in topic, while same-topic (ST) sources share the topic but differ in method. Every source appears once in each role, so additive source-quality effects cancel in the equally weighted aggregate contrast. Evaluation spans five base models and three training seeds per design, with embedding and lexical similarity measures also computed.

Result: SA outperforms ST in all 40 seed-pooled model-target comparisons. Model-level advantages range from 8.2 to 16.2 percentage points in the primary design (mean 10.8) and 12.0 to 16.0 in the second design (mean 14.3); all ten model-level 95% confidence intervals exclude zero. ST sources are more similar to targets under embedding and lexical measures, so the SA advantage runs opposite to the measured ordering of statement-level resemblance.

Conclusion: Reasoning approach is a more effective matching criterion than topic for mathematical transfer across the evaluated topic-approach combinations. Statement-level surface similarity does not predict transfer, indicating that method-level alignment, not topical or lexical resemblance, drives fine-tuning gains.

Abstract: When selecting mathematical training data for LLMs, a natural organizing principle is topic: probability examples for probability targets. An alternative is reasoning approach: worked solutions that share a solution method with the target, even when the mathematical domain differs. We ask which relation produces greater transfer after fine-tuning. We evaluate two counterbalanced $2\times2$ designs: probability and combinatorics crossed with invariant reasoning and double counting (2,000 problems), and number theory and geometry crossed with complement and pigeonhole reasoning (800 problems). In each design, every cell serves as the held-out target in turn: same-approach (SA) sources share the target's method but change the topic, while same-topic (ST) sources share the topic but change the method. Every source appears once in each role, so additive source-quality effects cancel from the equally weighted aggregate contrast. Across five base models and three training seeds per design, SA outperforms ST in all 40 seed-pooled model--target comparisons. Model-level advantages range from 8.2 to 16.2 percentage points in the primary design (mean: 10.8) and from 12.0 to 16.0 in the second design (mean: 14.3); all ten model-level 95% confidence intervals exclude zero. In both designs, ST sources are more similar to targets under embedding and lexical measures, so the SA advantage runs opposite to the measured ordering of statement-level resemblance. These findings identify reasoning approach as a more effective matching criterion than topic for mathematical transfer across the evaluated topic--approach combinations.

</details>


### [103] [Fault-Tolerant Budget Conservation in Distributed Multi-Agent Delegation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00349)
*Genliang Zhu,Chu Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper formalizes fault-tolerant budget conservation for distributed multi-agent delegation, using exclusive escrow credits moving through a delegation DAG with lineage-bound operation reservations and quarantine permits. It proves safety properties (ownership partition, ledger/effect conservation, non-amplification, at-most-once settlement, late-completion safety, partition confinement) and shows that partition-local availability requires exclusive preallocation, validated by TLA+ checking, an independent explorer, and crash-injected SQLite experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Resource limits are becoming an authorization boundary for AI agents that delegate work across concurrent, failure-prone workers. Existing approaches—parent-child allocation constraints, affine objects, and distributed escrow—do not by themselves prevent overspend under lost replies, effects completing after timeout, repeated messages, branch partitions, or DAG joins that alias a single lineage.

Method: Budgets are quantized resource vectors represented by exclusive escrow credits flowing through a delegation DAG. Before dispatch, a branch converts credit into an operation reservation bound to lineage, epoch, normalized effect, maximum charge, receiver, and idempotency key, persisting a signed dispatch permit with quarantine; the gateway verifies the permit before first acceptance. Uncertain effects remain charged until authenticated settlement, a fenced authoritative no-effect proof, or permanent retirement. Safety is proven under explicit mediation, durability, authentication, normalization, and gateway assumptions, with an indistinguishability argument linking partition-local availability to exclusive preallocation.

Result: Bounded TLA+ checking, an independent JavaScript explorer, and crash-injected two-process SQLite experiments exercise the declared scope and detect timeout-refund and historical-certificate-validation mutants. The mechanism preserves the issued budget bound across evaluated crash, retry, duplicate, partition, join, and late-completion schedules.

Conclusion: The proposed reservation-and-quarantine escrow mechanism conserves budgets fault-tolerantly across distributed multi-agent delegation and provably prevents overspend under the evaluated failure and concurrency schedules.

Abstract: Resource limits are becoming an authorization boundary for AI agents that delegate work across concurrent and failure-prone workers. Parent-child allocation constraints, affine objects, and distributed escrow do not by themselves prevent overspend when replies are lost, effects complete after timeout, messages repeat, branches partition, or DAG joins alias one lineage. We formalize fault-tolerant budget conservation for distributed multi-agent delegation. Budgets are quantized resource vectors represented by exclusive escrow credits that move through a delegation DAG. Before dispatch, a branch converts credit into an operation reservation bound to lineage, epoch, normalized effect, maximum charge, receiver, and idempotency key. It persists a signed dispatch permit with quarantine; the gateway verifies that permit before first acceptance. Uncertain effects remain charged until authenticated settlement, a fenced authoritative no-effect proof, or permanent retirement. We prove ownership partition, ledger and effect conservation, descendant non-amplification, at-most-once settlement, late-completion safety, and partition confinement under explicit mediation, durability, authentication, normalization, and gateway assumptions. An indistinguishability result shows that partition-local availability requires exclusive preallocation. Bounded TLA+ checking, an independent JavaScript explorer, and crash-injected two-process SQLite experiments exercise the declared scope and detect timeout-refund and historical-certificate-validation mutants. The mechanism preserves the issued budget bound across the evaluated crash, retry, duplicate, partition, join, and late-completion schedules.

</details>


### [104] [JusticeAxis: Benchmarking Legal Judgment between Rigid Rule Application and Ungrounded Discretion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00353)
*Zhengkai Tu,Mingda Zhang,Zijia Wang,Xiaoying Tang,Jimmy Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper formalizes legal judgment as a reference-anchored task and introduces JusticeAxis (256 real-world criminal cases from 18 countries with multimodal evidence and lawyer-written judgments) plus JusticeAgent, an agentic harness whose element agents establish facts and judge agent applies law using experience-derived skills admitted under Bayesian credible bounds. Experiments show failure modes reverse with model scale and JusticeAgent lifts a frozen open-weight backbone to commercial level.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods oscillate between rigid statute matching and ungrounded discretion; benchmarks only score a label or rubric; and the experiential balance that sound judgment requires remains unverified. Legal judgment demands applying law to established facts while weighing surrounding circumstances, which current approaches fail to jointly capture.

Method: The authors (1) formalize legal judgment as a reference-anchored task targeting a single decision tied simultaneously to statute and circumstances; (2) build JusticeAxis, 256 real-world criminal cases from 18 countries with audio, image, and text evidence, each paired with three lawyer-written judgments (the recorded one plus one for each failure); (3) propose JusticeAgent, a harness with element agents that establish facts and a judge agent that applies law under skills encoding circumstance experience; (4) distill skills from execution trajectories and admit them only under Bayesian credible bounds.

Result: Failure direction reverses with scale: open-weight backbones drift toward unsupported grounds, while frontier models default to the statutory baseline. JusticeAgent, as a simple plugin, raises a frozen open-weight backbone to commercial-level performance.

Conclusion: Reference-anchored formalization plus a skill-augmented multi-agent harness can correct scale-dependent failure modes and bridge the gap between open-weight and commercial legal judgment systems. Resources are released at the project GitHub repository.

Abstract: A sound judgment applies the law to established facts and weighs the circumstances in which they arose. However, existing methods swing between rigid statute matching and ungrounded discretion, benchmarks score a label or a rubric, and the experience that would supply the balance stays unverified. We formalize legal judgment as a reference-anchored task, whose object is a single decision that stays tied to the statute and to the circumstances at once. We introduce JusticeAxis, 256 real-world criminal cases from 18 countries with audio, image, and text evidence, and three lawyer-written judgments for every case: the recorded one and one for each failure. We further propose JusticeAgent, a harness whose element agents establish the facts and whose judge agent applies the law under skills carrying experience of the circumstances. Skills are distilled from execution trajectories and admitted only under Bayesian credible bounds. Experiments show that failure turns direction with scale: open-weight backbones drift to unsupported grounds, frontier models to the statutory default. We further verify that JusticeAgent, as a simple yet effective plugin, carries a frozen open-weight backbone to commercial level. Project resources are available at https://github.com/beita6969/JusticeAxis.

</details>


### [105] [What Should an Agent Remember? Disentangling Retention from Retrieval in Bounded-Memory Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00366)
*Juli Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A streaming-recall benchmark that crosses retention and selection rules shows that conflating access, retention, and selection inflates reported gains; evaluations should hold access fixed and report retention and selection separately.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Persistent agents must decide what to retain as information arrives and what to surface at query time. Existing memory evaluations often confound these two decisions by comparing methods that differ in both retention and selection (and sometimes access too), making it unclear what actually drives performance gains.

Method: The authors construct a streaming-recall benchmark that factorially crosses retention rules and selection rules, evaluating every condition on the same 300 seeded episodes. They decompose reported advantages by holding access fixed versus allowing it to vary, and they compare selection strategies (query-aware, dense, oracle, recency) under bounded retention. They also repeat the evaluation on SQuAD to test external validity on natural text.

Result: With access held fixed, query-aware selection improves required-fact recall by 15.5 percentage points (95% CI: 12.8 to 18.2). A mixed comparison that also changes history access reports a 68.7-point advantage, of which 53.2 points are attributable to access. Under bounded retention, query-aware, dense, and oracle selection all reach the retention ceiling, and all 319 observed failures in the bounded recency condition stem from eviction rather than ranking errors. Recall drops to 0% as targets recede far enough into the past. On SQuAD, the retention ceiling persists and dense retrieval can outperform lexical retrieval on natural text.

Conclusion: Bounded-memory evaluations should hold access fixed and report retention and selection separately, since most apparent gains in confounded comparisons come from access differences rather than improved selection.

Abstract: A persistent agent must decide both what to retain as information arrives and what to surface once a query appears, yet memory evaluations can confound these decisions by comparing methods that differ in both retention and selection. We build a streaming-recall benchmark crossing retention and selection rules and evaluate every condition on the same 300 seeded episodes. Holding access fixed, query-aware selection improves required-fact recall by 15.5 percentage points (95% CI: 12.8 to 18.2), whereas a mixed comparison that also changes history access reports a 68.7-point advantage, of which 53.2 points are attributable to access. Under bounded retention, query-aware, dense, and oracle selection reach the retention ceiling, and all 319 observed failures in the bounded recency condition are caused by eviction rather than ranking errors. Recall falls to 0% as targets recede sufficiently far into the past. Repeating the evaluation on SQuAD preserves the retention ceiling while showing that dense retrieval can outperform lexical retrieval on natural text. These results show that bounded-memory evaluations should hold access fixed and report retention and selection separately.

</details>


### [106] [When Harnesses Lose the Signal: Causal Evaluation of Recovery in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00372)
*Shuyao Xiao,Shengling Wang,Xuan Chen,Ke Chao,Ming Cui,Feifei Qian,Chaoyang Mei,Fanlin Meng,Ziming Yu,Junxi Yin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper reframes error recovery in LLM agents as a causal decision problem, introducing the Causal Intervention Router (CIR), a lightweight policy that decides when recovery is worthwhile. On ALFWorld with Qwen3-14B, CIR raises success from 70.33% to 73.33% while leaving correct trajectories untouched.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents rely on external harnesses to pass information and recover from errors, but recovery is typically evaluated only by average task success. This masks a key tension: the same recovery operation can rescue a failing trajectory or disrupt one that would otherwise succeed. The work motivates the need to distinguish beneficial recovery (rescue) from harmful recovery and to make intervention selective.

Method: The authors frame recovery as a causal decision problem: from the same execution state, they compare outcomes with and without recovery, separate rescue from harm, and analyze how recovery value changes over time. They then propose the Causal Intervention Router (CIR), a lightweight policy that uses information available prior to recovery to decide when to intervene.

Result: On long-horizon ALFWorld tasks with Qwen3-14B, CIR improves success from 70.33% to 73.33% (+3.00 percentage points). It leaves all evaluated trajectories with correct observations untouched. Additional controls indicate the benefit cannot be attributed solely to the new observation returned by the environment.

Conclusion: Recovery should be evaluated causally and applied selectively rather than uniformly. The proposed CIR provides a practical means to assess and route recovery interventions, improving success without disturbing already-correct trajectories, and offering a general framework for judging when intervention is worthwhile.

Abstract: Large language model agents rely on external harnesses to pass information between the model and its environment and to recover from execution errors. Yet recovery is usually judged only by average task success. This hides an important tension. The same operation can rescue a failing trajectory or disrupt one that would otherwise succeed. We frame recovery as a causal decision problem. Starting from the same execution state, we compare what happens with and without recovery, separate rescue from harm, and study how the value of recovery changes over time. We then introduce the Causal Intervention Router (CIR), a lightweight policy that uses information available before recovery to decide when intervention is worthwhile. On long-horizon ALFWorld tasks with Qwen3-14B, CIR raises success from 70.33% to 73.33%, a gain of 3.00 percentage points. It leaves all evaluated trajectories with correct observations untouched. Additional controls show that the benefit of recovery cannot be explained solely by the new observation returned by the environment. These results provide a practical way to evaluate recovery and apply it selectively.

</details>


### [107] [Benchmarking Prompt Optimization of Large Language Models With Chess](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00416)
*Timothée Lesort,Alejandra López de Aberasturi Gómez,Tristan Karch,Tom Veniat,Philippe Modard,Karl Tuyls,Ludovic Denoyer*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a chess benchmark of 1,118 Lichess puzzles for studying automatic prompt optimization (APO) on frozen LLMs, evaluating six APO algorithms across eight models and linking puzzle gains to game-play rollouts, all for around $800.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluating advancing LLMs is increasingly difficult: benchmarks saturate, public test sets risk contamination, and harder tasks demand expensive grading or execution. These issues are amplified in APO, where evaluation is repeated throughout prompt search, necessitating a benchmark that is cheap and deterministic to score, hard enough to leave headroom, and renewable as models evolve.

Method: The authors build a chess benchmark from 1,118 Lichess puzzles to study APO for frozen LLMs (prompt optimization without weight updates). Chess provides exact-match scoring, engine-based evaluation of alternative moves, and a renewable supply of adjustable-difficulty problems. Beyond isolated test-item success, the benchmark connects puzzle-solving gains to short game-play rollouts in the same domain. Six APO algorithms are evaluated on eight target models, measuring baseline strength, responsiveness to optimization, and prompt transfer across models and to game play.

Result: The benchmark is challenging—the strongest evaluated model, Gemini 3.5 Flash (used as meta-model), solves only about 55% of puzzles—and discriminative, revealing gains, unchanged performance, and regressions across methods and models. It is renewable via fresh puzzles to reduce contamination and adjustable difficulty to maintain headroom, and affordable, with the complete study costing around $800.

Conclusion: Chess is a well-suited benchmark for APO, being challenging, discriminative, renewable, and affordable. The authors release the puzzles, optimization and evaluation code, and dataset-renewal scripts at https://github.com/imec-ailabs/Automatic-Prompt-Optimization-with-Chess.

Abstract: Evaluating large language models becomes increasingly challenging as their capabilities advance: benchmarks can saturate, public test sets risk contamination, and assessing harder tasks can require expensive grading or execution infrastructure. These challenges are amplified in automatic prompt optimization (APO), where evaluation is repeated throughout the search for better prompts. Studying APO therefore requires a benchmark that is cheap and deterministic to score, hard enough to leave room for improvement, and renewable as models evolve. We introduce a chess benchmark built from 1,118 Lichess puzzles to study APO for frozen LLMs: we optimize their prompts without updating their model weights. Chess combines inexpensive exact-match scoring, engine-based evaluation of alternative moves, and a renewable supply of problems with adjustable difficulty. Unlike evaluations that report only success on isolated test items, the benchmark also connects puzzle-solving gains to short game-play rollouts within the same domain. We use it to evaluate six APO algorithms on eight target models, measuring not only baseline strength but also how much each model responds to optimization and whether optimized prompts transfer across models and to game play. Chess is thus a well-suited benchmark for APO: it is (i) challenging, as even the strongest evaluated model, Gemini 3.5 Flash (used as the meta-model), solves only about 55\% of puzzles; (ii) discriminative, revealing gains, unchanged performance, and regressions across methods and models; (iii) renewable, with fresh puzzles to reduce contamination risk and adjustable difficulty to maintain headroom as models improve; and (iv) affordable, as the complete study runs for around \$800. We release the puzzles, optimization and evaluation code, and dataset-renewal scripts (https://github.com/imec-ailabs/Automatic-Prompt-Optimization-with-Chess).

</details>


### [108] [JevSpawn: Adaptive Agentic Inference through Compositional Action Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00437)
*Haoyang Su,Weiran Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: JevSpawn is a compositional policy that links natural language task specs to finite probabilistic exploration via parallel action spawning and feedback-driven branch selection, achieving better task performance and faster navigation without extra training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents generate reasoning/actions token by token, making extended interactions slow and computationally expensive. Jev-style models offer fast probabilistic predictions over finite fields but require those fields to be specified in advance, which limits autonomous task solving where actions must be derived from natural language and adapted through interaction.

Method: JevSpawn connects natural language task specifications to finite probabilistic exploration. It couples parallel action spawning with feedback-driven branch selection, representation revision, and recovery from retained alternatives. Shared action structure and model prefixes reduce repeated generation and context computation without additional training.

Result: Evaluations on eight benchmark tasks against seven agent baselines and a TypeSafe Jev variant show improved task performance and faster navigation.

Conclusion: JevSpawn is a promising approach to structured agentic inference.

Abstract: LLM agents generate intermediate reasoning and actions token by token, making extended interactions slow and computationally expensive. Jev-style models offer fast probabilistic predictions over finite fields, but require those fields to be specified in advance. This requirement limits autonomous task solving, where the available actions must be derived from natural language instructions and adapted through interaction. We introduce JevSpawn, a compositional policy that connects natural language task specifications to finite probabilistic exploration. Parallel action spawning is coupled with feedback driven branch selection, representation revision, and recovery from retained alternatives. Shared action structure and model prefixes reduce repeated generation and context computation without additional training. Evaluations on eight benchmark tasks against seven agent baselines and a TypeSafe Jev variant establish JevSpawn as a promising approach to structured agentic inference, with improved task performance and faster navigation.

</details>


### [109] [Frozen Scenes, Shifting Winners: Configuration Fragility in Text-to-3D Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00447)
*Anson Y. Lam,Shuqing Li,Michael R. Lyu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A leaderboard audit across 300 frozen text-to-3D scenes shows that render and caption protocol choices create score variance exceeding between-generator variance for 17/19 alignment evaluators, destabilizing point-estimate winners, while pairwise directions largely survive selection-aware inference and evaluators show limited sensitivity on layout scrambling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Text-to-3D rendered-image evaluation treats camera settings and caption wording as incidental, but these protocol choices may actually dominate measurement. The paper asks whether leaderboard conclusions can shift even when the generated scenes themselves are held fixed.

Method: The authors audit 300 frozen scenes from six generators, varying eight render and caption factors across 19 alignment evaluators plus one perceptual-quality control, and add four targeted scene degradations. They decompose variance, compare rankings versus scores, build pairwise protocol margin envelopes, apply simultaneous inference over the full search, and test tie-adjusted directional discrimination on layout scrambling.

Result: Peak configuration variance exceeds between-generator variance for 17/19 alignment evaluators, with prompt-bootstrap lower bounds above 1 for 11/19. Rankings are more stable than scores, but 18/19 evaluators change their point-estimate winner under some configuration. Pairwise margin envelopes preserve directions for selected pairs, yet no reversal survives simultaneous inference. No evaluator, including the prompt-free control, exceeds 67% tie-adjusted directional discrimination on layout scrambling.

Conclusion: Winner changes under different protocols are descriptive rather than confirmed changes in generator superiority. The audit separates score stability, decision uncertainty, and targeted sensitivity, and recommends reporting (generator, score, card ID) with protocol-dependent comparisons and selection-aware uncertainty.

Abstract: Can a text-to-3D leaderboard change when every generated scene stays fixed? We audit this question for rendered-image evaluation, where camera settings and caption wording become part of the measurement protocol. Across 300 frozen scenes from six generators, we vary eight render and caption factors for 19 alignment evaluators plus one perceptual-quality control, then test four targeted scene degradations. Peak configuration variance exceeds between-generator variance for 17/19 alignment evaluators, with prompt-bootstrap lower bounds above 1 for 11/19. Rankings are more stable than scores, yet 18/19 evaluators change their point-estimate winner under some configuration. Pairwise protocol margin envelopes show which comparisons keep their direction across the tested settings. Selected pairs have opposite pointwise intervals, but no reversal survives simultaneous inference over the full search. Thus the observed winner changes are descriptive, not confirmed changes in generator superiority. Sensitivity remains separate: no evaluator, even the prompt-free control, exceeds 67% tie-adjusted directional discrimination on layout scrambling, which is diagnostic rather than human-validated ground truth. The audit separates score stability, decision uncertainty, and targeted sensitivity, and recommends reporting (generator, score, card ID) with protocol-dependent comparisons and selection-aware uncertainty.

</details>


### [110] [Before Agents Decide: Epistemic Action in LLM-Based Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00511)
*Yizhi Liu,Balaji Padmanabhan,Siva Viswanathan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper argues that LLM-based agent design must attend to how decision-ready evidence is produced, introducing "epistemic actions" (acquiring, transforming, and probing evidence) and "epistemic scaffolding" (the interfaces, tools, and environments enabling and auditing these actions).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: People often act before a difficult decision simply to better understand the situation (turning an object, comparing alternatives, changing a condition), not to complete the task but to improve the evidence for the next choice. While LLM-based agents can search and explore, agent design gives little attention to an earlier question: is the available evidence ready for the decision? Evidence may be missing, present but hidden in form, or lacking the comparison needed to judge it.

Method: Conceptual/position argument. The paper imports the cognitive science notion of epistemic actions into LLM-based agents and distinguishes three modes: acquiring missing evidence, transforming available evidence, and probing a system to create a revealing response. It introduces the term "epistemic scaffolding" for the interfaces, tools, and environments that make these actions possible and auditable.

Result: The paper articulates a framework of three epistemic action modes and the concept of epistemic scaffolding, reframing the pre-decision problem as one of producing decision-ready evidence.

Conclusion: Agent design must address how decision-ready evidence is produced, positioning epistemic actions and scaffolding as core design concerns rather than incidental behaviors.

Abstract: Before a difficult decision, people often act simply to understand the situation better. We turn an object to see another side, place alternatives next to each other, or change one condition and observe what happens. These actions may not complete the task, but they improve the evidence needed for the next choice. LLM-based agents can search and explore, yet agent design gives less attention to an earlier question: is the available evidence ready for the decision? Sometimes necessary evidence is missing. In other cases, the evidence is present but its form hides what matters, or the comparison needed to judge it does not yet exist. Cognitive science calls actions that improve the basis for a later choice epistemic actions. We bring this idea to LLM-based agents and distinguish three modes: acquiring missing evidence, transforming available evidence, and probing a system to create a revealing response. We use the term epistemic scaffolding for the interfaces, tools, and environments that make these actions possible and auditable. This paper argues that agent design must address how decision-ready evidence is produced.

</details>


### [111] [Ontology-Based Contextual AI Evaluations (OB-CAIE) Methodology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00529)
*Julie Krugler Hollek,Michael Zargham,Mala Kumar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Ontology-Based Contextual AI Evaluation (OB-CAIE), a methodology using two ontologies—a Domain-Specific Ontology (what) and an Evaluation Process Ontology (how)—to rigorously define the testable problem space, balance human expertise with automation, and enable traceable, reproducible AI evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI evaluations suffer from a lack of scientific rigor due to unclear testing coverage, an inadequate balance between human expertise and automation, and non-reproducible testing environments.

Method: OB-CAIE defines the testable problem space with two ontologies: the Domain-Specific Ontology (DSO, the "what") and the Evaluation Process Ontology (EPO, the "how"). This problem space can support one or multiple AI evaluations and incorporates human judgment at specific, scientifically grounded points where human feedback is irreducible or machine-irreplaceable.

Result: The methodology addresses the first step of the scientific method by clearly specifying what will be tested, thereby strengthening evaluation rigor and supporting the reuse of a single problem space across multiple evaluations.

Conclusion: OB-CAIE enables traceable, visualizable, and analyzable failure points within a canonical problem space, improving reproducibility and the balance between human and automated evaluation.

Abstract: The ontology-based contextual AI evaluation (OB-CAIE) methodology was developed to address a lack of scientific rigor that arises from unclear testing coverage, to balance human expertise and automations, and to address a lack of reproducibility of AI evaluation testing environments. OB-CAIE strengthens the current state of AI evaluations by addressing the first step in the scientific method by clearly defining what will be tested. Two ontologies represent the tractable problem space in the OB-CAIE methodology: the Domain-Specific Ontology (DSO) and the Evaluation Process Ontology (EPO). The DSO is the what; the EPO is the how. An OB-CAIE problem space can be used for one or multiple AI evaluations. The OB-CAIE methodology allows for human judgment at specific points, in scientifically grounded ways, and in complex subject areas where human feedback is genuinely irreducible or machine irreplaceable. A key advantage of the OB-CAIE methodology is that failure points can be traced, visualized and analyzed within the canonical OB-CAIE methodology problem space.

</details>


### [112] [Science or Slop?: Benchmarking and Mitigating Scientific Slop in AI-Generated Papers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00531)
*Yerim Oh,Young-Jun Lee,Jaewoo Ahn,Gunhee Kim,Dongyeop Kang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces "scientific slop" as detectable global-reasoning failures in AI-generated papers, benchmarks them via SciSlopBench (390 AI papers paired with human-matched papers across six measures), and proposes SciSlopHarness to reduce slop with strict evidentiary grounding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI slop is spreading in academia, but slop in scientific papers has complex patterns that token-based AI detectors cannot catch. Each section looks plausible while the scientific reasoning linking them breaks down, misleading readers' assessment of the work. Existing detectors therefore miss a fundamental failure mode.

Method: The authors define scientific slop through six measures spanning Structure, Argument, and Artifacts, and build SciSlopBench: 390 AI-generated papers (mostly CS, plus life, social, and natural sciences), each paired with a human-written paper matched on research problem and contribution type. They then propose SciSlopHarness, a harness-level framework that guides a fixed LLM to revise slop only where experimental records support the change, avoiding human reference targets.

Result: The six measures identify the AI paper in each pair with 85.9% accuracy vs. 68.7% for Binoculars. Higher scientific slop correlates with lower ICLR ratings and distinguishes rejected from accepted papers above chance every year from 2017-2025. Direct slop-aware prompting triggers reward hacking and standard revisions leave residual slop, while SciSlopHarness cuts the remaining AI-human gap by 63% over the strongest revision baseline without human reference targets.

Conclusion: AI-generated scientific papers leave fundamental traces in their global reasoning, not just surface prose. Responsible mitigation requires strict evidentiary grounding rather than mere prose refinement.

Abstract: AI-generated content, often called AI slop, is increasingly common everywhere, particularly in academia. Slop in AI-generated scientific papers, however, has more complex patterns that cannot be easily detected by existing token-based AI detectors. Each part of such a paper looks plausible while the scientific reasoning that connects the parts breaks down, which can mislead how readers assess the work. We benchmark these failures as scientific slop through six measures across Structure, Argument, and Artifacts. We construct SciSlopBench with 390 AI-generated papers, mostly in computer science but spanning the life, social, and natural sciences, each paired with a human-written paper matched by research problem and contribution type. Our measures identify the AI paper in each pair with 85.9% accuracy, compared with 68.7% for Binoculars. Higher scientific slop accompanies lower ICLR ratings and distinguishes rejected from accepted papers above chance in every year from 2017 to 2025. Reducing these patterns, however, is not as simple as directly optimizing the measures. We therefore propose SciSlopHarness, a harness-level framework that guides a fixed LLM to revise slop only where the experiment records support the change. While standard revisions leave residual slop and direct slop-aware prompting triggers reward hacking, SciSlopHarness reduces the remaining AI-human gap by 63% over the strongest revision baseline without requiring human reference targets. Overall, we demonstrate that AI-generated scientific papers leave fundamental traces in their global reasoning, and that responsible mitigation demands strict evidentiary grounding rather than mere prose refinement.

</details>


### [113] [Worse Together: How Performance Breaks Down in Multi-User Multi-Agent Teams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00583)
*Sahan Paliskara,Nattaput Namchittai,Andrew Lampinen*

Main category: cs.AI
