TL;DR: This paper studies multi-user, multi-agent coordination across 5 frontier models and 77 scenarios in 4 environments, finding that teams of per-user agents consistently underperform a single coordinator agent, both with and without communication channels, and introduces MAMUBench to evaluate this setting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: People increasingly delegate tasks to AI agents that share resources (codebase, calendar, budget) with other users' agents. When agents act for different users with divergent goals, coordination often fails and the group performs worse than if a single agent had served everyone, motivating a systematic study of this multi-user, multi-agent setting.

Method: The authors evaluate five frontier models across 77 scenarios in four environments: an API key environment (shared compute budget), a clinic (shared calendar), a personal assistant environment (shared group order or booking), and a merge queue (shared release cutoff). For each scenario, they compare a single coordinator agent serving all users against a team where each agent serves one user, with and without an inter-agent communication channel, and analyze behavioral failure modes and mitigations.

Result: Teams deliver worse group outcomes than the coordinator in every environment. Without a communication channel, teams completely collapse in two environments; even with one, coordination overhead creates substantial gaps—for example, in the personal assistant environment the coordinator fulfills a targeted user request about twice as often as teams. Failure behaviors include stalling as teams grow, overriding each other's actions, and fabricating claims.

Conclusion: Effective but environment-specific mitigations were identified, including a team lead, explicit procedural instructions, and a platform check forcing agents to read peers' messages before committing. The authors will release the API key, clinic, and personal assistant environments as MAMUBench, comprising 74 scenarios for evaluating multi-user, multi-agent coordination.

Abstract: People are increasingly delegating tasks to AI agents, and those agents are increasingly encountering other people's agents over shared resources such as a codebase, a calendar, or a budget. When each agent acts for a different user with different goals, coordination often fails, and the group ends up worse off than if a single agent had acted for everyone. We study this multi-user, multi-agent setting across five frontier models and 77 scenarios in four environments: an API key environment in which agents share a compute budget, a clinic in which they share a calendar, a personal assistant environment in which they share a group order or booking, and a merge queue in which they share a release cutoff. In each scenario, we compare a single agent that serves every user (a coordinator) to a team in which each agent serves one user, with and without a communication channel between the agents. Teams deliver worse group outcomes than the coordinator in every environment: without a channel, they completely collapse in two environments, and even with one, coordination overhead creates substantial gaps. For example, in the personal assistant environment, the coordinator fulfills a targeted user request about twice as often as teams. We identify distinct behaviors associated with this poor group-level performance, including stalling as teams grow, overriding each other's actions, and fabricating claims. We find effective but environment-specific mitigations, such as a team lead, explicit procedural instructions, and a platform check that makes an agent read its peers' messages before committing. We will release the API key, clinic, and personal assistant environments as MAMUBench, comprising 74 scenarios for evaluating multi-user, multi-agent coordination.

</details>


### [114] [Legal Research Bench: Measuring End-to-End Reliability in Long-Horizon Legal Research Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00609)
*Katrina Drozdov,Oliver Chen,Langston Nashold,Rayan Krishnan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Legal Research Bench (LRB), a benchmark of 413 expert-written U.S. legal research questions with gold answers, supporting authorities, and binary rubrics, to measure the reliability of LLM agents on retrieval-intensive legal research tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Legal research is a core, time-consuming workflow requiring identification of controlling authority, validity verification, reconciliation of statutes and cases, and grounded synthesis. LLM agents suit this retrieval-intensive task, but their value hinges on reliability: a single missing authority, stale citation, or wrong conclusion can render an otherwise plausible answer unusable. Existing evaluations do not capture this strict reliability requirement.

Method: The authors build LRB, comprising 413 open-ended U.S. legal research questions authored by experts, each paired with a gold answer, supporting authorities, and a binary grading rubric. Thirteen frontier models are evaluated in a harness equipped with web search, case-law search, page parsing, and retrieval tools. Responses are scored via all-pass grading with source verification, requiring every rubric criterion to be met and cited authorities to verify. The LLM judge is validated against expert attorneys to confirm scores track attorney judgment.

Result: Agents remain far from reliable: the strongest model tested, Claude Opus 4.8, is fully correct on only 42.9% of questions. All-pass rates vary substantially across areas of law and are lower on questions requiring reconciliation of conflicting authorities. Across models, more turns, tool calls, and inference cost do not predict higher accuracy.

Conclusion: Frontier LLM agents fall well short of the reliability legal research demands, with accuracy dependent on task setting rather than compute or interaction volume. LRB provides a source-verified, attorney-validated benchmark for measuring progress on this workflow.

Abstract: Legal research is a core and time-consuming legal workflow. Lawyers must identify controlling authority, verify that it remains valid, reconcile statutes and cases, and synthesize a grounded answer. Language model agents are a natural fit for this retrieval-intensive workflow, and automating even part of it would be valuable. But that value depends on reliability: a single missing authority, stale citation, or wrong legal conclusion can make an otherwise plausible answer unusable. We introduce \textbf{Legal Research Bench} (LRB), a benchmark of 413 open-ended U.S. legal research questions written by experts, each paired with a gold answer, supporting authorities, and a binary grading rubric. We evaluate thirteen frontier models in a harness with web search, case-law search, page parsing, and retrieval tools. We score agent responses through all-pass grading with source verification, where a response is correct only if every required criterion is satisfied and its cited authorities verify. We also validate the LLM judge against expert attorneys ensuring that benchmark scores track attorney judgment. Agents remain far from reliable: among the models we tested, the strongest, Claude Opus 4.8, is fully correct on 42.9\% of questions. Performance also varies substantially by task setting: all-pass rates differ across areas of law and are lower on questions requiring reconciliation of conflicting authorities. Across models, more turns, tool calls, and inference cost do not predict higher accuracy.

</details>


### [115] [Spatial Strategies, Not Actions: Vector-Quantized Geodesics as Tools for LLM-Driven Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00613)
*Gabriel Turinici*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes an LLM-agent architecture for grid-world navigation that separates learning into two levels: unsupervised vector quantization of geodesic trajectories to discover reusable "tools," each annotated offline with a natural-language behavioral description, and online LLM-based tool selection. Using an open vision-language model (Qwen3.6-35B-A3B), a fast non-reasoning configuration matches the goal-reaching rate of a much costlier chain-of-thought variant while reducing per-decision latency from minutes to seconds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based agents are criticized for lacking spatial understanding and mainly exploiting statistical text patterns. The authors aim to investigate whether LLM agents can achieve genuine spatial comprehension when geometry-derived tools are combined with an LLM acting as a high-level orchestrator, rather than relying on raw statistical text priors.

Method: An architecture combines geometrical tools with an LLM serving as a high-level orchestrator in grid-world environments. The agent first collects geodesic trajectories, which are vector-quantized to extract a representative subset. Offline, the LLM associates a natural-language description of the underlying behavioral patterns with each selected trajectory, turning it into a tool. Online, the LLM selects the appropriate tool conditioned on the current state and goal. Low-level control is handled by primitive actions that execute the trajectory associated with the chosen tool. This separates agentic learning into two levels: tool discovery via unsupervised quantization of trajectories, and reasoning/decision-making by the LLM. Additional agent-centered zoom and collision-detection tools are paired with the geometry-derived tool library.

Result: Evaluated in a partially observable, dynamic 2D grid environment with an open vision-language model (Qwen3.6-35B-A3B). Pairing the geometry-derived tool library with an agent-centered zoom tool and a collision detection tool enables a fast, non-reasoning configuration to match the goal-reaching rate of a much more costly chain-of-thought version, while cutting the cost of a decision from minutes to seconds.

Conclusion: Combining geometry-derived spatial tools with an LLM orchestrator gives LLM agents spatial competence and efficient decision-making. The two-level separation—unsupervised trajectory quantization for tool discovery and LLM reasoning for selection—lets a fast non-reasoning configuration attain the goal-reaching performance of expensive chain-of-thought reasoning at a fraction of the decision cost.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) based agents are often criticized for lacking spatial understanding and mainly exploiting statistical text patterns. We investigate their spatial comprehension through an architecture combining geometrical tools with a LLM serving as a high-level orchestrator in grid-world environments. The agent first collects geodesic trajectories, which are then vector-quantized to extract a representative subset. Offline, the LLM associates a natural language description of the underlying behavioral patterns to each selected trajectory, making it a tool. Online, the LLM chooses the appropriate tool conditioned on the current state and goal. Low-level control is handled by primitive actions that execute the trajectory associated with the tool. From an agentic AI perspective, this approach separates learning into two levels: tool discovery is handled through unsupervised quantization of trajectories, while reasoning and decision-making are handled by the LLM. We test the approach in a partially observable dynamic 2D grid environment with an open vision-language model (Qwen3.6-35B-A3B). Pairing the geometry-derived tool library with an agent-centered zoom tool and a collision detection tool lets a fast, non-reasoning configuration match the goal-reaching rate of a much more costly chain-of-thought version, while cutting the cost of a decision from minutes to seconds.

</details>


### [116] [CompMat-Bench: Benchmarking AI Agents for Computational Materials Science](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00636)
*Chenmu Zhang,Levi Felix,Jun-Jie Zhang,Xingfu Li,Xuelian Jiang,Tao Jiang,Subhendu Mishra,Xixi Qin,Boris Yakobson*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CompMat-Bench is a benchmark of 94 tasks derived from published computational materials studies that evaluates AI agents on preparing inputs and analyzing outputs for expensive simulations, avoiding the need to rerun simulations during evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluating AI agents on scientific research tasks is limited by the time and resources required for the underlying experiments or calculations; in computational materials research, repeating expensive simulations across agents and trials makes evaluation impractical.

Method: The authors reproduce research steps in advance and assess agents on preparing simulation inputs and analyzing outputs, using the reproduced inputs and results as ground truth for fixed-rule grading without an LLM judge. The benchmark supports four evaluation conditions: single tasks and workflows of related tasks, each with full or reduced methodological guidance.

Result: With full guidance on single tasks, agents based on three LLMs complete individual materials research steps with pass rates of 66.0-90.4% across 94 tasks. Longer workflows and reduced guidance limit performance differently: they lower pass rates of weaker agents, whereas the strongest agent falls only when a long workflow is combined with reduced guidance. Failure analysis attributes most failures to scientific errors rather than software usage errors.

Conclusion: CompMat-Bench provides a basis for comparing agents on the steps of real materials research and for analyzing agent failure modes.

Abstract: Evaluating AI agents on scientific research tasks is constrained by the time and resources required for the underlying experiments or calculations. In computational materials research, repeating the same expensive simulations across agents and trials can make evaluation impractical. We introduce CompMat-Bench, a benchmark of 94 tasks derived from recently published computational materials studies, each asking agents to complete a step toward achieving the study's scientific goal. We reproduce the research steps in advance and assess agents on preparing inputs and analyzing outputs for expensive simulations, so expensive simulations can be avoided during evaluation. The reproduced inputs and results serve as ground truth for grading agents with fixed rules, without an LLM judge. The benchmark supports four evaluation conditions: single tasks and workflows composed of related tasks, each with full or reduced methodological guidance. With full guidance on single tasks, agents based on three LLMs demonstrate the ability to complete individual materials research steps, with pass rates of 66.0-90.4% across 94 tasks. Both longer workflows and reduced guidance can limit agent performance, but in different ways for different agents: they lower the pass rates of the weaker agents, whereas the strongest agent falls only when a long workflow is combined with reduced guidance. Failure analysis attributes most failures to scientific errors rather than to errors in software usage. CompMat-Bench provides a basis for comparing agents on the steps of real materials research and for analyzing agent failure modes.

</details>


### [117] [Incident-Arena: Getting agents to the last nine of reliability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00648)
*Andre Fu,Malik Drabla,Leon Liu,Meji Abidoye,Marek Suppa,Lata Mishra,Adnan El Assadi,Yiyuan Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Incident-Arena is a human-built benchmark of 20 tasks for agentic site-reliability-engineering, deploying production open source apps on ephemeral Kubernetes clusters with injected faults and sustained load, paired with functional verifiers instead of static checks; frontier models score below 64.3%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI coding agents are widely used in engineering workflows, yet their ability to handle production incident response has received relatively little attention. Existing benchmarks in this emerging agentic SRE field are limited by unrealistic environments (typically toy repositories), non-standard framework implementations, and simple static verifiers.

Method: The authors introduce Incident-Arena, a human-built benchmark of 20 carefully selected tasks grounded in real-world deployed open source software. Each task deploys a production application to an ephemeral Kubernetes cluster, injects a fault from the config layer through underlying images, and applies a sustained load profile based on task requirements. They also propose a novel verification method that moves beyond static checks to functional verifiers, holding system-level metrics stable while ensuring repairs are performed safely.

Result: Agent trials run an average of 2.81M tokens and 41 turns, exceeding existing benchmarks and demonstrating agentic long-horizon reasoning. Across 20 tasks and 3 application substrates, frontier models score below 64.3%. Failures span diagnosis/localization errors, incomplete repairs, and unsafe regressions.

Conclusion: Incident-Arena establishes a more realistic and demanding benchmark for agentic SRE, combining production-grade environments, injected faults, sustained load, and functional verification. It reveals that current frontier models remain far from reliable at long-horizon production incident response, with substantial room for improvement in diagnosis, repair completeness, and safety.

Abstract: AI coding agents are ubiquitous in engineering workflows amongst industry and academia. Yet, despite their use in app coding, relatively less attention has been paid to their ability to execute on production incident response. This emerging field, termed agentic site-reliability-engineering (SRE) contains benchmarks limited by (1) unrealistic environments, typically toy repositories (2) non-standard framework implementations and (3) simple static verifiers. We introduce Incident-Arena, a human-built benchmark of 20 carefully selected tasks grounded in real-world deployed open source software. Each task deploys a production application to an ephemeral Kubernetes cluster, injecting a fault from the config layer through underlying images, and a sustained load profile given the task requirements. We also present a novel verification method, going beyond static checks to functional verifiers, holding systems level metrics stable, while ensuring repairs are done safely. Agent trials run an average of 2.81M tokens and 41 turns, going beyond existing benchmarks, demonstrating agentic long horizon reasoning. Across 20 tasks and 3 application substrates, frontier models score below 64.3%, with failures extending from diagnosis/localization errors, through incomplete repairs and unsafe regressions.

</details>


### [118] [Agent Evaluation Reliability: More Tasks Won't (Always) Fix An Agent Leaderboard](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00651)
*Michael Hardy,Ruhana Azam,Anka Reuel,Mykel Kochenderfer,Sanmi Koyejo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Agent leaderboard rankings conflate model and evaluation-condition effects, so reliability is claim-dependent; a Bayesian variance-decomposition framework on 22 benchmarks shows fixed model-scaffold systems rank reliably (0.935-0.994) but underlying models much less so (0.148-0.841), with scaffold coverage a dominant limit that pooling diverse benchmarks can mitigate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agent evaluations inform deployment decisions, but observed ranks reflect not only the model but also evaluation conditions like scaffolds and tasks. This makes reliability dependent on the claim being made: an evaluation that reliably ranks deployed systems may not reliably rank underlying models. The paper asks which conclusions current agent evaluations reliably support and what additional evaluation would improve them.

Method: A Bayesian variance-decomposition framework for sparse, imbalanced agent leaderboards that separates signal (performance differences relevant to the intended claim) from noise (irrelevant variation that can still change rankings). It is applied to 22 benchmarks from the Holistic Agent Leaderboard and Harbor Index, including inter-scaffold reliability analysis measuring whether scaffolds preserve model rankings.

Result: (1) Reliability depends on the measurement goal: fixed model-scaffold systems rank reliably (0.935-0.994), while underlying-model reliability is substantially lower (0.148-0.841). (2) Scaffold choice can change conclusions, with scaffold effects varying substantially across evaluations. (3) More tasks cannot resolve all uncertainty: even infinitely many similarly constructed tasks improve model-ranking reliability by at most 0.097 when uncertainty is dominated by limited scaffold coverage. (4) Pooling diverse benchmarks can improve cross-task rankings at lower cost, raising projected reliability from 0.44 to 0.75 at the same task budget and reducing projected cost by up to 83%.

Conclusion: Evaluation design should follow the intended claim: identify what a score or ranking should mean, diagnose what limits its reliability, and spend evaluation budget on the sources of uncertainty that matter.

Abstract: Agent evaluations are increasingly used to compare LLMs and inform deployment decisions, yet ranks can reflect not only the model but also the effects of the evaluation conditions such as the scaffolds or tasks. This makes reliability claim-dependent: an evaluation that reliably ranks deployed systems may not reliably rank underlying models. We ask which conclusions current agent evaluations reliably support and what additional evaluation would improve them. We develop a Bayesian variance-decomposition framework for sparse, imbalanced agent leaderboards and apply it to 22 benchmarks from the Holistic Agent Leaderboard and Harbor Index. The framework separates signal, performance differences relevant to the intended claim, from noise, irrelevant variation that can still change rankings. We find: (1) Reliability depends on the measurement goal. Fixed model-scaffold systems are ranked reliably (0.935-0.994), while underlying-model reliability is substantially lower (0.148-0.841). (2) Scaffold choice can change conclusions. Inter-scaffold reliability measures whether scaffolds preserve model rankings, showing that scaffold effects vary substantially across evaluations. (3) More tasks cannot resolve all uncertainty. Even infinitely many similarly constructed tasks improve model-ranking reliability of a benchmark by at most 0.097 when uncertainty is dominated by limited scaffold coverage. (4) Pooling diverse benchmarks can improve cross-task rankings at lower cost. For rankings across diverse agentic tasks, pooling benchmarks raises projected reliability from 0.44 to 0.75 at the same task budget and can reduce projected cost by up to 83\%. Evaluation design should follow the intended claim: identify what a score or ranking should mean, diagnose what limits its reliability, and spend evaluation budget on the sources of uncertainty that matter.

</details>


### [119] [When More Data Is Not Enough: The Context-Sufficiency Frontier in Generative AI Personalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00654)
*Merieme Askour,Ayoub Merimi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces "context sufficiency" theory, arguing that for generative AI personalization, the relevance of provider-supplied context to the customer's current intent matters more than its volume, and proposes the Context-Sufficiency Frontier to identify the minimal relevant context set.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional personalization relies on customer evidence (historical behavior and preferences). Generative AI now lets providers inject changing situational information at response time without pre-encoding every condition, but this ease of supplying context creates a new risk: more context is not necessarily better, and determining how much context is actually needed becomes a central problem.

Method: The authors develop a theory of context sufficiency distinguishing provider-side context ("what is possible, permitted, or advisable now") from customer evidence, define four states (insufficiency, sufficiency, saturation, interference), introduce the Context-Sufficiency Frontier to locate the minimal relevant set, and test the theory via a full-factorial experiment with a generative recommender at a large home-furnishing retailer.

Result: Relevant context improved response appropriateness, whereas irrelevant context reduced appropriateness and destabilized retrieval, confirming that relevance rather than volume drives personalization quality.

Conclusion: The framework reframes personalization away from supplying more context and toward identifying what the current interaction actually requires, enforcing constraints throughout the service process.

Abstract: Personalization has long relied on customer data to infer what an individual is likely to value. We call this customer evidence: the customer's historical behavior and preferences. Generative AI extends personalization by allowing providers to supply changing situational information at the moment a response is produced, without encoding every condition in advance. We define this provider-side context as information about what is possible, permitted, or advisable now. This flexibility creates a new problem: once context becomes easy to supply, more is not necessarily better. We develop a theory of context sufficiency in which the relevance of context to the customer's current intent matters more than its volume. The theory identifies four states, insufficiency, sufficiency, saturation, and interference, and introduces the Context-Sufficiency Frontier to locate the minimal relevant set. In a full-factorial experiment with a generative recommender at a large home-furnishing retailer, relevant context improved appropriateness, while irrelevant context reduced it and destabilized retrieval. The framework shifts personalization from supplying more context toward identifying what the current interaction actually requires and enforcing constraints throughout the service process.

</details>


### [120] [Backdoor Containment via Expert Quarantine and Shutdown in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00663)
*Jianwei Li,Min-Seon Kim,Jung-Eun Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Quarantined Expert Shutdown (QES), a new "learn, but channel" backdoor-containment strategy for LLMs that routes trigger-conditioned behavior into a designated quarantined expert (via routed LoRA branches and routers in a MoE-like setup), then disables it at deployment by zeroing its routing weight — cutting ASR from 100% to 0-10% on most settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Backdoored LLMs behave normally on benign inputs but emit attacker-specified outputs under hidden triggers. Existing defenses (across prior-training, in-training, post-training, and inference-time stages) rely on only two strategies: suppress backdoor learning, or learn-then-purify. Both have limitations, motivating a third unexplored regime for containment.

Method: QES operates in a regularization-steered MoE-like setting. Given a poisoned dataset, it augments a Transformer-based LM with routed expert-specific LoRA branches and lightweight routers, using auxiliary routing objectives to attract trigger-conditioned behavior into a designated expert while preserving benign capability elsewhere. Deployment mitigation is a single constant-time operation: zeroing the quarantined expert's routing weight, with no trigger screening or weight updates.

Result: Across two tasks, three attacks, and four model families, QES reduces attack success rate (ASR) from 100% to 0-10% on most settings, while downstream utility is often preserved or only modestly affected.

Conclusion: The results establish "learn, but channel" as a previously unexplored regime for backdoor containment in generative LLMs, offering a computationally efficient containment strategy.

Abstract: Backdoored large language models (LLMs) can behave normally on benign inputs while producing attacker-specified outputs under hidden triggers. Existing defenses span four stages--prior-training, in-training, post-training, and inference-time--and share one of two underlying strategies: either suppress backdoor learning (by filtering poisoned data or interrupting its acquisition during optimization) or learn, then purify (by repairing model weights or gating inputs after a fully backdoored model has formed). We propose a third strategy, learn, but channel: allow backdoor formation during training but route it into a designated, quarantined component that can be disabled at deployment. To this end, we propose Quarantined Expert Shutdown QES, a computationally efficient containment strategy built in a regularization-steered MoE-like setting. Specifically, given a poisoned dataset, QES augments a Transformer-based language model with routed expert-specific LoRA branches and lightweight routers, and uses auxiliary routing objectives to attract trigger-conditioned behavior into a designated expert while preserving benign capability elsewhere. At deployment, mitigation reduces to a single constant-time operation: zeroing the quarantined expert's routing weight, without trigger screening or further updating model weights. Empirically, our methods reduce the attack success rate ASR from 100% to 0-10% on most settings across two tasks, three attacks, and four model families, while downstream utility is often preserved or only modestly affected. These results establish learn, but channel as a previously unexplored regime for backdoor containment in generative LLMs.

</details>


### [121] [A Simple Doxastic Deontic Logic for Norm-Guided Decision Making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00668)
*Thorsten Engesser,Agata Ciabattoni*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a doxastic deontic logic (a classically reducible fragment of Chellas' Minimal Deontic Logic with explicit conditional norms plus multi-agent KD45) and defines the Doxastic Norm Compliance Optimization Problem, giving conditions for subjective/objective optimization to coincide and for reduction to weighted partial MaxSAT in polynomial time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous systems must make decisions despite conflicting norms and incomplete or unreliable information, motivating a formalism that captures norms depending on agents' beliefs about both facts and norms.

Method: A simple doxastic deontic logic built as a classically reducible fragment of Chellas' Minimal Deontic Logic, extended with explicit conditional norms and combined with multi-agent KD45. On this logic, the Doxastic Norm Compliance Optimization Problem is defined, where an agent selects a decision minimizing weighted norm violations; subjective optimization (relative to beliefs) is distinguished from objective optimization (relative to actual facts).

Result: Conditions are given under which (i) subjective and objective optimization coincide and (ii) optimal decision-making can be reduced to weighted partial MaxSAT in polynomial time.

Conclusion: The proposed doxastic deontic logic provides a tractable framework for norm-compliant decision-making under uncertainty, bridging belief-dependent normative reasoning with efficient optimization.

Abstract: Making decisions despite conflicting norms and incomplete or unreliable information is a fundamental challenge for autonomous systems. We introduce a simple doxastic deontic logic for this setting: a classically reducible fragment of Chellas' Minimal Deontic Logic, extended with explicit conditional norms and combined with multi-agent KD45, so that norms can depend on agents' beliefs about both facts and norms. On this logic we define the Doxastic Norm Compliance Optimization Problem, where an agent chooses a decision minimizing weighted norm violations. We distinguish subjective optimization (relative to the agent's beliefs) from objective optimization (relative to the actual facts). We give conditions under which (i) the two coincide and (ii) optimal decision-making can be reduced to weighted partial MaxSAT in polynomial time.

</details>


### [122] [Ontology-Grounded, Reasoner-Verified Benchmarks for Evaluating LLM Reasoning in Scientific AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00682)
*Nishtha N. Vaidya,Stephan Grimm,Thomas Hubauer,Thomas A. Runkler*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes an automated pipeline that generates ontology-grounded multiple-choice question benchmarks from OWL 2 ontologies, with answers formally verified by a reasoner, and evaluates it on three ontologies and six LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs underpin scientific AI applications requiring structured knowledge reasoning, but their logical reasoning is often flawed, producing factual inaccuracies unacceptable in such settings. Reliable evaluation is difficult: manual dataset construction scales poorly, and LLM-based generation risks embedding the flaws it should measure. High-quality benchmarks must ground both correct and incorrect examples in explicit background knowledge that a standard reasoner can formally verify.

Method: The authors build a pipeline that automatically generates ontology-grounded MCQs from any sufficiently axiomatised OWL 2 ontology. Correct answers are grounded in the ontology by design. Distractors are created by perturbing the right-hand-side class expressions of class definition axioms, and their incorrectness is formally verified by an OWL reasoner via entailment checks. Generated distractors span four semantic categories, from class unsatisfiability to weakened subsumptions, enabling diagnostic evaluation of specific reasoning failures.

Result: The pipeline was evaluated on three ontologies: Pizza (small, academic), PMDco (complex, materials science), and DOID (large, biomedical), producing 112, 2,491, and 15,216 MCQs respectively. Items meet natural language quality standards with mean LLM judge scores of 4.02, 4.36, and 3.36 out of 5, and correct-answer-to-distractor similarity above 0.8 shows wrong options cannot be dismissed by surface form alone. Six LLMs evaluated zero-shot achieve 41.1-76.8% accuracy, well above the 25% random-guessing baseline, indicating the benchmarks are challenging and discriminative.

Conclusion: The work represents a step towards more reliable benchmarks for assessing logical reasoning in scientific AI.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) increasingly underpin scientific AI applications that reason over structured knowledge, from biomedical question answering to materials informatics. However, their logical reasoning often falls short, producing factual inaccuracies unacceptable in these settings. Reliable evaluation remains challenging: manual dataset construction scales poorly, and LLM-based generation risks embedding the very flaws it aims to measure. High-quality benchmarks must ground both correct and incorrect labelled examples in explicit background knowledge, formally verifiable by a standard reasoner. We propose a pipeline that automatically generates ontology-grounded multiple-choice question (MCQ) benchmarks from any sufficiently axiomatised OWL 2 ontology, with correct answers grounded in the ontology by design. Distractors are generated by perturbing the right-hand-side class expressions of class definition axioms, and their incorrectness is formally verified by an OWL reasoner via entailment checks. We evaluate the pipeline on three ontologies: Pizza (small, academic), PMDco (complex, materials science), and DOID (large, biomedical), generating 112, 2,491, and 15,216 MCQs respectively. Distractors span four semantic categories from class unsatisfiability to weakened subsumptions, enabling diagnostic evaluation of specific reasoning failures. Items meet natural language quality standards: mean LLM judge scores of 4.02, 4.36, and 3.36 out of 5 confirm fluency, and correct-answer-to-distractor similarity above 0.8 shows that wrong options cannot be dismissed on surface form alone. Six LLMs evaluated zero-shot achieve 41.1-76.8% accuracy, well above the 25% random-guessing baseline, indicating the benchmarks are challenging and discriminative. This work is a step towards more reliable benchmarks for assessing logical reasoning in scientific AI.

</details>


### [123] [Backdoor Purification for LoRA-Tuned LLMs via Null-Space Projection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00685)
*Jianwei Li,Jung-Eun Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a backdoor purification method for LoRA-tuned LLMs that removes backdoors by projecting LoRA updates onto orthogonal null spaces, reducing attack success rates from ~100% to <10% without trigger knowledge, clean references, or retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Backdoor attacks on LLMs and PEFT methods like LoRA are increasingly severe, but existing purification methods rely on strong assumptions (prior trigger knowledge, clean reference data, or aggressive retraining) and lack thorough evaluation, limiting real-world applicability.

Method: The approach purifies LoRA-tuned LLMs without those assumptions and without post-hoc retraining of suspect parameters. It uses careful data curation and feature approximation to extract high-fidelity backdoor directions, then constructs orthogonal null spaces in both input and output channels for each layer or head, projecting LoRA updates onto them. The method is scaled from a single layer in text classification to a full-parameter LLM in generative tasks via ablation studies.

Result: The null-space projection method reduces attack success rate from nearly 100% to less than 10%, while preserving the base model's benign general capabilities and the adapter's learned downstream task abilities.

Conclusion: The proposed null-space projection offers an effective, assumption-free backdoor purification for LoRA-tuned LLMs, achieving substantial ASR reduction while maintaining both base-model performance and downstream adaptation quality.

Abstract: With the rapid adoption of large language models (LLMs) and parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) methods, the risk of backdoor attacks has become more severe. Existing backdoor purification methods typically rely on at least one of the strong assumptions, such as prior knowledge of triggers, access to clean references, or aggressive retraining, and they often lack comprehensive evaluations. These constraints substantially limit their practical applicability. To overcome these challenges, our work proposes purifying LoRA-tuned LLMs without these assumptions and even without post-hoc retraining of the suspect parameters. Our objective is to significantly reduce the attack success rates (ASR) while preserving both (i) the base model's general capabilities and (ii) the new downstream skills learned through the adapter. Through a series of ablation studies, we progressively scale our approach from a single layer in a text classification setting to a full-parameter LLM in the generative task. Through careful data curation and feature approximation, we extract high-fidelity backdoor directions and, for each layer or head, construct orthogonal null spaces in both the input and output channels, onto which the LoRA updates are projected. Empirically, our null-space projection method reduces the ASR from nearly 100% to less than 10%, while preserving the base model's benign performance and the adapter's learned abilities during downstream task adaptation.

</details>


### [124] [R-GroundBench: A Diagnostic Benchmark for R-Group Groundingin Markush Molecular Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00700)
*Xin Wang,Zichuan Ying,Xinna Lin,Junqi Zhang,Hanyi Xiong,Tianyu Gao,Hairong Zhang,Qixiang Hua,Botian Shi,Zhenhailong Wang,Kaicheng Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: R-GroundBench is a diagnostic benchmark of real patent Markush structures with VQA and generation tracks, revealing that current models recognize but fail to ground R-groups, especially on hard and visually-dependent tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI for scientific discovery is advancing molecular understanding and design, but reasoning over incomplete chemical representations is poorly understood. Markush structures encode molecular families via variable R-group placeholders (R1, R2, X, etc.) and are ubiquitous in pharmaceutical patents, requiring grounding across molecular, textual, and chemical information. Existing molecule-language benchmarks focus on fully specified molecules, leaving R-group grounding largely unevaluated.

Method: The authors introduce R-GroundBench, a diagnostic benchmark built from real patent Markush structures. It comprises a Multiple-Choice (VQA) track with controlled difficulty and modality splits, plus an open-ended Generation track. Evaluation focuses on distinguishing recognition from molecular grounding and on controlling shortcuts.

Result: A substantial gap emerges between recognition and molecular grounding. Models exceed 90% accuracy on Easy VQA but drop to 56-66% on Hard VQA when shortcuts are controlled. Chemical-domain VLMs remain unreliable, reaching only 25.7-46.2% on Hard VQA despite domain-specific pretraining. Generation Exact Match stays below 20% for most models and below 8% when visual input is required.

Conclusion: Current AI systems lack reliable grounding and execution for Markush editing, highlighting key challenges for AI-driven scientific discovery.

Abstract: Recent advances in AI for scientific discovery enable molecular understandingand design, yet reasoning over incomplete chemical representations remainsunclear.Markush structures, which encode molecular families through variable R-groupplaceholders (\textit{R\textsubscript{1}}, \textit{R\textsubscript{2}}, \textit{X}, etc.), are ubiquitous in pharmaceutical patents and requiregrounding across molecular, textual, and chemical information.However, existing molecule-language benchmarks focus on fully specifiedmolecules, leaving R-group grounding largely unevaluated.We introduce R-GroundBench:, a diagnostic benchmark built from real patent Markushstructures, featuring a Multiple-Choice (VQA) track with controlled difficultyand modality splits, and an open-ended Generation track.Our results reveal a substantial gap between recognition andmolecular grounding.While models achieve over 90\% accuracy on Easy VQA, performance drops to56--66\% on Hard VQA when shortcuts are controlled.Chemical-domain VLMs also remain unreliable, achieving only 25.7--46.2\% on HardVQA despite domain-specific pretraining.Moreover, Generation Exact Match remains below 20\% for most models and below8\% when visual input is required.These findings reveal that current AI systems lack reliable grounding andexecution for Markush editing, highlighting challenges for AI-drivenscientific discovery.

</details>


### [125] [Meta-Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning for Fast Adaptation of Interactive Policies with Applications to Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00705)
*Huiwen Yan,Kyriakos G. Vamvoudakis,Mushuang Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a meta-multi-agent reinforcement learning (meta-MARL) framework that models multi-agent problems as Markov games, introduces the meta-NE solution concept, and shows faster adaptive policy learning than pretrained MARL baselines on autonomous-driving tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing meta-reinforcement learning (meta-RL) methods generally focus only on single-agent systems. Extending them to multi-agent systems is difficult because tasks depend not only on the environment but also on agents' strategic interactions, so rapid interactive policy adaptation across related multi-agent tasks remains an open challenge.

Method: The authors model multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) problems as Markov games (MGs) and develop a meta-MARL framework for rapid interactive policy adaptation across a distribution of MGs, based on a bi-level optimization mechanism. They define a new solution concept, meta-NE, to characterize the desired equilibrium in meta-MARL, and establish sufficient conditions under which meta-NE is equivalent to a stationary point of the gradient-play-based meta-MARL algorithm.

Result: Evaluations on autonomous-driving tasks show that the proposed meta-MARL method achieves faster adaptation than pretrained MARL baselines, validating the effectiveness of the framework.

Conclusion: The work extends meta-RL to multi-agent settings through the meta-MARL framework and the meta-NE concept, providing theoretical grounding via the equivalence with stationary points of gradient-play-based meta-MARL and demonstrating faster interactive policy adaptation in autonomous-driving tasks.

Abstract: This paper develops a meta-multi-agent reinforcement learning (meta-MARL) framework to enable fast adaptation of interactive policies in a multi-agent system (MAS). Meta-reinforcement learning (meta-RL) enables agents to rapidly adapt to new tasks/environments using a bi-level optimization mechanism. However, existing meta-RL generally focuses on single-agent systems. Extending these frameworks and algorithms to multi-agent systems poses additional challenges, as tasks are characterized by not only the environment but also agents' strategic interactions. To address these challenges, we model multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) problems as Markov games (MGs) and develop a meta-MARL framework for rapid interactive policy adaptation across a distribution of MGs. A new concept, called meta-NE, is defined to describe the desired solution concept in a meta-MARL problem. Sufficient conditions for the equivalence between a meta-NE and a stationary point of the gradient-play-based meta-MARL algorithm are established. Our evaluation on autonomous-driving tasks demonstrates that the proposed meta-MARL method achieves faster adaptation than pretrained MARL baselines, validating the effectiveness of our framework.

</details>


### [126] [ReLiveGym: Evaluating Long-Lived Agents over Weeks of Replayed Reality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00710)
*Xisen Jin,Jingheng Li,Zhenglun Chen,Junyi Du,Xiang Ren*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces ReLiveGym, a diagnostic evaluation environment for long-lived LLM agents that act sparsely over chronologically replayed real-world streams, and studies how model choice, action-timing mechanism, and hindsight feedback affect performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents are increasingly deployed for persistent monitoring or recurring tasks (e.g., market analysis), expected to run unattended for days or weeks, act at the right timing, and adapt to a dynamic environment. Existing long-horizon agent work largely assumes a static environment that does not change over time, so it fails to capture these temporally evolving challenges.

Method: The authors build ReLiveGym, a diagnostic evaluation environment of long-lived tasks in which agents act sparsely over simulated weeks of chronologically replayed real-world news, market, and social-media streams. Tasks span diverse levels of time sensitivity, reasoning intensity, and recurrence. They evaluate eight base language models, examining how model choice and harness design affect performance, how agents determine when to act, and how continuous learning from hindsight feedback affects performance and failure modes.

Result: Across eight base models, the mechanism by which agents decide when to act emerges as an important harness-design axis for long-lived tasks; the optimal design varies across tasks and sometimes across model choices. Continuous learning from hindsight feedback is also evaluated for its effect on performance and on addressing observed failure modes.

Conclusion: Model choice, action-timing mechanism, and use of feedback are key considerations in designing long-lived agents. The code is released at https://github.com/SaharaLabsAI/ReLiveGym.

Abstract: As large language model (LLM) agents become widely adopted, they are increasingly deployed for tasks that require persistent monitoring or recurring actions (e.g., market analysis). These agents are expected to operate unattended for days or weeks, act at the right timing, and adapt to the dynamic environment over time. These challenges are not fully captured in the existing long-horizon agent work, as they often consider a static environment that is not temporally changing. We introduce ReLiveGym, a diagnostic evaluation environment of long-lived tasks in which agents act sparsely over simulated weeks of chronologically replayed real-world news, market, and social-media streams. The tasks span diverse levels of time sensitivity, reasoning intensity, and recurrence. Across eight base language models, we investigate how model choice and harness design affect agent performance on such long-lived tasks. Our results show that how agents determine when to act arises as an important harness-design axis for long-lived tasks; and that the optimal design varies across tasks and sometimes model choices as well. We also evaluate how continuous learning from hindsight feedback affects performance and addresses failure modes observed in these long-lived tasks. These findings indicate model choice, action timing mechanism, and use of feedback as important considerations in the design of long-lived agents. Code: https://github.com/SaharaLabsAI/ReLiveGym

</details>


### [127] [Robust Nash Alignment under Preference Uncertainty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00715)
*Shihab Ahmed,Debamita Ghosh,David Tang,Yudan Wang,Alvaro Velasquez,Yue Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Robust Nash Alignment, a game-theoretic framework that optimizes policy against uncertain pairwise preferences via an ambiguity set, solved with a four-player primal-dual proxy game using single-loop optimistic mirror descent-ascent with O(1/sqrt(T)) convergence guarantees.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Preference-based alignment methods typically optimize against a single preference model, making them brittle when pairwise preferences are noisy, heterogeneous, or shift after deployment.

Method: Formulates alignment to uncertain pairwise preferences as a game with a major learner seeking a policy with a large worst-case win rate against both an adversarial competitor and any preference kernel in an ambiguity set around a nominal preference. To handle computational difficulty, introduces a four-player primal-dual proxy game (leader policy, follower policy, adversarial kernel, dual variable) and develops a single-loop optimistic mirror descent-ascent algorithm.

Result: Shows the proxy always lower-bounds the truncated hard-constrained objective, quantifies the proxy-to-hard gap, and characterizes an exactness condition under which the proxy recovers the robust objective. Proves O(1/sqrt(T)) average-iteration convergence for the proxy-game duality gap, implying a near-optimal robust policy. Experiments on controlled tabular games and LLM alignment with uncertain preference validate the convergence theory and show improved performance over nominal baselines.

Conclusion: Robust Nash Alignment provides certified worst-case guarantees for preference alignment under uncertainty, with tractable optimization and demonstrated empirical gains over nominal baselines.

Abstract: Preference-based alignment methods typically optimize against a single preference model, and can therefore be brittle when pairwise preferences are uncertain: noisy, heterogeneous, or shift after deployment. To address these issues, we propose Robust Nash Alignment, a game-theoretic framework for alignment to uncertain pairwise preferences. Our formulation has a major learner seeking a policy with a large worst-case win rate against both an adversarial competitor and any preference kernel lying in an ambiguity set around a nominal preference. When the ambiguity set captures the uncertainty in preferences, the resulting robust objective of the game directly yields a certified lower bound on worst-case performance. However, we note this problem is computationally challenging to optimize, and to address this, we introduce a four-player primal-dual proxy game involving the leader policy, follower policy, adversarial kernel, and dual variable, and develop a single-loop optimistic mirror descent-ascent algorithm for it. We show that the proxy always lower-bounds the truncated hard-constrained objective, quantify the proxy-to-hard gap, and characterize an exactness condition under which the proxy recovers the robust objective. We then prove an \(\mathcal{O}(1/\sqrt{T})\) average-iteration convergence for the proxy-game duality gap, which implies a near-optimal robust policy for the original robust objective. Experiments on controlled tabular games and LLM alignment with uncertain preference further validate the convergence theory and show improved performance over nominal baselines.

</details>


### [128] [Enterprise Representation Simplification (ERS): Reducing Representational Complexity for Enterprise AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00791)
*Terry Dorsey,Kevin Huggins*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces Enterprise Representation Simplification (ERS) and Enterprise Representation Complexity (ERC), a representation-neutral model for measuring and reducing representational complexity in enterprise information artifacts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise information is embodied in artifacts shaped by applications, projects, technologies, organizational boundaries, and local requirements. These structures accumulate over time, producing representational complexity that must be maintained by the enterprise and interpreted by both information consumers and AI systems. This creates a need for a principled way to reduce unnecessary complexity while preserving required information.

Method: The paper defines ERS as reducing unnecessary representational complexity while preserving required information within a defined scope, and defines ERC as a representation-neutral model for comparing complexity across representation states. ERC characterizes representational extent along four dimensions: Representation Objects, Interactions, Behaviors, and Supporting Sources, with Objects, Interactions, and Behaviors forming dependent categories and Supporting Sources characterizing representation exposure. ERC is defined at both representation and task levels. The paper also develops an economic model distinguishing recurring global representation cost, recurring task-level cost, and one-time transformation cost, enabling evaluation over a defined time horizon.

Result: Two consequences of ERS are developed. First, representational structures create lifecycle obligations for maintenance, governance, dependencies, change, enhancement, and operation, which the economic model captures. Second, reductions in task-level ERC reduce the representational extent an AI system must identify, relate, and interpret; Text-to-SQL research provides evidence that reduced schema and reasoning complexity can improve reasoning accuracy.

Conclusion: ERC is explicitly not a universal complexity, performance, or cost metric. Instead, it provides measurable architectural variables for comparing representational alternatives, transformation effects, economic outcomes, and AI reasoning performance, enabling comparison and distinguishing architectural simplification from retrieval optimization.

Abstract: Enterprise information is represented through artifacts shaped by applications, projects, technologies, organizational boundaries, and local requirements. These structures accumulate over time, creating representational complexity that must be maintained by the enterprise and interpreted by information consumers and AI systems. This paper introduces Enterprise Representation Simplification (ERS) as reducing unnecessary representational complexity while preserving required information within a defined scope, and Enterprise Representation Complexity (ERC), a representation-neutral model for comparing complexity across representation states.
  ERC characterizes representational extent through four dimensions: Representation Objects, Interactions, Behaviors, and Supporting Sources. Objects, Interactions, and Behaviors form dependent categories, while Supporting Sources characterize representation exposure. ERC is defined at representation and task levels, enabling comparison and distinguishing architectural simplification from retrieval optimization.
  The paper develops two consequences of ERS. First, representational structures create lifecycle obligations for maintenance, governance, dependencies, change, enhancement, and operation. An economic model distinguishes recurring global representation cost, recurring task-level cost, and one-time transformation cost, enabling evaluation over a defined time horizon. Second, reductions in task-level ERC reduce the representational extent an AI system must identify, relate, and interpret. Text-to-SQL research provides evidence that reduced schema and reasoning complexity can improve reasoning accuracy.
  ERC is not a universal complexity, performance, or cost metric. It provides measurable architectural variables for comparing representational alternatives, transformation effects, economic outcomes, and AI reasoning performance.

</details>


### [129] [Sapien: A Stateful Policy Engine for Autonomous AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00797)
*Corinn Tiffany,Wen Zhang,Eugene Bagdasarian,Lillian Tsai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Sapien is a policy engine that enforces stateful contextual policies for AI agents, expressed as regular expressions extended with stateful predicates, deferred generation, and scoped semantic checks, achieving near-unconstrained utility while blocking most attacks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Contextual security defenses synthesize task-specific policies and enforce them on an agent's tool calls to prevent rogue actions. However, in multi-step tasks, the validity of actions depends on the agent's accumulated history and knowledge, so static policies are inadequate. This motivates a stateful policy enforcement approach.

Method: The authors present Sapien, a policy engine that enforces stateful contextual policies. A Sapien policy specifies permitted tool-call sequences via a regular expression augmented with three features: stateful predicates, deferred policy generation, and scoped semantic checks.

Result: Sapien stays within a few percent of an unconstrained agent's utility. Even when the agent is fully hijacked, Sapien's policies rule out 93-95% of attacks on AgentDojo and 62-85% on Toolathlon, which is roughly twice as many as tool allowlists on long-horizon tasks.

Conclusion: Sapien demonstrates that stateful contextual policy enforcement can substantially reduce attack success across both short- and long-horizon benchmarks while preserving most of the agent's utility, outperforming simpler allowlist-based defenses.

Abstract: Contextual security defenses prevent AI agents from taking rogue actions by synthesizing a task-specific policy and enforcing it on the agent's tool calls. In multi-step tasks, however, which actions are valid often depends on what the agent has already done and learned. We present Sapien, a policy engine for enforcing stateful contextual policies. A Sapien policy specifies permitted tool-call sequences using a regular expression extended with stateful predicates, deferred policy generation, and scoped semantic checks. We show that Sapien stays within a few percent of an unconstrained agent's utility. Even if the agent is fully hijacked, Sapien's policies rule out 93-95% of attacks on AgentDojo and 62-85% on Toolathlon (twice as many as tool allowlists on long-horizon tasks).

</details>


### [130] [Kepler: Auditable World Models for ARC-AGI-3](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00834)
*Wensen Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Kepler, an open-source harness representing hypotheses as executable world models validated by retrospective transition and conditional prediction checks, achieved a server-verified 100.00 RHAE on all 25 public ARC-AGI-3 games under a single frozen configuration, but the paper argues that saturated public-set scores have limited discriminative value and proposes first-attempt, cost-conditioned, verification-aware reporting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ARC-AGI-3 requires agents to infer rules and objectives purely from interaction, making evaluation of such interactive agents difficult. Public-set scores alone appear to saturate, so the field needs richer, more discriminative evaluation protocols and honest reporting of failure modes.

Method: Kepler represents hypotheses as executable world models and validates them through retrospective transition checks and conditional prediction checks. Evaluation used one frozen Claude Opus 5 configuration with no per-game model selection or score-conditioned reruns; comparisons included median-human action baselines, retained provider-session token/cost accounting, a single-game animation-frame observation case study, and cross-model comparison with GPT-5.6 Sol.

Result: Server-verified 100.00 RHAE on all 25 public games. On 181 of 183 completed levels the final Opus attempt used no more actions than the median-human baseline. Retained boards used 8,256 environment actions (7,292 in scored levels); provider-session records show 858.0M tokens, 97.37% cache reads, and \$777.72 at September 1, 2026 list-equivalent rates. Three evaluation failures were documented: source-code leakage yielding an invalid perfect run, harness reconstruction in a control condition, and autonomous repair masking a broken planner. Animation frames contained task-relevant information absent from settled text grids. Across the final Opus 5 and GPT-5.6 Sol boards, 48 of 50 game-model cells reached 100.

Conclusion: Public-set ARC-AGI-3 score alone is weakly discriminative once near saturation. The authors recommend first-attempt, cost-conditioned, and verification-aware reporting to expose leakage, harness reconstruction, and hidden planner failures.

Abstract: ARC-AGI-3 evaluates agents in interactive environments whose rules and objectives must be inferred from observation. We present Kepler, an open-source harness that represents hypotheses as executable world models and validates them through retrospective transition checks and conditional prediction checks. Under one frozen Claude Opus 5 configuration, Kepler obtained a server-verified 100.00 RHAE on all 25 public games, with no per-game model selection or score-conditioned reruns. On 181 of 183 completed levels, the final Opus attempt used no more actions than the corresponding median-human baseline. The retained board runs used 8,256 environment actions, of which 7,292 occurred in scored levels. Retained local provider-session records yield 858.0 million tokens, 97.37% cache reads, and a \$777.72 cost at September 1, 2026 API list-equivalent rates. We also report three evaluation failures: source-code leakage that produced an invalid perfect run, agents reconstructing a removed harness in a control condition, and autonomous repair masking a broken planner. A single-game observation case study showed that animation frames contained task-relevant information absent from settled text grids. Across the final Claude Opus 5 and GPT-5.6 Sol boards, 48 of 50 game-model cells reached 100. These results indicate that public-set score alone has limited discriminative value and motivate first-attempt, cost-conditioned, and verification-aware reporting.

</details>


### [131] [Learning Multiple Timescales for Goal-Conditioned Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00849)
*Pedro Robles Dutenhefner,Dikshant Shehmar,Wagner Meira,Marlos C. Machado*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GITA conditions a single value function on the temporal abstraction scale k and aggregates advantage-weighted supervision across multiple k values, resolving the fixed-k trade-off between local resolution and long-range signal in offline goal-conditioned RL.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Offline GCRL fails on long-horizon tasks because discounting shrinks value differences between distant states below function approximation error, removing any signal for ranking states. Temporal abstraction (treating k steps as one transition) restores long-range signal, but no single fixed k fits all state-goal distances: large k preserves long-range value differences while blurring nearby states, and small k does the opposite.

Method: GITA makes this trade-off explicit by conditioning a single value function on k. It trains one policy by aggregating advantage-weighted supervision across multiple k values, so scales assigning larger positive advantages to a state-goal pair contribute more strongly to that pair's update. This retains both local resolution and long-range signal without committing to a single k.

Result: On OGBench, GITA outperforms a broad range of offline GCRL baselines, raising average success rate across all tasks by 25 percentage points (73% relative improvement) over HIQL, and improving over the strongest fixed-k method, OTA, by 7 percentage points (14% relative).

Conclusion: Conditioning value learning on multiple temporal abstraction scales and weighting updates by advantage lets offline GCRL retain both fine-grained local resolution and long-range ranking signal, yielding substantial gains over both fixed-k and non-abstracted baselines on long-horizon tasks.

Abstract: Existing approaches to offline goal-conditioned reinforcement learning (GCRL) struggle with long-horizon tasks. Discounting shrinks value differences between distant states until they fall below the function approximation error, leaving the agent with no signal for ranking states. Temporal abstraction, which treats k environment steps as a single transition, restores this signal at long range, but no single fixed k suits all state-goal distances: large k preserves value differences across long temporal distances while collapsing distinctions between nearby states, and small k does the reverse. We make this trade-off explicit and introduce Generalized Implicit Temporal Abstraction (GITA), which conditions a single value function on k. GITA trains one policy by aggregating advantage-weighted supervision across multiple k values, so scales assigning larger positive advantages to a state-goal pair contribute more strongly to its update. GITA does not need to choose between local resolution and long-range signal; it retains both without committing to a single k. On OGBench, GITA outperforms a broad range of offline GCRL baselines, raising average success rate across all tasks by 25 percentage points (73% relative improvement) over HIQL. It also improves over the strongest fixed-k method, OTA, by 7 percentage points (14% relative).

</details>


### [132] [An Educator-Guided LLM Pedagogical Agent for Scaffolded Feedback in Conceptual Database Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00870)
*Sara Riazi,Pedram Rooshenas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents an educator-guided LLM pedagogical agent embedded in an ERD editor that delivers scaffolded, artifact-grounded feedback through a four-stage workflow with hidden diagnosis and progressive disclosure; a deployment across three ERD environments and 383 episodes showed 71.1% of target-level changes incorporated the hidden diagnostic target, with surveys favoring delayed disclosure and student agency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conceptual database design is a challenging skill for students, and feedback in this domain must be grounded in the student's artifact, assignment requirements, rubrics, and instructional resources rather than generic. Existing LLM feedback systems often lack pedagogical scaffolding and control over how much diagnostic information is revealed, risking premature answers or misdirected revisions.

Method: The authors designed an architecture that separates hidden, artifact-grounded diagnosis from the student-facing workflow controlling feedback form and disclosure level. They instantiated it as a four-stage workflow in an ERD editor: concept checks, guided application, low-detail feedback, and localized clarification. Each feedback request creates a stateful episode linked to versioned ERD states. Evaluation used deployment data across three ERD environments (383 feedback episodes), qualitative analysis of interaction traces, and a self-selected survey.

Result: 71.1% of observed target-level changes fully or partially incorporated the hidden diagnostic target, including many after Stages 1--2. Qualitative analysis showed staged disclosure sometimes withheld inaccurate details, supported selective uptake, or allowed later recovery, though some errors still shaped revisions. Survey respondents favored delayed disclosure and student agency but noted indirectness and repetition as drawbacks.

Conclusion: Staged, hidden-diagnosis-based scaffolding can effectively guide students toward correct ERD revisions while preserving agency, but indirectness and repetition remain limitations, and some erroneous details still influenced student work, pointing to the need for refinement of disclosure strategies and error containment.

Abstract: We present an educator-guided LLM pedagogical agent for scaffolded feedback in conceptual database design. Integrated into an entity--relationship diagram (ERD) editor, the system grounds feedback in the student artifact, assignment requirements, educator-authored rubrics, and instructional resources. Its architecture separates hidden, artifact-grounded diagnosis from the workflow that controls the form and disclosure level of student-facing support.
  We instantiate the architecture as a four-stage workflow progressing from concept checks and guided application to low-detail feedback and localized clarification. Each feedback request creates a stateful episode linked to versioned ERD states. In a deployment spanning three ERD environments and 383 feedback episodes, 71.1\% of observed target-level changes fully or partially incorporated the hidden diagnostic target, including many after Stages~1--2. Qualitative analysis showed that staged disclosure sometimes withheld inaccurate details, supported selective uptake, or allowed later recovery, though some errors still shaped revisions. Survey responses from a self-selected sample favored delayed disclosure and student agency but noted indirectness and repetition.

</details>


### [133] [MemFit: Efficient Long-Term Agentic Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00872)
*Mitchell Piehl,Muchao Ye*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MemFit is a long-term memory system for conversational LLM agents that stores each turn verbatim with LLM-free insertion and indexing, using an LLM-free multi-path retrieval strategy, achieving state-of-the-art results on three benchmarks while cutting memory construction time and cost several-fold.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current long-term memory systems for LLMs rely on LLM agents to organize and consolidate memory, which makes write operations costly and inefficient.

Method: MemFit stores each turn verbatim in an append-only store with near-instantaneous, LLM-free insertion, indexing turns with segment summaries instead of replacing them. It also employs an LLM-free, multi-path retrieval strategy combining lexical and semantic signals with cross-encoder reranking over caption-augmented episodes, supporting both textual and multimodal settings.

Result: On three widely used benchmarks—LoCoMo, MemGallery, and LongMemEval-S—MemFit achieves state-of-the-art performance while reducing memory construction time and cost several-fold.

Conclusion: MemFit provides a scalable and efficient solution for persistent agentic memory, avoiding expensive LLM-based memory construction while preserving surface-level detail.

Abstract: Long-term memory systems for large language models (LLMs) have gained popularity for extending reasoning capabilities across applications. Current memory systems rely on LLM agents to organize and consolidate memory, resulting in costly, inefficient write operations. To address this limitation, we propose MemFit, a long-term memory system for conversational agents that reduces the cost and latency of memory operations. Unlike existing systems that rely on expensive LLM calls for memory construction or discard surface-level details through compression, MemFit stores each turn verbatim in an append-only store with near-instantaneous, LLM-free insertion, indexing turns with segment summaries rather than replacing them. Additionally, MemFit uses an LLM-free, multi-path retrieval strategy that combines lexical and semantic signals with cross-encoder reranking over caption- augmented episodes in both textual and multimodal settings. Empirical results on three widely used benchmarks, LoCoMo, MemGallery, and LongMemEval-S, show that MemFit achieves state-of-the-art performance while reducing memory construction time and cost several-fold, providing a scalable and efficient solution for persistent agentic memory.

</details>


### [134] [ActiveSaddler: Automated Curriculum Learning for Agent Harness Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00906)
*Sungho Park,Wonjoong Kim,Jue Zhang,Wook-Shin Han,Pengfei Gao,Chanyoung Park,Yongqiang Yao,Rao Fu,Elsie Nallipogu,Qingwei Lin,Victor Rühle*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces ActiveSaddler, which frames the missing dimension of harness optimization—selecting which training scenarios generate feedback—as an automated curriculum learning problem, modeled as a non-stationary bandit over reusable failure-pattern arms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing harness optimization methods iteratively improve LLM agent prompts, tool interfaces, and control logic from execution feedback, but they largely fix the set of training scenarios that produce that feedback. As the harness evolves, the most useful scenarios for further optimization change, so the training curriculum should adapt alongside the harness. This dimension is currently missing.

Method: ActiveSaddler models the evolving curriculum as a non-stationary bandit with dynamically instantiated optimization targets. It abstracts recurring failures into reusable failure-pattern arms, estimates potential learning progress from targeting each pattern, and adaptively balances revisiting known weaknesses against exploring unseen scenarios. Optimization outcomes continually update both the discovered failure patterns and their priorities, letting the curriculum co-evolve with the harness.

Result: On GAIA2 and Terminal-Bench 2.0, ActiveSaddler consistently discovers stronger harnesses, improving test Pass@1 by 4.4 and 7.5 percentage points over the same harness optimizer using a scenario order fixed before optimization. Ablations show the gains depend on dynamically constructing optimization targets, estimating their evolving utility, and balancing continued optimization with new failure discovery.

Conclusion: Automated curriculum learning is established as a new, crucial optimization dimension for harness optimization.

Abstract: Automated harness optimization can substantially improve LLM agents by iteratively updating their prompts, tool interfaces, and control logic from execution feedback. However, existing methods primarily optimize how the harness is updated while largely fixing which training scenarios generate the feedback that drives those updates. As the harness evolves, the scenarios most useful for further optimization can change, suggesting that the training curriculum itself should adapt alongside the harness. We formulate this missing dimension of harness optimization as an automated curriculum learning problem and introduce ActiveSaddler. ActiveSaddler models the evolving curriculum as a non-stationary bandit with dynamically instantiated optimization targets. It abstracts recurring failures into reusable failure-pattern arms, estimates the potential learning progress from further targeting each pattern, and adaptively balances revisiting known weaknesses with exploring unseen scenarios for new ones. Optimization outcomes continually update both the set of discovered failure patterns and their priorities, allowing the curriculum to co-evolve with the harness. Experiments on GAIA2 and Terminal-Bench 2.0 show that ActiveSaddler consistently discovers stronger harnesses, improving test Pass@1 by 4.4 and 7.5 percentage points over the same harness optimizer using a scenario order fixed before optimization, respectively. Ablations further show that these gains depend on dynamically constructing optimization targets, estimating their evolving utility, and balancing continued optimization with new failure discovery. Together, these results establish automated curriculum learning as a new crucial optimization dimension for harness optimization.

</details>


### [135] [OR for AI That Does OR: Routing LLMs up the Escalator inside the OSCAR Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00912)
*Jinzhi Bu,Haixin Tang,Huanan Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OSCAR is an LLM-based framework that translates business descriptions into verified optimization models, using a certified offline Simulator, a Coder, and a Reviewer, with cost-aware allocation across LLMs; it reaches 95-100% accuracy using two small open-weight LLMs while costing far less than Codex and Claude Code.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs can generate optimization model code, but the executable code may misrepresent the true constraints or objectives, letting a solver return an optimum to the wrong problem. Even feasible solutions may not be the best possible plan. Organizations need an LLM-based framework that yields accurate formulations at low cost and can run locally, while verifying real improvements and allocating limited modeling attempts across LLMs with differing price and capability.

Method: The authors develop OSCAR (Optimization modeling by Simulator, Coder, and Reviewer). A Simulator is certified against labeled decision examples and used offline to compare candidate formulations, continuing the search beyond mere feasibility toward certified improvement. The search for the next certified improvement is modeled as sequential decisions under unobserved difficulty: which LLMs to call and when to stop. For a simplified known-prior setting, conditions are given under which cost-ordered escalation is optimal; for general menus, a prior-free competitive guarantee is derived.

Result: On five benchmark problems, OSCAR achieves 95%-100% accuracy at the reported settings using two small open-weight LLMs, each deployable locally on a single GPU. Their single-attempt accuracies average 29% and 48%. Across five runs per problem, Codex and Claude Code incur average token costs 3.1 and 5.8 times OSCAR's, respectively.

Conclusion: OSCAR supports open-weight models locally or in the cloud, depending on budget and confidentiality requirements. Firms should maintain labeled examples of feasible and infeasible decisions to clarify plain-language operating rules, and OSCAR follows these labels when an LLM's interpretation conflicts with them.
