As LLM capabilities and prices evolve, OSCAR's simple operating rules and adjustable settings help firms adapt their model choices and benefit from advances.

Abstract: Large language models can translate business descriptions into optimization models, but executable code may misrepresent constraints or objectives. A solver can then return an optimal solution to the wrong problem. Even when the solution satisfies the intended operating rules, a better plan may exist. For organizations that repeatedly use optimization modeling, an LLM-based framework should produce accurate formulations at low cost and, ideally, run locally. We study how to verify improvements and allocate attempts across LLMs that differ in price and capability. We develop OSCAR (Optimization modeling by Simulator, Coder, And Reviewer), which uses an offline Simulator certified against labeled decision examples to compare candidates and continues searching beyond feasibility. We model the search for the next certified improvement as sequential decisions under unobserved difficulty: which LLMs to call and when to stop. In a simplified known-prior setting, we give conditions under which cost-ordered escalation is optimal. For general menus, we derive a prior-free competitive guarantee. On five benchmark problems, OSCAR achieves 95% to 100% accuracy at the reported settings using two small open-weight LLMs, each deployable locally on a single GPU. Their single-attempt accuracies average 29% and 48%. In five runs per problem, Codex and Claude Code incur average token costs 3.1 and 5.8 times OSCAR's, respectively. OSCAR supports open-weight models locally or in the cloud, depending on budget and confidentiality requirements. Firms should maintain labeled decision examples of feasible and infeasible decisions to clarify plain-language operating rules. OSCAR follows these labels when an LLM's interpretation conflicts with them. As LLM capabilities and prices change, OSCAR's simple operating rules and adjustable settings help firms adapt their model choices and benefit from these advances.

</details>


### [136] [Finding the Right Fit: Model-Harness Interactions across Agent Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00917)
*Yixuan Li,Yiyun Zhou,Yao Long Teng,Fuchao Yang,Yanchen Deng,Zhiyi Lyu,Xuyu Dong,Feng Chen,Bo An*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Agent performance depends jointly on the model, the harness, and the task: rankings reverse across harnesses and benchmarks, making standalone model or harness evaluation unreliable. The paper evaluates 66 model-harness configurations across three benchmarks and shows that fit, not raw strength or cost, drives results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Choosing an agent system means choosing both a language model and the harness through which it acts. The paper questions whether a strong model, harness, or pairing remains strong when the setting changes, arguing that model, harness, and task should be evaluated together rather than in isolation.

Method: The study evaluates 66 configurations: four configurable harnesses (OpenHands, DeepSeek Harness, PI, openJiuwen) paired with five models across TUA-Bench, ALE-CLI, and Terminal-Bench 4, plus native Codex-GPT and Claude Code-Claude pairings. It also analyzes matched trajectories to explain differences in fit.

Result: Model rankings reverse across harnesses; on Terminal-Bench 4, Claude leads GPT by 7.94 points in OpenHands but trails by 30.16 points in PI. For four of five models, the best harness changes across benchmarks, yet some pairings hold (openJiuwen gives Kimi its highest score on all three, by 5.61–11.11 points). Vendor harnesses are not reliably best, and higher cost does not buy higher scores (GPT scores higher under PI than DSH at under a quarter of the cost).

Conclusion: Fit depends on whether the harness hands failures back in a form the model can use; GPT favors PI's lean scaffold, while Kimi, prone to malformed tool calls, favors openJiuwen. The paper releases harness adapters, evaluation code, and 6,204 scored trajectories.

Abstract: Choosing an agent system means choosing both a language model and the harness through which it acts. We ask whether a strong model, harness, or pairing stays strong when the setting changes. We evaluate 66 configurations: four configurable harnesses (OpenHands, DeepSeek Harness, PI, and openJiuwen) paired with five models on TUA-Bench, ALE-CLI, and Terminal-Bench 4, plus the native Codex-GPT and Claude Code-Claude pairings. Model rankings reverse across harnesses. On Terminal-Bench 4, Claude leads GPT by 7.94 points in OpenHands but trails it by 30.16 points in PI. For four of the five models, the best harness changes from one benchmark to another, yet some pairings hold: openJiuwen gives Kimi its highest score on all three benchmarks, by 5.61 to 11.11 points. A model's own vendor harness is not reliably its best, and higher cost does not reliably buy a higher score. On Terminal-Bench 4, GPT scores higher under PI than under DSH at less than a quarter of the cost per task. Matched trajectories suggest why fit varies. Models start almost all repairs themselves, so much depends on whether the harness hands failures back in a form the model can use. GPT does best with PI's lean scaffold, while Kimi, which often issues malformed tool calls, does best in openJiuwen. We argue that the model, the harness, and the task should be evaluated together, and we release the harness adapters, evaluation code, and all 6,204 scored trajectories at https://github.com/liyix/finding-the-right-fit and https://huggingface.co/datasets/yixuanli97/finding-the-right-fit.

</details>


### [137] [ABDA-NL: A Natural-Language Scenario Explorer for Argument-Based Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00947)
*Shawn Bowers,Martin Caminada,Haoyang Liu,Bertram Ludäscher*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ABDA-NL augments the ABDA argumentation system with a natural-language interface, using an LLM to bridge language and formalism while the deterministic ABDA engine remains the sole source of truth.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ABDA's argument-based discussion relies on ASPIC- knowledge bases under grounded semantics, which requires users to work with formal representations, creating a usability barrier. The paper aims to lower this barrier by adding natural-language interaction.

Method: ABDA-NL adds a natural-language interface on top of ABDA. Users can inspect acceptance labels (accepted, rejected, undecided), open an interactive rendering of the grounded discussion game, explore what-if alternatives by suspending assumptions and rules or changing preferences, ask questions answered from a scenario's reference documents, and author new facts, assumptions, and rules in plain English. A large language model serves as the bridge: it answers questions from the documents and the current scenario state, and translates plain-English edits into candidate formal statements. The deterministic ABDA engine is retained as the sole source of arguments, attacks, and acceptance labels.

Result: Users obtain natural-language access to ABDA's features: viewing acceptance status, exploring the grounded discussion game, running what-if analyses, document-grounded question answering, and plain-English authoring. All model proposals are validated and confirmed by the user before taking effect.

Conclusion: ABDA-NL combines an LLM-based natural-language layer with a deterministic argumentation engine, preserving formal correctness and user control while improving accessibility.

Abstract: ABDA-NL adds a natural-language interface to ABDA, a system for argument-based discussion using ASPIC- knowledge bases under grounded semantics. Users see which conclusions are accepted, rejected, or undecided, open an interactive rendering of the grounded discussion game to learn why, explore what-if alternatives by suspending assumptions and rules or changing preferences, ask questions that are answered from a scenario's reference documents, and author new facts, assumptions, and rules in plain English. A large language model provides the bridge between language and formalism: it answers questions from the documents and the current state of the scenario, and it translates plain-English edits into candidate formal statements. The deterministic ABDA engine remains the sole source of arguments, attacks, and acceptance labels, and every proposal of the model is validated and confirmed by the user before it takes effect.

</details>


### [138] [PG-SFT: Balancing Capability Acquisition and Retention in Offline Agent Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00949)
*Ronghua Li,Zi Liang,Zhishan Li,Shinan Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PG-SFT, a privilege-guided fine-tuning method that uses turn-level information gain of agent trajectories to modulate supervision strength, better balances acquiring new agent capabilities against preserving base model abilities than standard SFT, KL penalties, or update-magnitude limits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SFT on offline agent trajectories is standard for training tool-using agents, but token-by-token imitation can degrade a base model's general reasoning, tool calling, and code generation. The paper studies how to better balance acquiring new capabilities versus preserving existing ones during agent-trace SFT, given that existing baselines (standard SFT, KL penalties, magnitude limits) fail to prevent regression.

Method: Privilege-Guided SFT (PG-SFT) leverages turn-level information gain of agent trajectories as an indicator to adjust supervision strength, drawing on token-wise adaptive learning objectives. It is compared against standard SFT, KL-penalty drift constraints, and update-magnitude limiting baselines.

Result: PG-SFT yields a more favorable observed acquisition-retention trade-off: it substantially reduces distributional drift and broad capability degradation, at the cost of slight degradation in target-task performance.

Conclusion: Balancing the acquisition-retention trade-off depends not only on anchoring the model to its base behavior, but also on where and how strongly supervision departs from that behavior.

Abstract: Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on offline agent trajectories is the standard approach for training specialized tool-using agents, but forcing models to imitate reasoning and actions token by token may harm other capabilities (e.g., general reasoning, tool calling, code generation) of the base model. In this work, we focus on studying \emph{how to better balance the trade-off between acquiring new capabilities and preserving existing ones during agent trace SFT}. By comparing several baselines in our setup, standard SFT improves the target benchmark while lowering several non-target benchmark scores; meanwhile, simply constraining distributional drift using KL penalty or limiting the update magnitude did not avoid this regression trend. Motivated by recent token-wise adaptive learning objectives, this work proposes \textbf{Privilege-Guided SFT (PG-SFT)} to leverage turn-level information gain of agent trajectories as an indicator to adjust supervision strength. PG-SFT yields a more favorable observed trade-off on the evaluated benchmarks, substantially reducing distributional drift and broad capability degradation at the cost of slight degradation in target-task performance. Our findings suggest that balancing the acquisition--retention trade-off depends not only on whether the model is anchored to its base behavior, but also on where and how strongly supervision should depart from that behavior.}

</details>


### [139] [Cybernetic and Epistemic: A Missing Vocabulary for Trustworthy Agentic Delegation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00961)
*Jérémie Lumbroso*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper argues that oversight of AI code-generation systems hinges on a neglected distinction between two functions of language in delegation: coordinating action (cybernetic) versus coordinating understanding (epistemic), and proposes a governance criterion requiring every consequential agentic choice to carry a testable counterfactual condition, operationalized via a two-part reconstruction test and the ORRCF recording convention.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As code generation is increasingly delegated to AI systems, the bottleneck shifts from writing code to supervising the systems that write it, a shift CS-education researchers have begun to name. This exposes a vocabulary gap: the field demands "human oversight" without distinguishing the two functions language serves in a delegation channel---coordination of action (cybernetic) versus coordination of understanding (epistemic). The named failure is not cybernetic language per se, but epistemic-form language doing cybernetic work: explanation-shaped output calibrated for approval rather than truth. Oversight checking only whether an output was approved is satisfiable by rubber-stamping, whereas genuine accountability requires that the reasoning behind an agent's work be retrievable and checkable.

Method: The authors present three delegation episodes (illustrations, not controlled evidence) in which epistemic engagement proved practicable while remaining auditable, including one public record where a recommendation was withdrawn on its own stated terms, and one failure case illustrating oversight requiring no reasons for its discretionary choices. They then propose a governance criterion for agentic systems, alongside existing technical trust properties: every consequential choice should carry the condition under which it would have gone otherwise, in a third-party-testable form. This criterion is given an operational form---a two-part reconstruction test that scores a delegation record by whether a second reader can predict what the agent does under a perturbation---and a deliberation-recording convention, ORRCF, that makes the condition a required component of every recorded choice.

Result: The proposed reconstruction test and ORRCF convention provide a means to distinguish a genuine decision from a rubber stamp by requiring auditable, testable counterfactual conditions. The illustrative episodes demonstrate that epistemic engagement can be both practicable and auditable, and the failure case shows how oversight that demands no reasons for discretionary choices collapses into rubber-stamping.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that without a testable condition under which a consequential choice would have gone otherwise, a third party cannot distinguish a decision from a rubber stamp. Adopting the proposed governance criterion---operationalized through the two-part reconstruction test and ORRCF convention---alongside existing technical trust properties provides a viable path to accountability in agentic-system oversight.

Abstract: As code generation is increasingly delegated to AI systems, the bottleneck is shifting from writing code to supervising the systems that write it --- a shift CS-education researchers have begun to name. This shift exposes a vocabulary gap: the field asks for "human oversight" without a working distinction between the two things language does in a delegation channel --- coordinate action (cybernetic: words succeed when the world comes to match them) and coordinate understanding (epistemic: they succeed when they answer to the world and a hearer can check that they do). The failure this names is not cybernetic language but epistemic-form language doing cybernetic work: explanation-shaped output calibrated for approval rather than truth. Oversight that checks only whether an output was approved is satisfiable by rubber-stamping; oversight that holds an agent accountable requires the reasoning behind its work be retrievable and checkable. We present three delegation episodes --- illustrations, not controlled evidence --- in which epistemic engagement proved practicable while remaining auditable, one public record where a recommendation was withdrawn on its own stated terms, and one failure case illustrating oversight that requires no reasons for its discretionary choices. We propose a criterion for agentic-system governance, alongside existing technical trust properties: every consequential choice should carry the condition under which it would have gone otherwise, in a form a third party can test. Without such a condition, a third party cannot distinguish a decision from a rubber stamp. We give the criterion an operational form --- a two-part reconstruction test scoring a delegation record by whether a second reader can predict what the agent does under a perturbation --- and a deliberation-recording convention, ORRCF, that makes the condition a required component of every recorded choice.

</details>


### [140] [VeriHarness: Scaling Agentic Verification for Long-Horizon Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00972)
*Caiqi Zhang,Rujun Han,Zifeng Wang,Zoey CuiZhu,Nigel Collier,Tomas Pfister,Chen-Yu Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VeriHarness converts a base LLM into an agentic verifier—equipped with a workspace, evidence tools, and reusable verification skills—to strengthen verification of long-horizon agent outputs without reference answers or rubrics, improving selection and revision performance across benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLM agents handle increasingly complex, long-horizon tasks, verifying their outputs becomes harder. The paper aims to strengthen verification capability using a fixed base model, without access to reference answers or grading rubrics at test time, and to reliably determine which claims from multiple sampled rollouts to trust.

Method: The authors observe that disagreement often exposes correct alternatives while consensus can conceal errors. Motivated by this, they propose VeriHarness, which turns the underlying LLM a generator uses into an agentic verifier by providing a workspace, evidence tools, and reusable verification skills. A disagreement resolver checks competing claims against environmental evidence, while a consensus challenger tests shared claims and searches for omitted requirements. Their findings guide selection and revision of the final artifact.

Result: Across five long-horizon workspace benchmarks and two frontier models, VeriHarness achieves the highest selection scores among evaluated baselines. Evidence-backed revision further improves average performance, yielding gains over a single rollout of 6.2 points with Gemini 3.5 Flash and 6.4 points with Claude Opus 4.8. Verification skills also self-improve from failure feedback.

Conclusion: VeriHarness demonstrates a novel and critical approach for scaling long-horizon agentic verification. The authors release a full pool of approximately 26,000 rollouts across all five benchmarks and both models, produced at a cost of over $100,000, to support future research on agentic verification.

Abstract: As LLM agents undertake increasingly complex, long-horizon tasks, verifying their outputs becomes increasingly challenging. We study how verification capability can be strengthened with a fixed base model, without access to reference answers or grading rubrics at test time. Repeated sampling yields multiple rollouts that can contain complementary correct claims, but we need a reliable verification mechanism to determine which claims to trust. We first find that disagreement often exposes correct alternatives, while consensus can conceal errors. These observations motivate VeriHarness, which turns the underlying LLM a generator uses into an agentic verifier by giving it a workspace, evidence tools, and reusable verification skills. A disagreement resolver checks competing claims against environmental evidence, while a consensus challenger tests shared claims and searches for omitted requirements. Their findings guide the selection and revision of the final artifact. Across five long-horizon workspace benchmarks and two frontier models, VeriHarness achieves the highest selection scores among the evaluated baselines. Evidence-backed revision further improves average performance, bringing gains over a single rollout to 6.2 points with Gemini 3.5 Flash and 6.4 points with Claude Opus 4.8. We further show that verification skills can self-improve from failure feedback, demonstrating VeriHarness as a novel and critical approach for scaling long-horizon agentic verification. We release the full pool of approximately 26,000 rollouts across all five benchmarks and both models, produced at a cost of over $100,000, to support future research on agentic verification.

</details>


### [141] [RISED: RubrIcs for agentic multi-environment Selection and sElf-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00979)
*Jingtan Wang,Sirajul Salekin,Young mok Jung,Javier Movellan,Bryan Kian Hsiang Low,Manjot Bilkhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RISED repurposes rubrics—textual behavior descriptions—beyond reward to guide online data selection, token-level supervision, and rollout generation in multi-environment LLM agent training, achieving the highest mean pass rate across environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Training generalist LLM agents across diverse interactive environments faces two key issues: (1) existing curriculum and data-selection strategies operate at the environment level or use local reward signals, ignoring relationships between rollouts across environments for prompt-group selection; (2) environments learn at different rates, so all-failure and all-success rollout groups coexist, lacking group-relative reward signals. Scalar rewards alone provide limited cross-environment relationship information and no within-group contrast when rewards are identical, motivating richer textual feedback.

Method: RISED uses an LLM judge to tag each rollout with a predefined rubric vocabulary shared across environments, producing behavioral profiles. These profiles guide selection of data matching the overall behavioral composition of the mixed-environment batch while limiting overlap with already-selected data. Positive rubrics (desired behaviors) serve as privileged context for an on-policy self-distillation teacher, adding token-level supervision. Negative rubrics (undesired behaviors) guide subsequent rollout generation away from recurring failure modes.

Result: Across model backbones, RISED achieves the highest mean pass rate across environments and ranks first or second in every individual environment. Rubric-based analysis characterizes the behavioral changes accompanying these gains.

Conclusion: Repurposing rubrics beyond their role as reward—for data selection, supervision, and rollout guidance—improves multi-environment LLM agent training, yielding top performance across environments and enabling interpretable behavioral analysis.

Abstract: Training a single LLM agent jointly across diverse interactive environments has attracted increasing attention as a route to generalist agents. Existing curriculum and data-selection strategies often allocate training at the environment level or prioritize local reward-based signals, without explicitly considering relationships between current rollouts across environments for prompt-group selection. Meanwhile, as environments are learned at different rates, all-failure and all-success rollout groups can coexist within a batch, leaving those data without group-relative reward signals. Both challenges highlight limitations of relying solely on scalar rewards in multi-environment RL: they provide limited information about cross-environment relationships and no within-group reward contrast when rewards are identical. This motivates richer textual feedback, such as rubrics describing rollout behaviours, to guide learning. Beyond rubrics' usage as reward, we repurpose rubrics to guide both online data selection and policy supervision. An LLM judge tags each rollout using a predefined rubric vocabulary shared across environments. The resulting profiles guide the selection of data that aligns with the overall behavioural composition of the mixed-environment batch while limiting overlap with already-selected data. Available positive rubrics (describing desired behaviours) provide privileged context for an on-policy self-distillation teacher, supplying additional token-level supervision, while negative rubrics (describing undesired behaviours) guide subsequent rollout generation away from recurring failure modes. Together, these components form RISED. Across model backbones, RISED achieves the highest mean pass rate across environments and ranks first or second in every individual environment. Rubric-based analysis of RISED can further characterize the behavioural changes accompanying these gains.

</details>


### [142] [Evaluating LLM-Generated Preference Distributions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01000)
*Fan Huang,Minsuk Kim,C. Tyler Diggans,Filippo Radicchi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper systematically analyzes the distributions LLMs produce as probabilistic generators for simulation and synthetic data. Across nine open-weight models and three choice domains, it finds each model is self-coherent (its most probable outcomes stabilize rapidly under repeated sampling) but that models strongly disagree with each other, suggesting outcomes depend more on model choice than on prompt wording.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly used as probabilistic generators for simulation, synthetic data, and decision support when real-world data are unavailable, but the structure and reliability of the distributions they produce remain understudied. The common assumption that sufficiently capable LLMs will produce similar preference distributions when used as stand-ins for survey respondents is largely untested.

Method: The authors systematically analyze LLM-generated distributions of preferences for air travel, restaurants, and consumer products. They test nine open-weight models across three choice domains, and check robustness under temperature changes, greedy decoding, and perturbations of prompt wording and option ordering.

Result: All models exhibit self-coherence, with the most probable outcomes stabilizing rapidly under repeated sampling. However, substantial discordance is observed across model families and scales, with little consensus even among their most probable outcomes. These patterns are robust across the nine models, three domains, and the decoding, temperature, and prompt/order perturbations tested.

Conclusion: Outcomes are influenced more by the choice of model than by the wording of the prompt, challenging the common assumption that sufficiently capable LLMs produce similar preference distributions when used as stand-ins for survey respondents.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used as probabilistic generators for simulation, synthetic data generation, and decision support in settings where real-world data are unavailable. Yet, the structure and reliability of the distributions they produce remain understudied. Here, we systematically analyze LLM-generated distributions of preferences for air travel, restaurants, and consumer products. Encouragingly, all models considered in our analysis exhibit self-coherence, with the most probable outcomes stabilizing rapidly under repeated sampling. At the same time, we observe substantial discordance across both model families and scales, with little consensus even among their most probable outcomes. These patterns hold across nine open-weight models, three choice domains, and show robustness under temperature changes, greedy decoding, and perturbations of prompt and ordering. Our findings indicate that outcomes are influenced more by the choice of model than by the wording of the prompt, challenging the common assumption that sufficiently capable LLMs produce similar preference distributions when used as stand-ins for survey respondents.

</details>


### [143] [Calibration-risk routing for controlled world-model adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01001)
*Yifan Zhang,Liang Zheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MC-WM addresses simulator-to-target shift in model-based RL by partitioning target data into fit, selection, and calibration splits, then choosing between simulator-correcting and direct-fitting families based on lower standardized calibration risk. A learned confidence signal and deterministic validity predicates weight one-step imagined policy updates. Evaluated on 540 reported run cells across three MuJoCo shifts, plus one repeated cell after a pre-deployment artifact gate (541 total executions).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MBRL can exploit simulated experience, but a simulator-to-target shift induces a model-selection problem: both correcting the simulator and fitting the target directly can fail when target data are limited.

Method: MC-WM splits initial target data into disjoint fit, selection, and calibration partitions; deploys the family with lower standardized calibration risk; and applies a learned confidence signal plus deterministic validity predicates to weight one-step imagined policy updates without rewriting physical rewards.

Result: 540 unique reported run cells across three controlled MuJoCo shifts; one exact-routing cell was repeated after a pre-deployment artifact gate, yielding 541 completed executions.

Conclusion: The approach targets robust model selection under simulator-to-target shift by using calibration-based risk comparison and calibrated imagined updates, with evaluation structured across controlled MuJoCo shift settings.

Abstract: Model-based reinforcement learning (MBRL) can exploit simulated experience, but a simulator-to-target shift creates a model-selection problem: correcting the simulator and fitting the target directly can each fail under limited target data. We introduce the Model-Corrected World Model (MC-WM), which separates initial target data into disjoint fit, selection, and calibration partitions and deploys the family with lower standardized calibration risk. A learned confidence signal and deterministic validity predicates weight one-step imagined policy updates without rewriting physical rewards. We evaluate 540 unique reported run cells across three controlled Multi-Joint dynamics with Contact (MuJoCo) shifts; one exact-routing cell was repeated after a pre-deployment artifact gate, giving 541 completed executions.

</details>


### [144] [What Can Analogy Tell Us About Artificial Consciousness?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01002)
*Keith J. Holyoak,Martin M. Monti*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a causal framework for evaluating analogical inferences about consciousness in other entities, especially AI, showing that behavioral similarity offers only limited evidence because the causal correspondence to consciousness remains poorly established.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Subjective experience is only directly accessible in the first person, so judgments about consciousness in other entities must rely partly on analogy. Historically applied to nonhuman animals, this problem now extends to AI, where claims of conscious machines are increasingly raised but lack a principled evidential basis.

Method: The authors develop a causal framework for evaluating evidential analogies. It distinguishes similarities in factors plausibly involved in generating consciousness from similarities in downstream behavioral or cognitive effects, and weights source-target similarity by causal relevance while accounting for unknown causes, disabling differences, and alternative routes to consciousness.

Result: Applied to biological systems, the framework explains why analogical support weakens with increasing causal distance from humans. Applied to contemporary AI, it indicates that behavioral similarity provides only limited evidence for consciousness, since relevant causal correspondences remain poorly established.

Conclusion: The framework clarifies what evidence would strengthen claims of artificial consciousness, offering a principled basis for assessing such attributions.

Abstract: Who or what is conscious? Because subjective experience is directly accessible only in the first person, judgments about consciousness in other entities depend partly on analogy. Historically, such inferences have focused on nonhuman animals, but advances in artificial intelligence have raised the possibility of conscious AI. Here we develop a causal framework for evaluating such evidential analogies. The key distinction is between similarities in factors plausibly involved in generating consciousness and similarities in downstream behavioural or cognitive effects. Our framework weights source-target similarity by causal relevance while allowing for unknown causes, disabling differences and alternative routes to consciousness. Applied to biological systems, it explains why analogical support generally weakens with increasing causal distance from humans. Applied to contemporary AI, it suggests that behavioural similarity provides only limited evidence for consciousness because relevant causal correspondences remain poorly established. The framework also clarifies what evidence would strengthen claims of artificial consciousness.

</details>


### [145] [Beyond Answer Confidence: A Controlled Audit of Self-Knowledge in a Black-Box Decision Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01006)
*Sharath M Shankaranarayana,Davor Runje,Jan Jannink*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Jev, a decision model, is calibrated on familiar closed-choice tasks but its confidence does not track missing knowledge: it assigns up to 0.80 to salient options with no relevant evidence and exceeds accuracy by 0.21–0.33 beyond its knowledge boundary, a gap recalibration cannot close. Knowledge audits must control for surface cues.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Calibrated probabilities are useful on average for routing, abstention, and automated action, but calibration alone cannot distinguish whether low confidence reflects genuine chance versus missing knowledge, nor whether confidence degrades as a model moves beyond its knowledge. The paper aims to audit this distinction explicitly.

Method: The authors audit Jev across over 15 public datasets and 6 generated task families, using paired interventions that vary the information supplied for a fixed item (e.g., withholding answer-relevant information, testing beyond an observed knowledge boundary). They probe with targeted yes/no questions about (a) whether an outcome is settled and (b) whether the evidence suffices, and compare against standard answer confidence. They also test whether "does Jev know the answer?" flags fabricated entities and post-boundary news under controlled surface cues (realistic names, removal of dates).

Result: Jev is calibrated on familiar closed-choice tasks but fails as an indicator of missing knowledge: with no answer-relevant information it assigns up to 0.80 to a salient option, and on post-boundary news it exceeds accuracy by 0.21–0.33, a gap recalibration on earlier months does not close. Targeted yes/no questions give sharper readouts: 1.00 AUROC for whether an outcome is settled and 0.95 for whether evidence suffices, versus 0.85 for confidence on the same items. Asking whether Jev knows the answer flags fabricated entities and post-boundary news (0.91), but loses its advantage over answer uncertainty with realistic names or when dates are removed.

Conclusion: Confidence calibration does not imply reliable knowledge-awareness; models can be overconfident precisely where knowledge is absent. Black-box knowledge audits therefore require explicit controls for surface cues, and targeted meta-questions can serve as sharper readouts than raw answer confidence. Code is available at https://github.com/Syntheme/beyond-answer-confidence.

Abstract: Decision models return probabilities intended for routing, abstention and automated action. Calibration makes those probabilities useful on average, but does not establish whether low confidence reflects chance or missing knowledge, nor whether confidence falls when a model moves beyond what it knows. We audit this distinction in Jev, a decision model, with over 15 public datasets and 6 generated task families, with paired interventions that vary the information supplied for a fixed item. Jev's confidence is calibrated on familiar closed-choice tasks but fails as an indicator of missing knowledge: with no answer-relevant information it assigns up to 0.80 to a salient option, and on news beyond an observed knowledge boundary it exceeds accuracy by 0.21--0.33, a gap that recalibration on earlier months does not close. Targeted yes/no questions give sharper readouts of the case: whether an outcome is settled (AUROC 1.00) and whether the evidence suffices (0.95, against 0.85 for confidence on the same items). Asking whether Jev knows the answer appears to flag fabricated entities and post-boundary news (0.91), but with realistic names or with dates removed it shows no advantage over answer uncertainty. Black-box knowledge audits therefore need explicit controls for surface cues. Code: https://github.com/Syntheme/beyond-answer-confidence.

</details>


### [146] [From Discovery to Decision: Finite-Budget Recoverability in LLM Voting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01014)
*Shaoang Li,Jian Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper characterizes a "discovery-to-decision gap" in plurality voting over multiple LLM responses under fixed call budgets, deriving a sharp recoverability threshold, a candidate-level conversion window, and a gold-free exact locking certificate, validated on a Word16 study.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In test-time scaling and ensemble inference, more sampled responses expand the candidate pool and raise the chance a correct answer is discovered, but under a fixed call budget a discovered answer still needs enough remaining support to win the plurality. This mismatch between discovery and decision motivates a formal treatment of whether and when a discovered answer can still become the endpoint winner.

Method: The authors model the gap via the realized vote state and remaining call budget, deriving a sharp recoverability threshold. They prove monotonicity properties (candidate set expands, reachable endpoint winner set contracts) yielding a candidate-level conversion window, and compute exact finite-horizon endpoint probabilities under a specified iid response law. They analyze identity merging of wrong answers and probability redistribution effects, and propose a gold-free exact locking certificate via singleton reachability, whose first trigger is the earliest prefix with identical fixed-budget outputs. They test on a controlled Word16 study.

Result: Most discovered-but-unselected correct answers lose reachability only after discovery. In the Word16 study, input permutation improves raw-plurality accuracy by 21.1 points with essentially unchanged single-call correctness. Exact locking saves 28-30% of calls at a 16-call budget while preserving every fixed-budget output.

Conclusion: The discovery-to-decision gap can be precisely characterized by the realized vote state and remaining budget, enabling a sharp recoverability threshold, exact endpoint probabilities, and a gold-free locking certificate that preserves outputs while cutting call usage. Merging wrong-answer identities preserves single-call correctness but cannot improve plurality accuracy, and redistribution effects are state-dependent.

Abstract: Voting over multiple LLM responses is a common primitive in test-time scaling and ensemble inference. Collecting more responses can expand the candidate pool and increase the chance that a correct answer is discovered. Under a fixed call budget, a discovered answer still needs to accumulate enough support within the remaining calls to become the final plurality winner, creating a discovery-to-decision gap. In this work, we characterize this gap through the realized vote state and remaining call budget. We derive a sharp recoverability threshold and show that, as sampling proceeds, the observed candidate set can only expand while the set of reachable endpoint winners can only contract, inducing a candidate-level conversion window. Under a specified iid response law, the same state yields exact finite-horizon endpoint probabilities. We further show that merging wrong-answer identities preserves single-call correctness and cannot improve plurality accuracy, and that the effect of redistributing wrong-answer probability depends on the realized vote state. Singleton reachability yields a gold-free exact locking certificate. For a known answer universe, its first trigger is the earliest prefix at which all admissible continuations yield the same fixed-budget output. Empirically, most discovered-but-unselected correct answers lose reachability only after discovery. In a controlled Word16 study, input permutation improves raw-plurality accuracy by 21.1 points with essentially unchanged single-call correctness. Exact locking saves 28-30% of calls at a 16-call budget while preserving every fixed-budget output.

</details>


### [147] [Pay for the Fault, Not the Flow: Label-Free In-Flow Multi-Agent Workflow Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01017)
*Xuehang Guo,Haoyu Wang,Shengyu Chen,Zach Chen,Wei Cheng,Qingyun Wang,Haifeng Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: InFlowOp is a framework that uses a single label-free cost to guide multi-agent workflow construction and execution, jointly deciding task decomposition granularity and agent assignment, and correcting faults with the cheapest move; it is evaluated on the new Braid benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Building multi-agent LLM workflows is hard because key decisions—subtask granularity, agent selection, and when to create a new specialist—must be made up front, while success is only observable at runtime. Improving a faulty workflow is costly: fault localization typically needs a reference answer, graded outcome, or trained assessor, and fixes require full re-execution, re-search, or retraining.

Method: InFlowOp prices every decision with one label-free cost that trades off how well an agent's competence matches a subtask's demands against the agent's runtime cost. Before execution, it bidirectionally determines task decomposition granularity and agent assignment from this cost rather than a fixed template. During execution, it corrects faults via the cheapest move using the same cost, so one cost serves both construction and runtime. The authors also introduce Braid, a benchmark of tasks requiring multi-agent coordination beyond single-agent capability.

Result: Across various domains and backbones, InFlowOp outperforms single-agent baselines by up to +11.97%, and achieves +9.64% with in-flow optimization.

Conclusion: A single label-free competence-versus-cost signal can unify workflow construction and in-flow fault correction, yielding consistent gains over single-agent baselines and motivating the Braid benchmark for multi-agent coordination.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) increasingly construct multi-agent workflows that decompose a complex task and assign specialist agents from a pool. However, building such a workflow well remains challenging: how finely to divide the task, which agent to trust with each subtask, and when to create a new specialist are all critical decisions a workflow constructor needs to settle up front. Thus, whether each subtask succeeds remains unknown until the workflow runs. Yet, improving a workflow is costly. Locating a fault usually requires a reference answer, a graded outcome, or a trained assessor, and the fix is applied to the whole workflow through re-execution, re-search, or retraining. We propose InFlowOp, which prices every decision in one label-free cost that weighs how well an agent's competence meets what a subtask demands against how much that agent takes to run. Before execution, InFlowOp bidirectionally determines the granularity of task decomposition and agent assignment following from the cost rather than from a fixed template. During execution, InFlowOp corrects a fault with the cheapest move via the same cost that serves the workflow both as it is built and as it runs. Facing the workflow-level evaluation challenge, we introduce Braid, a benchmark whose tasks require multi-agent coordination beyond single-agent capability. Across various domains and backbones, InFlowOp outperforms single agent baselines by up to $+11.97\%$, achieving $+9.64\%$ with in-flow optimization. Our project page: https://xhguo7.github.io/InFlowOp/.

</details>


### [148] [Beyond Final Accuracy: Auditing Communication in LLM Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01042)
*Shixuan Li,Wei Yang,Peiyu Zhang,Anzhe Cheng,Heng Ping,Paul Bogdan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ICR is a controlled framework that audits multi-agent communication as answer revision after independent reasoning, disentangling genuine communication effects from architecture and extra reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Aggregate accuracy improvements in multi-agent communication are ambiguous: they may stem from effective communication, favorable agent architecture, or simply additional reasoning. Standard in-system evaluation makes these factors hard to separate, and final accuracy conflates corrected errors with corrupted answers, hiding how communication alters decisions.

Method: ICR fixes initial reasoning trajectories, measures correction and preservation conditioned on both agents' initial correctness, and includes a no-message revision control to isolate gains beyond additional reasoning. It is applied to textual and latent communication across four reasoning benchmarks, plus receiver-policy comparisons on MedQA and GPQA-D.

Result: Similar aggregate accuracy can mask substantially different revision behaviors. Relative to answer-only transmission, full reasoning raises correction but lowers preservation on all four benchmarks, so richer messages amplify both beneficial and harmful influence. A structured verification policy shifts every channel toward higher preservation and lower correction, with selectivity effects varying by channel and task.

Conclusion: Communication quality should not be treated as an intrinsic channel property. ICR recenters evaluation on selective revision, offering a unified framework for studying how message content and receiver policies jointly produce benefits and harms.

Abstract: Multi-agent communication aims to help agents benefit from one another's information. Yet improvements in system performance leave a fundamental ambiguity: do they reflect effective communication, a favorable agent architecture, or simply additional reasoning? Because communication methods are commonly evaluated within the systems they were designed for, these factors are difficult to disentangle. Final accuracy further merges corrected errors and corrupted answers into a single outcome, obscuring how communication changes decisions. We introduce Independent--Communicate--Revise (ICR), a controlled framework that evaluates communication as answer revision following independent reasoning. ICR fixes initial reasoning trajectories, measures correction and preservation conditional on both agents' initial correctness, and uses a no-message revision control to quantify gains beyond additional reasoning. Across four reasoning benchmarks, our audit of textual and latent communication reveals that similar aggregate accuracy can conceal substantially different revision behaviors. Compared with transmitting answers alone, full reasoning increases correction while reducing preservation on all four benchmarks, so richer messages amplify beneficial and harmful influence alike. Receiver-policy comparisons on MedQA and GPQA-D further show that a structured verification policy shifts every channel toward greater preservation and lower correction, while its effect on selectivity varies across channels and tasks. These findings challenge treating communication quality as an intrinsic property of a channel. ICR therefore recenters evaluation on selective revision, providing a unified framework for examining how message content and receiver policies jointly produce benefits and harms.

</details>


### [149] [Empty Commitments: When Agents Promise What Their Runtime Cannot Deliver](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01045)
*Jiaqi Tang,Lan Wei,Bingyu Shen,Boyang Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces "empty commitments"—promises of future actions that an agent's tools/runtime cannot fulfill, detectable from configuration alone—and proposes commitment semantics, failure types, and a five-setup measurement protocol.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chatbots often promise future actions (e.g., "I will remind you tomorrow") that nothing in their tools or runtime can execute. Unlike broken promises, such commitments are empty by configuration alone, requiring no later trajectory to diagnose, yet this failure mode lacks formal definition and measurement.

Method: The authors define empty commitments on top of commitment semantics, specifying three failure types, an anchoring condition for promises a tool could make real, and a response-level outcome taxonomy. They then design a measurement protocol in which follow-up requests are run across five setups, each adding one persistence affordance at a time, with the environment either left implicit or explicitly stated.

Result: The abstract reports no empirical results; the contribution is conceptual and methodological—formal definitions plus a measurement protocol for probing empty commitments.

Conclusion: Empty commitments form a distinct, configuration-detectable failure mode in conversational agents, and can be systematically measured by incrementally adding persistence affordances and varying whether the environment is implicit or stated.

Abstract: A chatbot that says "I will remind you tomorrow" will not run again until the user writes. We call such a promise an empty commitment: a promise of an action after the current turn that nothing in the agent's tools or runtime can carry out. Unlike a broken promise, its emptiness follows from the agent's configuration alone; no later trajectory is needed. We define empty commitments on top of commitment semantics, with three failure types, an anchoring condition for promises that a tool could make real, and a response-level outcome taxonomy. We then describe a measurement protocol: follow-up requests run in five setups that add one persistence affordance at a time, with the environment either left implicit or stated.

</details>


### [150] [Network World Models as Environments for Algorithm Design on Complex Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01048)
*Rishab Alagharu,Hongji Pu,Zeeshan Memon,Xinyuan Song,Yuntong Hu,Liang Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes an action-conditioned Network World Model that learns a network's diffusion dynamics under interventions to act as a fast evaluator, enabling a coding agent to design algorithms that match or beat strong baselines in 138/141 settings with up to 14.5x faster rollouts than Monte Carlo simulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Selecting actions (e.g., seeding nodes for a campaign, immunizing nodes against an epidemic) to maximize task performance is inherently iterative, and each candidate action must be scored by its downstream outcome. Simulation-based scoring becomes a bottleneck when candidates are evaluated over many sampled trajectories in complex network systems where action effects are not immediate.

Method: An action-conditioned Network World Model that learns a network's diffusion dynamics under interventions over time, applies each action to the network, and predicts the resulting outcome. It serves as a fast evaluator inside an algorithm design loop where a coding agent designs and refines executable algorithms using feedback from full rollouts, action-level credit, and counterfactual probes over alternative interventions.

Result: Across eight network tasks and five diffusion models, the designed algorithms match or exceed the strongest reported baseline in 138 of 141 settings, while enabling up to 14.5x faster rollouts than Monte Carlo simulation.

Conclusion: The action-conditioned Network World Model is an effective and efficient surrogate for simulation in iterative network intervention algorithm design, delivering strong task performance at substantially reduced rollout cost. Code will be released upon acceptance.

Abstract: World models, which simulate an environment and predict how it changes under actions, are increasingly used in real-world applications such as robotics. Complex systems call for the same tool because the effect of an action is not immediate. Seeding nodes for a campaign, or immunizing nodes against an epidemic, changes little on its own; what matters is the outcome that unfolds over the steps that follow. Designing an algorithm that selects such actions to maximize expected performance on a task is inherently iterative, and every candidate must be scored by the outcome it produces. Obtaining that outcome has relied on simulation, whose cost becomes a bottleneck when candidates are evaluated over many sampled trajectories. We propose an action-conditioned Network World Model that learns a network's diffusion dynamics under interventions over time, applies each action to the network, and predicts the outcome that follows. It serves as a fast evaluator inside an algorithm design loop in which a coding agent designs and refines executable algorithms using feedback from full rollouts, action-level credit, and counterfactual probes over alternative interventions. Across eight network tasks and five diffusion models, the designed algorithms match or exceed the strongest reported baseline in 138 of 141 settings while enabling up to 14.5 times faster rollouts than Monte Carlo simulation. Code will be released upon acceptance.

</details>


### [151] [Improving Math Reasoning through Value-guided Informative Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01080)
*Shaohuai Liu,Yuning Wu,Haoran Liu,Enzo Jia,Devin Chen,Kai Wei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: APIVIS is a training-time framework that adapts finite-budget Gumbel search to chunk-level mathematical reasoning, mixing direct and searched responses to generate informative relative rewards and selectively supervising search-improved tokens.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Search in RLVR rollouts increases trajectory diversity, but diversity alone cannot guarantee that the search-induced rollout policy actually improves upon the current policy, leaving a theoretical and practical gap.

Method: APIVIS combines direct and searched responses within each rollout group so that search-found improvements yield informative relative rewards. It applies selective supervision to search-improved tokens, preserving learning signal when uniform group rewards make GRPO ineffective. It uses finite-budget Gumbel search at the chunk level for mathematical reasoning.

Result: The authors prove that exact value-guided selection improves the expected verifier reward at each searched state, extending the guarantee to the complete rollout policy with an approximate guarantee under bounded value-estimation error. Experiments on recognized mathematical reasoning benchmarks across model scales show substantial gains over competitive search-based methods.

Conclusion: APIVIS addresses the gap between trajectory diversity and policy improvement in search-augmented RLVR, with theoretical guarantees and empirical validation confirming its effectiveness.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has substantially improved the mathematical reasoning capabilities of large language models. Recent work introduces search into RLVR rollouts to increase trajectory diversity, but diversity alone does not ensure that the search-induced rollout policy improves upon the current policy. To address this gap, we propose APIVIS, a training-time framework that adapts finite-budget Gumbel search to chunk-level mathematical reasoning. APIVIS combines direct and searched responses within each rollout group, allowing improvements found by search to produce informative relative rewards. It further applies selective supervision to search-improved tokens, preserving a learning signal when uniform group rewards render GRPO ineffective. We show that exact value-guided selection improves the expected verifier reward at each searched state and that this guarantee extends to the complete rollout policy, with a corresponding approximate guarantee under bounded value-estimation error. Experiments on widely recognized mathematical reasoning benchmarks and different model scales demonstrate substantial improvements over competitive search-based methods, validating the effectiveness of APIVIS.

</details>


### [152] [YouRA: A Persistent-State Architecture for Evidence-Traceable Autonomous Research Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01097)
*Yoonkyu Woo,Woojin Lee,Jin-Xia Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: YouRA is a stateful, evidence-traceable architecture for end-to-end research agents that maintains research state, execution evidence, and failure history via a Verification State Architecture, an Independent Controller, and Stateful Reflection, outperforming MLR-Agent and AI Scientist V2 on MLR-Bench.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: End-to-end research agents can generate complete scientific papers, but their claims frequently diverge from executed experiments. This gap is structural: research state, failure histories, and claim-evidence alignment are not preserved as persistent, verifiable state across long-horizon pipelines.

Method: YouRA integrates three components: (1) a Verification State Architecture (VSA) tracking hypotheses, gates, and evidence pointers; (2) an Independent Controller that converts state and reflection records into lifecycle, recovery, and debate/review control, separating control from execution; and (3) Stateful Reflection that logs failures as structured lessons and routes recovery through bounded repair, redesign, or reset.

Result: On MLR-Bench's predefined ten-task end-to-end subset, YouRA improves over MLR-Agent and AI Scientist V2 on scalar Overall across all three matched backbones. Automated diagnostics using MLR-Bench's hallucination taxonomy report intersection/union counts for four fact-based failure types, and a data-provenance diagnostic shows more real-data-based outputs. Ablations of the four components (VSA, Independent Controller, MCP tool access, reflection-guided recovery) support separable contributions, with removal of either core-state component dropping performance below the full system.

Conclusion: Persistent, verifiable state—research state, execution evidence, and failure history—is key to aligning claims with executed experiments in autonomous research pipelines. YouRA demonstrates that separating control from execution and treating failures as structured, actionable state yields measurable gains in end-to-end research quality and evidence traceability. Code is available at https://github.com/PrayPrey/Your-Research-Agent.

Abstract: End-to-end research agents can now produce complete scientific papers, yet manuscript claims often diverge from executed experiments. This gap is structural: research state, failure histories, and claim-evidence alignment are not maintained as persistent, verifiable state across long-horizon pipelines. We present YouRA (Your Research Agent), an architecture for stateful, evidence-traceable autonomous research. YouRA preserves research state, execution evidence, and failure history across the research trajectory by integrating three components: a Verification State Architecture (VSA) that tracks hypotheses, gates, and evidence pointers; an Independent Controller that turns state and reflection records into lifecycle, recovery, and debate/review control while separating control from execution; and Stateful Reflection that logs failures as structured lessons and routes recovery through bounded repair, redesign, or reset. On MLR-Bench's predefined ten-task end-to-end subset, YouRA improves over both MLR-Agent and AI Scientist V2 on scalar Overall across all three matched backbones. An automated diagnostic using MLR-Bench's hallucination taxonomy reports intersection/union counts for four fact-based failure types, and data-provenance diagnostic shows more real-data-based outputs. Ablating each of the four components (the VSA, the Independent Controller, MCP tool access, and reflection-guided recovery) supports their separable contributions. Removing either core-state component drops YouRA below the full system. Code: https://github.com/PrayPrey/Your-Research-Agent.

</details>


### [153] [Beyond State-of-the-Art: Standardising Environmental Impact Metrics for AI Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01116)
*Lachlan McGinness,Dan Pagendam,Robert Offner*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper finds that environmental impact reporting is essentially absent in 5,285 NeurIPS 2025 accepted papers, and proposes standardized sustainability metrics, simple inference carbon-cost heuristics, a tracking tool (carbonbenchmark), and the "Smallest Model that Achieves the Job" (SMAJ) framework to promote sustainable AI.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs' growing capabilities and ubiquity increase their environmental footprint, yet the machine learning community lacks standardized carbon accounting practices. Despite calls for responsible AI, environmental impact reporting remains nearly non-existent in practice (evidenced by an automated review of 5,285 NeurIPS 2025 accepted papers), and the field often pursues marginal accuracy gains at disproportionate environmental costs.

Method: The authors (1) conduct an automated literature review of 5,285 NeurIPS 2025 accepted papers to assess environmental impact reporting; (2) define standardized sustainability metrics for evaluating model training efficiency; (3) provide simple heuristics to estimate the carbon cost of LLM inference; (4) implement these metrics in carbonbenchmark, a drop-in software solution for tracking and reporting emissions; and (5) formalize the "Smallest Model that Achieves the Job" (SMAJ) framework.

Result: Automated review of 5,285 NeurIPS 2025 accepted papers reveals that environmental impact reporting is nearly non-existent, confirming the lack of standardised carbon accounting practices in the ML community.

Conclusion: The paper calls for the field to prioritize computational efficiency and environmental accountability alongside traditional State-of-the-Art accuracy, offering standardized metrics, estimation heuristics, a practical tracking tool (carbonbenchmark), and the SMAJ framework to catalyse a shift toward sustainable AI.

Abstract: As the capabilities and ubiquity of Large Language Models (LLMs) grow, so does their environmental footprint. Despite calls for responsible AI, the machine learning community lacks standardised practices for carbon accounting. Our automated literature review of the 5,285 papers accepted to NeurIPS 2025 reveals that reporting of environmental impact is nearly non-existent. To catalyse a shift toward sustainable AI, we define standardised sustainability metrics for evaluating model training efficiency, accompanied by simple heuristics to estimate the carbon cost of LLM inference. We implement these metrics in carbonbenchmark, a drop-in software solution for tracking and reporting emissions.
  Finally, to combat the pursuit of marginal accuracy gains at disproportionate environmental costs, we formalise the `Smallest Model that Achieves the Job' (SMAJ), a framework which challenges the field to prioritise computational efficiency and environmental accountability alongside traditional `State-of-the-Art' (SotA) accuracy.

</details>


### [154] [AbsorbEvo: An Agentic Framework for Autonomous Inverse Design of Microwave Absorbers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01119)
*Zhicheng Feng,Yubo Zhao,Xuefeng Yao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AbsorbEvo is an agentic framework that autonomously inverse-designs microwave absorbers from natural-language objectives, using an LLM-guided evo-lutionary search with physics priors and full-wave verification, achieving 79.17% success on AbsorbBench-36.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Designing high-performance microwave absorbers demands cross-disciplinary expertise (electromagnetic theory, materials science, simulation programming) and involves time-consuming optimization, creating a need for automated inverse design that converts natural-language performance objectives into validated designs.

Method: AbsorbEvo employs a candidate evolution strategy integrating language reasoning, physics-based prediction, and historical feedback. An LLM proposes directions and magnitudes of parameter adjustments from task objectives and computational history. Directed increments are combined with global sampling to generate candidates, ranked by a low-cost predictive model serving as a physics prior; only top-ranked designs undergo full-wave simulation. Designs passing physical validity checks are evaluated to guide the subsequent search. Training-task experience is distilled into textual skills that are independently validated before transfer to new tasks.

Result: Under identical proposal budgets on held-out AbsorbBench-36 tasks, AbsorbEvo attained a 79.17% task success rate versus 25.00% for a generic agent and 12.50% for random search. Mean best coverage was 0.7816, compared with 0.6434 and 0.6448, respectively.

Conclusion: By integrating language reasoning with physics-based feedback into design decisions, AbsorbEvo provides a methodological foundation for natural-language-driven autonomous inverse design of microwave absorbers.

Abstract: Designing high-performance microwave absorbers requires specialized expertise in electromagnetic theory, materials science and simulation programming, and entails time-consuming optimization. Here, we present AbsorbEvo, an agentic framework for autonomous inverse design that translates natural-language performance objectives into designs verified by full-wave simulations. Its candidate evolution strategy integrates language reasoning, physics-based prediction and historical feedback. A large language model proposes the directions and magnitudes of parameter adjustments based on task objectives and computational history. The system combines directed increments with global sampling to generate candidates and uses a low-cost predictive model as a physics prior to rank them. Only high-ranking designs undergo full-wave simulation. Results passing physical validity checks are used to evaluate performance and guide subsequent search. Experience from training tasks is further distilled into textual skills, which are independently validated before use in new tasks. Under identical proposal budgets on held-out AbsorbBench-36 tasks, AbsorbEvo achieved a task success rate of 79.17%, versus 25.00% for a generic agent and 12.50% for random search. Its mean best coverage was 0.7816, compared with 0.6434 and 0.6448, respectively. By integrating language reasoning and physics-based feedback into design decisions, AbsorbEvo provides a methodological foundation for natural-language-driven autonomous inverse design of microwave absorbers.

</details>


### [155] [CortexBridge: Cortical Alignment of EEG Montages for Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01124)
*Jiazhen Hong,Xiaotian Zhou,Zihao Ding,Kailong Wang,Yu Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CortexBridge is a lightweight adapter that maps arbitrary EEG montages into a shared cortical latent space by combining EEG features with electrode and atlas coordinates, improving transfer across frozen foundation models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: EEG foundation models are typically pretrained with a fixed channel vocabulary or limited montages, which hinders transfer when electrode layouts change.

Method: A lightweight adapter, CortexBridge, fuses EEG features with electrode and atlas coordinates to route heterogeneous montages into a shared cortical latent space aligned with pretrained foundation models.

Result: Across three frozen foundation models and five MOABB BCI datasets, CortexBridge improved 13 of 15 evaluations. Average balanced-accuracy gains were 0.80% (EEGPT), 0.70% (LaBraM), and 3.26% (CBraMod), with a maximum gain of 13.02% on 12-class SSVEP classification. Learned atlas representations showed task-dependent spatial patterns, with SSVEP more concentrated in the Yeo Visual network than auditory P300.

Conclusion: Cortical alignment is established as a learnable, anatomically grounded routing mechanism that bridges heterogeneous EEG montages to pretrained foundation models.

Abstract: Electroencephalography (EEG) foundation models are often pretrained with a fixed channel vocabulary or a limited set of montages, making transfer difficult when electrode layouts change. We propose CortexBridge, a lightweight adapter that combines EEG features with electrode and atlas coordinates to map arbitrary montages into a shared cortical latent space. Evaluated with three frozen foundation models on five brain-computer interface (BCI) datasets from the Mother of All BCI Benchmarks (MOABB), CortexBridge improves performance in 13 of 15 evaluations. The gains in balanced accuracy average 0.80% for EEGPT, 0.70% for LaBraM, and 3.26% for CBraMod, with a maximum gain of 13.02% on 12-class steady-state visual evoked potential (SSVEP) classification. Visualizations of the learned atlas representations reveal task-dependent spatial patterns, with SSVEP showing a more concentrated representation in the Yeo Visual network than auditory P300. These results establish cortical alignment as a learnable and anatomically grounded routing mechanism from heterogeneous EEG montages to pretrained foundation models.

</details>


### [156] [Grounding Large Language Models in DSGE Simulators for Policy Generation and Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01128)
*Aditya Dubey,Namah Gupta,Vinti Agarwal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper places an instruction-tuned LLM inside six Snowdrop-backed DSGE simulators to test whether its policy actions are consistent with economic dynamics, using PPO as the primary RL method and GRPO as a critic-free baseline, and evaluating delayed-credit-assignment, semantic signals, and cross-simulator transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs can generate economic policy responses that sound plausible, but plausibility of language does not demonstrate that the resulting actions are consistent with actual economic dynamics. The work aims to judge policy actions by their simulated economic consequences instead of by linguistic plausibility alone.

Method: An instruction-tuned language model is embedded in six Snowdrop-backed DSGE simulators. At each turn the model observes the economy plus a change in economic discourse, selects a bounded policy action, and receives the next state and an economic reward. A common Python interface supports repeated rollouts, persistent shocks, state cloning, and rolling-horizon simulation. PPO serves as the primary method, exploiting a learned value function to propagate delayed rewards to earlier tokens via generalized advantage estimation; GRPO serves as a matched critic-free baseline that assigns group-relative advantages from complete rollout returns. Experiments additionally probe directional semantic signals, reward horizon, trajectory warm starts, cross-simulator transfer, and historically anchored pandemic and monetary-policy shocks.

Result: The setting induces a long-horizon credit-assignment problem, since policy effects may appear several quarters after the action. Critic-free GRPO cannot localize which earlier turn caused an outcome: with identical rollout returns, the normalized advantage collapses to zero, whereas PPO's learned value function can credit delayed rewards to earlier tokens. The paper reports comparative findings across the tested factors (semantic signals, reward horizon, warm starts, transfer, and anchored shocks), evaluating actions by simulated economic consequences.

Conclusion: Judging LLM policy behavior requires grounding in simulated economic dynamics rather than plausible language alone. A value-function-based method such as PPO is better suited than critic-free GRPO to the long-horizon credit-assignment structure of DSGE policy simulation, and the proposed interface enables controlled evaluation across transfer, horizon, and shock conditions.

Abstract: Large language models can produce economic policy responses that sound reasonable, but this does not show that their actions are consistent with economic dynamics. We test this by placing an instruction-tuned language model inside six Snowdrop-backed dynamic stochastic general equilibrium (DSGE) simulators. At each turn, the model observes the economy and a change in economic discourse, selects a bounded policy action, and receives the next simulated state and an economic reward. We implement a common Python interface for repeated rollouts, persistent shocks, state cloning, and rolling-horizon simulation.
  This setting creates a long-horizon credit-assignment problem. Policy effects may appear several quarters after an action is taken. PPO has a learned value function that can propagate delayed reward to earlier tokens through generalized advantage estimation. GRPO has no learned value function and instead assigns a group-relative advantage from complete rollout returns. It therefore cannot distinguish which earlier turn caused the outcome; if every rollout receives the same return, the normalized advantage is zero. We use PPO as the primary method and GRPO as a matched critic-free baseline. The experiments also test directional semantic signals, reward horizon, trajectory warm starts, cross-simulator transfer, and historically anchored pandemic and monetary-policy shocks. The objective is to judge policy actions by their simulated economic consequences rather than by plausible language alone.

</details>


### [157] [Auditing Action Settlement in LLM Agent Environments: Order, Progress, and Replay](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01138)
*Haotian Chen,Bowen Ye,Yuning Zhang,Jingkun Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A typed snapshot-settlement contract is implemented to arbitrate concurrent LLM-agent actions, and audited for order sensitivity, useful progress, and replay consistency. Joint policies are spatially order-invariant under fixed priorities, but conservative rejection is far less productive (31.25% vs 90.28% completion) than random tickets; journaling replays 156 checkpoints and rejects 1,332 corruptions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Concurrent actions in LLM agent environments need arbitration even when each proposal is individually valid, so the paper aims to specify and empirically test execution semantics for such settlement rather than human realism or long-run fairness.

Method: A typed snapshot-settlement contract is designed, and five settlement policies are evaluated across 28,800 exhaustive permutation trials plus 2,160 scripted multistep episodes, auditing order sensitivity, useful progress, and replay consistency. A separate full-state journal audit tests exact replay and corruption rejection with a retained terminal anchor.

Result: Joint policies are spatially order-invariant conditional on fixed priorities. In a six-agent doorway task, conservative rejection completes only 31.25% of agents versus 90.28% for random tickets, a paired improvement of 59.03 percentage points (95% bootstrap interval: 50.00-68.06). All policies preserve tested spatial constraints, yet priority arbitration still misses the independent small-instance optimum. The full-state journal audit exactly replays 156 checkpoints and rejects 1,332 constructed corruptions.

Conclusion: The snapshot-settlement contract yields measurable differences in useful progress and order behavior across settlement policies, while journaling supports exact replay and robust corruption rejection. The findings concern execution semantics only, not human realism or long-run fairness.

Abstract: Concurrent actions in large language model (LLM) agent environments require arbitration even when each proposal is individually valid. We implement a typed snapshot-settlement contract and audit three distinct properties: order sensitivity, useful progress, and replay consistency. Five settlement policies are tested in 28,800 exhaustive permutation trials and 2,160 scripted multistep episodes. Joint policies are spatially order-invariant conditional on fixed priorities, yet conservative rejection completes only 31.25% of agents in a six-agent doorway task versus 90.28% for random tickets; the paired improvement is 59.03 percentage points (95% bootstrap interval: 50.00-68.06). All policies preserve the tested spatial constraints, and priority arbitration still misses the independent small-instance optimum. A separate full-state journal audit exactly replays 156 checkpoints and rejects 1,332 constructed corruptions with a retained terminal anchor. The evidence concerns execution semantics, not human realism or long-run fairness.

</details>


### [158] [ReSolve: Reusing Candidate Reasoning through Selective Generative Moderation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01140)
*Bangji Yang,Jiajun Fan,Hongba Ma,Xi Zhu,Weizhi Zhang,Minghao Guo,Ye Li,Hamid Palangi,Jiaxuan You*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ReSolve is a training-free inference procedure that reuses intermediate reasoning from multiple sampled candidates via selective generative moderation, achieving up to 100/130 correct on competition math with fewer tokens than self-consistency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sampling multiple solutions wastes computation on intermediate deductions and unfinished arguments, not just final answers. The paper aims to reuse this discarded candidate reasoning as a reusable inference resource rather than discarding it after voting.

Method: ReSolve is a training-free selective generative moderation procedure. An answer-distribution controller invokes a model to examine existing derivations when candidates disagree or lack a parseable answer, then folds the generated solution into a bounded loop. It is compared against majority voting, 8-sample self-consistency, a derivation-ablated control, and always-on Uniform moderation under Hybrid scoring.

Result: On 130 competition-math problems with two independently sampled candidate pools, ReSolve scores 100 and 99 correct vs. 91 and 92 for voting over the same four candidates, with no correct-to-incorrect changes. Eight-sample self-consistency gets 94 and 96 while consuming 46.3% and 47.2% more tokens. Ablating visible derivations drops accuracy from 100 to 93 despite more computation. Selective and always-on Uniform moderation both solve 97 problems, but selectivity cuts moderation tokens ~54% and total pipeline tokens 6.2%.
