Conclusion: Candidate reasoning can serve as reusable inference computation. However, the results do not establish an accuracy advantage over additional sampling or a distinct benefit from specialized route instructions.

Abstract: Sampling multiple solutions spends computation on intermediate deductions and unfinished arguments as well as final answers. We introduce ReSolve, a training-free inference procedure that reuses this candidate reasoning through selective generative moderation. An answer-distribution controller invokes a model to examine existing derivations when candidates disagree or lack a parseable answer, then incorporates the generated solution into a bounded loop. Under Hybrid scoring on 130 competition-mathematics problems evaluated with two independently sampled candidate pools, ReSolve obtains 100 and 99 correct answers, compared with 91 and 92 for voting over the same four candidates, with no correct-to-incorrect changes relative to that vote in either pool. Eight-sample self-consistency obtains 94 and 96 correct answers while consuming substantially more tokens; ReSolve uses 46.3% and 47.2% fewer tokens in the two evaluations. A controlled ablation removes visible derivations while retaining answer keys, vote counts, and the per-state output-cap rule, reducing accuracy from 100 to 93 correct despite increasing computation. Selective and always-on Uniform moderation both solve 97 problems, while selectivity reduces moderation tokens by approximately 54% and total pipeline tokens by 6.2%. These results support candidate reasoning as reusable inference computation. They do not establish an accuracy advantage over additional sampling or a distinct benefit from specialized route instructions.

</details>


### [159] [When Does Exercise-Specific Joint Selection Help? An Audit of Evaluation and Control Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01188)
*Haotian Chen,Jingkun Yu,Yuning Zhang,Bowen Ye*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An exploratory audit of exercise-specific joint selection for skeleton-based correctness classification shows that the reported gain is highly sensitive to the evaluation estimand and to structurally appropriate controls, and therefore does not establish a new algorithm or clinical benefit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Exercise-specific joint selection has been claimed to improve skeleton-based correctness classification, but the robustness and interpretation of this gain are unclear. The authors aim to determine what, if anything, the gain actually establishes by probing evaluation aggregation, subset structure, and temporal representation.

Method: The authors audit 1,057 repetitions from ten REHAB24-6 subjects. They compare pooled out-of-fold AUROC against equal-weight within-person AUROC, evaluate 1,000 dimension-matched random maps with varying structural matching (bilateral structure and trunk inclusion), test multiple classifiers (kNN, RBF-SVM, logistic regression, random-convolution comparator), and apply sequence-order and paired-seed controls.

Result: The manual-subset kNN gain drops from 0.055 (pooled out-of-fold AUROC) to 0.020 (equal-weight within-person AUROC), with both paired intervals including zero. Among 1,000 dimension-matched random maps, 14 match or exceed the manual pooled result, rising to 145 when bilateral structure and trunk inclusion are also matched. RBF-SVM shows a positive within-person gain, while logistic regression and the random-convolution comparator show negative point gains under that estimand.

Conclusion: The audit demonstrates that joint-selection claims are contingent on explicit estimands and structurally appropriate controls. The findings are exploratory and do not establish a new algorithm or clinical benefit, but they highlight methodological requirements for validating such claims.

Abstract: Exercise-specific joint selection can improve skeleton-based correctness classification, but what does that gain establish? We audit 1,057 repetitions from ten REHAB24-6 subjects, separating evaluation aggregation, subset structure, and temporal representation. The manual-subset kNN gain changes from 0.055 for pooled out-of-fold AUROC to 0.020 for equal-weight within-person AUROC; both paired intervals include zero. Among 1,000 dimension-matched random maps, 14 match or exceed the manual pooled result, versus 145 when bilateral structure and trunk inclusion are also matched. RBF-SVM retains a positive within-person gain, whereas logistic regression and a random-convolution comparator have negative point gains under that estimand. Sequence-order and paired-seed controls further qualify the interpretation. This exploratory audit shows why joint-selection claims require explicit estimands and structurally appropriate controls; it does not establish a new algorithm or clinical benefit.

</details>


### [160] [Federated Agent Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01195)
*Qiang Yang,Zhiqiang Kou,Xueyi Zhang,Dong-Dong Wu,Hanlin Gu,Jing Guo,Yang Liu,Di Jiang,Qian Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper formulates Federated Agent Optimization (FAO), a framework for distributed LLM agents to collaboratively improve via controlled information exchange while preserving privacy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents accumulate valuable experience (tasks, tools, feedback, local knowledge), but this experience is siloed across organizations due to privacy and proprietary constraints. Conventional federated learning is inadequate because agent capabilities extend beyond model parameters to memory, tools, rewards, skills, and structured knowledge.

Method: The authors formulate FAO as a multi-objective problem balancing agent utility, privacy leakage, and communication cost. They organize the optimization space across policy, memory, tool use, reward, and structured knowledge/skills, and characterize how private experience can be abstracted, protected, aggregated, and adapted into transferable capabilities.

Result: The paper provides a unified view of how agents can benefit from one another without direct experience sharing, while keeping raw data, complete trajectories, and private knowledge local.

Conclusion: The authors identify key challenges of FAO and outline promising future research directions toward trustworthy federated agent systems.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly operate in private environments and accumulate valuable experience from task execution, tool use, feedback, and local knowledge. Yet such experience is distributed across organizations and cannot be directly shared because of privacy and proprietary constraints. Conventional federated learning is insufficient for this setting, as agent capabilities extend beyond model parameters to memory, tools, rewards, skills, and structured knowledge. In this paper, we formulate \textbf{Federated Agent Optimization (FAO)}, which studies how distributed agents can collaboratively improve through controlled information exchange while keeping raw data, complete trajectories, and private knowledge local. We define FAO as a multi-objective problem balancing agent utility, privacy leakage, and communication cost, and organize its optimization space across policy, memory, tool use, reward, and structured knowledge and skills. We further characterize how private experience can be abstracted, protected, aggregated, and adapted into transferable capabilities, providing a unified view of how agents can benefit from one another without direct experience sharing. Finally, we identify the key challenges of FAO and outline several promising directions for future research toward trustworthy federated agent systems.

</details>


### [161] [Dependency-Aware Reward Shaping for Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01207)
*Ziyi Chen,Yan Zhang,Jianhui Wei,Daoan Zhang,Zuozhu Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DARS (Dependency-Aware Reward Shaping) assigns step-level credit over a predicate dependency graph so that progress on independent work is rewarded even in failed episodes, improving success on reasoning and agentic tasks without changing base algorithms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Terminal (success/failure) rewards give little guidance on which steps matter. Existing step-credit methods overlook that work built on uncorrected mistakes is wasted while independent work remains valid; with only a final reward, every step in a failed episode has zero future reward even when it made progress.

Method: Represent task progress as predicates linked by prerequisite relations. An annotator marks which predicates each step verifies, invalidates, or repairs. Verified predicates are discounted by graph distance from the nearest broken prerequisite; independent predicates are unaffected. Repairs update weights based on remaining errors; invalidated predicates need re-verification to regain credit. A fixed potential converts annotations into signed per-step rewards. A common reward/annotation interface lets DARS plug into methods like GiGPO and ARPO/AEPO without altering rollouts or optimizers.

Result: Across five task families and 1.5B–8B models: up to +10 points success over GiGPO under the same budget/harness (ALFWorld); raises WebShop task score and Search-R1 QA accuracy; complements AEPO's entropy-based training on AIME24/25 with a Python interpreter; exceeds OmniOPD in controlled tool-free reasoning at 1.7B and 4B. Ablations confirm step-level credit, dependency attenuation, and graph topology each contribute. A distilled 8B annotator matches the API annotator on ALFWorld.

Conclusion: Dependency-aware reward shaping provides effective, compatible step-level credit by modeling prerequisite structure, yielding consistent gains and enabling efficient deployment with a distilled local annotator.

Abstract: When training large language models with reinforcement learning, terminal rewards provide little guidance about which steps matter. Common methods for assigning step credit overlook that work built on uncorrected mistakes is wasted while independent work remains valid. With only a final success/failure reward, every step in a failed episode has zero total future reward, even when it made progress. We propose Dependency-Aware Reward Shaping (DARS), which represents task progress as predicates linked by prerequisite relations and assigns step-level credit over the dependency graph. An annotator marks which predicates each step verifies, invalidates, or repairs. Verified predicates are discounted according to graph distance from the nearest broken prerequisite, while independent predicates are unaffected. Repairs update these weights based on any errors that remain; invalidated predicates need re-verification to regain credit. A fixed potential converts these annotations into signed per-step rewards. A common reward and annotation interface allows DARS to integrate with a range of reasoning and agentic training methods, such as GiGPO and ARPO/AEPO, without changing their rollout strategies or optimizers. Across five task families and models from 1.5B to 8B, DARS improves success by up to 10 points over GiGPO trained with the same budget and harness (ALFWorld), raises the WebShop task score and Search-R1 QA accuracy, complements AEPO's entropy-based training on AIME24/25 with a Python interpreter, and exceeds OmniOPD in controlled tool-free reasoning comparisons at 1.7B and 4B. Ablations show that step-level credit, dependency attenuation, and graph topology each contribute. On ALFWorld, a distilled 8B annotator matches the API annotator, enabling DARS to run efficiently without a frontier judge. Code is available at https://github.com/JianhuiWei7/DARS.

</details>


### [162] [Reputation, Strategy, and Emotion Effects on Generative AI Cooperation: A Comparison Across Reasoning and Non-Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01222)
*Celso de Melo,Zishan Feng,James Hale,Kazunori Terada,Giorgio Coricelli,Jonathan Gratch*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Frontier Gen AI models show human-like, cue-driven cooperation in iterated prisoner's dilemma games; reasoning models rely more on strategy and reputation, drop the "Potemkin" signaling effect, and display distinct end-game exploitability profiles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As Gen AI systems take on autonomous roles in economically and socially consequential interactions, understanding their propensity to cooperate—and the signals shaping it—becomes essential for responsible deployment in negotiation and other multi-round settings.

Method: Iterated prisoner's dilemma experiments manipulating counterpart reputation (positive/unknown/negative), strategy (extortion vs. generosity), and non-verbal emotional signaling (competitive/cooperative facial expressions). Study 1 tested non-reasoning models (Claude 3.5, Gemini 2.0 Flash, GPT-4o); Study 2 tested reasoning models (Claude 4.6, Gemini 3, GPT-5.2), including a diagnostic harmony game and end-game analysis.

Result: Non-reasoning models' cooperation was shaped by all three factors, paralleling human behavioral findings but with model-specific weighting. Reasoning models concentrated on strategy and reputation, nearly eliminated the Potemkin effect, showed a more conditional (hierarchically weighted) role for emotion, and exhibited heterogeneous end-game behavior from sustained cooperation to systematic last-round defection.

Conclusion: Gen AI models are increasingly sophisticated yet heterogeneous social actors. Standardized cooperation benchmarks are practically valuable to inform responsible deployment of Gen AI in interactive, socially consequential settings.

Abstract: As generative AI (Gen AI) systems take on increasingly autonomous roles in economically and socially consequential interactions, understanding their propensity to cooperate -- and the signals that shape this propensity -- has become essential. We examine cooperative behavior in frontier Gen AI models using the iterated prisoner's dilemma, manipulating counterpart reputation (positive, unknown, negative), strategy (extortion vs. generosity), and non-verbal emotional signaling (facial expressions conveying competitive or cooperative appraisals). In a first study with non-reasoning models (Claude 3.5, Gemini 2.0 Flash, GPT-4o), cooperation was systematically shaped by all three factors, paralleling patterns long documented in human behavioral research, though models varied substantially in how heavily each factor was weighted. A second study with reasoning models (Claude 4.6, Gemini 3, GPT-5.2) revealed a more concentrated reliance on strategy and reputation, a near-elimination of the Potemkin effect observed in non-reasoning models (evidenced by near-uniform cooperation in a diagnostic harmony game), and a more conditional role for emotion consistent with a hierarchical cue-weighting strategy rather than a simple loss of social sensitivity. Reasoning models also showed heterogeneous end-game behavior, ranging from sustained cooperation to systematic last-round defection effect, revealing model-specific exploitability profiles with direct practical relevance for deployment in negotiation and other multi-round interactions. Together, these findings characterize Gen AI models as increasingly sophisticated, though heterogeneous, social actors, and underscore the practical value of developing standardized cooperation benchmarks to inform the responsible deployment of Gen AI in interactive, socially consequential settings.

</details>


### [163] [HHR: Hierarchical Hash Retrieval for Efficient LLM Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01230)
*Lianjun Liu,Tiantian Zheng,You Huang,Weiqi Yan,Mingte Qiu,Huazhong Liu,Xiaofeng Zhu,Yunshan Zhong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HHR is a coarse-to-fine hash-based retrieval framework for efficient long-context LLM inference that aligns Hamming distance with attention relevance via two learned components, improving accuracy and speed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-context inference in LLMs is a computational bottleneck. Hash-based retrieval is efficient but suffers a mismatch between Hamming distance and attention relevance: QK logits depend on both directional similarity and feature magnitude, while hashing discards magnitude, causing false-positive retrieval of low-logit keys and false-negative omission of high-logit keys.

Method: HHR is a coarse-to-fine framework with two components: (1) Geometry-Aware Key Routing (GKR), which learns a head-wise orthogonal transformation to redistribute feature magnitudes and derive more discriminative page-level logit bounds for pruning low-logit keys; and (2) Learned Hash Projection (LHP), which learns a head-wise projection space aligning Hamming distance with true Query-Key relevance ranking for fine-grained retrieval.

Result: On LongBench, HHR improves the average score by 1.10 points. At 128K context length, it achieves up to 3.30x decoding speedup and 2.83x end-to-end speedup for Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, outperforming existing methods across diverse LLMs and benchmarks.

Conclusion: By combining GKR and LHP, HHR suppresses false positives and recovers false negatives, substantially improving the fidelity of hash-based sparse attention and delivering superior accuracy and efficiency for long-context inference.

Abstract: Efficient long-context inference is essential for large language models (LLMs), yet it poses a severe computational bottleneck. Hash-based retrieval offers an efficient alternative by encoding queries and keys into binary codes and using Hamming distance for key selection. However, this leads to a critical mismatch between Hamming distance and attention relevance. Query-Key logits depend jointly on directional similarity and feature magnitudes, whereas hash binarization discards magnitude information, causing both false-positive retrieval of low-logit keys and false-negative omission of high-logit keys. To address these failures, we propose Hierarchical Hash Retrieval (HHR), a coarse-to-fine framework that progressively improves retrieval accuracy through Geometry-Aware Key Routing (GKR) and Learned Hash Projection (LHP). GKR learns a head-wise orthogonal transformation to redistribute feature magnitudes and derive more discriminative page-level logit bounds, enabling effective pruning of low-logit keys while preserving important candidates. LHP then learns a head-wise projection space that aligns Hamming distance with the true Query-Key relevance ranking for fine-grained retrieval. By combining GKR and LHP, HHR suppresses false positives and recovers false negatives, substantially improving the fidelity of hash-based sparse attention. Extensive experiments across diverse LLMs and benchmarks demonstrate that HHR achieves superior performance over existing methods. For example, on LongBench, HHR improves the average score by 1.10 points and, at a context length of 128K, achieves up to a 3.30x decoding speedup and a 2.83x end-to-end speedup for Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct. The code is publicly available at https://github.com/lianjunl13-sudo/HHR.

</details>


### [164] [Learning to Ask: Information Acquisition for SLM-LLM Collaboration, under a budget](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01236)
*Yongjun Kim,Xiaoxiao Li,Jaeho Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper reframes SLM-LLM collaboration from computation allocation to information acquisition under an API budget, using a three-stage RLVR framework where the SLM selectively queries a black-box LLM advisor, improving performance-cost tradeoffs and transferability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing SLM-LLM collaboration approaches frame the problem as computation allocation—deciding which model handles each reasoning portion. In black-box API settings, this paradigm is inefficient due to coarse-grained delegation and repeated context transmission across model switches, creating a need for a more efficient collaboration formulation.

Method: The authors formulate SLM-LLM collaboration as an information acquisition problem under an API budget constraint. The SLM acts as the primary reasoner and selectively queries a black-box LLM advisor with targeted queries rather than delegating reasoning. They develop a three-stage RLVR framework that learns whether to call the advisor, how to formulate useful queries, and how to integrate collaboration into reasoning by jointly refining advisor invocation and information use.

Result: Across mathematical reasoning and coding tasks, the approach improves the performance-cost tradeoff over existing collaboration baselines and, in some settings, matches or exceeds oracle problem-level routing. The strategy also transfers to other advisor model families without further training.

Conclusion: Formulating SLM-LLM collaboration as information acquisition under an API budget, realized via a three-stage RLVR framework, yields better efficiency and transferability than computation-allocation-based collaboration methods.

Abstract: Collaboration between a small language model (SLM) and a large language model (LLM) offers an opportunity to combine the efficiency of smaller models with the strong reasoning capabilities of larger ones. Existing approaches primarily frame such collaboration as a computation allocation problem, determining which model should handle each portion of the reasoning process. In black-box API-based settings, however, this paradigm can be inefficient due to coarse-grained delegation or repeated transmission of context across model switches. In this work, we instead formulate SLM-LLM collaboration as an information acquisition problem, under an API budget constraint. The SLM remains the primary reasoner and selectively queries a black-box LLM advisor only when needed, issuing targeted queries rather than delegating the reasoning process itself. To realize this strategy, we develop a three-stage RLVR framework that learns whether to call the advisor, how to formulate useful queries, and how to integrate the collaboration into the reasoning process by jointly refining advisor invocation and information use. Across mathematical reasoning and coding tasks, our approach improves the performance--cost tradeoff over existing collaboration baselines and, in some settings, matches or exceeds oracle problem-level routing. Finally, we show that our strategy can transfer to other advisor model families, without further training.

</details>


### [165] [Revision-Aware Independent Agent Graphs for Dynamic Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01249)
*Yan Luo,Selim-Antoine Lali,Jeremy Moebel,Iliass Khoutaibi,Ahmadou Aidara,Mengyu Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces dynamic task routing, where an event stream revises document-version bindings and the system must pick the valid version at query time. It repurposes six benchmarks into 31,119 dynamic episodes with 373,428 temporally categorized queries, and proposes RIAG, a bounded multi-agent policy separating temporal resolution from reasoning. Homogeneous RIAG achieves 54.24% joint routing-and-answer accuracy at 0.62 calls/query versus 32.22% at 18.00 calls/query for the strongest baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional reasoning protocols present a fixed, preselected task, so they cannot test whether an agent propagates relevant updates, preserves unaffected work, or reconstructs a historical task binding. The authors want to evaluate agents under evolving task bindings, exposing a central trade-off: recomputing after every event wastes work, while unguarded reuse returns stale conclusions.

Method: The authors repurpose six widely used benchmarks (MMLU, MMLU-Pro, MedMCQA, MATH, GPQA, HumanEval) into 31,119 dynamic episodes comprising 373,428 temporally categorized queries. They propose the Revision-Aware Independent Agent Graph (RIAG), a bounded multi-agent policy that separates deterministic temporal resolution from task reasoning. RIAG caches solutions by immutable document identity, starts each fresh task with two unexposed attempts, conditionally invokes audit and repair, and uses at most four calls per document version.

Result: Homogeneous RIAG achieves 54.24% joint routing-and-answer accuracy at 0.62 calls/query, compared with 32.22% at 18.00 calls/query for the strongest comparison method. Heterogeneous RIAG reaches 49.78% at 0.63 calls/query.

Conclusion: RIAG substantially outperforms the strongest baseline on joint routing-and-answer accuracy while using roughly 29x fewer calls per query, showing that separating deterministic temporal resolution from task reasoning enables efficient and accurate handling of dynamically revised task bindings.

Abstract: Conventional reasoning protocols present a fixed, preselected task, so they cannot test whether an agent propagates relevant updates, preserves unaffected work, or reconstructs a historical task binding. We therefore study \emph{dynamic task routing}, in which an event stream revises task bindings and a system must select the document version valid at each query time before solving it. To study this problem, we repurpose six widely used benchmarks: MMLU, MMLU-Pro, MedMCQA, MATH, GPQA, and HumanEval into 31{,}119 dynamic episodes comprising 373{,}428 temporally categorized queries. This setting exposes a central trade-off: recomputing after every event wastes work, whereas unguarded reuse returns stale conclusions. We introduce the Revision-Aware Independent Agent Graph (RIAG), a bounded multi-agent policy that separates deterministic temporal resolution from task reasoning. RIAG caches solutions by immutable document identity, starts each fresh task with two unexposed attempts, and conditionally invokes audit and repair, using at most four calls per document version. On this collection, homogeneous RIAG achieves 54.24\% joint routing-and-answer accuracy at 0.62 calls/query, compared with 32.22\% at 18.00 calls/query for the strongest comparison method; heterogeneous RIAG reaches 49.78\% at 0.63 calls/query.

</details>


### [166] [DeFA: Dependency-Guided Failure Attribution for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01256)
*Bo Deng,Xinlei Zheng,Yi Wei,Kang Zhou,Chongyang Tao,Renzhao Liang,Xuanren Chen,Lifan Guo,Chi Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DeFA is a dependency-guided framework for LLM agent failure attribution that builds an event dependency graph, traces failure propagation, and localizes decisive errors via segment-wise diagnosis with global context.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In LLM agent executions, errors and their visible consequences are often separated by many steps, so localizing the decisive error requires understanding both step content and step dependencies. This makes failure attribution challenging, especially in long trajectories.

Method: DeFA (1) combines protocol relations and semantic dependencies into an event dependency graph spanning the trajectory; (2) identifies events that may violate task requirements and traces their sources and subsequent effects to build a failure propagation graph; (3) uses step evidence and each step's role in failure propagation to identify the decisive error, responsible agent, and error category. For long trajectories, it partitions executions into segments and combines the current segment's detailed content with summaries of other segments, giving local diagnosis access to global context.

Result: On Who and When and the Who and When Pro text subset, DeFA achieves the highest responsible-agent and exact step accuracy across all evaluated backbones, and the highest failure-mode accuracy among taxonomy-aligned methods on Pro. It also generalizes to image and video trajectories for multimodal failure attribution. Ablations confirm the contributions of segmentation, the event dependency graph, and the failure propagation graph. Using DeFA's diagnostic feedback for skill evolution in Trace2Skill improves downstream task accuracy by 6-15 percentage points over the native pipeline.

Conclusion: DeFA provides effective, dependency-guided failure attribution for LLM agents that scales to long and multimodal trajectories, and its diagnostic feedback can also drive agent improvement on subsequent tasks.

Abstract: Errors in LLM agent executions and their visible consequences can be separated by many steps, making decisive-error localization a matter of understanding both step content and step dependencies. We introduce DeFA, a dependency-guided framework for agent failure attribution. DeFA first combines protocol relations and semantic dependencies into an event dependency graph spanning the trajectory. It then identifies events that may violate task requirements and traces their sources and subsequent effects to construct a failure propagation graph. Finally, DeFA uses step evidence and the steps' roles in failure propagation to identify the decisive error, responsible agent, and error category. To support long trajectories, DeFA partitions executions into segments and combines the current segment's detailed content with summaries of the other segments, giving local diagnosis access to global execution context. Across Who and When and the Who and When Pro text subset, DeFA achieves the highest responsible-agent and exact step accuracy with all evaluated backbones, and the highest failure-mode accuracy among taxonomy-aligned methods on Pro. Further experiments on image and video trajectories demonstrate its applicability to multimodal failure attribution. Ablations support the contributions of segmentation, the event dependency graph, and the failure propagation graph. Using DeFA's diagnostic feedback for skill evolution in Trace2Skill improves downstream task accuracy by 6-15 percentage points over the native pipeline, showing that the diagnoses can also support agent improvement on subsequent tasks.

</details>


### [167] [Feedback Without the Wait: Piloting a Generative AI Practice Platform in a Large Maths Class](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01262)
*Lili Chen,Gavin Buskes,Yuxin Ren,Chin Tong Leong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This work designs and pilots a Generative AI practice platform that provides immediate, scaffolded feedback during self-directed practice in a large electrical engineering class, shifting human oversight from real-time grading to upfront verification of solutions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Timely, specific feedback strongly influences student learning but is hard to sustain in large electrical engineering classes with high student-to-demonstrator ratios, where stuck learners may wait days for explanations. While GenAI can scale conversational feedback, using it for assessed work raises trust and accountability concerns, and keeping a human in the loop reintroduces the delay that erodes feedback value. This creates a tension between immediacy and human oversight.

Method: The authors designed and piloted a GenAI practice platform that delivers immediate, scaffolded feedback during self-directed practice. Rather than relying on real-time human grading, the approach relocates human oversight to the upfront verification of solutions, ensuring trustworthy feedback content while preserving immediacy during student practice.

Result: The study examined how students engaged with the tool, how they perceived the value and reliability of its feedback, and which lessons transfer to other engineering subjects. (Specific quantitative or qualitative findings are not detailed in the provided abstract.)

Conclusion: The proposed design resolves, in practice, the tension between immediate feedback and human oversight by moving human verification upstream. The pilot yields insights into student engagement, perceived feedback value and reliability, and transferable lessons for other engineering subjects.

Abstract: Timely and specific feedback is one of the strongest influences on student learning, yet it is difficult to sustain in large electrical engineering classes where the ratio of students to demonstrators is high and a learner who is stuck may wait days to find out why an approach was wrong. Generative Artificial Intelligence (GenAI) offers a way to scale conversational feedback, but using it to grade assessed work raises trust and accountability concerns, and keeping a human in the loop to assure its judgements reintroduces the very delay that erodes the value of feedback. The result is a tension between the immediacy that makes feedback so impactful and the human oversight that makes it trustworthy. In this work, we set out to resolve that tension in practice by designing and piloting a GenAI practice platform that delivers immediate, scaffolded feedback during self-directed practice. This relocates human oversight from real-time grading to the upfront verification of solutions. Our goal was to understand how students engaged with the tool, how they perceived the value and reliability of its feedback, and what lessons transfer to other engineering subjects.

</details>


### [168] [SCOPE-AD: Sequential cost-aware ordinal-belief planning with energy-based models for diagnostic agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01278)
*Ziwen Yu,Ivan Koychev,Elizabeth Coulthard,Ting Zhou,Bolin Chen,Dian Hong,Zinuo You,Yujiao Wang,Anthony Mulholland,Qiang Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SCOPE-AD is a sequential, cost-aware diagnostic agent for Alzheimer's disease that jointly learns which test to acquire and when evidence is sufficient, achieving 77.70% Macro-F1 at $50.46 average cost on ADNI.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AD diagnosis requires sequential evidence acquisition under heterogeneous test costs and patient burden. Fixed-modality predictors neither decide which test to acquire nor when available evidence suffices for diagnosis, limiting cost-effectiveness and clinical practicality.

Method: SCOPE-AD (Sequential Cost-Aware Ordinal-Belief Planning with Energy-Based Models). A mask-aware ordinal model encodes uncertainty along the ordered CN–MCI–AD continuum. Retrospective training records supply sampled Bellman targets for an energy-based teacher, whose action distributions are distilled into a Qwen policy. At deployment, the agent chooses acquisition or diagnosis actions under availability and budget constraints, without observing unacquired values, updating evidence and ordinal belief after each acquisition.

Result: On ADNI, SCOPE-AD reaches 77.70% Macro-F1 at an average acquisition cost of $50.46, surpassing the strongest evaluated baseline by 9.34 percentage points. Full-modality evaluation improves Macro-F1 by only 1.89 points while raising acquisition cost 116.7-fold.

Conclusion: Selective, sequential acquisition enables cost-effective AD diagnosis; marginal accuracy gains from full-modality evaluation come at prohibitive cost, supporting adaptive test selection under budget constraints.

Abstract: Alzheimer's disease (AD) diagnosis requires sequential evidence acquisition under heterogeneous test costs and patient burden. Fixed-modality predictors do not jointly decide which test to acquire or when the available evidence is sufficient for diagnosis. We propose SCOPE-AD (Sequential Cost-Aware Ordinal-Belief Planning with Energy-Based Models for Diagnostic Agents) for cost-aware classification of cognitively normal (CN), mild cognitive impairment (MCI), and AD cases. A mask-aware ordinal model represents uncertainty along the ordered CN--MCI--AD continuum. Retrospective training records provide sampled Bellman targets for an energy-based teacher, whose action distributions are distilled into a Qwen policy. At deployment, the agent selects acquisition or diagnosis actions under availability and budget constraints without access to unacquired values. After each acquisition, the evidence and ordinal belief are updated before the next decision. On ADNI, SCOPE-AD achieves 77.70\% Macro-F1 at an average acquisition cost of \$50.46, exceeding the strongest evaluated baseline by 9.34 percentage points. Full-modality evaluation raises Macro-F1 by only 1.89 points while increasing acquisition cost by 116.7 times. These results support selective acquisition for cost-effective diagnosis.

</details>


### [169] [Trustworthy Data- and ML-Ops for Intelligent Transportation Systems and Logistics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01282)
*Antonio Emanuele Cinà,Giovanni Scodeller,Cecilia Caterina Pasquale,Silvia Siri,Davide Anguita,Fabio Roli,Simona Sacone,Luca Oneto*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A comprehensive review of Trustworthy DataOps and MLOps in Intelligent Transportation Systems and Logistics (ITS&L), covering their necessity, components, tools, case studies, trustworthiness concerns, and open challenges, intended as a foundational reference for researchers, practitioners, and policy makers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ITS&L is a cornerstone of the modern social economy but depends heavily on integrating Data, AI, and ML. Current literature lacks a consolidated treatment of Trustworthy DataOps and MLOps specifically for ITS&L, so the paper identifies these gaps to justify its contribution.

Method: A narrative/comprehensive literature review. The authors first identify gaps in existing work, then examine the complexities of DataOps and MLOps (necessity, key components, available tools, practical insights, and case studies within ITS&L), and subsequently analyze methods and tools for ensuring Trustworthiness of AI in real-world ITS&L settings.

Result: The review consolidates context on DataOps and MLOps practices and tooling for ITS&L, presents practical insights and case studies, and surveys trustworthiness-enhancing methods and tools for AI systems in ITS&L, while also cataloguing persisting challenges and future prospects.

Conclusion: The paper establishes a foundational understanding of DataOps and MLOps in ITS&L and charts a path for future research and innovation toward more efficient, sustainable, and trustworthy intelligent transportation and logistics systems, serving as a resource for researchers, industry practitioners, and policy makers.

Abstract: The rapid evolution of Intelligent Transportation Systems and Logistics (ITS\&L) has become a cornerstone of the modern social economy, relying heavily on the integration of Data, Artificial Intelligence (AI), and, more specifically, Machine Learning (ML). This paper provides a comprehensive review of Trustworthy Data and Machine Learning Operations (DataOps and MLOps) in the ITS\&L domain, underscoring their importance in improving efficiency, reliability, and decision-making precision within transportation and logistics services. We begin by identifying gaps in current literature, offering clear context for our contribution. Subsequently, we explore the complexities of DataOps and MLOps, discussing their necessity, key components, available tools, practical insights, and case studies relevant to ITS\&L. Additionally, we address the critical issue of Trustworthiness in AI applications, examining methods and tools designed to strengthen confidence in AI systems - especially in real-world ITS\&L scenarios. The paper concludes with a discussion of persisting challenges and future prospects in this rapidly advancing field, aiming to serve as a vital resource for researchers, industry practitioners, and policy makers. Overall, this work not only establishes a foundational understanding of DataOps and MLOps in ITS\&L but also charts a path for further research and innovation in developing more efficient, sustainable, and trustworthy intelligent transportation and logistics systems.

</details>


### [170] [ITC-MoE: Importance-guided Token-aware Compression for MoE Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01296)
*Lianjun Liu,Shipeng Li,You Huang,Weiqi Yan,Mingte Qiu,Huazhong Liu,Xiaofeng Zhu,Yunshan Zhong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ITC-MoE is an importance-guided, token-aware compression framework for Mixture-of-Experts Diffusion Language Models that jointly factorizes expert weights with adaptive rank allocation and applies token-aware compensation and routing to cut computation and storage while preserving generation quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MoE DLMs offer flexible parallel decoding and high model capacity, but their many expert parameters incur substantial computation and storage costs. Existing low-rank MoE compression methods rely on static factorization and fixed rank allocation, ignoring two distinctive properties of MoE DLMs: cross-mode non-uniform redundancy (redundancy and rank-truncation sensitivity vary across input, output, and expert modes) and token-wise utilization variation (hot and cold tokens show distinct spectral characteristics and expert activation patterns).

Method: ITC-MoE has two components. (1) Importance-guided Adaptive Tucker Compression (IATC): incorporates activation and gradient importance into expert weight transformation, jointly factorizes expert weights across multiple modes, and adaptively allocates ranks under a fixed parameter budget. (2) Token-aware Compensation and Routing (TCR): applies lightweight low-rank compensation to compression-sensitive hot tokens and restricts the candidate expert set for cold tokens with concentrated routing patterns, jointly adapting compression capacity and inference execution to parameter redundancy and token-wise variation.

Result: On SDAR-30B-A3B-Chat-b32, ITC-MoE maintains 96.33% accuracy on MultiArith under a 30% compression budget while achieving up to a 7.22x end-to-end speedup.

Conclusion: By jointly adapting compression capacity and inference execution to both parameter redundancy and token-wise variation, ITC-MoE substantially reduces the computation and storage costs of MoE DLMs while preserving generation quality. Code is publicly available.

Abstract: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) Diffusion Language Models (DLMs) offer flexible parallel decoding and increased model capacity, but their large number of expert parameters incurs substantial computation and storage costs. Existing low-rank MoE compression methods largely rely on static factorization and fixed rank allocation, which overlook the distinctive properties of MoE DLMs. Specifically, we identify two properties: cross-mode non-uniform redundancy, where parameter redundancy and sensitivity to rank truncation vary across the input, output, and expert modes, and token-wise utilization variation, where hot and cold tokens exhibit distinct spectral characteristics and expert activation patterns. To address these challenges, we propose ITC-MoE, an Importance-guided Token-aware Compression framework for MoE DLMs. ITC-MoE consists of two complementary components. First, Importance-guided Adaptive Tucker Compression (IATC) incorporates activation and gradient importance into expert weight transformation, jointly factorizes expert weights across multiple modes, and adaptively allocates ranks under a fixed parameter budget. Second, Token-aware Compensation and Routing (TCR) applies lightweight low-rank compensation to compression-sensitive hot tokens and restricts the candidate expert set for cold tokens with concentrated routing patterns. By jointly adapting compression capacity and inference execution to both parameter redundancy and token-wise variation, ITC-MoE substantially reduces the computation and storage costs of MoE DLMs while preserving their generation quality. For example, on SDAR-30B-A3B-Chat-b32, ITC-MoE maintains an accuracy of 96.33% on MultiArith under a 30% compression budget, while achieving up to a 7.22x end-to-end speedup. The code is publicly available at https://github.com/lianjunl13-sudo/ITC-MoE.

</details>


### [171] [Questionnaire-Guided Disaggregation of Energy Appliance Use for Domestic Smart Meter Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01297)
*Achal Nanjundamurthy,Rupam Misra,Suzanne Little,Alan F. Smeaton*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A label-free appliance disaggregation system splits 30-minute Irish smart meter data into 9 appliance categories using event detection for high-power loads and questionnaire-guided estimation, outperforming two comparators on multiple datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ireland's smart meters, installed in over 80% of households by late 2025, record electricity use at 30-minute resolution. This granularity suffices for billing time-of-use tariffs but is too coarse to identify domestic appliance usage, motivating a disaggregation approach.

Method: A label-free disaggregation system combines event detection for high-power loads with questionnaire-guided estimation to break usage data into 9 appliance categories. Evaluation uses four datasets: a calibration household with a commercial comparator, two public benchmarks (UK-DALE and REFIT) with per-appliance sub-metering, and a smart meter dataset covering over 4,800 years of use from 2,968 Irish consumers.

Result: Against two independently developed disaggregation systems, the hybrid method (usage data analysis plus questionnaire results) achieves the lowest whole-decomposition error on all buildings across the datasets, with better month-level performance over 54 paired months ($p<0.001$, Holm-corrected).

Conclusion: The method offers useful advice on household energy consumption patterns and on reducing or shifting usage of certain appliances to cut costs.

Abstract: Ireland's smart metering programme records electricity use at 30-minute resolution, with smart meters installed in over 80\% of households as of late 2025. While this is useful for billing of smart, time-of-use tariffs, it is too coarse to capture use of domestic appliances. We present a label-free disaggregation system that breaks usage data into 9 appliance categories by combining event detection for high-power loads with questionnaire-guided estimation. Our evaluation draws on four datasets: a calibration household with a commercial comparator, two public benchmarks (UK-DALE and REFIT) with per-appliance sub-metering, and a smart meter dataset of more than 4,800 years of use from 2,968 Irish consumers. Compared against two independently developed disaggregation systems our hybrid method combining analysis of usage data with questionnaire results, achieves the lowest whole-decomposition error on all buildings across the datasets, with better month-level performance over 54 paired months ($p<0.001$, Holm-corrected). Our method provides useful advice on a household's energy consumption patterns and advice on how to reduce or shift usage on some appliances in order to reduce costs.

</details>


### [172] [DAYJOB: A Benchmark for Long-Horizon Professional Work](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01306)
*Stephanie Finley,Liudas Panavas,Thomas Mikkelson,Cam Hinton,Stacey Ganss,Bradley Monton,Emily Kendall,Michelle Spradlin,Lydia Bye,Michael O'Brien,Lauren Ylvisaker,Derek Ray,Suhaas Garre,Sushant Mehta,Edwin Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DAYJOB is a benchmark of 130 professional tasks (50 healthcare, 80 finance) from real brief requests, graded by expert rubrics applied by an agentic judge. The best model configuration (Claude Opus 5.5) passes only ~24% of attempts, and the median configuration passes under 3%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Professional work typically begins with a brief request that leaves the professional to infer what is needed, which documents matter, and whether the request's premise holds. Existing evaluations do not capture this open-ended, judgment-heavy nature of professional tasks.

Method: The authors build DAYJOB, a benchmark of 130 tasks created by healthcare (50) and finance (80) professionals, estimated to take 13.6 (healthcare) and 16.6 (finance) hours on average. Each task is a containerized Harbor environment paired with an expert rubric of binary criteria (median 47.5 and 57.5 per task) that an agentic judge applies to the delivered files; a task passes only if every criterion is met.

Result: Evaluating 30 model configurations from 13 developers, the best (Claude Opus 5.5) passes 24.7% of healthcare and 23.9% of finance attempts, while the median configuration passes 0.6% and 2.5%. Case studies show agents accepting premises the record contradicts and propagating wrong inputs through otherwise consistent analyses.

Conclusion: Current models remain far from reliably completing realistic professional tasks. The authors release all healthcare tasks, 50 of the 80 finance tasks, the evaluation harness, and the leaderboard to support further research.

Abstract: Professional work often starts with a brief request that leaves the professional to work out what is needed, which documents matter, and whether the request's premise holds. We introduce DAYJOB, a benchmark of 130 tasks built by professionals in healthcare (50) and finance (80). The tasks are estimated to take a professional 13.6 hours on average in healthcare and 16.6 in finance. Each task is a containerized Harbor environment with an expert rubric of binary criteria (median 47.5 and 57.5 per task) that an agentic judge applies to the delivered files, and an attempt passes only if it meets every criterion. Across 30 model configurations from 13 developers, the strongest, Claude Opus 5.5, passes 24.7% of healthcare and 23.9% of finance attempts, and the median configuration passes 0.6% and 2.5%. In case studies, agents accept premises that the record contradicts and carry wrong inputs through otherwise consistent analyses. We release all healthcare tasks, 50 of the 80 finance tasks, the evaluation harness, and the leaderboard.

</details>


### [173] [ProtoFlow: Prototype-Guided Flow Matching for Multivariate Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01320)
*Shibo Feng,Wanjin Feng,Yang Qiu,Deheng Ye,Peilin Zhao,Chunyan Miao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ProtoFlow combines vector-quantized autoencoding with prototype-prior flow matching for efficient non-autoregressive multivariate time series forecasting, using the trained VQ codebook as an informative prior for a DiT-based rectified flow.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion-based MTS forecasting performs well but needs many inference sampling steps. VAE-based non-iterative frameworks with vector quantization (VQ) offer efficiency and controllable discrete latents, yet existing VQ methods rely on autoregressive token generation, which suffers exposure bias and training-inference mismatch. Flow matching is a non-autoregressive alternative, but existing formulations initialize transport from a generic Gaussian prior, ignoring the structure already learned in the codebook.

Method: ProtoFlow has three stages: (1) map multivariate sequences into a discrete latent space via vector-quantized autoencoding; (2) construct a structured prototype prior from the learned VQ codebook, which captures representative latent prototypes; (3) learn a DiT-based rectified flow that transports samples from this prototype prior to future latent representations, conditioned on historical observations.

Result: Extensive experiments on benchmark datasets show ProtoFlow consistently achieves superior forecasting performance with efficient inference. Replacing generic noise initialization with a learned prototype prior avoids the rollout mismatch of AR token prediction and promotes faster training convergence.

Conclusion: The trained VQ codebook serves as an informative prior for flow matching, yielding a non-autoregressive forecasting framework that sidesteps AR exposure bias while retaining efficient inference and strong accuracy.

Abstract: Generative modeling has shown strong promise for multivariate time mseries (MTS) forecasting, especially scale to high-dimensional settings. Diffusion-based methods achieve competitive performance but typically require many sampling steps at inference. VAE-based non-iterative forecasting frameworks have therefore emerged as an efficient alternative. Within this line of work, vector quantization (VQ) enables controllable latent space modeling by mapping multivariate series into compact discrete representations. Existing VQ-based forecasting methods, however, typically rely on autoregressive (AR) token generation, which suffers from exposure bias and training-inference mismatch. Flow matching provides an efficient non-autoregressive alternative for latent forecasting, but existing formulations usually initialize transport from a generic Gaussian prior. We instead observe that the trained VQ codebook already captures representative latent prototypes and can thus serve as a more informative prior for flow matching. Based on this insight, we propose ProtoFlow, a forecasting framework that combines vector-quantized autoencoding with Prototype-prior Flow matching. Our method first maps multivariate sequences into a discrete latent space, then constructs a structured prior from the learned codebook, and finally learns a DiT-based rectified flow to transport samples from this prior to future latent representations conditioned on historical observations. By replacing generic noise initialization with a learned prototype prior, ProtoFlow avoids the rollout mismatch of AR token prediction and promotes faster training convergence. Extensive experiments on benchmark datasets show that it consistently achieves superior forecasting performance with efficient inference.

</details>


### [174] [TRACE: Trajectory Return Attribution and Contrastive Erasure for Multi-Turn Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01323)
*Fengpeng Li,Kemou Li,Qizhou Wang,Haiwei Wu,Jiantao Zhou,Di Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TRACE is a token-level training objective that bounds multi-turn trajectory risk by attributing refusal credit across tokens and erasing harmful continuations, achieving the lowest ASR in all 35 model-attack pairs with minimal utility loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety-aligned LLMs can be jailbroken by spreading a harmful goal across multiple turns. Existing preference objectives score whole responses to single prompts, so their training loss cannot control risk on unseen multi-turn histories. The paper seeks sufficient conditions under which single-turn suppression bounds multi-turn trajectory risk.

Method: The analysis derives a risk bound accounting for coverage, transfer slack, and leakage, characterizing contraction relative to a base-policy risk budget evaluated on the trained policy's contexts. TRACE (Trajectory Return Attribution and Contrastive Erasure) operationalizes this: on safe responses, each token is weighted by the discounted return of a refusal-attributable advantage, computed by comparing a frozen reference model with its refusal-ablated copy so earlier tokens receive credit from later refusal evidence. At high-gap positions on rejected responses, TRACE combines the observed token with policy-selected alternatives as the erasure target, and a gradient-norm penalty replaces the retain set.

Result: Across five open-weight models and seven multi-turn attacks, TRACE achieves the lowest attack success rate (ASR) in all 35 model-attack pairs. Model utility on MMLU and HellaSwag drops by at most 1.23 points.

Conclusion: Token-level return attribution and contrastive erasure provide a principled, effective defense against multi-turn jailbreaks, substantially reducing ASR without materially degrading general model utility.

Abstract: Safety-aligned large language models (LLMs) often refuse a harmful request but comply once the same goal is spread over several turns. Preference objectives score whole responses to single prompts, so their training loss alone cannot control risk on unseen histories. Our analysis gives sufficient conditions under which suppression at supervised single-turn contexts yields a bound on multi-turn trajectory risk. The bound accounts for coverage, transfer slack, and leakage, and characterizes contraction relative to a base-policy risk budget evaluated on the trained policy's contexts. TRACE (Trajectory Return Attribution and Contrastive Erasure) turns this principle into a token-level objective. On the safe response, each token is weighted by the discounted return of a refusal-attributable advantage. The advantage compares a frozen reference model with its refusal-ablated copy, allowing earlier response tokens to receive credit from later refusal-related evidence. At high-gap positions on rejected responses, TRACE combines the observed token with policy-selected alternatives in the erasure target. A gradient-norm penalty replaces the retain set. Across five open-weight models and seven multi-turn attacks, TRACE gives the lowest attack success rate (ASR) in all 35 model and attack pairs, while the model utility evaluated on MMLU and HellaSwag drop by at most 1\.23 points. Source code can be found in the supplemental material.

</details>


### [175] [PPO-HRAP: Proximal Policy Optimization with a Hybrid Regime-Aware Policy for Risk-Controlled Trading](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01325)
*Duong Hien Chi Kien,Thanh Trung Huynh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PPO-HRAP blends a PPO actor with an interpretable regime prior and a VIX-conditioned reward to trade SPY, improving risk-adjusted returns and cutting max drawdown versus Buy-and-Hold.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RL trading struggles to balance upside participation and drawdown control: profit-only policies collapse to passive long exposure on drifting assets, while heavily risk-penalized rewards turn overly defensive in volatile markets. A method is needed that adapts exposure to market regimes while retaining learned flexibility.

Method: PPO-HRAP is a hybrid regime-aware policy combining Proximal Policy Optimization with an interpretable regime prior. The agent observes market features and portfolio-state variables; the reward combines portfolio log return, a VIX-conditioned drawdown-increase penalty, target-exposure deviation, and turnover cost. Actions are blended between the PPO actor output and a regime-derived target exposure.

Result: On the held-out 2020-2022 SPY test window: 27.62% total return, 8.48% annualized return, 0.6447 Sharpe, 0.8588 Sortino, 0.4592 Calmar, with max drawdown reduced from 34.10% (Buy and Hold) to 18.47%. Over five SPY seeds: mean total return 0.2725 ± 0.0109, mean Sharpe 0.6219 ± 0.0565. Single-run cross-asset tests on QQQ and DIA rank the method first on total return and Sharpe for all three assets.

Conclusion: Blending learned actions with a volatility-aware regime prior is a practical approach to improving risk-adjusted trading behavior. Limitations: the policy still incurs high turnover, and cross-asset robustness beyond SPY rests only on single-run evidence.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning for trading often struggles to balance upside participation with drawdown control. Profit-only policies can collapse toward passive long exposure on upward-drifting assets, while aggressively risk-penalized rewards can become too defensive during volatile periods. This paper proposes PPO-HRAP, a hybrid regime-aware policy that combines Proximal Policy Optimization with an interpretable regime prior. The agent observes both market features and portfolio-state variables, receives a reward combining portfolio log return, VIX-conditioned drawdown-increase penalty, target-exposure deviation, and turnover cost, and executes a blended action between the PPO actor output and a regime-derived target exposure. On the held-out 2020-2022 SPY test window, PPO-HRAP achieves 27.62% total return, 8.48% annualized return, 0.6447 Sharpe ratio, 0.8588 Sortino ratio, and 0.4592 Calmar ratio, while reducing maximum drawdown from 34.10% for Buy and Hold to 18.47%. Across five SPY seeds, PPO-HRAP remains stable with mean total return $0.2725 \pm 0.0109$ and mean Sharpe ratio $0.6219 \pm 0.0565$. Single-run cross-asset tests on QQQ and DIA further show that the proposed method ranks first on total return and Sharpe ratio for all three reported assets. These results suggest that blending learned actions with a volatility-aware regime prior is a practical way to improve risk-adjusted trading behavior, although the current policy still incurs high turnover and cross-asset robustness beyond SPY remains limited to single-run evidence.

</details>


### [176] [An ontology for cross-sectoral crisis management: core and public health modules](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01326)
*Aldo Gangemi,Rita T. Sousa,Luigi Asprino,Giorgia Lodi,Andrea G. Nuzzolese,Valentina Presutti,Johannes Gysen,Diana F. Sousa,Luigi Spagnolo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces ECMO, a modular OWL ontology for cross-sectoral disaster risk reduction and response, validated by building a knowledge graph from epidemiological news.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Crisis management data is fragmented across sectors and sources, lacking a consistent, unified formal representation for integration and interoperability.

Method: ECMO is organized as a network of ontological modules. ECMO-CORE captures core crisis concepts (hazard, event, exposure, impact, response measure) using ontology design patterns and OWL2 punning to disambiguate hazard types from event manifestations. Domain modules are added, e.g., a public health module aligned with SNOMED CT and ICD-11.

Result: ECMO was applied to data from the Joint Research Centre's Epidemic Intelligence from Open Sources system, producing an end-to-end pipeline that populates an ECMO-compliant knowledge graph from unstructured epidemiological news. Initial results show ECMO supplies the formal guardrails needed for consistent, unified knowledge representation and integration.

Conclusion: ECMO offers a reusable, modular reference ontology for crisis management, supporting unified knowledge representation and integration; it is publicly available on Zenodo under a CC BY 4.0 license.

Abstract: This paper presents the European Crisis Management Ontology (ECMO), a modular OWL-based ontology intended as a cross-sectoral reference for disaster risk reduction and response. ECMO is designed to be organised as a network of ontological modules. Among the modules, ECMO-CORE captures fundamental crisis management concepts such as hazard, event, exposure, impact, and response measure and uses ontology design patterns and the OWL2 punning technique to resolve ambiguities between hazard types and event manifestations. In addition, domain-specific modules are defined as in the case of the public health module aligned with SNOMED CT and ICD-11. To demonstrate the resource's utility, we used ECMO to represent the data of the Epidemic Intelligence from Open Sources system of the Joint Research Centre to generate an end-to-end pipeline that populates an ECMO-compliant knowledge graph from unstructured epidemiological news. Initial results demonstrate that ECMO provides the formal guardrails necessary for consistent and unified knowledge representation and integration. The ontology is publicly available at https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.20070268 and is released under the Creative Commons Attribution 4.0 International (CC BY 4.0) license.

</details>


### [177] [Verify Claims, Not Scores: Evidence-Based Verification of Modular Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01348)
*Ali Atiah Alzahrani*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a claim-specific verification audit for modular agents that scores the evidence behind each conclusion rather than the agent's aggregate task score, using oracle policies, component-swap tests, and verifier validity tests; applied to a portfolio-allocation agent, it reveals that attainable improvement depends on the action set, the scenario generator discards most regime signal, and the runtime verifier can be bypassed without visible outcome change.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Developers typically judge changes to a single agent component (controller, learned model, verifier) by an aggregate task score. Such a score cannot indicate whether improvement was attainable, which component lost value, or what the agent's own checks actually certify. This obscures diagnosis and can misattribute gains or losses.

Method: A claim-specific verification audit for modular agents that plan, act, check, and refine. Rather than scoring the agent, it scores evidence: each conclusion is recorded with its supporting evidence, one of four verdicts (supported, unsupported, unresolved, not evaluated), and the boundary within which it holds. Three tools supply evidence: (1) oracle policies measuring attainable improvement under an explicitly stated action set; (2) one-at-a-time component replacement with a perfect counterpart to locate lost value, with null results read as unresolved when a downstream component could mask them; (3) a separate test of whether the verifier's score identifies the quantity it is read as bounding.

Result: Applied to a constrained portfolio-allocation agent in a synthetic market with known hidden regimes: the value of perfect regime information depends on the action set used to measure it; the scenario generator discards most of the regime signal while better local fidelity does not improve decisions; and the runtime verifier can be bypassed with no visible change in outcomes.

Conclusion: The contribution is the audit protocol and the evidential distinctions it enforces; the empirical findings are specific to the agent and environment studied.

Abstract: When developers change one component of an agent, such as its controller, a learned model or its verifier, they usually judge the change by an aggregate task score. That score cannot tell whether improvement was attainable, which component lost value, or what the agent's own checks certify. We introduce a claim-specific verification audit for modular agents that plan, act, check and refine. Instead of scoring the agent, the audit scores the evidence: each conclusion is recorded with the evidence behind it, one of four verdicts (supported, unsupported, unresolved or not evaluated) and the boundary within which it holds. Three tools supply that evidence. Oracle policies measure attainable improvement under an explicitly stated action set, so that a low value can be traced to the evaluation rather than to the environment. Replacing one component at a time with a perfect counterpart locates lost value, with null results read as unresolved whenever a downstream component could mask them. A separate test asks whether the verifier's score identifies the quantity it is read as bounding. Applied to a constrained portfolio-allocation agent in a synthetic market with known hidden regimes, the audit shows that the value of perfect regime information depends on the action set used to measure it, that the scenario generator discards most of the regime signal while better local fidelity does not improve decisions, and that the runtime verifier can be bypassed with no visible change in outcomes. The contribution is the protocol and the evidential distinctions it enforces; the empirical findings are specific to the agent and environment studied.

</details>


### [178] [Generation Provenance Before Behavior Attribution: Auditing Synthetic Speech Research Objects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01378)
*Sidi Chang,Peiying Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a generation-provenance substrate that binds each synthetic training item to its source specification, generated content, waveform, target, fact requirements, quality signals, review lineage, and immutable manifest identity. Audited in a private Japanese care-handoff pipeline (113 assets, 1.552 hours, six scenario families), it argues provenance is necessary but not sufficient for behavior attribution: it defines the candidate causal graph and audit units, while contributive attribution still requires frozen training runs plus intervention or influence evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Attributing model behavior to synthetic training data requires knowing what produced each training item before estimating what that item caused. A waveform-label pair does not preserve this knowledge, since producer and selection mechanism determine evidentiary meaning, not storage location or variable name.

Method: Proposes a generation-provenance substrate in which a synthetic research object binds source specification, generated content, waveform, target, fact requirements, quality signals, review lineage, and immutable manifest identity. Audits this substrate in a private Japanese care-handoff pipeline using 113 assets (1.552 hours of synthetic speech, six scenario families) with linked audio, transcripts, candidate notes, and fact checklists, and two faithful-only, scenario-seed-disjoint, immutably versioned manifests.

Result: All items have linked audio, transcripts, candidate notes, and fact checklists, but human evidence is selective and source-specific. Exact upstream attribution remains blocked by floating generator aliases, missing per-clip TTS and code stamps, and an unversioned checking prompt.

Conclusion: Generation provenance is necessary but not sufficient for behavior attribution: it defines the candidate causal graph and audit units, whereas contributive attribution still requires frozen training runs and intervention or influence evidence. The paper contributes a compact provenance contract, an audit protocol, and a bounded case study; it does not claim causal training-data attribution, clinical validity, or unrestricted public release.

Abstract: Attributing model behavior to synthetic training data requires knowing what produced each training item before estimating what that item caused. A waveform-label pair does not preserve this knowledge. We propose a generation-provenance substrate in which a synthetic research object binds source specification, generated content, waveform, target, fact requirements, quality signals, review lineage, and immutable manifest identity. Producer and selection mechanism determine evidentiary meaning; storage location and variable name do not. We audit this substrate in a private Japanese care-handoff pipeline. A 113-asset review population contains 1.552 hours of synthetic speech across six scenario families; all items have linked audio, transcripts, candidate notes, and fact checklists, but human evidence is selective and source-specific. Two faithful-only manifests are scenario-seed-disjoint and immutably versioned, while exact upstream attribution remains blocked by floating generator aliases, missing per-clip TTS and code stamps, and an unversioned checking prompt. We argue that generation provenance is necessary but not sufficient for behavior attribution: it defines the candidate causal graph and audit units, whereas contributive attribution still requires frozen training runs and intervention or influence evidence. The paper contributes a compact provenance contract, an audit protocol, and a bounded case study for synthetic-data attribution; controlled research access may be offered, but we do not claim causal training-data attribution, clinical validity, or unrestricted public release.

</details>


### [179] [Gacha Decoding: Eliciting Diverse Generations Through Instruction Following](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01382)
*Scott Geng,Yufei Zhang,Joseph Lee,Jerry Li,Marjan Ghazvininejad,Pang Wei Koh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Gacha Decoding is an inference-time method that elicits diverse LM generations by treating diversity as an instruction-following problem, using an external RNG to identify and realize distinct response modes. It outperforms prior diversity approaches up to 2.4x Vendi at equal quality and improves as models get stronger.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing diversity methods rely on the LM's token entropy, which creates a tension: as models become more capable, token entropy and diversity under prior approaches decline. The paper aims to invert this diversity-capability tradeoff.

Method: Gacha Decoding combines the LM's instruction-following capability with randomness from an external RNG tool ("planning with dice"), rather than relying on token entropy, to scalably identify and realize distinct modes of the response space via instruction following.

Result: Across open-ended domains (in-the-wild chat, creative writing, planning for image generation, protein design), Gacha Decoding outperforms existing approaches at equal quality: up to 2.4x Vendi over the next-best prior, reaching the same number of high-quality modes with 11.0x fewer samples, and discovering novel modes no other approach surfaces. Diversity improves consistently as the LM becomes a better instruction follower.

Conclusion: Instruction following, rather than token entropy alone, can drive generation diversity, enabling diversity to scale with model capability.

Abstract: We introduce Gacha Decoding, an inference-time method for eliciting diverse language model generations that scales with model capability. Across open-ended domains (in-the-wild chat, creative writing, planning for image generation, and protein design), Gacha Decoding significantly outperforms existing generation diversity approaches at equal quality (up to 2.4x Vendi over the next-best prior approach), reaching the same number of high-quality modes with over an order of magnitude fewer samples (11.0x) and discovering novel modes that no other approach surfaces. Our key insight is to treat diversity as an instruction-following problem: rather than relying on the LM's token entropy, we combine its instruction-following capability with randomness from an external RNG tool to scalably identify and realize distinct modes of the response space. This approach of "planning with dice" enables Gacha to invert the long-observed tension between diversity and model capability. As the underlying LM becomes a better instruction follower, diversity under Gacha Decoding consistently improves--even as its token entropy and diversity under prior approaches decline. Together, our results highlight that instruction following, rather than token entropy alone, can drive generation diversity.

</details>


### [180] [PRISM: A Category-Theoretic Framework for Measuring and Refining Multimodal Analogies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01383)
*Mirella Zeisler,Ojas Shirekar,Mircea Licǎ,Chirag Raman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PRISM is a modality-agnostic, category-theory-grounded framework that measures and improves relational alignment in multimodal analogies via a pullback score and an iterative VLM refinement loop, evaluated on visual metaphor generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AI-driven multimodal analogy generation lacks an interpretable measure of whether the underlying relational structure is actually understood and preserved in the generated output. This gap motivates a principled, interpretable metric and a way to use it to improve generation.

Method: PRISM represents analogies as explicit relational mappings grounded in category theory and uses VLMs to instantiate these structures across modalities. Its first component, the pullback score, quantifies relational alignment from the resulting graph representation. Its second component is an iterative refinement loop that feeds the pullback score back as an in-context signal to revise the generated image toward greater relational depth.

Result: On AnaloBench, selecting the correct analogy purely by pullback score yields 82.5% accuracy. VLM-as-a-judge and human evaluations show PRISM improves metaphor consistency and analogy appropriateness over zero-shot generation, with humans preferring refined outputs in 57.65% of pairwise comparisons. Qualitative analysis shows refinement can favor visually crowded compositions over genuinely deeper relational correspondences.

Conclusion: The pullback score captures meaningful relational information and serves as an effective feedback signal, but refinement may conflate visual complexity with relational depth, indicating a limitation in the metric's operationalization.

Abstract: Analogical reasoning involves identifying and preserving relational structures across domains. However, existing approaches to AI-driven multimodal analogy generation lack an interpretable measure of whether this structure is understood and maintained in the generated output. We address this gap with Pullback Refinement via Interpretable Structural Mapping (PRISM), a modality-agnostic framework for measuring and improving relational alignment in multimodal analogies, evaluated on visual metaphor generation. PRISM represents analogies as explicit relational mappings grounded in category theory and uses VLMs to instantiate these structures across modalities. Its first component, the pullback score, quantifies relational alignment from the resulting graph representation. On the AnaloBench benchmark, selecting the correct analogy purely by pullback score achieves 82.5% accuracy, demonstrating that the score captures meaningful relational information. PRISM's second component is an iterative refinement loop that uses the pullback score as an in-context feedback signal to iteratively revise the generated image towards greater relational depth. VLM-as-a-judge and human evaluations show that PRISM consistently improves metaphor consistency and analogy appropriateness over zero- shot generation, with human participants preferring the refined output in 57.65% of pairwise comparisons. However, a qualitative analysis reveals that refinement can favour visually crowded compositions rather than genuinely deeper relational correspondences.

</details>


### [181] [AiSearch: Interactive Multi-Modal Search with VLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01389)
*Ali Koksal,Mei Chee Leong,Vicky Sintunata,Ching Ling Chin,Wee Teck Fong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AiSearch is a flexible multimodal retrieval framework that uses zero-shot Vision Language Models for natural language search over images and videos, supporting interactive refinement and model benchmarking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern retrieval systems need to be both automated and interactive, allowing users to search and refine results in real time, which existing approaches may not fully address in a multimodal setting.

Method: AiSearch leverages the zero-shot capabilities of Vision Language Models (VLMs) to enable natural language search over images and videos. It supports interactive search refinement driven by user feedback to align results with user intent, and provides visual benchmarking across multiple VLMs.

Result: The framework enables natural language image and video search, interactive result refinement via user feedback, and comparative benchmarking that lets users select the most suitable VLM for their task.

Conclusion: AiSearch offers a flexible, interactive, and automatically benchmarkable multimodal retrieval solution built on zero-shot VLMs, empowering users to tailor search results and choose appropriate models for their tasks.

Abstract: Modern retrieval systems must both be automated and interactive, allowing users to search and refine results in real time. We present AiSearch, a flexible multimodal retrieval framework that leverages the zero shot capabilities of Vision Language Models (VLMs) for natural language search over images and videos.
