AiSearch supports interactive search refinement through user feedback to tailor results to the user's intent, and allows visual benchmarking across multiple VLMs, enabling users to select the most suitable model for their task.

</details>


### [182] [Contrastive Attention Mitigates Spectral Bias in Spiking Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01403)
*Xiaoli Liu,Malu Zhang,Yang Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Spiking Transformers underperform ANN counterparts because spiking neurons and spiking self-attention act as low-pass filters that dissipate high-frequency components. The proposed Spiking Contrastive Attention (SCA), inspired by biological edge-detection and differential sensing, restores high-frequency information via global contrastive aggregation and local differential refinement, consistently boosting performance across tasks while reducing complexity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spiking Transformers combine SNN energy-efficiency with self-attention representational power, yet a performance gap remains versus ANN counterparts. Prior work blamed binary activations; this paper instead identifies low-pass filtering behavior in spiking neurons and spiking self-attention as the root cause of high-frequency information loss.

Method: Multiscale spectral analysis is used to demonstrate that spiking neurons and SSA behave as low-pass filters. To counteract high-frequency dissipation, the authors propose Spiking Contrastive Attention (SCA), inspired by edge-detection and differential sensing in biological vision. SCA extracts contrast prototypes through global contrastive aggregation and applies local differential refinement.

Result: SCA consistently improves Spiking Transformers on image classification, semantic segmentation, and event-based tracking. It also achieves lower complexity than original SSA, yielding superior efficiency.

Conclusion: SCA is a general, efficient module that addresses the spectral limitation of Spiking Transformers, positioning it as a fundamental building block for energy-efficient Spiking Transformers.

Abstract: Spiking Transformers merge the energy-efficiency of spiking neural networks (SNNs) with the representational power of self-attention, creating a promising architecture for high-performance, energy-efficient computation. However, a performance gap persists versus its counterparts in artificial neural networks (ANNs). Unlike prior works attributing this to binary activations, we reveal that both spiking neurons and spiking self-attention (SSA) act as low-pass filters through multiscale spectral analysis. This characteristic leads to the dissipation of high-frequency components. To address this issue, we propose the Spiking Contrastive Attention (SCA) paradigm, which draw inspiration from the edge-detection and differential sensing properties of biological visual system. By extracting contrast prototypes via global contrastive aggregation and applying local differential refinement, SCA effectively enhances high-frequency information. Extensive experiments show that SCA is a general module that consistently boosts Spiking Transformers across image classification, semantic segmentation, and event-based tracking. Furthermore, it achieves lower complexity, offering superior efficiency over original SSA. These results establish its potential as a fundamental building block for energy-efficient Spiking Transformers.

</details>


### [183] [Beyond Memory: Harnessing Long-Horizon Agents with Explicit Belief States](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01415)
*Yu Luo,Jiamin Jiang,Yimin Zuo,Xidao Wen,Rongchen Gao,Yongqian Sun,Shenglin Zhang,Guiyang Liu,Cheng Zhang,Fang Situ,Qi Zhou,Dan Pei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PoS is an inference-time framework that builds and continuously maintains explicit belief states combining world-state estimates with unresolved task requirements, validating consistency and detecting "Belief Trapping" to enable tailored recovery, achieving top overall performance across four benchmarks and three LLM backbones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents handle increasingly complex tasks, but organizing interaction history into memory does not guarantee a coherent understanding of the current world. Existing approaches rely on history retention and compression, which are insufficient for long-horizon context management.

Method: PoS constructs and continually maintains explicit belief states as the decision context, where each belief combines an estimate of the current world state with unresolved task requirements. It validates belief consistency and monitors task progress to detect Belief Trapping—where the agent acts without meaningful progress—and applies recovery tailored to the trapping pattern and the type of unresolved task requirement.

Result: On four benchmarks spanning execution and diagnosis, PoS achieves the highest overall performance on every benchmark with all three LLM backbones. Ablations show the importance of consistency validation and recovery, and context-scaling experiments demonstrate resilience to context growth.

Conclusion: The results support belief construction and continual maintenance as a foundation for long-horizon context management, going beyond history retention and compression.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents can now undertake increasingly complex tasks, but the way they organize interaction history into memory does not ensure a coherent understanding of the current world. We introduce PoS, an inference-time framework that constructs and continually maintains explicit belief states as the agent's decision context. Each belief combines an estimate of the current world state with unresolved task requirements, making explicit what the agent still needs to learn and accomplish. To keep this belief reliable and actionable, PoS validates its consistency and monitors task progress to detect Belief Trapping, where the agent continues to act without making meaningful progress toward the goal. Recovery is then tailored to both the trapping pattern and the type of unresolved task requirement. Experiments on four benchmarks spanning execution and diagnosis show that PoS achieves the highest overall performance on every benchmark with all three LLM backbones. Ablations demonstrate the importance of consistency validation and recovery, while context-scaling experiments show resilience to context growth. Together, these results support belief construction and continual maintenance as a foundation for long-horizon context management beyond history retention and compression.

</details>


### [184] [SpikeMoE: Brain-Inspired Competitive Routing for Flexible Spiking Mixture-of-Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01418)
*Xiaoli Liu,Yujie Liang,Jialin Li,Malu Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes SpikeMoE, a framework integrating spiking neural dynamics with model-scale Mixture-of-Experts, using a novel spike-based k-WTA router and a two-stage missing-modality modeling module, achieving state-of-the-art SNN performance, ANN-comparable results, and robustness under missing-modality conditions with favorable energy efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SNNs provide event-driven, biologically inspired computation at the neuronal scale, while MoE enables conditional computation via expert selection at the model scale. Combining these could yield flexible neural architectures, but the key challenge is designing an expert selection mechanism based on spiking activity. Additionally, incomplete multisensory inputs in multimodal tasks need to be addressed.

Method: Introduces a spike-based k-WTA Router inspired by competition-inhibition in the hippocampal CA1 region, using lateral inhibition and refractory period to select Top-K experts from discrete spike counts. Builds SpikeMoE integrating neuronal-scale spiking dynamics with model-scale expert selection. Adds a two-stage missing-modality modeling module combining empirical prototypes from an observed-modality pool with modality-specific learnable embeddings to construct missing-modality representations.

Result: On vision, language, and multimodal benchmarks, SpikeMoE achieves state-of-the-art performance among SNN baselines, matches or exceeds ANN counterparts, and remains robust across diverse missing-modality conditions, showing a favorable performance-energy efficiency trade-off.

Conclusion: The integration of spiking dynamics with sparse expert computation is validated, positioning SpikeMoE as a promising approach to energy-efficient brain-inspired computing.

Abstract: Spiking Neural Networks (SNNs) enable event-driven computation through biologically inspired dynamics at the neuronal scale, while Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) perform conditional computation through expert selection at the model scale. Integrating their strengths offers potential for flexible neural architectures. A key challenge, however, lies in designing an expert selection mechanism based on spiking activity. To address this, we introduce a spike-based k-WTA Router inspired by competition-inhibition observed in the hippocampal CA1 region. The router incorporates lateral inhibition and refractory period to select Top-K experts according to discrete spike counts. Building on this, we present SpikeMoE, a framework that integrates neuronal-scale spiking dynamics with model-scale expert selection. To address incomplete multisensory inputs in multimodal tasks, we further equip SpikeMoE with a two-stage missing-modality modeling module that combines empirical prototypes from an observed-modality pool with modality-specific learnable embeddings to construct missing-modality representations. Experiments on vision, language, and multimodal benchmarks demonstrate that SpikeMoE achieves state-of-the-art performance among the SNN baselines, matches or exceeds the performance of ANN counterparts, and maintains robustness across diverse missing-modality conditions. These results demonstrate a favorable trade-off between performance and energy efficiency, validating the integration of spiking dynamics with sparse expert computation and highlighting SpikeMoE as a promising approach to energy-efficient brain-inspired computing.

</details>


### [185] [A Deterministic and Auditable AI Security Risk Assessment Framework with ATLAS Aligned Executable Rules and Formal Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01436)
*Yixuan Huang,Basel Halak,Boojoong Kang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An evidence-driven AI security assessment framework that formalizes assessment as a deterministic, machine-evaluable decision function over a Control ID taxonomy and MITRE ATLAS technique predicates, formally verified and evaluated on five open-source AI projects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI systems are increasingly deployed in high-impact, safety-critical settings, but security assessment remains hard to reproduce and defend under audit. Existing approaches rely on narrative checklists or assessor-driven scoring and lack an explicit, machine-evaluable mapping from observable engineering artefacts to stable technique-level outcomes.

Method: The framework operationalises assessment as a deterministic decision function. It normalises heterogeneous artefacts into a project-independent Control ID taxonomy scored on a bounded four-level ordinal scale, compiles technique-level predicates from a pinned MITRE ATLAS snapshot via an explicit mitigation-to-control mapping, and outputs technique-indexed feasibility and impact levels with traceable links to triggering evidence. All normative choices are packaged as a versioned assessment policy object for repeatable reassessment across snapshots. Semantic correctness is ensured via formal verification of boundedness, totality, ordered semantic consistency, and monotonicity of the compiled evaluator over the full declared score domain.

Result: Evaluated on five public open-source AI projects pinned to explicit repository snapshots, with before/after quantification under a unified hardening intervention. Responsiveness to real engineering changes was validated through fork-based implementations of SBOM generation and CI security scanning gates. Results show consistent downward shifts in feasibility profiles under strengthened observable controls, while worst-case residual feasibility persists when technique-specific core controls remain absent from the evidence scope.

Conclusion: The framework enables reproducible, auditable, and deterministic AI security assessment with traceable evidence, formally verified semantics, and demonstrated responsiveness to real engineering changes, though residual feasibility remains when core technique controls are not evidenced.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence systems are increasingly deployed in high impact and safety critical settings, yet security assessment remains difficult to reproduce and defend under audit. Existing approaches often rely on narrative checklists or assessor driven scoring, and they lack an explicit, machine evaluable mapping from observable engineering artefacts to stable technique level outcomes. We present an evidence driven AI security assessment framework that operationalises assessment as a deterministic decision function. The framework normalises heterogeneous artefacts into a project independent Control ID taxonomy scored on a bounded four level ordinal scale, compiles technique level predicates from a pinned MITRE ATLAS snapshot via an explicit mitigation to control mapping, and outputs technique indexed feasibility and impact levels with traceable links back to the triggering evidence. We package all normative choices as a versioned assessment policy object to support repeatable reassessment across snapshots. To ensure semantic correctness, we formally verify boundedness, totality, ordered semantic consistency, and monotonicity of the compiled evaluator over the full declared score domain. We evaluate the framework on five public open source AI projects pinned to explicit repository snapshots, quantify before and after changes under a unified hardening intervention, and validate responsiveness to real engineering changes through fork based implementations of Software Bill of Materials (SBOM) generation and Continuous integration (CI) security scanning gates. Results show consistent downward shifts in feasibility profiles under strengthened observable controls, while worst case residual feasibility persists when technique specific core controls remain absent from the evidence scope.

</details>


### [186] [DRelay: Global Draft Context for Prefix-Aware Parallel Speculative Decoding Repair](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01439)
*Zhuoyu Wang,Junnan Huang,Xinyu Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DRelay improves parallel speculative decoding by using global block information to detect and repair early candidate-selection errors before verification, extending the accepted prefix and boosting decoding speed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Parallel drafting speeds up speculative decoding for LLMs, but its benefit is capped by the accepted prefix length: a single early selection error can invalidate all correct subsequent predictions, even if the right token exists in the candidate pool. This calls for correcting early mistakes rather than only relying on native per-position choices.

Method: DRelay performs prefix-aware selective repair of candidate selections using global information from the whole draft block prior to target-model verification. A global reader extracts predictive information across positions for each candidate; a causal selector combines this candidate-level information with tokens selected at preceding positions to judge whether the native choice is consistent with global evidence and the selected prefix, then retains or replaces the token. The draft backbone and selector are jointly trained, combining candidate-support learning with a repair objective, whose loss is weighted by each position's potential contribution to the consecutive accepted prefix.

Result: On an H800 GPU across eight diverse benchmarks, DRelay consistently improves average acceptance length and end-to-end decoding over DFlash, Domino, and DSpark. Under SGLang serving, it achieves average end-to-end speedups of 14.7%-16.8%, 8.7%-9.3%, and 8.1%-9.3% over the three baselines respectively.

Conclusion: Prefix-aware, globally informed selective repair of early draft errors effectively extends accepted prefix length, yielding consistent acceptance-length and end-to-end speedup gains in parallel speculative decoding.

Abstract: Parallel drafting reduces the drafting overhead of speculative decoding for large language models (LLMs), but its gains remain limited by the accepted prefix length. Even when the correct token is present in the candidate pool, a single early selection error prevents subsequent predictions from being used. We propose DRelay, which uses global information from the entire draft block to perform prefix-aware selective repair of candidate selections before target-model verification. DRelay bases its decisions on candidate correlations and the selected path: a global reader extracts predictive information across positions for each candidate. While a causal selector combines candidate-level information extracted by the global read with the tokens selected at preceding positions to determine whether the native choice at the current position is consistent with the global evidence and the selected prefix. It then decides whether to retain or replace the token, thereby repairing early errors and extending the accepted prefix. We further jointly train the draft backbone and the selector, combining candidate-support learning with a repair objective, while weighting the repair loss according to each block position's potential contribution to the consecutive accepted prefix. Across eight diverse benchmarks on an H800 GPU, DRelay consistently improves both average acceptance length and end-to-end decoding performance over DFlash, Domino, and DSpark. Under SGLang serving, DRelay improves average end-to-end speedup over DFlash, Domino, and DSpark by 14.7%-16.8%, 8.7%-9.3%, and 8.1%-9.3%, respectively.

</details>


### [187] [A Multi-Agent LLM Framework for Personalized Health Checkup Interpretation and Guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01451)
*HyungJun Kim,Taehan Lee,Soojin Cheon*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A multi-agent LLM system for personalized health checkup interpretation improves LLM-judge and human-rated answer quality on compound Korean queries over a single-agent baseline, at about 1.3x latency and 2x cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Personalized interpretation of health checkup results requires reasoning across longitudinal records, medical knowledge, lifestyle guidance, and healthcare navigation, which a single-agent LLM may handle poorly, especially for compound queries with multiple requirements.

Method: Build a multi-agent LLM system that identifies multiple intents, routes each to a task-specific agent, executes the agents in parallel, and synthesizes their outputs. Compare Single Agent vs. Multi Agent on 120 Korean compound queries (2-4 requirements each) using synthetic health checkup records, with a weighted LLM-judge score, three additional LLM judges, two human evaluators, and cost/latency measurement.

Result: Multi Agent raised the weighted LLM-judge score from 1.695 to 1.797 (p = 0.027); three other LLM judges agreed (Δ = +0.111 to +0.186, all p < 0.05). Gains concentrated in usefulness, consistency, and coverage of every requirement; numerical accuracy and grounding improved under only one of four judges; medical safety did not differ and critical failure rates were similar (15.0% vs. 13.3%). Two human evaluators preferred Multi Agent in 66.7% and 68.3% of pairwise comparisons. Latency and cost rose 1.31x and 2.02x. Exploratory subgroup analyses localized the improvement to queries involving personal-record lookup.

Conclusion: Multi-agent decomposition improves perceived answer quality and requirement coverage for compound health checkup queries, particularly personal-record lookup, but does not clearly improve numerical accuracy, grounding, or medical safety, and incurs higher latency and cost.

Abstract: Personalized interpretation of health checkup results requires reasoning across longitudinal records, medical knowledge, lifestyle guidance, and healthcare navigation. We present a multi-agent large language model (LLM) system that identifies multiple intents, maps each to a task-specific agent, executes them in parallel, and synthesizes their outputs. We compared answers generated in Single Agent and Multi Agent settings on 120 Korean compound queries combining two to four requirements, using synthetic health checkup records. The Multi Agent improved the weighted LLM-judge score from 1.695 to 1.797 (p = 0.027), and three additional LLM judges showed consistent improvements ($Δ$ = +0.111 to +0.186, all p < 0.05). The gains came from usefulness, consistency, and the handling of every requirement in compound queries, whereas numerical accuracy and grounding improved significantly under only one of the four judges and medical safety did not differ, and critical failures occurred at similar rates (Single Agent 15.0% vs. Multi Agent 13.3%). Two human evaluators preferred Multi Agent in 66.7% and 68.3% of pairwise comparisons. Multi Agent execution increased latency and cost by 1.31$\times$ and 2.02$\times$, respectively. In exploratory subgroup analyses, the improvement was concentrated in queries involving personal-record lookup.

</details>


### [188] [Rethinking Probability-Based Reinforcement Learning From Posterior Concentration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01458)
*Shiu-Hong Kao,Yubo Zhao,Zhenyu Tian,Pengzhan Sun,Yicong Li,Angela Yao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper identifies the Posterior Concentration Phenomenon (PCP), a length-dependent failure of probability-based rewards in verifier-free RL, and proposes RLCPR, a framework with uncertainty-aware sampling and concentration-aware regularization that improves stability and token efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Verifier-free reinforcement learning with probability-based rewards enables LLM training on general reasoning tasks lacking external verifiers. However, the reliability of these rewards, particularly in long-horizon reasoning, is underexplored.

Method: The authors identify PCP: the probability of a reference answer conditioned on a reasoning trace collapses to a low-variance interval as the trace grows long, yielding nearly indistinguishable rewards and unstable GRPO-based optimization. They propose RLCPR with two components: (1) uncertainty-aware data sampling to reduce concentration-prone rollouts before generation, and (2) concentration-aware regularization to penalize unnecessarily long traces when posterior rewards collapse.

Result: Extensive experiments show RLCPR achieves higher token efficiency and outperforms the state-of-the-art verifier-free RL baseline by up to 4.0% on six of seven benchmarks, covering general-domain and mathematical reasoning tasks.

Conclusion: Explicitly accounting for PCP yields better optimization stability and token efficiency in verifier-free RL for LLM reasoning.

Abstract: Verifier-free reinforcement learning with probability-based rewards offers a promising way to train LLMs on general reasoning tasks where external verifiers are unavailable. Yet the reliability of these rewards, especially in long-horizon reasoning, remains underexplored. This work identifies a length-dependent failure mode of probability rewards, which we call the Posterior Concentration Phenomenon (PCP). We show that the probability of a reference answer conditioned on a reasoning trace often collapses to a low-variance interval as the trace becomes lengthy. This phenomenon results in nearly indistinguishable rewards, which, under GRPO-based settings, makes probability-based policy optimization unstable and inefficient. Motivated by this, we propose Reinforcement Learning with Concentration-aware Posterior Rewards (RLCPR), a verifier-free RL framework to explicitly account for PCP for better optimization stability and token efficiency. It has two components: uncertainty-aware data sampling, which reduces concentration-prone rollouts before generation, and concentration-aware regularization, which penalizes unnecessarily long traces when posterior rewards collapse. Extensive experiments show that, alongside higher token efficiency, RLCPR outperforms the state-of-the-art verifier-free RL baseline by up to 4.0% on six of seven benchmarks, including general-domain and mathematical reasoning challenges.

</details>


### [189] [NextMe-800: Anticipating Personal Behavior from Months of Egocentric Video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01461)
*Zhaoxu Meng,Yiming Sun,Mingyuan Gao,Jiachang Zhang,Zhuhan Dai,Yipeng Du,Zheng Lian,Jian-Qiao Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces NextMe-800, an ~800-hour first-person dataset from one volunteer over 126 days with 1 Hz images, gaze, and audio captioned at five abstraction levels, and NextAct, a 1,500-point benchmark for personalized open-vocabulary K-step action anticipation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: People often plan ambitiously but act habitually, and hindsight reveals that past decisions might have been made differently. Simulating hindsight at decision time via plausible personal trajectories could enable more realistic plans and better-informed choices, motivating long-horizon personalized behavior anticipation.

Method: The authors collect NextMe-800, an approximately 800-hour egocentric dataset from a single volunteer across 126 days, recorded at 1 Hz with images, gaze, and audio, annotated with captions at five hierarchical abstraction levels from atomic actions to major activities. They frame personalized action anticipation as open-vocabulary K-step sequence prediction and build NextAct, a 1,500-point benchmark combining NextMe-800 with the multi-person EgoLife dataset. Models are evaluated using an embedding-based soft edit distance across abstraction levels and prediction horizons.

Result: NextMe-800 and NextAct provide a months-long resource and evaluation framework to measure how far ahead personal behavior can be anticipated from egocentric observation, with performance assessed via embedding-based soft edit distance across abstraction levels and prediction horizons.

Conclusion: The work offers a long-duration dataset and benchmark for studying personalized action anticipation, enabling evaluation of how well models can predict personal behavior from egocentric data over extended horizons and multiple abstraction levels.

Abstract: We often plan ambitiously yet act habitually and wonder, in retrospect, whether we would have planned differently had we known what we would actually do. Hindsight offers a valuable perspective on past decisions, although we often wish we could have simulated hindsight at the moment of choosing. If a system could generate plausible trajectories from one's personal history, such previews might help people formulate more realistic plans and make better informed decisions. We introduce NextMe-800, an approximately 800-hour first-person dataset from one volunteer over 126 days with 1 Hz images, gaze, and audio, captioned at five hierarchical abstraction levels from atomic actions to major activities. We formulate personalized action anticipation as open-vocabulary K-step sequence prediction and construct NextAct, a 1,500-point benchmark combining NextMe-800 with the multi-person EgoLife dataset. Using an embedding-based soft edit distance as the metric, we evaluate how well different models can anticipate personal behavior across abstraction levels and prediction horizons. NextMe-800 and NextAct provide a months-long resource and evaluation framework for studying how far ahead personal behavior can be anticipated from egocentric observation.

</details>


### [190] [Auditing Routing Entropy as an Uncertainty Signal in Attention-Residual Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01495)
*Wenhao Liang,Lin Yue,Wei Emma Zhang,Mingyu Guo,Olaf Maennel,Weitong Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper audits whether routing entropy in Attention-Residual Swin-Tiny and DeiT-Small models carries information about prediction correctness beyond the model's own confidence. It finds control-dependent gains and incomplete estimator recovery, but not the absence of conditional routing information.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffuse routing traces are often read as signs that a prediction is unreliable. The authors ask whether these traces genuinely carry correctness information beyond what the model's confidence already reveals.

Method: The study audits routing entropy in AR variants of Swin-Tiny and DeiT-Small, trained from scratch on CIFAR-10/100 with a soft-binned calibration auxiliary loss. Three checks probe the increment: whether a routing signal appears at fixed confidence, whether it replicates across training seeds, and whether a held-out predictor can exploit it against output-only and shuffled-trace controls. A sensitivity audit injects effects of known size and measures the fraction recovered by the probes.

Result: No test in the fixed 30-test binned family survives multiplicity correction, and neither the nominal hit nor a borderline result recurs in sibling seeds. Across 24 paired runs, a scalar routing probe yields no pooled improvement in routing-stratified calibration, and an entropy-profile probe predicts correctness better than with shuffled profiles but worse than a confidence-only predictor in binary log-loss and Brier score. Conditioning on the complete logit vector leaves the comparison unresolved. The audit bounds non-detections: at an injected effect of 0.010 nats the profile probe recovers 24-59% of the oracle gain, and a reference-preserving correction probe recovers 8% and 23% in the two CIFAR-100 settings, below the threshold for applying it to real labels.

Conclusion: The results establish control-dependent gains and incomplete estimator recovery, not the absence of conditional routing information.

Abstract: Dynamic architectures leave a per-example routing trace beside each prediction, and diffuse routing is easy to read as a sign that the prediction is unreliable. We audit that reading for routing entropy in Attention-Residual (AR) variants of Swin-Tiny and DeiT-Small, trained from scratch on CIFAR-10/100 with a soft-binned calibration auxiliary loss, asking whether the trace carries information about correctness beyond what the model's own confidence already reveals. Three checks probe this increment: does a routing signal appear at fixed confidence, does it replicate across training seeds, and can a held-out predictor exploit it against output-only and shuffled-trace controls? A sensitivity audit then injects effects of known size and measures the fraction of each that the probes recover. No test in the fixed 30-test binned family survives multiplicity correction, and neither the nominal hit nor a borderline result recurs in its sibling seeds. Across 24 paired runs a scalar routing probe yields no pooled improvement in routing-stratified calibration, and an entropy-profile probe predicts correctness better than the same probe given shuffled profiles yet worse than a confidence-only predictor in both binary log-loss and Brier score: a gain over shuffled traces does not become a gain over the output. Conditioning on the complete logit vector leaves the corresponding comparison unresolved. The audit bounds how far these non-detections can be read: at an injected effect of 0.010 nats the profile probe recovers 24-59% of the oracle gain, and a reference-preserving correction probe recovers 8% and 23% in the two CIFAR-100 settings, below the threshold we fixed for applying it to real labels. The results establish control-dependent gains and incomplete estimator recovery, not the absence of conditional routing information.

</details>


### [191] [OpenMTB-Audit: Exposing Over-Refusal and Clinical Expert Perspectives in LLM-Based Molecular Tumor Board Safety Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01497)
*Negin Ashrafi,Jia Luo,Stacey M. Frumm,Roxana Daneshjou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OpenMTB-Audit is an open-source benchmark of 500 synthetic NSCLC cases for auditing LLM safety in molecular tumor boards; it reveals pervasive over-refusal in eight LLM configurations, and the proposed MTB-AuditAgent reduces over-refusal to 6.7% with 91.2% accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI enters molecular tumor board workflows, a key safety challenge is distinguishing truly unsupported recommendations from evidence-supported options that still require oncologist review due to incomplete information, poor ECOG performance status, or other clinical caveats.

Method: The authors introduce OpenMTB-Audit, a benchmark of 500 synthetic non-small cell lung cancer cases spanning five adversarial error categories and four safety labels (Supported, Partially Supported, Unsupported, Insufficient Information). They evaluate eight large language model configurations and develop MTB-AuditAgent, a deterministic seven-module framework separating evidence verification, missing-information detection, safety classification, and abstention. A two-oncologist annotation study is also conducted.

Result: All LLM configurations failed to retain the Partially Supported label in 83.3-100% of true Partially Supported cases, exhibiting over-refusal and achieving high aggregate safety scores through label collapse. MTB-AuditAgent reduces over-refusal to 6.7% and achieves 91.2% accuracy (95% CI: 88.6-93.6%). The two-oncologist study found disagreement concentrated at the boundary between information sufficiency and treatment optimization.

Conclusion: Current LLMs achieve apparent safety through label collapse rather than clinically calibrated reasoning, and there is a need to preserve clinically meaningful distinctions between information sufficiency and treatment optimization. The proposed MTB-AuditAgent offers a more calibrated alternative.

Abstract: Molecular tumor boards integrate genomic findings, clinical context, and therapeutic evidence to support precision oncology. As AI enters this workflow, a key safety challenge is distinguishing truly unsupported recommendations from evidence-supported options that still require oncologist review because of incomplete information, poor ECOG performance status, or other clinical caveats. We introduce OpenMTB-Audit, an open-source benchmark of 500 synthetic non-small cell lung cancer cases spanning five adversarial error categories and four safety labels: Supported, Partially Supported, Unsupported, and Insufficient Information. Across eight large language model configurations, we identify pervasive over-refusal: all LLM configurations failed to retain the Partially Supported label in 83.3-100% of true Partially Supported cases, achieving high aggregate safety scores through label collapse rather than clinically calibrated reasoning. To address this limitation, we developed MTB-AuditAgent, a deterministic seven-module framework separating evidence verification, missing-information detection, safety classification, and abstention. It reduces over-refusal to 6.7% and achieves 91.2% accuracy (95% CI: 88.6-93.6%). A two-oncologist annotation study found disagreement concentrated at the boundary between information sufficiency and treatment optimization, underscoring the need to preserve clinically meaningful distinctions.

</details>


### [192] [MCRI: A Four-Dimensional Framework for Analyzing and Evaluating Agent Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01506)
*Zongrui Yang,Li Xintong,Runchen Xu,Zhongsheng Wang,Zhedong Lin,Haoyuan Li,Jiamou Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces the four-dimensional MCRI Framework and its operationalization, MCRI-Eval, an LLM-based method for analyzing and evaluating agent skills, validated on 63,812 skills with 58,275 skill-conditioned executions showing improved ranking agreement and top-1 skill selection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As agents evolve from single-tool systems into modular, composite architectures, skills become a key mechanism for capability development and distribution, yet the academic community lacks a structured framework for systematically analyzing and evaluating skills.

Method: Drawing on information gain and behavioral constraint, the authors propose the four-dimensional MCRI Framework and operationalize it as MCRI-Eval, an LLM-based evaluation method. They evaluate it using 63,812 public skills from the OpenClaw skill Hub with 58,275 skill-conditioned model executions across BigCodeBench, BFCL-Fundamental, and Mind2Web.

Result: MCRI-Eval scores are positively associated with community popularity signals and achieve the highest downstream ranking agreement among evaluated methods. It improves top-1 skill selection on all three benchmarks, advancing downstream performance rank by 17.7, 22.8, and 19.6 percentile points on BigCodeBench, BFCL-Fundamental, and Mind2Web, respectively, versus the strongest baseline.

Conclusion: MCRI-Eval provides a useful pre-execution signal for prioritizing promising skills before costly execution-based evaluation.

Abstract: As agents evolve from single-tool systems into modular, composite architectures, skills are becoming an important mechanism for capability development and distribution. However, the academic community lacks a structured framework for systematically analyzing and evaluating skills. Drawing on information gain and behavioral constraint, we propose the four-dimensional MCRI Framework and operationalize it as MCRI-Eval, a large language model-based evaluation method. We evaluate MCRI-Eval using 63,812 public skills from the OpenClaw skill Hub, with 58,275 skill-conditioned model executions across BigCodeBench, BFCL-Fundamental, and Mind2Web. MCRI-Eval scores are positively associated with community popularity signals and achieve the highest downstream ranking agreement among the evaluated methods. MCRI-Eval also improves top-1 skill selection across all three benchmarks: compared with the strongest baseline on each benchmark, the skills selected by MCRI-Eval advance by 17.7, 22.8, and 19.6 percentile points in downstream performance rank on BigCodeBench, BFCL-Fundamental, and Mind2Web, respectively. These results indicate that MCRI-Eval provides a useful pre-execution signal for prioritizing promising skills before costly execution-based evaluation.

</details>


### [193] [Sharpening Tax in Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01509)
*Changdae Oh,Qi Zeng,Qi Qi,Andrey Zhmoginov,Deren Lei,Yun He,Hoang Phan,Hangoo Kang,Azalia Mirhoseini,Sharon Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Pre-trained LLMs with a light inference harness can act as capable agents, often exceeding their post-trained counterparts in pass@K (solution coverage) given enough test-time budget, despite lower pass@1. RL post-training "sharpens" tasks toward always-solved or never-solved, trading coverage for sampling efficiency. The authors introduce "Sharpening Tax," a diagnostic metric for lost test-time scalability, and PTGS, a Bayesian temperature-adapting sampler that reduces this tax.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The prevailing hypothesis is that RL post-training of LLMs merely sharpens existing base-model behaviors, improving single-shot accuracy at the expense of solution coverage. This trade-off is documented in math and coding, but the authors argue it need not generalize to agentic tasks, where multi-turn tool use and interaction may require capabilities newly acquired during post-training. This motivates re-examining the sharpening hypothesis in the agentic setting.

Method: The authors run pre-trained LLMs with a light inference harness as agents and compare them against post-trained counterparts across 14 base/post-trained model pairs from four families and three agentic benchmarks (42 cases). They analyze the mechanism behind post-training's effect on task solvability, propose the Sharpening Tax metric to quantify test-time scalability loss, and design posterior-tempered group sampling (PTGS), a plug-and-play Bayesian sampler that adapts sampling temperature per prompt to estimated difficulty, applied during RL training in two agentic environments.

Result: Pre-trained LLMs often surpass post-trained counterparts in pass@K given sufficient test-time budget, despite far lower pass@1. Post-training pushes tasks toward two extremes—always solved or never solved—improving sampling efficiency and consistency but reducing solution coverage. The Sharpening Tax is prevalent in most of the 42 cases, can be estimated from a few rollouts, and correlates well with other metrics. PTGS pays a smaller tax than the fixed-temperature baseline, solving more tasks under repeated sampling while also improving single-shot accuracy.

Conclusion: The sharpening trade-off extends to agentic tasks: post-training sacrifices solution coverage for sampling efficiency and consistency. The proposed Sharpening Tax quantifies this cost, and PTGS offers a practical way to mitigate it, achieving both better coverage and higher single-shot accuracy than fixed-temperature sampling.

Abstract: An emerging hypothesis about reinforcement learning (RL) post-training of large language models (LLMs) is that it merely sharpens existing behaviors of a base model, improving single-shot accuracy at the cost of solution coverage. Although this trade-off has been observed in math and coding tasks, it need not extend to agentic tasks, where multi-turn tool use and interaction may require capabilities newly acquired during post-training. Our surprising finding is that pre-trained LLMs, equipped with a light inference harness, can serve as capable agents. Despite far lower accuracy (pass@1), they often surpass their post-trained counterparts in solution coverage (pass@K) given a sufficient test-time budget. We further analyze the underlying mechanism and show that post-training pushes tasks toward two extremes, always solved or never solved, and thereby improves sampling efficiency and consistency at the cost of solution coverage. To measure this cost, we propose Sharpening Tax, a diagnostic metric that quantifies the loss in test-time scalability after post-training. Across 14 base/post-trained model pairs from four families and three agentic benchmarks (42 cases in total), the tax is prevalent in most settings, can be estimated from a few rollouts, and correlates well with other metrics. Finally, we present posterior-tempered group sampling (PTGS), a simple plug-and-play Bayesian sampler that adapts the sampling temperature per prompt to its estimated difficulty. Applied during RL training in two agentic environments, PTGS pays a smaller tax than the fixed-temperature baseline, solving more tasks under repeated sampling while also improving single-shot accuracy.

</details>


### [194] [Decision Titan: Test-Time Training for Long-Term Memory in Offline Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01513)
*Jude Waide,Robert Lieck*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Decision Titan, augmenting a Decision Transformer with Test-Time Training (TTT) layers for offline RL, and evaluates its memory capabilities in the X-Maze environment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-term dependencies are challenging for sequential decision-making: RNNs suffer from vanishing gradients and limited hidden-state expressivity, while Transformers scale quadratically with attention. TTT, which stores episodic memory in network parameters via gradient descent, has succeeded in NLP but has not been applied to RL, nor has its memory mechanism been analyzed.

Method: The authors augment a Decision Transformer with TTT layers, creating Decision Titan, and evaluate it in X-Maze, an extension of T-Maze designed to test sequential memory. They visualize gate values over time to study how the memory mechanism learns.

Result: Decision Titan learns long-term dependencies with ranges 20x longer than the context window and generalizes to lengths 1.7x the training data. However, temporal generalization depends on the time embeddings used, and learning long-term dependencies depends on how relevant information is encoded.

Conclusion: TTT layers show promise for offline RL, enabling long-range memory beyond the context window, but their effectiveness is conditioned on time embedding choice and information encoding scheme.

Abstract: Long-term dependencies remain a major challenge for sequential decision-making in the field of AI: RNNs suffer from vanishing gradients and the limited expressivity of vector-based hidden states, whilst Transformer-based models are limited by the quadratic scaling of attention. Recent work has proposed tackling this problem with the Test-Time Training (TTT) framework, which stores episodic memories in the parameters of a neural network through gradient descent at both train and test-time. This approach has seen success in the domain of Natural Language Processing, however, to the best of our knowledge it has not yet been applied to the domain of Reinforcement Learning (RL), nor has there been a study analysing how this memory practically functions. In this paper, we study the potential of the TTT framework for offline RL by augmenting a Decision Transformer with TTT layers, dubbed the Decision Titan. We analyse performance and properties of the model in the X-Maze environment, an extension of T-Maze designed to test sequential memory, and investigate how the memory mechanism learns by visualising gate values over time. Our key findings are that Decision Titan can learn long-term dependencies with ranges 20x longer than the context window, generalises to lengths 1.7x the training data, but crucially temporal generalisation depends on the time embeddings used, and the ability to learn long-term dependencies depends on how the relevant information is encoded.

</details>


### [195] [Towards Reliable Vision-Language Models for Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01531)
*Manasa Mariam Mammen,Priyanka Mary Mammen,Zafer Kayatas,Stefan Wagner*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper evaluates five VLMs on four driving-related QA datasets under varying visual input settings and corruption, and tests whether Visual Evidence Augmentation (VEA) improves reliability under degraded visual conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLMs are increasingly used in autonomous driving, where visual inputs can be degraded by sensor imperfections and environmental conditions. Since safety-critical decisions require accurate predictions and reliable confidence estimates, it is important to understand how visual corruption affects both accuracy and confidence calibration across different models, datasets, and input configurations.

Method: The study benchmarks five VLMs (Qwen3.5-9B, Gemma4-E4B, LLaVA-OneVision-7B, DriveFusion/DriveFusionQA-4B, and NVIDIA Alpamayo-1.5-10B) across four driving-related QA datasets under different visual input settings: single-frame, multi-view, multi-frame, and monocular. It measures changes in accuracy and confidence reliability under visual corruption, then applies Visual Evidence Augmentation (VEA), an inference-time method, to assess its effect on model reliability in degraded visual conditions.

Result: The effects of visual corruption vary across models, datasets, and input settings, with changes in both accuracy and confidence reliability differing across conditions. VEA improves performance for some models and datasets, but the gains are not consistent across all settings.

Conclusion: Visual corruption has heterogeneous impacts on VLM accuracy and confidence reliability in driving scenarios, and while VEA can offer benefits, it does not uniformly enhance robustness. Consistent, generalizable mitigation methods remain an open challenge.

Abstract: Vision-Language models (VLMs) are increasingly being explored in autonomous driving for tasks such as scene understanding, driving reasoning, decision-making, and end-to-end driving. As their role becomes more prominent, ensuring their robustness and reliability is increasingly important. In real-world conditions, visual inputs may be degraded by sensor imperfections and environmental conditions, potentially affecting both model predictions and their associated confidence. Such degradation is especially concerning in autonomous driving, where safety-critical decisions require models to make accurate predictions and recognize when their predictions may be unreliable. In this work, we evaluate five VLMs (Qwen3.5-9B, Gemma4-E4B, LLaVA-OneVision-7B, DriveFusion/DriveFusionQA-4B, and NVIDIA Alpamayo-1.5-10B) across four driving-related QA datasets with different visual input settings, including single-frame, multi-view, multi-frame, and monocular inputs. Our results show that the effects of visual corruption vary across models, datasets, and input settings, with changes in accuracy and confidence reliability and also differing across conditions. We then apply Visual Evidence Augmentation ($\mathrm{V}{\scriptstyle \mathrm{EA}}$), a recent inference-time method to examine whether it can improve model reliability under degraded visual conditions. We find that $\mathrm{V}{\scriptstyle \mathrm{EA}}$ improves performance for some models and datasets, although the gains are not consistent across all settings.

</details>


### [196] [Neither Black nor White: Balancing Semantic and Collaborative Signals with Graph-Informed Semantic IDs (GrIS)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01533)
*Aleksei Medvedev,Alejandro Ariza-Casabona,Steven Derby,Gonzalo Fiz Pontiveros,Xinyang Shao,Florian Spiess*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SID construction for generative recommendation is reframed as recursive graph clustering, yielding GrIS, a unified framework that subsumes RQ-VAE/RQ-KMeans as empty-graph special cases and improves Hit@10 by up to +52%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work treats Semantic ID (SID) construction as a representation learning problem: items are encoded into a quantized latent space and codes are read off. The authors argue this view is incidental and obscures the true nature of the task, collapsing design choices that should be explored separately.

Method: The paper recasts SID construction as a recursive clustering problem over a graph whose nodes carry semantic content and whose edges carry collaborative signal, making SID assignment a hierarchical graph partition. This yields GrIS, a unified framework structured along two previously collapsed axes: graph construction and recursive partition algorithm. Two instantiations are explored: RecDMoN, which performs hierarchical assignment via differentiable graph pooling, and RQ-GAE, which augments RQ-VAE with graph-aware item representations and a graph reconstruction objective.

Result: RQ-VAE and RQ-KMeans are recovered as the special case of an empty graph, exposing content-only quantization as one corner of a larger design space. On multiple real-world datasets, GrIS consistently outperforms CF-aware state-of-the-art methods, with gains of up to +52% Hit@10.

Conclusion: Because graph construction and partition are explicit, separately configurable components, improvements on either axis can be combined and systematically evaluated, providing a principled design space for SID construction in generative recommendation.

Abstract: Existing work on Semantic IDs (SIDs) for generative recommendation treats SID construction as a representation learning problem: encode items into a quantised latent space and read off codes. We argue this view is incidental. SID construction is, at heart, a recursive clustering problem, and once stated this way the natural object to cluster is a graph whose nodes carry semantic content and whose edges carry collaborative signal; SID assignment becomes a hierarchical graph partition. This reframing yields a unified framework, Graph-Informed Semantic IDs (GrIS), that subsumes prior approaches rather than displacing them. RQ-VAE and RQ-KMeans are recovered as the special case where the graph is empty, exposing content-only quantisation as one corner of a larger design space along two so-far-collapsed axes: graph construction and recursive partition algorithm. We explore two contrasting instantiations: RecDMoN, which performs hierarchical assignment via differentiable graph pooling, and RQ-GAE, which extends RQ-VAE with graph-aware item representations and a graph reconstruction objective. On multiple real-world datasets, GrIS consistently improves over CF-aware SOTA, with gains of up to +52\% Hit@10. Because graph construction and partition are explicit, separately configurable components, improvements on either axis can be combined and evaluated systematically.

</details>


### [197] [The AI Assessment Sandbox Configurator: A Framework to Support Technical Assessment in AI Regulatory Sandboxes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01539)
*Alessio Buscemi,German Castignani,Daniele Pagani,Maxime Cordy,Jordi Cabot*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The EU AI Act mandates AI Regulatory Sandboxes (AIRS) in all Member States by August 2027, but fragmented tooling hampers technical testing at scale. This paper derives 11 architectural and governance requirements for AIRS infrastructure and presents the AI Assessment Sandbox Configurator, an open-source framework (curated Catalogue, plug-in API, shared data model, role-specific dashboards, segmented reporting). An early pilot ran harmonisation and reporting layers in a live AIRS engagement and fed an official Exit Report.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The AI Act obliges Member States to set up AIRS by August 2027, bringing together Competent Authorities, technical experts, and assessed organisations. Where AIRS engagements include structured technical testing, scaling it requires dedicated infrastructure, but the tooling ecosystem is structurally fragmented: heterogeneous tools yield outputs that are hard to compare, trace, and reuse. The paper addresses the missing infrastructural and governance basis for operationalising technical testing within an AIRS.

Method: Requirements-driven architectural design. From (i) the procedural conditions of AIRS engagements and (ii) AI Act obligations for high-risk systems, the authors derive 11 architectural and governance requirements for AIRS testing infrastructure. They then instantiate these requirements in the AI Assessment Sandbox Configurator: an open-source framework comprising a curated Catalogue of tests and controls exposed via a stable plug-in API, a shared data model harmonising heterogeneous outputs, role-specific dashboards for multi-disciplinary interpretation, and audience-segmented reporting. An early-stage pilot exercised the harmonisation and reporting layers inside a live AIRS engagement.

Result: The paper specifies the 11 derived requirements, describes the Configurator's architecture and current release, and reports an early-stage pilot that ran the harmonisation and reporting layers within a live AIRS engagement, contributing to an official Exit Report. The pilot is early-stage; no quantitative evaluation of scaling or comparability gains is reported.

Conclusion: The Configurator offers an open-source infrastructural response to AIRS testing needs. The authors discuss the roadmap, governance questions raised by the Catalogue's tiered contribution model, and institutional pathways through which an open-source assessment ecosystem could emerge across Member States.

Abstract: The EU's Artificial Intelligence Act requires all Member States to establish AI Regulatory Sandboxes (AIRS) by August 2027: supervised environments bringing together national Competent Authorities, technical experts, and the organisations under assessment. When AIRS engagements include structured technical testing, running such testing at scale demands dedicated infrastructure, yet the tooling ecosystem remains structurally fragmented, with heterogeneous tools producing outputs that are difficult to compare, trace, and reuse. From the procedural conditions of AIRS engagements and the AI Act obligations for high-risk systems, we derive 11 architectural and governance requirements for the infrastructure that operationalises technical testing within an AIRS. In response to these requirements, we introduce the AI Assessment Sandbox Configurator, an open-source framework combining a curated Catalogue of tests and controls accessed through a stable plug-in API, a shared data model that harmonises heterogeneous outputs, role-specific dashboards for multi-disciplinary interpretation, and audience-segmented reporting. We describe the architecture and current release, and report an early-stage pilot that exercised the harmonisation and reporting layers within a live AIRS engagement and contributed to an official Exit Report. We discuss the roadmap, the governance questions raised by the Catalogue's tiered contribution model, and the institutional pathways through which an open-source assessment ecosystem could emerge across Member States.

</details>


### [198] [Evaluating Physical Consistency and Plausibility in Generative Scenario Models for Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01581)
*Manasa Mariam Mammen,Zafer Kayatas,Stefan Wagner*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A layered evaluation protocol with five layers (internal representation, network, and output analysis) is proposed to assess generative AI scenario generators for autonomous driving beyond output-level metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generative AI models for autonomous driving scenario generation produce realistic-looking outputs but offer limited transparency into their learned representations and may not maintain consistency with real-world vehicle dynamics. This lack of formal assurance restricts their adoption in safety-critical validation and certification workflows.

Method: A layered evaluation protocol spanning five layers: (1) kinematic alignment, (2) statistical baseline comparison, (3) latent controllability, and (4) activation analysis for internal representations and network layers; plus a fifth layer (5) that evaluates model outputs against vehicle dynamics constraints, such as lateral jerk thresholds. The protocol is demonstrated on a VAE-based scenario generator and applied to additional generative models.

Result: Although standard output-level metrics and visualizations suggest the generated scenarios are realistic, the protocol reveals deeper insights into the extent to which the latent space aligns with kinematic features and whether visually plausible trajectories satisfy vehicle-dynamics constraints.

Conclusion: The layered protocol enables more rigorous, transparent evaluation of generative scenario generators, and its demonstrated applicability across multiple generative architectures shows it is not limited to the VAE.

Abstract: Generative AI models are increasingly used for scenario generation in autonomous driving. While they can generate realistic-looking scenarios, they often provide limited transparency into learned representations and consistency with real-world vehicle dynamics. This lack of formal assurance limits their use in safety-critical validation and certification workflows. To address this aspect, we introduce a layered evaluation protocol that complements existing methods by assessing models across five layers. The first four layers inspect internal representations and network layers through kinematic alignment, statistical baseline comparison, latent controllability, and activation analysis. The fifth layer evaluates model outputs against vehicle dynamics constraints such as lateral jerk thresholds. We demonstrate the protocol on a Variational Autoencoder (VAE)-based scenario generator. Although standard output-level metrics and visualizations suggest that the generated scenarios are realistic, our protocol provides deeper insight into the extent to which the model's latent space aligns with kinematic features and whether visually plausible trajectories satisfy vehicle-dynamics constraints. We further apply the protocol to additional generative models, demonstrating its applicability beyond the VAE architecture.

</details>


### [199] [Agents Are Systems, Not Models: Rethinking Agentic Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01618)
*Luis Wiedmann,Leander Girrbach,Cordelia Schmid,Zeynep Akata*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper argues that agents should be evaluated as configurable systems rather than fixed entities, studying how five configuration choices (task information, reasoning, self-verification, time budget, backbone model) affect performance, consistency, and cost on a new four-task scientific benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agent evaluations increasingly report metrics beyond a single success rate (cost, consistency, robustness), yet they typically treat the agent itself as fixed. In reality, an agent is configurable: users choose what to tell it, how long to let it run, and which model to use—each affecting performance and consistency. The paper aims to systematically study these configuration choices and their interactions.

Method: The authors introduce a new benchmark of four scientific tasks in which a coding agent must find and correctly operate a published specialist model. They investigate five parts of the agent's configuration: task information, reasoning, self-verification, time budget, and backbone model. They analyze outcome variance decomposition across configurations and run-to-run repeats, examine interactions among choices, and build a trajectory-based taxonomy of agent behavior (leveraging over 18,000 released trajectories).

Result: Substantial run-to-run variability is found, with approximately 54% of outcome variance arising from repeating the same configuration rather than changing it. Across configurations, the information provided to the agent has the largest effect, exceeding both time budget and model size, while also reducing cost and improving calibration. Configuration choices interact: additional time helps only when the agent has sufficient information or a capable enough model. The trajectory-based taxonomy reveals that prompting an agent to verify its answer has little effect on verification behavior, whereas providing a dedicated verification tool substantially changes it.

Conclusion: Agents should be evaluated as configurable systems themselves, not as fixed entities. Moreover, some desired behaviors are more effectively implemented in the system than requested through prompting. The benchmark and more than 18,000 agent trajectories are released.

Abstract: Agent evaluations increasingly go beyond a single success rate, reporting metrics such as cost, consistency, and robustness. Yet they typically treat the agent itself as fixed. In practice, an agent is a configurable system: users decide what to tell it, how long to let it run, and which model to use, and each of these choices can change how well and how consistently it performs. We study these choices on a new benchmark of four scientific tasks, where a coding agent must find and correctly operate a published specialist model. We investigate five parts of the agent's configuration: task information, reasoning, self-verification, time budget, and backbone model. We find substantial run-to-run variability, with approximately 54% of the outcome variance coming from repeating the same configuration rather than changing it. Across configurations, the information provided to the agent has the largest effect, exceeding both time budget and model size, while also reducing cost and improving calibration. Configuration choices also interact: additional time helps only when the agent has sufficient information or a capable enough model to use it. Finally, a trajectory-based taxonomy of agent behavior reveals that prompting an agent to verify its answer has little effect on its verification behavior, whereas providing a dedicated verification tool changes that behavior substantially. These results suggest that agents should be evaluated as configurable systems themselves, and that some desired behaviors are more effectively implemented in the system than requested through prompting. We release the benchmark and more than 18,000 agent trajectories.

</details>


### [200] [FedLore: Communication and Memory Efficient Federated Learning via Shared Gradient Low-Rank Projection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01620)
*Junkang Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FedLore shares a low-rank optimization basis within each federated round and refreshes it across rounds, avoiding subspace fragmentation and enabling exact low-rank aggregation, with a proven O(T^{-1/2}) stationarity bound.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Federated training of foundation models is bottlenecked by client memory and communication. LoRA-style fixed-rank adapters limit adaptability, while gradient low-rank optimization suffers from "subspace fragmentation": independently chosen client subspaces combined with data heterogeneity bias aggregated directions, and aggregation inflates update rank and communication cost. Accurate local gradient compression therefore does not guarantee global descent.

Method: FedLore shares a single low-rank optimization basis within each round and refreshes this basis across rounds. The shared basis permits exact aggregation in low-rank coordinates, removing the identified projection bias, while cross-round subspace refresh lets the cumulative model update exceed the per-round rank budget. A projected-SGD variant is analyzed, characterizing the aggregation bias and deriving an O(T^{-1/2}) stationarity bound under a global-gradient coverage condition plus standard smoothness, variance, and bounded gradient-heterogeneity assumptions.

Result: Experiments on vision and language tasks, including federated pre-training, show FedLore outperforms the evaluated low-rank adapter baselines and matches or exceeds full-parameter training, while reducing communication and optimizer-state memory.

Conclusion: Sharing and periodically refreshing a low-rank optimization basis resolves subspace fragmentation, enabling exact aggregation, rank-budget amortization across rounds, and theoretical convergence guarantees, yielding strong empirical performance at lower communication and memory cost.

Abstract: Federated training of foundation models is constrained by client memory and communication costs. LoRA-based methods reduce these costs through low-rank adapters, but their fixed rank budget can limit adaptation. Gradient low-rank optimization offers greater flexibility, yet independently chosen client subspaces create a problem we term \emph{subspace fragmentation}: local projections interact with data heterogeneity to bias aggregated directions, while aggregation can increase update rank and communication cost. Thus, accurate local gradient compression need not preserve global descent. We propose \texttt{FedLore}, which shares a low-rank optimization basis within each round and refreshes it across rounds. The shared basis enables exact aggregation in low-rank coordinates and eliminates the identified projection bias. Subspace refresh allows the accumulated model update to exceed the per-round rank budget. We characterize the aggregation bias and establish an $O(T^{-1/2})$ stationarity bound for the projected-SGD variant under a global-gradient coverage condition and standard smoothness and variance assumptions, with bounded gradient heterogeneity. Experiments on vision and language tasks, including federated pre-training, show that \texttt{FedLore} outperforms the evaluated low-rank adapter baselines and matches or exceeds full-parameter training, while reducing communication and optimizer-state memory.

</details>


### [201] [Measuring the Stability Assumption Behind Action Chunking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01626)
*Aryan Goyal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how an injected action error propagates under open-loop (chunk replay) vs. closed-loop (replanning) execution across twelve manipulation tasks, finding that stable states are rare, amplification is common and front-loaded, and that open-loop propagation is predictable from observation while closed-loop propagation only partially is—implying error compounding is an incomplete explanation of action chunking and that closed-loop reactivity should be trained explicitly.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Action chunking is known to improve behavioural-cloning policies, but existing explanations (temporal consistency, horizon reduction, representation learning, reduced error compounding) are not fully validated. The authors shift focus from why chunking helps to what actually happens to an action error once it enters the system, questioning whether error-compounding alone can account for the benefits.

Method: At each state, a small action error is injected and its growth/decay rate measured under two regimes: open-loop (rest of chunk replayed without replanning) and closed-loop (policy replans after perturbation). The fitted propagation rate labels each state as contracting, expanding, or unresolved. Experiments span twelve manipulation tasks from three benchmark suites. The authors additionally analyse sensitivity to fitting horizon and train predictors on the labels.

Result: Confidently stable (contracting) states are rare, while error amplification is common among states with resolvable propagation rates. Measured rates depend strongly on the fitting horizon: amplification is typically front-loaded, so short windows overestimate longer-horizon propagation. Predictors recover a state's open-loop regime from camera frames and proprioception alone, but closed-loop propagation is only partially recoverable, as it depends on post-perturbation policy behaviour.

Conclusion: Error-compounding arguments alone do not fully explain action chunking: neither passive open-loop dynamics nor policy replanning consistently contracts injected errors, and replanning rarely converts open-loop amplification into confident contraction. The authors recommend explicitly training closed-loop reactivity via perturbation- and tree-coverage-oriented training to expose policies to deviations they must recover from, rather than expecting it to emerge from standard imitation learning.

Abstract: Action chunking improves the performance of policies learned by behavioural cloning, and several mechanisms have been proposed to explain why, including temporal consistency, horizon reduction, representation learning, and reduced error compounding. We instead study what happens to an action error once it enters the system. At each state, we inject a small action error and measure how fast it grows or shrinks under two execution regimes: open-loop, where the rest of the chunk is replayed without replanning, and closed-loop, where the policy replans after the perturbation. The fitted rate labels each state as contracting, expanding, or unresolved. Across twelve manipulation tasks from three benchmark suites, we find that confidently stable states are rare, while error amplification is common among states whose propagation rate can be resolved. We further find that the measured propagation rate depends strongly on the fitting horizon: amplification is typically front-loaded, so short windows can overestimate longer-horizon propagation. Finally, we train predictors on these labels and find that a state's open-loop regime can be recovered from camera frames and proprioception alone, while its closed-loop propagation is only partially recoverable because it also depends on how the policy acts after the perturbation. These results suggest that error-compounding arguments alone do not provide a complete account of action chunking: neither passive open-loop dynamics nor policy replanning consistently contracts an injected error, and replanning rarely turns open-loop amplification into confident contraction. This suggests that closed-loop reactivity should be trained explicitly, using perturbation- and tree-coverage-oriented training to expose policies to deviations they must recover from, rather than expected to emerge reliably from standard imitation learning.

</details>


### [202] [CoEvolve: Construct-to-Edit Visual Grounding with Bidirectional State Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01710)
*Dongwei Sun,Yujie Zhang,Bowen Yao,Pei Liu,Jing Yao,Xiangyong Cao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CoEvolve reformulates visual grounding as explicit state construction plus state editing, using Region-Evolution Reinforcement and a Bidirectional Denoising Refiner to make intermediate spatial states measurable and correctable, achieving strong accuracy with a 9B backbone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most multimodal grounding models collapse target identification, spatial reasoning, and boundary estimation into a single terminal prediction. Free-form rationales make reasoning linguistically explicit but fail to expose measurable, editable spatial states, so intermediate localization errors are hard to diagnose and correct, letting wrong region choices and imprecise boundaries persist.

Method: A construct-to-edit framework separating grounding into explicit state construction and state editing. Region-Evolution Reinforcement (RER) structures grounding analysis into a progressive semantic-spatial trajectory where each step commits to an explicit candidate region. Bidirectional Denoising Refiner (BDR) treats reasoning text as fixed semantic context and refines trajectory coordinate fields via bidirectional same-position reconstruction. Geometry- and behavior-level objectives supply target geometry and edit-preference signals to consolidate reliable candidates, preserve accurate inputs, or correct toward annotations.

Result: Evaluated on natural-image and remote-sensing grounding. With a 9B backbone, CoEvolve rivals models up to 241B parameters in grounding accuracy. Under controlled corruption, a single BDR pass improves mean box overlap by over 27 percentage points. State-source comparisons support the complementarity of explicit state construction and source-matched editing.

Conclusion: Explicit, editable spatial states with construction and source-matched editing enable diagnosable, correctable grounding, delivering competitive accuracy at far lower parameter scale and strong recovery from substantial localization errors.

Abstract: Visual grounding localizes an object described by language with a bounding box. Most multimodal grounding models compress target identification, spatial reasoning, and boundary estimation into one terminal prediction. Free-form rationales make reasoning linguistically explicit but do not necessarily expose measurable, editable spatial states. Intermediate localization errors are therefore difficult to diagnose and correct, allowing incorrect region choices and imprecise boundaries to persist in the final box. We introduce CoEvolve, a construct-to-edit framework that separates grounding into explicit state construction and state editing. Region-Evolution Reinforcement (RER) organizes grounding analysis into a progressive semantic--spatial trajectory, with each reasoning step committing to an explicit candidate region. Bidirectional Denoising Refiner (BDR) treats the reasoning text as fixed semantic context and refines the trajectory's coordinate fields through bidirectional same-position reconstruction. Geometry- and behavior-level objectives provide target geometry and edit-preference signals for consolidating reliable candidates, preserving accurate inputs, or correcting toward annotations. Evaluations cover natural-image and remote-sensing grounding. With a 9B backbone, CoEvolve rivals models up to 241B parameters in grounding accuracy. Under controlled corruption, a single BDR pass improves mean box overlap by over 27 percentage points, demonstrating strong recovery from substantial localization errors. State-source comparisons further support the complementarity of explicit state construction and source-matched editing. The project is at https://sundongwei.github.io/CoEvolve_Project/.

</details>


### [203] [vFedProtoQNAS: Prototype-Guided Personalized Quantum Neural Architecture Search for Virtual Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01718)
*Seok Bin Son,Samuel Yen-Chi Chen,Soohyun Park,Joongheon Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: vFedProtoQNAS is a prototype-guided personalized quantum NAS method for virtual FL that avoids parameter aggregation by sharing class-wise prototypes, improving accuracy by 3.70% over FedAvg.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Device capability differences make a single shared QNN architecture unsuitable for all clients, and averaging parameters across structurally different QNNs mixes semantically inconsistent circuit operations in QFL.

Method: Each client independently searches and trains a client-specific QNN via personalized QNAS, computes class-wise local prototypes from latent representations, and refines them using global prototypes from the server as federated semantic anchors, with no cross-client parameter aggregation.

Result: vFedProtoQNAS improves accuracy by 3.70% over FedAvg and enhances class-consistent representation alignment.

Conclusion: Prototype-based virtual federated collaboration offers an effective alternative to parameter averaging for personalized QNAS in QFL.

Abstract: Quantum federated learning (QFL) has emerged as a promising approach for collaboratively training compact quantum neural networks (QNNs) over distributed private data on resource-constrained devices. However, differences in device capabilities make a single shared QNN architecture unsuitable for all clients. While personalized quantum neural architecture search (QNAS) allows each client to select a device-specific QNN, averaging parameters across structurally different QNN architectures mixes semantically inconsistent circuit operations. To address this, prototype-guided personalized QNAS for virtual FL (vFedProtoQNAS) is proposed, where model parameters are never aggregated across clients and federated collaboration is achieved through class-wise prototype sharing. Each client independently searches and trains a client-specific QNN, computes class-wise local prototypes from latent representations, and refines them using global prototypes from the server as federated semantic anchors. Experiments demonstrate that vFedProtoQNAS improves accuracy by 3.70\% over FedAvg and enhances class-consistent representation alignment.

</details>


### [204] [TopK-Guided: Adaptive, Budget-Aware Activation Sparsity for Efficient LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01763)
*Mukund Agarwalla,Chih-Jen Lin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TopK-Guided is a training-free activation-sparsity method that combines token-level sparsity adaptation with sensitivity-aware block-level budget allocation, improving perplexity and downstream accuracy over TEAL and WINA on Llama-2 and Llama-3 at comparable compute.
