<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing training-free activation sparsity methods trade off control and adaptivity: threshold-based methods (e.g., TEAL) adapt sparsity per token but do not tightly control the realized sparsity, while TopK-based methods (e.g., WINA) enforce a fixed sparsity level but use the same budget for every token. Both also apply a uniform budget across transformer blocks despite large differences in block sensitivity.

Method: TopK-Guided, a training-free approach, combines bounded token-level sparsity adaptation with sensitivity-aware block-level budget allocation to address both limitations simultaneously.

Result: Across Llama-2 and Llama-3 models, TopK-Guided consistently improves perplexity and downstream accuracy over TEAL and WINA while preserving essentially the same sparsity-dependent projection compute as WINA, with the largest gains at high sparsity. Ablations confirm that both components yield complementary improvements.

Conclusion: TopK-Guided offers an effective, training-free way to improve activation-sparsity inference quality without additional projection compute relative to WINA, with both its token-level and block-level components contributing complementary benefits.

Abstract: Activation sparsity speeds up large language model (LLM) inference by setting unimportant activations to zero so that the corresponding computations can be skipped. Existing training-free methods, however, make different trade-offs: threshold-based methods such as TEAL adapt the sparsity level to each token but do not tightly control the realised sparsity, while TopK-based methods such as WINA enforce a fixed sparsity level but use the same sparsity budget for every token. Both also apply the same budget across transformer blocks, despite large differences in block sensitivity. We introduce TopK-Guided, a training-free method that addresses both limitations by combining bounded token-level sparsity adaptation with sensitivity-aware block-level budget allocation. Across Llama-2 and Llama-3 models, TopK-Guided consistently improves perplexity and downstream accuracy over TEAL and WINA while preserving essentially the same sparsitydependent projection compute as WINA, with the largest gains at high sparsity. Ablations show that both components provide complementary improvements.

</details>


### [205] [VideoEvolve: Evolving Agent Harnesses for Video Temporal Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01766)
*Bingjun Luo,Yuhuan Fan,Jialin Guo,Siqi Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VideoEvolve automatically evolves agent harnesses for video temporal grounding, using a Cloze-Structured Harness Representation and Branch-Guided Harness Evolution to improve grounding performance across benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: For agents built around frozen video-language models, the harness controls how queries guide temporal predictions and how predictions are refined. Manually refining these harnesses is costly, requiring diagnosis of grounding failures and coordinated changes to both agent workflows and instructions. This motivates an automated approach.

Method: The framework uses a Cloze-Structured Harness Representation that preserves stage interfaces while leaving agent workflows and instructions open to evolution. Branch-Guided Harness Evolution preserves promising code branches for continued refinement, using execution feedback to guide local edits and validation to select which improvements are carried forward.

Result: Experiments show improved grounding performance across multiple benchmarks. Component analyses identify instruction refinement as a consistent source of gains, while the benefits of evolved code vary across evaluation settings.

Conclusion: The results support automated harness evolution as an effective approach to improving video temporal grounding. Code is available at the provided repository.

Abstract: Video temporal grounding aims to localize events in videos from natural-language queries. For agents built around frozen video-language models, the harness determines how queries guide temporal predictions and how those predictions are refined. Manually refining these harnesses requires diagnosing grounding failures and coordinating changes to both agent workflows and instructions. We introduce VideoEvolve, a framework that automatically evolves agent harnesses for video temporal grounding. VideoEvolve uses a Cloze-Structured Harness Representation that preserves stage interfaces while leaving agent workflows and instructions open to evolution. Branch-Guided Harness Evolution preserves promising code branches for continued refinement, using execution feedback to guide local edits and validation to determine which improvements are carried forward. Experiments demonstrate improved grounding performance across multiple benchmarks. Component analyses identify instruction refinement as a consistent source of gains, while the benefits of evolved code vary across evaluation settings. Together, these results support automated harness evolution as an effective approach to improving video temporal grounding. Code is available at https://github.com/bingjunluo/VideoEvolve .

</details>


### [206] [RealCompanion: Benchmarking Human Understanding from Reasoning over Longitudinal Real-World Conversations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01780)
*Arman Behnam,Sunglyoung Kim,Liangwei Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper releases BENCH, ten real long-term human–AI companion relationships (27,218 messages over up to 120 days), each with a conversation plus four derived artifacts (profile, persona, chat ground truth, question set) and per-label reasoning traces; it shows that long-horizon memory is rarely required, that memory-need detection fails on real messages, and that agent systems vary 31-fold in cost at equal persona-reconstruction F1.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: A companion that talks with a person for months should understand them: remember what they said, infer who they are, and know when the past bears on the current message. Testing this requires a real person's record, but such records are private, so existing benchmarks fabricate the person and the questions and decide in advance what matters—yielding an unrealistic and potentially misleading evaluation of long-term memory. The motivation is thus to build a benchmark grounded in real relationships and real interactions, and to measure what memory actually contributes.

Method: The authors release BENCH, comprising ten real relationships with an AI companion: 27,218 messages spanning up to 120 days. Each relationship is released as the raw conversation plus four files derived from it—a profile, a persona, a chat ground truth, and a question set—each citing the messages it rests on. Every chat label carries the reasoning trace that produced it, checked stage by stage against the conversation. Analysis then proceeds along three axes: (1) quantifying how often and how far back the required past message lies and how much gain comes from supplying remembered evidence; (2) testing detectors for when memory is needed versus authored-question cues and memory-labeling manipulations; (3) comparing agent systems on persona reconstruction at differing cost.

Result: First, the past is rarely needed and far away, and pooled measures mislead: a recency window finds the required message for 95.9% of probes and only 2.2% of those that truly need memory, and at the natural rate 96% of the gain from supplying recorded evidence comes from messages that need none. Second, no detector tried can tell when memory is needed on real messages; authored questions over the same histories leak the cue, and labeling the same messages as memories raises their use by ten to fourteen points. Third, three agent systems reconstruct the persona with the same F1 at a 31-fold difference in cost.

Conclusion: Real long-term companion relationships make memory demands far rarer and more distant than fabricated benchmarks suggest, and aggregate memory metrics are misleading because most apparent benefit comes from messages that need no memory. Memory-need detection is unsolved on natural messages, while authored-question framing and memory-labeling artificially inflate memory use. Because equally accurate persona reconstruction can differ 31-fold in cost, BENCH argues for evaluating long-horizon companion memory on real records and reporting cost alongside accuracy.

Abstract: A companion that talks with a person for months should come to understand them. It should remember what they said, infer who they are, and know when the past bears on the message in front of it. Testing this requires a real person's record, and such records are private, so benchmarks generate the person and the questions and settle in advance what matters. We release \bench, ten real relationships with an AI companion: 27,218 messages over up to 120 days, released as the conversation and four files derived from it, a profile, a persona, a chat ground truth and a question set, each citing the messages it rests on. Every chat label carries the reasoning trace that produced it, checked stage by stage against the conversation. Three findings follow. First, the past is rarely needed and far away. Pooled measures mislead: a recency window finds the required message for 95.9\% of probes and 2.2\% of those that need memory, and at the natural rate 96\% of the gain from supplying recorded evidence comes from messages that need none. Second, no detector we tried can tell when memory is needed on real messages, authored questions over the same histories leak the cue, and labeling the same messages as memories raises their use by ten to fourteen points. Third, three agent systems reconstruct the persona with the same F1 at a 31-fold difference in cost.

</details>


### [207] [Q-Learning for Reachability in MEC-Free MDPs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01781)
*Lu-Chin Chang,Suguman Bansal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Quasar is the first model-free reinforcement learning algorithm with asymptotic guarantees for reachability specifications, working on MDPs without non-terminal maximal end components.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reachability specifications are fundamental to sequential decision-making in RL. Prior methods achieve asymptotic convergence to optimal policies only via model-based approaches that must explicitly estimate the transition probabilities of the underlying MDP, which is costly in memory and samples.

Method: The authors propose Quasar, a model-free algorithm following the classical Q-learning approach with temporal-difference updates. It converges to an optimal policy without ever learning transition probabilities, targeting the fragment of MDPs free of non-terminal maximal end components (MECs), a building block to which every MDP reduces via the standard MEC quotient.

Result: Quasar reduces the memory footprint from O(|S|^2|A|) required by model-based methods to O(|S||A|). On the standardized Quantitative Verification Benchmark Set, it converges to the optimal policy with orders of magnitude fewer samples than the previous model-based state-of-the-art.

Conclusion: These results represent a concrete step toward the practical deployment of reachability learning and, with it, specification-guided reinforcement learning.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) for reachability specifications is fundamental to sequential decision-making. Prior work establishes asymptotic convergence to optimal policies, but only through model-based methods that must explicitly estimate the transition probabilities of the underlying Markov Decision Process (MDP). We present Quasar, the first model-free algorithm with asymptotic guarantees for reachability on the fragment of MDPs free of non-terminal maximal end components (MECs), a building block to which every MDP reduces by the standard MEC quotient. Our algorithm follows the classical Q-learning approach, using temporal-difference updates to converge to an optimal policy without ever learning the transition probabilities. The resulting learner reduces the memory footprint from the O(|S|^2|A|) that model-based methods require to O(|S||A|). On the standardized Quantitative Verification Benchmark Set, our algorithm converges to the optimal policy with orders of magnitude fewer samples than the previous model-based state-of-the-art. Together these results are a concrete step toward the practical deployment of reachability learning and, with it, of specification-guided RL.

</details>


### [208] [Not All Experience Belongs in the Weights: Component Routing for Self-Improving GUI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01787)
*Beining Wu,Zihao Ding,Jun Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper argues that the disagreement over whether self-improving GUI agents should reuse trajectories by fine-tuning (weights) or retrieval (context) stems from treating a trajectory as a single unit, and shows that routing individual experience components to different destinations outperforms whole-trajectory baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work on self-improving GUI agents disagrees on whether produced trajectories should be returned via fine-tuning into the weights or via retrieval into the prompt. The authors attribute this inconsistency to the unit of experience: a trajectory bundles heterogeneous items, so conclusions about the bundle depend on its mix rather than on the destinations themselves.

Method: (i) Component routing splits experience into locators, procedures, state facts and lessons, assigning each component to context or weights, compared on identical items across three backbone families, two environments and three seeds. (ii) A rule based on two pre-training properties (recurrence and state-conditionality) predicts the destination, validated on a held-out backbone family, tested with two boundary-moving interventions and benchmarked against whole-trajectory baselines. (iii) Analysis of how training dynamics and producer-consumer differences alter the value of each destination.

Result: Locators and lessons perform better in the weights, while procedures and state facts perform better in the context. The rule recovers the destination of a held-out backbone family in 24 of 24 cells, and interventions move components toward the predicted boundary. Routing by the rule beats every whole-trajectory baseline and exceeds, by +3.5 points on average, the better single destination per backbone. Note readout decreases after a component is written into the weights, most for highly recurrent items; context gains increase with the information gap, and weights gains decrease with the policy gap.

Conclusion: Destination choice for self-improving GUI agent experience should be made per component rather than per trajectory. Pre-training properties of recurrence and state-conditionality predict the correct destination, and the observed training and producer-consumer effects provide a mechanistic account of when context or weights are preferable.

Abstract: Self-improving GUI agents keep the trajectories they produce and return them to the agent, by fine-tuning or by retrieval into the prompt, and studies that compare the two destinations disagree. We attribute this to the unit of experience: a trajectory bundles items with different properties, so a conclusion about the bundle depends on its mix. To address this, (i) we introduce component routing, which splits the experience into locators, procedures, state facts and lessons and sends each component to the context or to the weights, compared on the same items across three backbone families, two environments and three seeds. One pool has two destinations: locators and lessons win in the weights, procedures and state facts in the context. (ii) We fit a rule in two properties measured before any training, recurrence and state-conditionality; it recovers the destination of a held-out backbone family in 24 of 24 cells, two interventions move a component toward the boundary, and routing by the rule beats every whole-trajectory baseline and, by +3.5 points on average, the better single destination of each backbone. (iii) We identify how training and producer-consumer differences change the value of the two destinations: note readout decreases after the same component is written into the weights, most for the items that recur most, context gains increase with the information gap, and weights gains decrease with the policy gap. Code and data will be released.

</details>


### [209] [LineupRL: Verifiable Reinforcement Learning for Time Series Captioning via Caption-to-Series Identification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01800)
*Haochen Zhang,Laura Yao,Zachary Plotkin,Gengwei Zhang,Tianlong Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LineupRL is an RLVR pipeline for time series captioning whose reward is caption-to-series identification, judged by a frozen off-the-shelf LLM verifier reading raw values rather than charts, outperforming SFT and RL baselines across captioning, forecasting, and reconstruction, with a 3B VLM beating a 72B teacher.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Time series captioning bridges signals and natural language, but supervised fine-tuning (SFT) is bounded by the quality of a larger model's captions. Reinforcement learning (RL) can exceed this limit, yet existing rewards were designed for other modalities and tasks and transfer poorly to open-ended generation in the time series domain.

Method: LineupRL, a reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) pipeline. Its reward is caption-to-series identification: a frozen off-the-shelf LLM verifier reads the generated caption and candidate time series as raw values (never the chart) and must select the described series from multiple distractors. Matching is a lighter demand on the verifier than writing questions or judging captions, so an off-the-shelf LLM can supply the reward.

Result: Across two captioning benchmarks, and on forecasting and reconstruction where the predictor sees only the caption, LineupRL outperforms SFT and RL baselines on every metric. A 3B VLM trained by LineupRL also outperforms, at 1/24 of the parameters, the 72B VLM from which the SFT baseline is distilled. A case study shows LineupRL resists reward hacking and that the trained captioner both traces the trend and names values at key points.

Conclusion: Caption-to-series identification provides an effective, hack-resistant verifiable reward for time series captioning, enabling efficient RLVR that surpasses SFT and prior RL while yielding compact, high-quality captioners.

Abstract: Time series captioning is a fundamental step in time series understanding and can also serve as the bridge between signal and natural language. Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) relies on a larger model's captions and cannot exceed their quality. Reinforcement learning (RL) can, but its rewards were designed for other modalities and other tasks, and they transfer poorly to open-ended generation in the time series domain. We address this by proposing LineupRL, a reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) pipeline whose reward is caption-to-series identification. The reward model is a frozen large language model (LLM) verifier that reads the generated caption and the candidate time series as raw values, never the chart, and must pick the described time series from multiple distractors. Matching is a far lighter demand on the verifier than writing questions or judging a caption, so an off-the-shelf LLM can supply the reward. Across two captioning benchmarks, and on forecasting and reconstruction where the predictor sees only the caption, LineupRL outperforms SFT and RL baselines on every metric. The 3B vision language model (VLM) trained by LineupRL also outperforms, at 1/24 of the parameters, the 72B VLM whose captions the SFT baseline is distilled from. Our case study shows that LineupRL resists reward hacking, and that the captioner it trains both traces the trend and names the values at key points.

</details>


### [210] [AI-assisted mitotic counting improves reproducibility and efficiency across multiple tumour types](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01813)
*Simon Graham,Mostafa Jahanifar,Quoc Dang Vu,Vygante Maskoliunaite,Donatas Petroska,Ruta Barbora Valkiuniene,Ayat Gamal Lashen,Jen Hong Ong,Amede Ogechi Nnorom,Sinclair Couper,Natasha Kardasz,Reshma Agrawal,Brinder Singh Chohan,Jose Luis Solorzano Rendon,Shonali Natu,Arvydas Laurinavicius,Nasir Rajpoot,David Snead*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MitPro, an AI tool that directs pathologists to predicted mitotic hotspots and highlights mitotic figures, improved inter-pathologist agreement (ICC 0.589 to 0.949) and halved assessment time (286.4 to 127.8 s) in a 13-pathologist, 385-slide paired reader study.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mitotic counting underpins tumour grading, diagnosis and prognosis across many tumour types, but manual counting is time-consuming and suffers from inter-pathologist variability. The authors sought a tool that improves consistency and efficiency while keeping the pathologist in control of region selection and the final count.

Method: Retrospective, non-interventional, paired reader study of MitPro. Dataset: 385 whole-slide images from 3 centres in 3 countries, 7 tumour types, 3 scanners. Thirteen pathologists each assessed every slide independently twice: unassisted and AI-assisted (after a ≥2-week washout). Endpoints were reproducibility (intraclass correlation coefficient) and efficiency (assessment time). Supporting analyses covered HALO AP and Sectra image management systems and 2 additional tumour types.

Result: AI assistance raised the ICC for mitotic counts from 0.589 to 0.949. Mean pathologist-level median assessment time fell from 286.4 s to 127.8 s, an average saving of 151.8 s per assessment. Agreement and efficiency gains replicated in supporting analyses (HALO AP, Sectra; 2 extra tumour types). AI-assisted counts skewed subtly higher, consistent with better hotspot identification and fewer missed mitotic figures. Frequency of score change between unassisted and AI-assisted assessment was comparable to routine inter-pathologist variation.

Conclusion: MitPro substantially improves both reproducibility and efficiency of mitotic counting in a multi-centre, multi-scanner, multi-tumour setting, with a modest upward shift in counts attributable to improved mitotic figure detection. The authors conclude MitPro is suitable as an assistive tool for more consistent and efficient mitotic assessment in routine practice.

Abstract: Mitotic counting is an important component of tumour grading, diagnosis and prognostic assessment across several tumour types, but manual assessment is time-consuming and subject to inter-pathologist variability. To help address these challenges, we developed MitPro, an AI tool designed to improve consistency and efficiency by directing pathologists towards regions with the highest predicted mitotic activity and highlighting mitotic figures for review, while retaining pathologist control over region selection and the final count. We evaluated its effect on the reproducibility and efficiency of mitotic counting in a retrospective, non-interventional, paired reader study comprising 385 whole-slide images from 3 centres in 3 countries and 7 tumour types using 3 different scanners. 13 pathologists participated, with each slide assessed independently by 3 pathologists without AI assistance and again with AI assistance after a minimum 2 week washout period. Across all slides, AI-assisted counting increased the intraclass correlation coefficient from 0.589 to 0.949. Mean pathologist-level median assessment time decreased from 286.4 to 127.8 seconds, corresponding to an average saving of 151.8 seconds per assessment. Improvements in agreement and efficiency were also observed in supporting analyses using HALO AP and Sectra image management systems and in 2 additional tumour types outside the main study population. AI-assisted assessment was associated with a subtle shift towards higher mitotic counts and scores, consistent with identification of more active mitotic hotspots and fewer missed mitotic figures. The frequency of score change between unassisted and AI-assisted assessment was comparable with inter-pathologist variation during routine counting. These findings support the use of MitPro as an assistive tool for more consistent and efficient mitotic assessment in routine practice.

</details>


### [211] [Continuous Process-Level Evaluation for Evolving Enterprise AI Agent Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01833)
*Ngoc Phuoc An Vo,Aarya Doshi,Vadim Sheinin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a continuous evaluation framework that couples outcome-level and process-level (trajectory) checks for enterprise AI agent skills, and shows that most runs passing final numerical checks still harbor process-level deviations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise AI agent skills drift behaviorally as tool APIs, models, and specifications change, but final-output-only evaluation can miss this process-level behavioral drift, creating a need for continuous evaluation that captures how agents reach their results.

Method: A continuous evaluation framework combining outcome-level and process-level checks, applied to Revenue and Productivity variants of a Business Value Determination skill in an enterprise Value Aware Resiliency system. The framework independently computes per-run ground truth, materializes reusable template tests, and evaluates tool selection, arguments, execution order, and database integrity via programmatic checks plus a narrowly scoped LLM judge. Evaluation covers 240 trials across two skills, two specification variants, two agent harnesses, and three models, with dependency attribution and bootstrap interaction analysis.

Result: Of 175 trials passing all applicable final numerical checks, 162 (92.6 percent; Wilson 95 percent CI: 87.7-95.6 percent) contained another evaluator-detected deviation. Under a broader seven-check final-state definition, 151 of 164 passing runs (92.1 percent; 95 percent CI: 86.9-95.3 percent) still violated a trajectory check. Dependency attribution reduced a mean of 6.34 failed checks per run to 2.65 roots. Specification sensitivity varied by model and harness, with exploratory bootstrap interaction intervals excluding zero for all three Revenue comparisons and one of three Productivity comparisons.

Conclusion: Final-output evaluation substantially overstates agent correctness, since the majority of runs passing numerical checks still exhibit process-level deviations. Runtime-resolved templates provide reusable regression coverage across the evaluated configurations, while longitudinal validation under actual API evolution remains future work.

Abstract: Enterprise AI agent skills evolve as tool APIs, models, and specifications change, yet final-output evaluation can miss process-level behavioral drift. We present a continuous evaluation framework combining outcome-level and process-level checks, applied to Revenue and Productivity variants of a Business Value Determination skill in an enterprise Value Aware Resiliency system. The framework independently computes per-run ground truth, materializes reusable template tests, and evaluates tool selection, arguments, execution order, and database integrity through programmatic checks and a narrowly scoped LLM judge. We evaluate 240 trials across two skills, two specification variants, two agent harnesses, and three models. Of 175 trials passing all applicable final numerical checks, 162 (92.6 percent; Wilson 95 percent CI: 87.7-95.6 percent) contained another evaluator-detected deviation. Under a broader seven-check final-state definition, 151 of 164 passing runs (92.1 percent; 95 percent CI: 86.9-95.3 percent) still violated a trajectory check. Dependency attribution reduced a mean of 6.34 failed checks per run to 2.65 roots. Specification sensitivity varied by model and harness, with exploratory bootstrap interaction intervals excluding zero for all three Revenue comparisons and one of three Productivity comparisons. Runtime-resolved templates provided reusable regression coverage across the evaluated configurations; longitudinal validation under actual API evolution remains future work.

</details>


### [212] [Code Owns the Simulation, Jev Owns the Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01834)
*Yaodong Yang,Hongyao Tang,Yi Ma,Xingyu Fan,Weixun Wang,Jinpeng Li,Tianpei Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: JEV-like judgment models succeed at "evaluation" (judging options from what the input describes) but fail at "simulation" (predicting something not in the input), and their performance is recovered when code supplies the missing prediction/simulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Judgment models return option probabilities in a single call without reasoning text, making them attractive as an agent's action-selection layer, but it is unclear which decisions they can be trusted with.

Method: Test JEV on reflection tests (Cognitive Reflection Test), one-shot matrix games, the text game ALFWorld, and robot control to probe the boundary between evaluation and simulation.

Result: JEV solves 99% of counterintuitive CRT questions, but fails when the correct option requires simulation: in games it plays suboptimally as if the rational opponent acted at random; in ALFWorld it favors commands mentioning objects/places in the task description (e.g., taking an unwashed knife straight to the drawer). These failures are not due to missing knowledge, since JEV answers correctly when asked separately about the opponent's action and responds well given that action; it fails only when one call must both simulate and evaluate. Providing code-supplied predictions (lookahead in ALFWorld, physics simulation in robot control) restores strong performance.

Conclusion: JEV is a reliable evaluator but not a simulator; the remedy is to let code perform the prediction or simulation, after which JEV becomes an expert controller through its general evaluation ability.

Abstract: Judgment models such as \jev{} return, in a single call and without reasoning text, a probability for each described option. This makes them attractive as an agent's action-selection layer, but it is unclear which decisions they can be trusted with. We test \jev{} on reflection tests, one-shot matrix games, the text game ALFWorld and robot control, and find a sharp boundary. \jev{} succeeds when the right option can be judged from what the input describes, which we call \emph{evaluation}. Specifically, it solves 99\% of the counterintuitive Cognitive Reflection Test questions. However, it fails when the right option depends on \emph{simulation} (i.e., predicting something not in the input), such as the opponent's action or the subgoal that must come first. In games, \jev{} plays suboptimally as if its rational opponent acted at random, because the opponent's action is not given. In ALFWorld, \jev{} favors commands that mention an object or place named in the task description. For example, given the task ``put a clean knife in the drawer'', \jev{} carries an unwashed knife straight to the drawer instead of first washing it at the sink. Surprisingly, many of these failures are not due to a lack of knowledge. Asked separately what the opponent will do, \jev{} usually answers correctly, and it responds well given the opponent's action. It fails when one call must both perform the simulation and evaluate based on it. This suggests letting code make the prediction or simulation. When code supplies it, such as a lookahead in ALFWorld and physics simulation in robot control, \jev{} becomes an expert controller through its general evaluation ability.

</details>


### [213] [On the Divergence of Accuracy and Mechanism Consistency in Time Series World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01842)
*Haochen Zhang,Jiaheng Guo,Zhen Xu,Zachary Plotkin,Nicholas Konz,Zhen Tan,Tianlong Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper formalizes and benchmarks time series world models (TSWMs), showing that frozen latent prediction spaces and gated output fusion improve accuracy, but prediction error and mechanism consistency diverge; directional supervision improves consistency without hurting MAE.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current TSWMs are trained and evaluated only on prediction error under the executed plan, yet world models are meant to compare unexecuted plans. It remains untested which design choices matter and whether accurate forecasters respond to changed plans the way real systems do.

Method: The authors provide a formalization separating state, actions, and exogenous inputs; distinguishing continuous, mode, and event actions; and introducing mechanism consistency, a metric based on declared action-state relations with known directions. They build a benchmark consolidating eight public datasets with real actions from engineered infrastructure and clinical care, varying prediction space, plan fusion, and plan encoding across seven backbones and five seeds. They also propose directional supervision, a loss penalizing the wrong-signed part of the response to a shifted action.

Result: A frozen latent prediction space lowers MAE by 9.9% over observation space, and gated output fusion lowers it by 12.7% over input concatenation on average, both improving all eight datasets; temporal plan encoding changes average MAE by at most 2.2%. Prediction error and mechanism consistency diverge: the lowest-error configuration is at or below chance in consistency on four of five datasets with declared mechanisms, and no design choice avoids this. Directional supervision significantly raises consistency on penalized mechanisms with no change in MAE.

Conclusion: The paper offers TSWMs a recipe: a frozen latent space and output-side fusion for accuracy, and a training objective (directional supervision) for mechanism consistency.

Abstract: A time series world model (TSWM) predicts a controlled system's state from its observed history and planned actions and exogenous inputs. Current approaches build forecasters with actions as covariates, trained and evaluated on prediction error under the executed plan. Yet world models compare unexecuted plans, but their responses to changed plans remain untested. We ask which design choices matter and whether accurate forecasters respond to changed plans as real systems do. We address both with a formalization and benchmark. The formalization separates state, actions and exogenous inputs, distinguishes continuous, mode and event actions, and introduces mechanism consistency, a metric built on declared action-state relations with known directions, such as a vasopressor raising blood pressure: it checks whether shifting an action moves the forecast in the declared direction. The benchmark consolidates eight public datasets with real actions from engineered infrastructure and clinical care, varying prediction space, plan fusion and plan encoding across seven backbones and five seeds. First, a frozen latent prediction space lowers MAE by 9.9% over observation space and gated output fusion lowers it by 12.7% over input concatenation on average, with both improving all eight datasets; temporal plan encoding changes average MAE by at most 2.2%. Second, prediction error and mechanism consistency diverge: the lowest-error configuration is at or below chance in consistency on four of five datasets with declared mechanisms, and no design choice avoids this. Finally, directional supervision, a loss penalizing the wrong-signed part of the response to a shifted action, significantly raises consistency on penalized mechanisms with no change in MAE. Together they give TSWMs a recipe: a frozen latent space and output-side fusion for accuracy, and a training objective for mechanism consistency.

</details>


### [214] [Temporal-Difference Learning for Dragonchess](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01845)
*Jim O'Connor,Annika Hoag,Sarah Goyette,Gary B. Parker*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper benchmarks two adaptive AI methods—evolutionary transfer learning and TD(λ)—in the 3D chess variant Dragonchess, using a re-implemented C++ engine for large-scale evaluation; both methods outperform all baselines with no significant performance difference between them.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dragonchess's unusual board structure and heavy computational load make it an ideal testbed for studying whether adaptive methods can successfully update evaluation heuristics in novel, structurally complex game domains.

Method: The authors re-implemented the Dragonchess engine, porting it from PyGame to C++ to enable faster gameplay. This speedup allowed running 10,000 games with confidence intervals and significance tests instead of a single small tournament, and they compared evolutionary transfer learning and TD(λ) against other agents in a round-robin tournament.

Result: Both adaptive methods outperformed all other agents in the round-robin tournament. There was no significant difference in performance between the evolved and learned evaluations.

Conclusion: This research establishes the efficacy of adaptive methods in structurally complex, novel game domains.

Abstract: Our research investigates how two adaptive AI methods, evolutionary transfer learning and TD(lambda), perform in the three-dimensional chess environment Dragonchess. The game challenges players with its unique board structure and computational load, making it an ideal setting to study how adaptive methods can update evaluation heuristics in novel environments. In this work we re-implement the Dragonchess engine, changing it from a PyGame engine to C++. This enables faster gameplay, allowing us to run 10,000 games with confidence intervals and significance tests, rather than a single small tournament. Both adaptive methods outperform all other agents in the round-robin tournament. Our results showed that there is no significant difference in the performance between the evolved and learned evaluations. This research establishes the efficacy of adaptive methods in structurally complex, novel game domains.

</details>


### [215] [AVSD-Scenes: A Dataset for Audio-Visual Description of Urban Scenes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01861)
*Dhanunjaya Varma Devalraju,Arshdeep Singh,Mark D. Plumbley*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces AVSD-Scenes, a paired audio-visual scene description dataset for urban environments containing 12,291 multimodal descriptions, and benchmarks it across multiple tasks to demonstrate improved semantic alignment and scene discrimination.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Natural language descriptions can provide rich semantic representations of audio-visual urban scenes, but datasets that jointly describe both auditory and visual information remain limited, hindering research on multimodal scene understanding.

Method: The authors build AVSD-Scenes from the TAU Urban Audio-Visual Scenes dataset. Audio-based descriptions are generated with Qwen2-Audio-7B and visual-based descriptions with Qwen2.5-VL-7B. These modality-specific descriptions are then fused using large language models (Qwen3-14B, Mistral-Small-3.2-24B-Instruct-2506, and Gemma-3-27B-it) to produce multimodal descriptions capturing complementary information. Evaluation uses semantic alignment, cross-modal retrieval, scene classification, LLM-as-a-judge, and human subjective assessment.

Result: Multimodal descriptions improve semantic alignment and cross-modal retrieval over modality-specific descriptions while preserving strong scene-discriminative information. The descriptions reach up to 94.5% accuracy in urban scene classification, and combining audio, visual, and description embeddings raises accuracy to 95.4%. Descriptions remain highly scene-discriminative even when scene labels are removed from prompting instructions.

Conclusion: AVSD-Scenes provides a valuable paired audio-visual scene description resource, and the results show that multimodal descriptions capture semantic information derived from audio-visual content rather than merely reflecting label information.

Abstract: Natural language descriptions can provide rich semantic representations of audio-visual urban scenes, yet datasets that jointly describe both auditory and visual information remain limited. In this paper, we introduce AVSD-Scenes, a paired audio-visual scene description dataset for urban environments. The dataset contains 12,291 audio-visual scene descriptions generated from the TAU Urban Audio-Visual Scenes dataset. To construct the dataset, we first generate audio- and visual-based descriptions using Qwen2-Audio-7B and Qwen2.5-VL-7B, respectively. These modality-specific descriptions are then combined using large language models, namely Qwen3-14B, Mistral-Small-3.2-24B-Instruct-2506, and Gemma-3-27B-it, to produce multimodal descriptions that capture complementary information from both modalities. We benchmark AVSD-Scenes using semantic alignment, cross-modal retrieval, scene classification, LLM-as-a-judge evaluation, and human subjective assessment. Results show that multimodal descriptions improve semantic alignment and cross-modal retrieval performance compared with modality-specific descriptions while preserving strong scene-discriminative information. The generated descriptions achieve up to 94.5% accuracy in urban scene classification, while combining audio, visual, and description embeddings further improves accuracy to 95.4%. Furthermore, the descriptions remain highly scene-discriminative even when scene labels are removed from the prompting instructions, indicating that they capture semantic information derived from the audio-visual content rather than merely reflecting label information.

</details>


### [216] [Mapping the RAG Landscape: A Four Axis Taxonomy of Efficiency, Defense, Interactivity, and Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01936)
*Meghana Sunil,Shravya V,Shravan Venkatraman,Joe Dhanith PR*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A consolidated survey of contemporary Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG), organized into a four-axis taxonomy covering retrieval efficiency, robustness and security, user-driven interactive workflows, and multi-step complex reasoning, alongside reviews of components, evaluation, applications, and architectural variants.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are limited by static, parameter-bound knowledge and are prone to hallucination. RAG mitigates this by grounding generation in external, verifiable, up-to-date sources. Prior surveys mainly cover core RAG architectures and standard pipelines, leaving broader recent challenges and capabilities underexamined.

Method: The survey formalizes key components of the RAG framework and proposes a four-axis taxonomy: improving retrieval efficiency, strengthening robustness and security, supporting user-driven and interactive workflows, and enabling multi-step or complex reasoning. It reviews methods spanning dense and sparse retrieval, fusion strategies, embedding optimizations, and reinforcement learning-based retrieval policies, and synthesizes evaluation practices, domain-specific applications, and architectural variants (Naive, Advanced, Modular RAG).

Result: The paper delivers a structured examination of contemporary RAG developments, highlighting how advances in retrieval methods and policies influence practical deployment and system design, and consolidating evaluation practices, domain applications, and architectural variants.

Conclusion: Persistent challenges remain in retrieval quality, reliability, domain adaptation, scalability, and explainability. The survey identifies opportunities for building RAG systems that are more reliable, adaptable, and transparent.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable fluency across many tasks but remain limited by their static, parameter bound knowledge and their susceptibility to hallucinating information. Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) addresses these issues by incorporating external retrieval into the generation process, grounding model outputs in verifiable and up to date sources. While prior surveys primarily focus on core RAG architectures and standard pipelines, recent research explores broader challenges and capabilities that extend beyond these foundational designs. This survey provides a consolidated and structured examination of contemporary RAG developments, organizing the field into a four axis taxonomy: improving retrieval efficiency, strengthening robustness and security, supporting user driven and interactive workflows, and enabling multi step or complex reasoning. We formalize key components of the RAG framework and review methods spanning dense and sparse retrieval, fusion strategies, embedding optimizations, and reinforcement learning based retrieval policies, highlighting how these advances influence practical deployment and system design. We also synthesize evaluation practices, domain specific applications, and architectural variants such as Naive, Advanced, and Modular RAG. Finally, we outline persistent challenges related to retrieval quality, reliability, domain adaptation, scalability, and explainability, and identify opportunities for building RAG systems that are more reliable, adaptable, and transparent.

</details>


### [217] [Counterfactual Auditing of Bias in Open-Source Large Language Models for Clinical Triage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01963)
*Manar Aljohani,Brandon Ho,Kenneth McKinley,Dennis Ren,Xuan Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A counterfactual audit of ten open-source LLMs for pediatric ESI triage shows bias sensitivity varies widely and does not track model size or medical pretraining; a fine-tuned Qwen2.5-7B was least sensitive.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ED triage is high-stakes, and demographic, socioeconomic, and system-context cues may improperly shift acuity. Open-source LLMs are attractive for privacy-preserving local clinical support, but it is unclear how counterfactual bias varies across model families, sizes, medical-domain models, and domain-adapted models.

Method: Paired counterfactual audit of ten open-source LLMs for pediatric ESI prediction. From real and handbook-style vignettes, the authors create variants altering only one injected demographic, socioeconomic, healthcare-access, behavioral, social, or system-context variable while fixing clinical presentation. Models include Qwen2.5-7B, Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct, QLoRA fine-tuned Qwen2.5-7B, MedGemma variants, MedLLaMA2-7B, GPT-OSS-20B, and GPT-OSS-120B. Metrics: any counterfactual shift, undertriage, overtriage, shifts greater than one ESI level, mean shift, and mean absolute shift.

Result: Counterfactual sensitivity varied substantially and did not consistently decrease with larger size or medical-domain pretraining. Fine-tuned Qwen2.5-7B had the lowest sensitivity (5.27% any-shift, mean absolute shift 0.0534) versus base Qwen2.5-7B (16.02%, 0.1706). Several larger or medical-domain models showed larger shifts. Stratified and correlation analyses exposed clinically important directionality and shared failure patterns hidden by aggregate rates.

Conclusion: Counterfactual auditing is a lightweight, clinically interpretable framework for comparing fairness risks across open-source LLMs prior to clinical deployment.

Abstract: Emergency department (ED) triage is a high-stakes prioritization task in which demographic, socioeconomic, and system-context information may improperly influence acuity assignment. Although open-source large language models (LLMs) are increasingly considered for local and privacy-preserving clinical decision support, it remains unclear how counterfactual bias varies across model families, sizes, medical-domain models, and domain-adapted models. We present a comparative counterfactual audit of ten open-source LLMs for pediatric Emergency Severity Index (ESI) prediction. Starting from real and handbook-style clinical vignettes, we construct paired counterfactual variants that change only one injected demographic, socioeconomic, healthcare-access, behavioral, social, or system-context variable while holding the clinical presentation fixed. Models include Qwen2.5-7B, Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct, a QLoRA fine-tuned Qwen2.5-7B, MedGemma variants, MedLLaMA2-7B, GPT-OSS-20B, and GPT-OSS-120B. We measure any counterfactual shift, undertriage, overtriage, shifts greater than one ESI level, mean shift, and mean absolute shift. Counterfactual sensitivity varied substantially and did not consistently decrease with larger model size or medical-domain pretraining. The fine-tuned Qwen2.5-7B showed the lowest overall sensitivity, with a 5.27% any-shift rate and mean absolute shift of 0.0534, versus 16.02% and 0.1706 for the base model. Several larger or medical-domain models showed more significant shifts. Stratified and correlation analyses further revealed clinically important directionality and shared failure patterns hidden by aggregate rates. These findings support counterfactual auditing as a lightweight, clinically interpretable framework for comparing fairness risks in open-source LLMs before clinical deployment.

</details>


### [218] [Can AI Oversight Be Zero Knowledge?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01995)
*Alessandro Chiesa,Ziyi Guan,Burcu Yildiz*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies whether AI outputs derived from confidential data can be verified without leaking the data. It proves that zero-knowledge interactive proofs for all oracle-aided computations are impossible in general (in the random oracle model), extending to debate. Positively, if the oracle cryptographically signs its answers, zero-knowledge verification becomes possible with efficient prover and verifier under collision-resistant hash functions, yielding an alternative scalable-oversight approach.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI systems increasingly produce outputs from confidential data (e.g., fitness-for-duty assessments from medical records, predicted drug properties from secret structures), so it is important to verify output correctness without revealing the underlying data. Prior work on verification of AI outputs via interactive proofs and debate for oracle-aided computation focuses on a verifier running much faster than the computation; this is impossible in general and requires additional assumptions. The authors instead target privacy, allowing a polynomial-time verifier and asking whether interactive arguments for oracle-aided computation can be zero knowledge, so the verifier learns nothing beyond output correctness.

Method: The authors analyze interactive arguments for oracle-aided computation in the random oracle model, considering zero-knowledge with both prover and verifier allowed to run much longer than the computation itself, and extend the analysis to the debate model for scalable oversight. On the positive side, they design a protocol where the oracle attaches a cryptographic signature to each answer, enabling zero-knowledge verification assuming only collision-resistant hash functions.

Result: They prove a general impossibility: in the random oracle model, there are no zero-knowledge proofs for all oracle-aided computations, even when both prover and verifier run much longer than the computation, and the impossibility extends to debate. Conversely, if the oracle cryptographically signs each answer, every oracle-aided computation can be verified in zero knowledge with an efficient prover and verifier, assuming only collision-resistant hash functions.

Conclusion: Zero-knowledge verification of general oracle-aided AI computations is impossible without additional structure, but becomes feasible when the oracle provides cryptographic signatures. Beyond privacy, this signed-oracle approach offers a scalable-oversight method that relies neither on an honest opponent (as in debate) nor on the robustness of the computation (as in prior single-prover protocols).

Abstract: AI systems increasingly produce outputs from confidential data, such as a fitness-for-duty assessment from medical records or the predicted properties of a drug candidate from its secret structure. It is important to verify that such outputs are correct without revealing the underlying data. A recent line of work studies verification of AI outputs via interactive proofs and debate for oracle-aided computation, where correctness may depend on an oracle such as human judgment, a physical experiment, or the web. These works focus on verification by a verifier that runs much faster than the computation. However, such efficient verification is impossible for general oracle-aided computation, and these works therefore rely on additional assumptions. We focus instead on privacy: allowing the verifier to run in time polynomial in the computation, we ask whether interactive arguments for oracle-aided computation can be zero knowledge, so that the verifier learns nothing about the confidential data beyond the correctness of the output.
  We prove that, in general, they cannot. In the random oracle model, there are no zero-knowledge proofs for all oracle-aided computations, even if both the prover and the verifier are allowed to run much longer than the computation itself. The impossibility extends to debate, a canonical model for scalable oversight.
  On the positive side, we show that if the oracle attaches a cryptographic signature to each of its answers, then every oracle-aided computation can be verified in zero knowledge with an efficient prover and verifier, assuming only collision-resistant hash functions. Beyond privacy, this also gives an alternative approach to scalable oversight that relies neither on an honest opponent, as in debate, nor on the robustness of the computation, as in prior single-prover protocols.

</details>


### [219] [Mingbird: A Local-First Agent Harness Enabling Small Open Models to Complete Real Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02001)
*Hao Wang,Ting Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Small open-weight models often fail real agentic tasks not because of model limits but because of the harness; the proposed local-first harness Mingbird, with ten failure-targeted mechanisms, substantially outperforms existing harnesses on a controlled benchmark and a third-party benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Small open-weight models (2-9B) run on ordinary laptops, but under cloud-scale agent harnesses they rarely complete real tasks: tool prefill overflows the context, self-correction diverges, tool demonstrations loop, and tasks are silently abandoned. The authors hypothesize that a substantial share of these failures is attributable to the harness rather than the model.

Method: Controlled single-machine comparison plus one third-party benchmark. Mingbird is a local-first agent harness for Windows and Ollama whose ten mechanisms compensate point-by-point for small-model failure forms, including a byte-level net-zero prefill budget, a finish gate that re-reads the task before accepting completion, and signature-level loop detection. Evaluation uses LRAB (4 harnesses x 4 open models (2B-35B) x 18 real tasks, deterministic artifact scoring, budgets and scoring fixed) and tau^2-bench (278 tasks, three arms, one protocol), plus a frontier-model probe on the same 18 tasks and a leave-one-mechanism-out ablation.

Result: On LRAB, Mingbird reaches 0.886 overall versus 0.631 (goose), 0.479 (opencode), and 0.405 (agent-mini), with all 288 cells published. On tau^2-bench it totals 0.856 versus 0.791 and 0.737. A frontier-model probe on the same 18 tasks spans 0.997 to 0.478 across harnesses, with well-formed scaffolds staying within 0.072 of each other. The leave-one-mechanism-out ablation is directional only: same-night replications move an arm's mean by up to 0.069, the size of every nominal single-trial delta; the one batch-matched comparison (full mechanism stack versus text re-read alone) gives the executable completion guards a paired +0.10 across three replications.

Conclusion: A substantial share of small-model agent failures is attributable to the harness rather than the model, and a local-first harness purpose-built to compensate for small-model failure forms yields large gains. The evidence carries stated limits: a self-built benchmark, a single machine, and single-trial scoring.

Abstract: Small open-weight models (2-9B) run on ordinary laptops, but under cloud-scale agent harnesses they rarely complete real tasks: tool prefill overflows the context, self-correction diverges, tool demonstrations loop, and tasks are silently abandoned. We present evidence, from a controlled single-machine comparison and one third-party benchmark, that a substantial share of these failures is attributable to the harness rather than the model. We introduce Mingbird, a local-first agent harness for Windows and Ollama whose ten mechanisms compensate point-by-point for small-model failure forms, three of them representative: a byte-level net-zero prefill budget, a finish gate that re-reads the task before accepting completion, and signature-level loop detection. On LRAB, a controlled comparison holding machine, models, budgets, and scoring fixed (4 harnesses $\times$ 4 open models (2B-35B) $\times$ 18 real tasks, deterministic artifact scoring), Mingbird reaches 0.886 overall against 0.631 (goose), 0.479 (opencode), and 0.405 (agent-mini), with all 288 cells published; on $τ^2$-bench (278 tasks, three arms, one protocol) it totals 0.856 against 0.791 and 0.737; and a frontier-model probe on the same 18 tasks spans 0.997 to 0.478 across harnesses, with well-formed scaffolds staying within 0.072 of each other. A leave-one-mechanism-out ablation is reported as directional only: same-night replications of the same arm move its mean by up to 0.069, the size of every nominal single-trial delta, and the one batch-matched comparison (full mechanism stack versus text re-read alone) gives the executable completion guards a paired +0.10 across three replications. The evidence carries stated limits: a self-built benchmark, a single machine, and single-trial scoring.

</details>


### [220] [Counting Moves, Weighing Voices: Bayesian Dialectical Argumentation for Calibrated Multi-LLM Councils under Persistent Adversaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02005)
*Ionel Eduard Stan,Paolo Napoletano*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BDA recasts multi-LLM council aggregation as reliability estimation: it treats deliberation moves (propose/challenge/concede) as observations of a classical annotator model with per-agent reliabilities, producing calibrated posterior probabilities over answers and inverting persistently unreliable agents instead of merely outvoting them.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-LLM councils should output confidence that represents a calibrated probability of correctness and stay robust when some agents are persistently unreliable. Existing council aggregation methods fail on both: their confidence measures decisiveness rather than correctness, and they cannot identify or discount persistently unreliable agents.

Method: Bayesian Dialectical Argumentation (BDA) treats the council's typed moves—who proposed, challenged, or conceded which answer—as observations of a classical annotator model with per-agent reliabilities. This recasts multi-agent deliberation as a reliability estimation problem, using the deliberation trace to infer agent reliability under persistent adversarial behavior. Evidence is then weighted by inferred reliability to yield calibrated posterior probabilities over candidate answers.

Result: Across binary and multi-class benchmarks, BDA achieves the best calibration among zero-cost council aggregation methods, requires no additional LLM calls, improves robustness under persistent adversarial coalitions, and remains competitive in clean settings.

Conclusion: Modeling deliberation as a reliability estimation problem with per-agent reliabilities enables calibrated, robust council aggregation. Persistently unreliable agents can be inverted rather than merely outvoted, at no extra LLM cost.

Abstract: A multi-LLM \emph{council} lets several large language models (LLMs) deliberate on a question and return an answer together with a confidence estimate. As these systems become increasingly used for reasoning, that confidence should represent a calibrated \emph{probability of being correct}, and the decision should remain robust when some agents are persistently unreliable. Existing \emph{council aggregation} methods fail on both fronts: their confidence estimates measure decisiveness rather than correctness, and they cannot identify or discount persistently unreliable agents. We introduce Bayesian Dialectical Argumentation (BDA), which treats the council's \emph{typed} moves---who proposed, challenged, or conceded which answer---as observations of a classical annotator model with \emph{per-agent} reliabilities. This formulation recasts multi-agent deliberation as a reliability estimation problem, using the deliberation trace to infer agent reliability under persistent adversarial behavior. By weighting evidence according to inferred agent reliability, BDA yields calibrated posterior probabilities over candidate answers while allowing persistently unreliable agents to be inverted rather than merely outvoted. Across binary and multi-class benchmarks, BDA achieves the best calibration among zero-cost council aggregation methods, requiring no additional LLM calls, and improves robustness under persistent adversarial coalitions while remaining competitive in clean settings.

</details>


### [221] [Atoms to Processes: The Role of Artificial Intelligence and Machine Learning in Chemical Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02014)
*Michael Baldea,Linda J. Broadbelt,Marianthi G. Ierapetritou,Akhilesh Jain,Ankur Kumar,Thomas A. Kwan,Fèlix Llovell,Andrew J. Medford,Ilias Mitrai,Joel Paulson,Junyi Qiao,Matthew P. Rivera,Kirti C. Sahu,Lev Sarkisov,Zachary P. Smith,Calvin Tsay,Ching-Mei Wen,Victor M. Zavala,Huacheng Zhang,Dan Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AI/ML are transforming chemical engineering across scales; the key trend is a shift from black-box models to hybrid, physics-informed frameworks that respect conservation laws and thermodynamic consistency, amplifying rather than replacing first-principles chemical engineering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rapid maturation of AI/ML, plus advances in computing, data availability, and learning algorithms, has created both the opportunity and the need to reassess how chemical engineering problems are formulated, analyzed, and solved, and to address persistent challenges such as predictive accuracy, data scarcity, extrapolation, interpretability, and model lifecycle management.

Method: A perspective/survey approach: the authors review recent methodological developments and representative applications across domains (atomic-scale simulation, materials and catalyst discovery, transport and thermodynamics, separations, process systems engineering, industrial operations), emphasizing integration of AI/ML with first-principles models.

Result: AI/ML methods now impact a broad range of chemical engineering applications, and a unifying cross-domain trend is observed: movement away from purely black-box approaches toward hybrid and physics-informed frameworks that explicitly enforce conservation laws, thermodynamic consistency, and known structural constraints, improving robustness and reliability and enabling human-AI collaboration at appropriate levels of abstraction.

Conclusion: AI and ML do not replace the core principles of chemical engineering but amplify them. As the field moves toward autonomous, adaptive, and sustainable systems, thoughtful integration of AI/ML with first-principles understanding and domain expertise is essential to realizing their full potential in research and industrial practice.

Abstract: The rapid maturation of artificial intelligence (AI) and machine learning (ML) has catalyzed a profound shift in how chemical engineering problems are formulated, analyzed, and solved. Advances in computing, data availability, and learning algorithms have enabled AI/ML methods to impact applications spanning atomic-scale simulations, materials and catalyst discovery, transport and thermodynamics, separations, process systems engineering, and industrial operations. This article provides a perspective on recent methodological developments and representative applications, emphasizing how AI/ML tools are being integrated with first-principles models to address challenges of predictive accuracy, data scarcity, extrapolation, interpretability, and model lifecycle management. Across domains, a unifying trend is the move away from purely black-box approaches toward hybrid and physics-informed frameworks that explicitly respect conservation laws, thermodynamic consistency, and known structural constraints. These approaches not only improve robustness and reliability, but also enable meaningful human-AI collaboration by providing information at an appropriate level of abstraction for the task and decision context. We conclude that AI and ML are not replacing the core principles of chemical engineering; rather, they are amplifying them. As the field advances toward increasingly autonomous, adaptive, and sustainable systems, the thoughtful integration of AI/ML with first-principles understanding and domain expertise will be essential to realizing their full potential across both research and industrial practice.

</details>


### [222] [SPHERE: Adaptive VR Indoor Scene Generation via LLM-Enhanced Spatial Preference Learning and Human-in-the-Loop RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02023)
*Hyeonmin Lee,Zheng Wei,Kyungmin Kwon,Jumin Seo,Jiwon Park,Hayoung Oh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SPHERE is an adaptive VR framework that turns isolated 3D scene synthesis into continuous human-AI co-creation by learning persistent user spatial preferences from multimodal interactions and refining them via human-in-the-loop RL.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM-based 3D indoor scene synthesis pipelines do not retain user-specific preferences across sessions, making immersive authoring repetitive and physically fatiguing.

Method: SPHERE extracts persistent spatial preferences from natural multimodal interactions (speech and controller edits). It abstracts raw edits into hierarchical constraints capturing local functional and global topological contexts for geometric resilience against spatial distortions, and uses a human-in-the-loop reinforcement learning mechanism that dynamically updates retrieval policies based on the user's final edited scenes.

Result: A mixed-design user study (N=42) and an offline ablation show SPHERE significantly reduces corrective edits and physical demand, avoiding bias toward shallow object-level traits and yielding geometrically resilient, profile-aligned layouts.

Conclusion: Capturing demonstrated spatial logic enables controlled spatial adaptation, establishing a reliable, governed human-AI collaboration framework for immersive authoring. Project page and source code will be available at https://github.com/hyeonmin11/SPHERE.

Abstract: While Large Language Models (LLMs) advance 3D indoor scene synthesis, current pipelines fail to retain user-specific preferences across sessions, making immersive authoring a repetitive and physically fatiguing process. We present SPHERE, an adaptive VR generation framework that transforms isolated synthesis into continuous human-AI co-creation. SPHERE extracts persistent spatial preferences from natural multimodal interactions (speech and controller edits). To ensure geometric resilience against spatial distortions, it abstracts these raw edits into hierarchical constraints modeling both local functional and global topological contexts. Furthermore, a human-in-the-loop reinforcement learning mechanism dynamically updates retrieval policies based on the user's final edited scenes. A mixed-design user study ($N=42$) and an offline ablation demonstrate that SPHERE significantly reduces corrective edits and physical demand, preventing bias toward shallow object-level traits to yield geometrically resilient, profile-aligned layouts. Ultimately, SPHERE demonstrates how capturing demonstrated spatial logic enables controlled spatial adaptation, establishing a reliable, governed human-AI collaboration framework for immersive authoring. Project page and source code will be available at: https://github.com/hyeonmin11/SPHERE

</details>


### [223] [Global Coherence: When Every Agent Is Right and the Team Is Still Wrong - A Local-to-Global Semantic Foundation for Multi-Agent Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02036)
*Xin Heng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AI agents can make locally valid decisions that jointly yield invalid results, a "global coherence problem" stemming from missing shared state rather than weak reasoning. The paper proves an Observation-Aliasing Impossibility Theorem setting the exact boundary of what any policy can guarantee, then introduces local-to-global runtime semantics X = (H, C, G, F; D) where a harness owns shared state. Nine studies confirm that smarter models cannot recover hidden global state, while harness-level commit enforcement and authoritative facts restore correctness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-agent and single-agent systems can each make locally valid decisions yet jointly produce invalid outcomes. The authors frame this as a global coherence problem: a failure of shared state, not merely of model intelligence. Existing approaches implicitly assume that more reasoning, roles, messages, or samples can fix coordination failures, but the paper argues the true bottleneck is missing global state that no amount of local computation can reconstruct.

Method: 1) A formal Observation-Aliasing Impossibility Theorem: a policy can guarantee a valid action exactly when all worlds producing the same observation share an admissible action; if k indistinguishable worlds require pairwise-disjoint actions, the best randomized worst-case success is 1/k. 2) Local-to-global runtime semantics X = (H, C, G, F; D): topology H records overlapping scopes, category C governs state-changing actions, groupoid G retains reversible translations, sheaf F tests whether local views glue into one world, and minimal history D keeps only distinctions that alter legal futures. 3) A harness architecture where models propose actions but the harness owns shared state and governs commit. 4) Nine studies evaluating both the failure mode and its theoretical boundary across controlled revision benchmarks, TeamBench, and tau2-bench Telecom.

Result: On a controlled revision benchmark, the same frontier model scores 40/40 when the deciding event is visible but only 12--17/40 (consistent with chance, 1/3) when hidden; restoring one authoritative fact returns 40/40. On TeamBench, ordinary teams exceed a shared budget in 5/5 runs, a visible live count leaves 4/5 violations, and commit enforcement leaves 0/5. In tau2-bench Telecom, current-state checks score 0.07 after silent reverts while the harness scores 1.00. Where a conventional solver already owns the complete relevant state, it ties the harness, as predicted.

Conclusion: The counterintuitive conclusion is that local intelligence cannot substitute for missing global state. The impossibility boundary is exact: additional reasoning, roles, messages, or samples cannot recover distinctions that observations do not contain. Correctness under global coherence requires architectural ownership of shared state and governed commits (the harness), not stronger individual models.

Abstract: AI agents can each make locally valid decisions yet jointly produce an invalid result. We call this the global coherence problem: a failure of shared state, not merely of model intelligence.
  Our Observation-Aliasing Impossibility Theorem gives the exact boundary. A policy can guarantee a valid action exactly when all worlds producing the same observation share an admissible action. If k indistinguishable worlds require pairwise-disjoint actions, the best randomized worst-case success is 1/k; more reasoning, roles, messages, or samples cannot recover the missing distinction. A stronger model can reason better within its context, but it cannot see beyond it.
  We then give local-to-global runtime semantics X = (H, C, G, F; D): topology H records overlapping scopes; category C governs state-changing actions; groupoid G retains reversible translations; sheaf F tests whether local views glue into one world; and minimal history D keeps only distinctions that alter legal futures. Models propose; the harness owns shared state and governs commit.
  Nine studies test both the failure and its boundary. On a controlled revision benchmark, the same frontier model scores 40/40 when the deciding event is visible; when it is hidden, tested arms score 12--17/40, consistent with chance (1/3); restoring one authoritative fact returns 40/40. On TeamBench, ordinary teams exceed a shared budget in 5/5 runs, a visible live count leaves 4/5 violations, and commit enforcement leaves 0/5. In tau2-bench Telecom, current-state checks score 0.07 after silent reverts, while the harness scores 1.00. Where a conventional solver already owns the complete relevant state, it ties the harness as predicted. The counterintuitive conclusion is that local intelligence cannot substitute for missing global state.

</details>


### [224] [Mimir: Physics-Grounded LLM Agents for Long-Horizon Irrigation Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02038)
*Yimeng Liu,Mi Zhang,Younsuk Dong,Zhichao Cao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Mimir, a physics-grounded LLM agent for long-horizon irrigation control, separates fast-timescale deterministic verification/selection from slow-timescale consolidation of failure patterns into persistent principles, achieving the lowest reported control cost with ~51% less irrigation while keeping the physical model and actuator authority immutable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most evidence for LLM agents comes from episodic tasks with immediate feedback and reset failures. Long-running physical control is a different regime: actions alter future states, errors compound, and the agent must improve from experience without rewriting the physical rules that keep execution safe. Irrigation is used as a testbed where daily decisions interact with soil-water dynamics across whole growing seasons.

Method: Mimir is organized around two repair timescales. Fast timescale: a structured physical interface and deterministic simulator convert LLM output into a proposal that is numerically checked, revised, and passed through bounded deterministic action selection before execution. Slow timescale: recurrent failure patterns are consolidated into persistent contextual principles that condition future proposals, while the physical model, evaluator, and execution constraints remain immutable.

Result: Under a common retrospective evaluator across multiple sites, crops, and years, Mimir attains the lowest reported aggregate control cost among evaluated references and uses about 51% less irrigation than historical schedule replay. Ablations show higher control cost when forward simulation, verified revision, or persistent context is removed. Model-scale and model-family studies show no monotonic gain from increasing LLM size.

Conclusion: Persistent physical agents can combine semantic reasoning with bounded, evidence-driven self-improvement while reserving physical truth and actuator authority for explicit numerical mechanisms.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly combine reasoning, tool use, and action, but most evidence comes from episodic tasks with relatively immediate feedback and reset failures. Long-running physical control operates in a different regime: actions alter future states, errors compound across decisions, and an agent must improve from experience without being allowed to rewrite the physical rules that make execution safe. We study this regime through irrigation, where daily decisions interact with soil-water dynamics over entire growing seasons. We present Mimir, a physics-grounded LLM agent organized around two repair timescales. At the fast timescale, a structured physical interface and deterministic simulator turn an LLM output into a proposal that we numerically check, revise, and subject to bounded deterministic action selection before execution. At the slow timescale, recurrent failure patterns are consolidated into persistent contextual principles that condition future proposals, while the physical model, evaluator, and execution constraints remain immutable. Under a common retrospective evaluator across multiple sites, crops, and years, Mimir attains the lowest reported aggregate control cost among the evaluated references and uses about 51% less irrigation than the historical schedule replay. The ablation study show higher control cost when forward simulation, verified revision, or persistent context is removed; model-scale and model-family studies show no monotonic gain from increasing LLM size. The resulting lesson show that persistent physical agents can combine semantic reasoning with bounded, evidence-driven self-improvement while reserving physical truth and actuator authority for explicit numerical mechanisms.

</details>


### [225] [HydroJEV: A one-second, training-free screen for cyber-attack and fault attribution in water distribution networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02048)
*Tianwei Mu,Shengyan Jiang,Mingzhe Yuan,Qing Luo,Min Xiao,Wenhong Wang,Jun Li,Manhong Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Jev, a training-free model returning class probabilities in about one second, was evaluated as a first-tier triage screen for SCADA alarm cause attribution and matched a rule tree, exceeded a supervised classifier on unseen event subtypes, and ran 20-40x faster than frontier LLMs while offloading ~35-38% of review load without accuracy loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: When a SCADA alarm is raised in a water distribution network, operators must quickly decide whether it reflects a cyberattack, physical fault, normal transient, or faulty sensor. Supervised classifiers require labelled incidents that utilities rarely have, and frontier LLMs take tens of seconds per decision, making fast, label-free triage necessary.

Method: A four-class cause-attribution benchmark was built on the C-Town network in EPANET. Jev, a training-free model, was compared against a hand-written rule tree, a supervised classifier, and seven cloud LLMs on identical evidence across four sealed, pre-registered rounds, using only a label-free prior correction. A gated cascade was formed by accepting only benign Jev verdicts confirmed by the rule tree, then transferred unchanged to two further networks.
