Result: Jev matched the rule tree (macro-F1 0.62-0.64 vs 0.56-0.61 in distribution) and exceeded the supervised classifier by 0.36-0.42 on event subtypes absent from its labels, in all four rounds, and outperformed the classifier whenever fewer than about four labelled events per class were available. Jev decided 20-40 times faster than frontier LLMs. The gated cascade spared an LLM reviewer 35-38% of windows on fresh sealed sets without loss of macro-F1, and on two further networks it stayed within the non-inferiority margin of its reviewer on all four sets.

Conclusion: A fast, training-free screen can take over about a third of the review load in SCADA anomaly triage while preserving the accuracy of deliberate review.

Abstract: When a SCADA alarm is raised in a water distribution network, operators must decide quickly whether it reflects a cyberattack, a physical fault, a normal transient or a faulty sensor. Supervised classifiers need labelled incidents that utilities rarely have, and frontier large language models (LLMs) take tens of seconds per decision. We tested whether Jev, a training-free model that returns class probabilities in about one second, can serve as the first tier of this triage. On a four-class cause-attribution benchmark built on the C-Town network in EPANET, Jev was compared with a hand-written rule tree, a supervised classifier and seven cloud LLMs on identical evidence in four sealed, pre-registered rounds. With only a label-free prior correction, Jev matched the rule tree (macro-F1 0.62-0.64 against 0.56-0.61 in distribution) and exceeded the supervised classifier by 0.36-0.42 on event subtypes absent from its labels, in all four rounds, and it outperformed the classifier whenever fewer than about four labelled events per class were available. Jev also decided 20-40 times faster than frontier LLMs. Accepting only benign Jev verdicts confirmed by the rule tree spared an LLM reviewer 35-38% of windows on fresh sealed sets without loss of macro-F1. Transferred unchanged to two further networks, this gated cascade stayed within the non-inferiority margin of its reviewer on all four sets. A fast, training-free screen can therefore take over about a third of the review load in SCADA anomaly triage while preserving the accuracy of deliberate review.

</details>


### [226] [External Observers May See More Clearly: Cross-Model Span-Level Hallucination Detection in Large Language Models via Hidden State Probing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02066)
*Kingshuk Gupta,Davide Buscaldi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes an internal hidden-state framework for span-level (fine-grained, sequential) hallucination detection in LLMs, isolating hallucination onset and continuation tokens via layer-wise activation probes, and introduces a cross-model detection setup where one model observes another's internal states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly used as reasoning engines but remain prone to hallucination. Existing internal state probes are faster than external retrieval but reduce hallucination detection to token-wise binary classification, which fails to capture the structured, sequential boundaries of semantic drift.

Method: The authors inspect layer-wise activation patterns to detect the exact hallucination onset and continuation tokens during generation, framing detection as a span-level task rather than token-wise classification. They further propose a cross-model detection framework in which an observer model inspects the internal representations elicited by another model's generation.

Result: The approach successfully isolates hallucination onsets and achieves substantial improvements in Precision-Recall AUC over random baselines despite extreme class imbalance. In the cross-model setting, an external observer matches or exceeds a generator's self-detection of its own hallucination onsets—even when the observer is the smaller model.

Conclusion: Internal hidden states support fine-grained, span-level hallucination detection, and self-detection is not the ceiling for onset localisation, since a separate (potentially smaller) observer model can perform as well or better.

Abstract: As Large Language Models (LLMs) increasingly serve as foundational reasoning engines, their tendency to hallucinate remains a critical vulnerability. While recent internal state probes offer a promising alternative to slow external retrieval systems, they largely reduce hallucination detection to a token-wise binary classification task, failing to capture the structured, sequential boundaries of semantic drift. Here, we introduce an internal hidden state framework for fine-grained, span-level hallucination detection. By inspecting layer-wise activation patterns, we attempt to detect the exact hallucination onset and continuation tokens in an LLM generation. Our experiments show that this approach successfully isolates hallucination onsets, achieving substantial improvements in Precision-Recall AUC over random baselines despite extreme class imbalance. Ultimately, we propose a novel cross-model detection framework in which one model observes the internal representations elicited by another model's generation. We find that an external observer can match or exceed a generator's self-detection of its own hallucination onsets, including when the observer is the smaller model, suggesting that self-detection is not the ceiling for onset localisation.

</details>


### [227] [Causal Memory Policy: Making Memory Utility Identifiable by Intervening on Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02070)
*Arman Behnam,Binghui Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper identifies a retrieval-level positivity violation that renders memory utility unidentifiable when memories are never retrieved, and proposes Causal Memory Policy (CMP), a propensity-based intervention on retrieval that restores identification, proves unbiasedness, and improves discrimination of required vs. non-required memories from 0.54 to 0.66 AUC.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing memory-augmented LLM systems estimate each memory's effect on task performance to decide what to retain, but these estimates rely entirely on retrieved memories. When a memory is never retrieved, store-level interventions yield identical outcomes, so its utility is unidentified—a retrieval-level positivity violation that is invisible to diagnostics examining only memory operations. This creates both a causal identification failure and a practical evaluation blind spot.

Method: The authors introduce Causal Memory Policy (CMP), a causal framework that restores identification by intervening on retrieval itself. CMP reserves a fixed number of context slots for memories sampled with known propensities, forming a balanced assignment design, and estimates memory utility via self-normalized inverse propensity weighting. Theoretically, they prove the causal factorization of memory utility through retrieval, the unbiasedness and exact variance of the estimator, and the optimal decision rule under irreversible operations.

Result: Identification fails for 54% of required memories on LongMemEval and 67% on LoCoMo, and the failure persists in a deployed memory system. CMP improves discrimination between required and non-required memories from 0.54 to 0.66 AUC. Additionally, identified memory utility alone is insufficient for retention decisions: per-query utility reaches 0.78 AUC on the query for which it is estimated, yet no aggregation available to a retention policy predicts a memory's value on unseen queries.

Conclusion: Retrieval policies, not just memory stores, must be part of the causal estimand for memory retention. Propensity-based interventions on retrieval restore identification and improve discrimination, but a gap remains: identified utility is query-specific and does not transfer to unseen queries. Future retention policies need mechanisms that generalize utility across queries rather than relying on aggregation of per-query effects.

Abstract: Memory-augmented large language models must decide which memories to retain, and recent systems do so by estimating each memory's effect on task performance. However, these estimates rely entirely on retrieved memories. When a memory is never retrieved, store-level interventions produce identical outcomes, leaving its utility unidentified. This is a retrieval-level positivity violation, invisible to diagnostics that examine only memory operations. We introduce Causal Memory Policy (CMP), a causal framework that restores identification by intervening on retrieval itself, reserving a fixed number of context slots for memories sampled with known propensities. CMP estimates memory utility by self-normalized inverse propensity weighting under a balanced assignment design. We prove the causal factorization of memory utility through retrieval, the unbiasedness and exact variance of the estimator, and the optimal decision rule under irreversible operations. Empirically, identification fails for 54% of required memories on LongMemEval and 67% on LoCoMo, and the failure persists in a deployed memory system. CMP improves discrimination between required and non-required memories from 0.54 to 0.66 AUC. Finally, we show that identified memory utility alone is insufficient for retention decisions: per-query utility reaches 0.78 AUC on the query for which it is estimated, yet no aggregation available to a retention policy predicts a memory's value on unseen queries. Code is available at: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/cmp-release-D0C3/.

</details>


### [228] [PyPottery: an AI-powered end-to-end suite for pottery processing and publication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02072)
*Lorenzo Cardarelli*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PyPottery is an open-source, AI-powered suite of four integrated modules that semi-automates the entire ceramic documentation workflow, from scanning and inking to tracing and layout, achieving a median perceived 40× speedup over traditional methods on a case study of 240 pottery drawings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pottery documentation is central to archaeological research, but the post-production workflow is labor-intensive and creates publication bottlenecks. There is a need for AI-assisted tools that augment rather than fully automate the archaeologist's work.

Method: The authors developed PyPottery, an open-source AI-powered suite with four integrated modules: PyPotteryScan (automated image extraction and handwriting recognition), PyPotteryInk (automatic inking of pencil drawings), PyPotteryTrace (semantically-aware vectorization), and PyPotteryLayout (automated layout generation). It was evaluated on 50 hand-drawn sheets containing 240 pottery drawings from Terramara di Montale (Italy), complemented by a usability study.

Result: The framework delivered substantial time savings. Usability study participants reported a median perceived speedup of 40× over traditional workflows, with a range of 17.5× to 120×.

Conclusion: AI-assisted tools show strong potential for archaeological documentation. The paper emphasizes strategic redistribution of cognitive labor toward augmentation rather than full automation of the documentation process.

Abstract: The study of ceramic materials constitutes a cornerstone of archaeological research, yet the post-production workflow for pottery documentation remains labor-intensive and creates significant publication bottlenecks. This paper presents PyPottery, an open-source, AI-powered suite designed to semi-automate the complete ceramic documentation pipeline. The suite comprises four integrated modules: PyPotteryScan for automated image extraction and handwriting recognition; PyPotteryInk for automatic inking of pencil drawings; PyPotteryTrace for semantically-aware vectorization; and PyPotteryLayout for automated layout generation. Evaluated on 50 hand-drawn sheets containing 240 pottery drawings from the Terramara di Montale (Italy), the framework achieved substantial time savings confirmed by usability study participants, who reported a median perceived speedup of 40$\times$ over traditional workflows (range: 17.5$\times$--120$\times$). These results highlight the potential of AI-assisted tools in archaeological documentation, while the paper addresses the strategic redistribution of cognitive labor toward augmentation rather than automation.

</details>


### [229] [Homomorphic Advantage Operator: Stabilizing Reinforcement Learning Under Fully Homomorphic Encryption Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02074)
*Abid Mohamed Nadhir,Ahmad Al Hanbali,Beggas Mounir*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces the Homomorphic Advantage Operator (HAO), a stabilization framework that prevents polynomial approximation divergence in FHE-based deep RL by applying a zero-mean centering projection to TD targets, achieving 0% boundary breaches and improved policy accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: FHE enables secure cloud computation for privacy-preserving machine learning, but applying it to RL requires replacing non-linear operations with polynomial approximations. These approximations diverge catastrophically due to a recursive error phenomenon called Bellman drift, creating a deployment challenge for confidential intelligent systems.

Method: HAO adapts the zero-mean centering projection from advantage-based value estimation directly to temporal-difference (TD) targets. This linear projection annihilates the uniform state-value baseline that drives Bellman drift, preserving per-state action rankings while adding zero non-linear multiplicative depth and avoiding ciphertext bootstrapping. Evaluation used a three-tier methodology: a tabular MDP, an encrypted CartPole environment with real CKKS operations, and a 20-node logistics routing benchmark with dense continuous features.

Result: HAO strictly bounds network pre-activations within the safe polynomial approximation domain, achieving 0% boundary breaches across all random seeds. Regularization alone (L2 weight decay and gradient clipping) breached the bound on 3 of 5 seeds, and the unstabilized baseline breached it in 83.8% of episodes. HAO improved optimal policy accuracy by 18.0 percentage points in tabular domains and remained stable under DP-SGD-style Gaussian noise added to clipped gradients.

Conclusion: HAO provides an effective stabilization mechanism for FHE-based deep RL, preventing polynomial approximation divergence without additional multiplicative depth or bootstrapping, and maintaining stability even under added noise.

Abstract: Privacy-preserving machine learning presents significant deployment challenges on the cloud for intelligent systems with confidential data. Fully Homomorphic Encryption (FHE) offers a compelling solution for secure computation, preserving data confidentiality of cloud computations. However, applying FHE to reinforcement learning (RL) requires replacing non-linear operations with polynomial approximations, which diverge catastrophically due to a unique recursive error phenomenon known as the Bellman drift. This article introduces the Homomorphic Advantage Operator (HAO), a stabilization framework designed to prevent polynomial approximation divergence in FHE-based deep RL. HAO adapts the zero-mean centering projection from advantage-based value estimation directly to temporal-difference (TD) targets. This linear projection annihilates the uniform state-value baseline that drives the Bellman drift, maintaining per-state action rankings while requiring zero additional non-linear multiplicative depth and avoiding expensive ciphertext bootstrapping. The proposed HAO framework was evaluated using a three-tier experimental methodology, including a tabular Markov Decision Process (MDP), an encrypted CartPole environment using real CKKS cryptographic operations, and a 20-node logistics routing benchmark with dense continuous features. The results demonstrate that the proposed HAO strictly bounds network pre-activations within the safe polynomial approximation domain. The proposed HAO RL agents achieved 0% boundary breaches across all random seeds used, whereas regularization alone (L2 weight decay and gradient clipping) breached the bound on 3 of 5 seeds and the unstabilized baseline did so in 83.8% of episodes. Finally, HAO agents improve optimal policy accuracy by 18.0 percentage points in tabular domains and remain stable when DP-SGD-style Gaussian noise is added to the clipped gradients.

</details>


### [230] [A Comparative Explainability Framework for DeBERTa-v3 in Zero-Shot Medical Abstract Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02116)
*Javier Diaz Esteban-Herreros,David Muñoz-Valero,Raquel Martínez-España,Jose M. Juarez,Juan Moreno-Garcia*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A comparative explainability framework audits DeBERTa-v3 in zero-shot classification of medical abstracts, comparing five attribution methods. Explanation stability mirrors predictive certainty, and three systemic failure mechanisms are identified, supporting multi-method agreement auditing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses the disagreement problem in Explainable AI, where different attribution methods produce divergent explanations for the same input and prediction, a critical issue when auditing transformer-based models in high-stakes medical text classification.

Method: A natural language inference engine over the Medical Abstracts corpus uses five enriched hypotheses per diagnostic category and a balanced sample of 1,000 texts per class. Five explanation methods are compared: SHAP and LIME (model-agnostic), occlusion and Input x Gradient (deep-learning-specific), and Attention x Gradient (transformer-specific). Explanations are standardized via top-token attribution, with pairwise agreement quantified by the Jaccard index.

Result: High predictive accuracy is achieved in well-defined clinical domains, degrading under high semantic ambiguity. Explanatory stability mirrors predictive certainty, showing strong convergence in univalent categories and a marked drop under diagnostic uncertainty. Qualitative error auditing reveals three systemic failure mechanisms: lexical hypersensitivity, semantic overlap, and loss of attribution coherence.

Conclusion: The results support combining multiple explanation methods with quantitative agreement metrics when auditing transformer-based medical text classification, and recommend prioritizing specific clinical ontologies over broad diagnostic labels.

Abstract: A comparative explainability framework is presented to audit DeBERTa-v3 under zero-shot classification of medical abstracts. The work addresses the disagreement problem in Explainable Artificial Intelligence, where different attribution methods produce divergent explanations for the same input and prediction. A natural language inference engine is implemented over the Medical Abstracts corpus with five enriched hypotheses per diagnostic category and a balanced sample of one thousand texts per class. Five explanation methods are compared: SHAP and LIME as model-agnostic approaches, occlusion and Input x Gradient as deep-learning-specific approaches, and Attention x Gradient as a transformer-specific approach. Explanations are standardized through top-token attribution, and pairwise agreement is quantified using the Jaccard index. High predictive accuracy is achieved across well-defined clinical domains, whereas performance degrades under high semantic ambiguity. Explanatory stability directly mirrors predictive certainty, exhibiting strong convergence in univalent categories and a marked drop under diagnostic uncertainty. Furthermore, qualitative error auditing uncovers three systemic failure mechanisms: lexical hypersensitivity, semantic overlap, and loss of attribution coherence. The results support the combined use of several explanation methods and quantitative agreement metrics when auditing transformer-based models in medical text classification, and suggest prioritizing specific clinical ontologies over broad diagnostic labels.

</details>


### [231] [VISTA: A Visual Harness for Reasoning in an Interactive World](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02200)
*Qiushi Han,Keya Hu,Linlu Qiu,Cathy Wu,Kaiming He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VISTA is a visual harness that equips general-purpose multimodal models with long-horizon vision via lossless visual memory and active retrieval, achieving perfect Relative Human Action Efficiency on ARC-AGI-3 and strong gains across four benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal models have strong latent reasoning abilities, but their potential is bottlenecked by inadequate scaffolding. Existing harnesses degrade visual information over long horizons, limiting performance in complex interactive visual environments.

Method: VISTA lets a model perceive the environment directly through visual observations and maintains a lossless visual memory storing past observations in original form. The model actively retrieves these observations and reorganizes its visual input during reasoning. The design is simple and transfers to varied environments with minimal adaptation.

Result: On ARC-AGI-3, VISTA raises Claude Opus 5.0's Relative Human Action Efficiency from 40.68 to 100.00, completing all 25 public games with 57.4% fewer actions than first-time human participants. On three additional benchmarks spanning visual games and puzzles, it substantially outperforms minimally-harnessed baselines using the same base model.

Conclusion: A simple visual harness with lossless visual memory and active retrieval unlocks multimodal models' reasoning in long-horizon interactive tasks, positioning VISTA as a general-purpose harness for advancing multimodal agents in complex visual environments.

Abstract: We show that multimodal models possess strong reasoning abilities and that an appropriate harness can unlock their potential to solve tasks across diverse interactive environments. We introduce VISTA, a visual harness that gives a general-purpose multimodal model long-horizon vision. VISTA allows the model to directly perceive the environment through visual observations and maintains a lossless visual memory that preserves past observations in their original form. The model can actively retrieve these observations and reorganize its visual input as it reasons. On ARC-AGI-3, VISTA improves Claude Opus 5.0's Relative Human Action Efficiency score from 40.68 to a perfect 100.00, with the model completing all 25 public games using 57.4% fewer actions than first-time human participants. VISTA's simple design also allows it to extend naturally to diverse visual environments with minimal adaptation. Across three additional benchmarks covering a diverse range of visual games and puzzles, it substantially outperforms baselines using the same underlying model with minimal harnesses. Our results highlight VISTA's potential as a general-purpose visual harness for advancing multimodal agents in complex visual environments.

</details>


### [232] [ScholarCatalyst: A Benchmark for Retrieving Papers That Inspire New Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02202)
*Sohyeon Kim,Yoonho Lee,Bo Liu,Dayoon Ko,Rulin Shao,Seungone Kim,Graham Neubig,Pang Wei Koh,Aakanksha Chowdhery,Akari Asai,Omar Khattab,Yejin Choi,Gunhee Kim,Chelsea Finn*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ScholarCatalyst introduces a retrieval benchmark built from author annotations of 207 recent CS papers, testing whether systems can identify the prior work that advanced a project. Current agentic search and LLM-based agents fail to beat simple embedding retrieval, underscoring the gap in "expert intuition" for literature search.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Great scientists excel at sensing which buried prior idea a new problem needs—a skill AI systems lack. The paper aims to formalize and measure this ability, moving toward scientific agents that can connect a half-formed research idea to the prior literature it requires.

Method: An automated, scalable annotation pipeline captures author judgments: 184 lead authors of 207 recent computer science papers label which candidate works did or could have advanced their projects, each with a rationale. From this, a retrieval task is defined—given an initial research question, retrieve the relevant papers from only the literature available at the project's start.

Result: Agentic search performs no better than embedding retrieval (0.42 vs. 0.48 Recall@20), despite using the same retriever as a tool. An agent built on Claude Fable 5.1—potentially exposed to the completed papers during training—reaches only 0.51 R@20.

Conclusion: Existing search agents and even strong LLMs fall short of expert-level retrieval, highlighting the need for new training recipes that instill expert intuition for searching broad corpora. ScholarCatalyst is positioned as a step toward scientific agents capable of identifying needed prior research from incomplete ideas.

Abstract: What makes great scientists great? Even as AI systems start to make progress on open problems, scientists remain far ahead of them at sensing which prior idea, buried in an ever-growing archive of research, a new problem needs. To study this skill, we draw on researchers who know firsthand which earlier work advanced their completed projects, with papers serving as pointers to the ideas within. Using our automated pipeline that makes author annotation scalable, we build ScholarCatalyst by having 184 lead authors of 207 recent computer science papers label which candidates did or could have advanced their project, each with a detailed rationale. We introduce a retrieval task with author-provided judgments: given an initial research question, retrieve these papers from only the literature available when the project began. Agentic search does no better than embedding retrieval (0.42 vs. 0.48 Recall@20) despite calling that same retriever as a tool. Even an agent built on Claude Fable 5.1, which may have seen the completed papers during training, reaches only 0.51 R@20. These results highlight the need for new training recipes that equip models with expert intuition for searching broad corpora. We envision ScholarCatalyst as a step toward scientific agents that can take a half-formed idea and point to the prior research it needs.

</details>
